Human existence requires the fiction of a starting line. Even science, that rigorous arbiter of truth, must invent a zero-point, a specific star in its endless trek, to declare "Time is Now." Its less precise cousin, Poetry, traditionally claims to begin in the middle; yet upon closer inspection, she merely follows suit. Both divide their units into vast fractions and launch *in medias res*, racing backward as quickly as forward. No memory can reach the primal dawn; whatever prologue we spin, whether celestial or terrestrial, is but a fragment of the pre-existing reality into which our tale steps.

Was she beautiful, or was she merely striking? What alchemy within her gaze transformed static features into a dynamic force? Did benevolence or malice reign supreme in those beams? Likely the latter. If goodness ruled, why did her effect stir unrest rather than serene contentment? Why did the urge to look again feel like a compulsion, a trap, rather than a willing surrender of the soul?

The woman posing these questions in Daniel Deronda's mind was engaged in gambling. Not in the sun-drenched ruins of antiquity, tossing copper coins amidst ragged limbs, but within a temple of modern luxury. The air here was thick with gilt moldings, somber hues, and the heavy, fleshy nudes of classical art—a perfect condenser for the breath of high society, a substance unavailable to lesser sorts in equal measure.

It was nearly four in the afternoon on a September day, the atmosphere brewed into a visible, golden haze. A profound silence hung over the room, shattered only by the soft clatter of chips, the gentle sweep of a brush, and the occasional, monotonous murmur of French, sounding as if spoken by a clockwork automaton. Two long tables hosted dense clusters of humanity, almost entirely focused downward upon the felt. The sole exception was a melancholy little boy. Clad in natural skin on his legs and calves, yet dressed in a fantastical costume above the waist, he stood gazing blankly toward the entrance. He resembled a masquerade advertisement pinned to the stage of a traveling circus, positioned directly behind a lady deeply engrossed at the roulette wheel.

Around this table, fifty or sixty souls gathered. In the outer rows sat mere spectators, though occasionally a woman would slip a five-franc note onto the table with a coquettish flutter, feigning curiosity about the fever of gambling. Those fully immersed in the game displayed a kaleidoscope of European types: the stoic Livonian, the fiery Spaniard, the languid Graeco-Italian, and various Germans. There was an aristocratic Englishman and a plebeian Englishman side by side, a stark testament to human equality. Beside the jeweled, white-fingered hand of an English countess rested a bony, yellow hand, resembling a crab, stretching a bare wrist to seize a pile of gold. That hand belonged to a woman whose face was a slight mutation of a vulture: gaunt, with deep-set eyes, grizzled brows, and hair combed with a frantic, ill-mannered haste. Near her sat another figure, prematurely aged, holding a shabby velvet reticule and occasionally poking her card with the pointed tip, her lips dry as parchment. Close by stood a respectable London tradesman, soft-handed and blonde, his hair parted with surgical precision. He carried the consciousness of circulars addressed to the nobility, holidays purchased with their patronage, and a leisure born of winning money in business to spend more lavishly in play. He reflected that Providence held no grudge against his amusement, remaining dispassionate enough to cease if the sweet taste of victory curdled into the sour rot of defeat. For the sin of gambling lies not in the wager, but in the loss. Though he bore the mannerisms of a merchant, in his pleasures he ranked with ancient title-holders. Beside him stood a handsome Italian, calm and statuesque, placing a fresh stack of napoleons from a newly opened bag. Within thirty seconds, these coins slid to an elderly, bewigged dame, her glasses pinching her nose. A faint, gleaming smile touched her lips, but the Italian remained impassive, secure in a system that placed his foot upon the neck of Chance, already preparing his next pile. Nearby, a man with the air of an emaciated beau, viewing life through a single monocle, held out a trembling hand for change. Perhaps it was not a rigid strategy, but a delusion of white crows, or the superstition that the eighth of the month was auspicious, fueling his fierce, tottering impetuosity.

Yet, despite the marked differences in their faces and hands, a uniform negativity permeated the assembly. Their expressions wore the mask of those who had eaten a fruit that bound their minds to a narrow, monotonous circle of action.

Deronda's initial impression of this scene, bathed in the dull, gas-choked light, was that the gambling of Spanish shepherd-boys seemed infinitely more enviable. Rousseau might be vindicated in claiming that art and science had served mankind poorly. But suddenly, the atmosphere shifted into drama. His attention seized by a young lady standing at an angle nearby, she was the final object of his gaze. She leaned toward a middle-aged woman seated at play, speaking English, before returning to the table. Instantly, she revealed the full stature of a graceful figure, possessing a face that, while perhaps not inspiring immediate admiration, demanded indifference be impossible.

An internal debate ignited within Deronda, sharpening his gaze from vague sensation to critical scrutiny. At moments, his eyes tracked the movement of her arms and hands as this problematic sylph bent to deposit her stake with an air of firm decision. Then, they snapped back to her face, steady and unbothered by observers. The sylph was winning. As her tapering fingers, delicately gloved in pale gray, adjusted the coins offered to her, she glanced around with a survey so cold and neutral it suggested art concealing inner exultation.

Then, her eyes met Deronda's. Instead of averting them, as she would have preferred, she became acutely aware of his arrestment. How long had he studied her? A darting sense told her he viewed her as inferior, measuring her from a height, regarding her as a specimen of lower order, distinct from the "human dross" surrounding her. This roused a tingling resentment, stretching the moment into conflict. It drained the blood from her lips, leaving them pale, though her cheeks remained untouched. She steeled herself with an inward defiance, turning back to the game without further sign. To Deronda, however, her gaze felt like an evil eye. Her stake vanished. It mattered not; she had been winning steadily since arriving with a few napoleons, accumulating a considerable reserve. Visions of a *cortège* worshipping her as a goddess of fortune swirled in her mind. Male gamblers achieved such supremacy; why not a woman? Her friend and chaperon, initially opposed, began to nod approval, offering the prudent counsel to stop and return to England. Gwendolen had dismissed this, insisting she sought the excitement of play, not the winnings. Thus, the present moment should have been the flood-tide of her experience. Yet, as her next stake was swept away, she felt the heat rising in her eyes. The certainty of that man's unwavering gaze pressed upon her like a torture. She resolved not to flinch, to play on as if indifferent to loss or gain. Her friend touched her elbow, suggesting they leave. In reply, Gwendolen laid ten louis on the same spot. She existed in a mood of defiant madness, where the mind loses sight of any goal beyond enraged resistance, foolishly including luck itself as an enemy to be defied. Since she was not winning spectacularly, the next best thing was to lose spectacularly. She controlled her muscles, masking any tremor. Every time her stake disappeared, she doubled it. Many watched her now, but she registered only Deronda's stare, sure he had not moved away.

Such dramas require no lengthy staging; development and catastrophe are measured by the turn of the hand. *"Faites votre jeu, mesdames et messieurs,"* intoned the automatic voice of destiny from behind the croupier's mustache and imperial. Gwendolen's arm stretched to deposit her last, pitiful heap of napoleons. *"Le jeu ne va plus,"* declared destiny. Within five seconds, Gwendolen turned from the table, but she turned resolutely toward Deronda, fixing her eyes upon him. An iron smile played in his eyes as their gazes collided; it was preferable that he keep his attention fixed on her than dismiss her as part of an indistinguishable insect swarm. Furthermore, despite his superciliousness, it was hard to believe he did not admire her spirit as well as her person. He was young, handsome, distinguished—an anomaly among the ridiculous, dowdy Philistines who would blight a gaming table with a sour look of protest. The universal conviction that we are admirable rarely yields to a single negative judgment. When members of Vanity's vast family find their performance received coldly, they instinctively believe that a little more of themselves will convert the dissident. Gwendolen operated on the assumption that she knew what was admirable and that she possessed it. This foundation, however, had received a disagreeable concussive blow, reeling slightly but refusing to collapse.

That evening, the room grew stiflingly hot, aglow with gas and the flowing trains of ladies' costumes.

A Nereid in sea-green robes and silver ornaments, a pale feather resting behind her green

"A secret conspiracy between us two,
To quell me with his gaze, like lions caged."

These lines were scrawled above the letter Gwendolen found upon her dressing table.

"DEAREST CHILD,—I have been awaiting news from you for seven days. In your last correspondence, you mentioned that the Langens contemplated departing Leubronn for Baden. How could you be so thoughtless as to leave me in suspense regarding your whereabouts? I am in profound anxiety lest this missive fail to reach you. In any event, you were to return home by the end of September, yet I implore you to hasten your return. Should you exhaust your funds, I shall possess no means to assist you further, and you must absolutely refrain from borrowing from the Langens, for I hold no capacity to indemnify them. This is the grim reality, my darling—I wish I could soften the blow, but a catastrophic ruin has overtaken us all. You know little of commerce and cannot comprehend its intricacies; however, Grapnell & Co. have collapsed with losses totaling a million pounds, leaving us utterly destitute—your Aunt Gascoigne included, save for your Uncle Gascoigne's clerical stipend. To survive, we must dispose of their carriage and rely on interest from the boys' investments. Every asset your poor father salvaged for us is now forfeited to creditors. I possess nothing of my own. It is preferable that you learn this truth immediately, though it shatters my heart to voice it. Naturally, we cannot avoid wondering why you departed precisely when you did. Yet I shall never blame you, my dearest child; I would shield you from all distress if I could. On your journey home, you will have time to steel yourself for the transformation awaiting you. We shall likely vacate Offendene forthwith, hoping that Mr. Haynes, who coveted the estate previously, is prepared to purchase it. Regrettably, we cannot reside in the rectory—it offers no space. We must secure some humble dwelling and subsist upon your uncle Gascoigne's charity until further prospects emerge. I shall be unable to settle debts to merchants or even cover servants' wages. Fortify your spirit, my dear child; we must submit to Divine Will. It is agonizing to yield to Mr. Lassman's malevolent recklessness, which rumors attribute to this failure. Your poor sisters can only weep alongside me, offering no aid. Were you present, perhaps a rift in the clouds would form—I struggle to believe fate intended you for poverty. If the Langens intend to linger abroad, you might arrange your travel under someone else's guardianship. Nevertheless, arrive as swiftly as possible with your afflicted and devoted mother,

The initial impact of this epistle upon Gwendolen was akin to a stupor. The unshakeable conviction that her destiny awaited in a realm of opulent ease, where adversity would merely be draped in silk and cushioned with gold, had resided more deeply within her youthful soul than in her mother's mind, nourished by her blood and a sense of inherent superiority that colored vast swathes of her consciousness. It proved nearly as arduous for her to accept the sudden shift from affluence to humiliating dependency as it would have been to anticipate the chilling sensation of her own mortality entering the stream of her flourishing life. Standing immobile for several moments, she discarded her hat and mechanically inspected the glass. The coils of her smooth, light-brown hair remained arranged with perfection suitable for a ballroom. On other evenings, Gwendolen might have lingered before her reflection with pleasure—a permissible indulgence—but now, she registered no conscious appreciation of her mirrored beauty. Instead, she stared fixedly ahead, as if jarred by a jarring, hateful noise and waiting for its source to manifest. Eventually, she collapsed into the corner of the crimson velvet sofa, retrieved the letter, and read it twice with deliberateness. Finally, she allowed it to drop to the floor, resting her interlaced hands upon her lap, sitting perfectly still, shedding no tear.

Her instinct was to survey and resist the circumstances rather than lament them. There was no internal outcry of "Poor Mamma!" Her mother had never derived significant joy from existence, and if Gwendolen felt pity in this moment, she would direct it toward herself—for was she not, by nature and right, the primary object of her mother's solicitude? What consumed her was anger, resistance, and bitter vexation at having squandered her winnings at roulette. Had fortune favored her through this single day, she would have returned home laden with a substantial sum, or continued gambling to sustain them all. Could it not still happen? Although only four napoleons remained in her purse, she possessed ornaments she could liquidate—a practice commonplace among stylish circles in German spas, requiring no shame. Indeed, even absent her mother's letter, she likely would have sold jewelry for an Etruscan necklace she had not worn since arriving, deriving a pleasurable sense of intensity and escape from the mundane. With ten louis at her disposal and a return to her former luck—which seemed probable—what could surpass continuing to gamble for a few days? If her friends at home disapproved of her methods, as they surely would, the money would nonetheless exist. Gwendolen's imagination dwelled upon this path, conjuring agreeable consequences, yet lacking the unbroken confidence and rising certainty characteristic of a gambler's mania. She had approached the roulette table not in the grip of passion, but in search of it; her mind remained sanely capable of visualizing balanced probabilities. While the allure of winning tempted her, the specter of loss thrust itself forward with alternating force, causing her pride to sink sensitively. She was resolved neither to inform the Langens of her family's misfortune nor to incur any debt to their compassion. If she parted with her jewelry to any noticeable degree, they would intervene with inquiries and remonstrances. The course posing the least risk of intolerable annoyance was to mortgage her necklace early in the morning, notify the Langens that her mother desired her immediate return without specifying reasons, and board the train for Brussels that evening. Though she lacked a maid, and the Langens might object to her solitary return, her will was absolute.

Rather than retiring to bed, she illuminated her chamber to its brightest and commenced packing, working diligently. Throughout, however, scenes of the approaching day haunted her: now the tedious explanations and farewells, the whirlwind journey toward a transformed home; now the alternative of delaying another day, standing again at the roulette table. Yet, in every latter vision, Deronda loomed, watching her with exasperating irony—and the two keen experiences inevitably resurfaced together—observing her once more abandoned by fortune. This importunate image undeniably swayed her resolve toward immediate departure, urging her packing to a point where changing her mind would prove inconvenient. It was past midnight when she entered her room, and by the time she verified that she had omitted nothing essential, the faint dawn was seeping through the white blinds, dimming her candles. Why sleep? A cold bath sufficed for refreshment, and she noted that a slight tracé of fatigue around her eyes rendered her appearance all the more striking. Before six o'clock, she was fully attired in her gray traveling dress, complete with her felt hat, intending to venture outdoors as soon as she could confirm the presence of other ladies heading to the springs. Chance placed her sideways before the long strip of mirror bisecting her twin windows; turning, she leaned her elbow upon the chair back in an attitude fit for a portrait. It is possible to harbor a robust self-love devoid of self-satisfaction, indeed coupled with a self-discontent intensified because one's tiny core of egocentric sensibility becomes a supreme preoccupation. Yet Gwendolen knew nothing of such inner strife. She possessed a *naïve* delight in her fortunate self, an aspect worthy of indulgence from anyone but the sternest saintliness, for she had daily witnessed a pleasant reflection of this self in her friends' flattery as well as in the glass. And even amidst these beginnings of trouble, while idly gazing at her image in the encroaching light, her face acquired a complacency gradual as the cheerfulness of the morning. Her beautiful lips curled into an increasingly decisive smile, until finally, removing her hat, she leaned forward and kissed the cold glass, which had appeared so warm. How could she believe in sorrow? If it assailed her, she felt the power to crush it, defy it, or flee from it, as she had already done. Anything seemed more plausible than enduring miseries, great or small.

Madame von Langen never ventured out prior to breakfast, allowing Gwendolen to conclude her early stroll by traversing the Obere Strasse, where lay the requisite shop, certain to open after seven. At that hour, any observers she cared to avoid would either be strolling in the spring region or still nestled in their bedrooms. However, there existed a grand hotel, the *Czarina*, from which eyes might track her ascent to Mr. Wiener's door. This was a risk worth taking: might she not be entering to purchase something that had caught her fancy? This implicit falsehood coursed through her mind as she recalled that the *Czarina* housed Deronda, yet she was already far up the Obere Strasse, walking with her customary floating gait. Every line of her figure and drapery fell in gentle curves attractive to all eyes save those perceiving too close a resemblance to the serpent, objects to the revival of serpent-worship. She looked neither right nor left, conducting her business in the shop with a coolness that yielded Mr. Wiener little to remark save

*"Let no flower of the spring pass by us; let us crown ourselves with rosebuds before they be withered."* — *Book of Wisdom.*

To Gwendolen Harleth, the idea of Offendene as a home for her childhood, or a place steeped in the tender, rooting memories of kinship, was a fantasy that offered little comfort. A human life, she felt, required a soil where love for the earth grew deep through the labor of daily life, where the sounds and accents of a native tongue haunted the soul, and where kindness toward neighbors—even the local dogs and donkeys—became a natural habit of the blood. Yet, at five years old, mortals are rarely prepared to transcend the parochial and embrace the abstract citizenship of the world. Our earliest prejudices, such as the blind preference for milk, are the necessary nourishment for the young body and soul until they learn to look outward.

In Gwendolen's case, however, that blessed persistence where affection takes root had been tragically absent. Only a year prior to her return from Leubronn, Offendene had been selected merely for its proximity to Pennicote Rectory. On a bleak late October afternoon, with rooks crowing overhead and yellow elm leaves swirling in the wind, Mrs. Davilow, Gwendolen, and her four half-sisters had been driven down the avenue for the first time, trailed by the governess and the maid in another carriage.

The season seemed oddly suited to the grim aspect of the old oblong red-brick house, which was anxiously adorned with stone at every line, barring nothing from the double rows of narrow windows nor the large square portico. Stone encouraged a greenish lichen, and the brick wore a powdery gray, lending the building a rigid, rectangular severity that softened slightly only by the surrounding plantation. One might have wished for the house to stand on a knoll, commanding a view of distant thatched roofs, church towers, and the sweeping greens of the park; but instead, screened by flat pastures, it possessed only a fleeting glimpse of the wider world in the lofty curves of the chalk downs.

Though modest in size and difficult to let due to its sombre furniture and faded upholstery, the house carried a mysterious prestige. To reside within walls once sufficient for dowager countesses imparted a tangible tinge of elevation to Mrs. Davilow's satisfaction. For Gwendolen, this possibility materialized abruptly upon the death of her stepfather, Captain Davilow, who had visited only briefly and fitfully for nine years. While Gwendolen cared little for the mechanics of the inheritance, she relished the result: her prospects had instantly improved. She had grown tired of their previous nomadic existence, constantly moving from foreign watering-places to Parisian apartments, forever rejecting new suites of furniture and meeting strangers who treated her with casual indifference. The two years spent at a showy school, where she was invariably placed foremost, had only hardened her belief that a person of her exceptional nature deserved nothing less than advantageous circumstances.

Any lingering fear of mediocrity vanished now that her mother had an establishment. Gwendolen was secure regarding her birth; knowing her maternal grandfather was a West Indian, she assumed the mystery of his fortune was solved, while her father's family remained so high that they ignored her mother, despite her proud display of a miniature of "Lady Molly." She knew little of her father, save for a painful incident at age twelve. When Mrs. Davilow, on rare occasions, produced memorials of her first husband, she recounted with fervor that dear papa had died when Gwendolen was still in long clothes. Gwendolen, recalling the unlovable stepfather she had known throughout her youth, had bluntly asked, "Why did you marry again, mamma? It would have been nicer if you had not." Mrs. Davilow, flushing deeply, had violently shut away the memorials and accused her daughter of lacking feeling. Hurt and ashamed, Gwendolen had never dared ask again.

This guilt was not isolated. It was customary for Gwendolen to sleep in her mother's room, a privilege reflecting Mrs. Davilow's tenderness for her eldest. Once, during a pain attack, the sick woman had forgotten her medicine beside the bed and begged Gwendolen to fetch it. The healthy young lady, cozy in her warm couch, had stubbornly refused to step into the cold, leaving her mother to suffer without relief. Though Mrs. Davilow never reproached her, Gwendolen felt the sting of her mother's disappointment and attempted to make amends with excessive caresses. As the spoiled pet of the household, waited upon by mother, sisters, and maids, Gwendolen found it difficult to prioritize anyone else's needs over her own. When thwarted, she resented it with a passionate, childish fury. Though she delighted in rescuing drowning insects, she retained a shameful memory of strangling her sister's canary in a fit of exasperation at its noise. Later, she had bought a white mouse for her sister in retribution, but the guilt of the bird's death lingered. By twenty, Gwendolen's raw nature had tempered into a calculated self-control, guarding her from penitential humiliation, though a sharper will burned beneath the surface.

Upon arriving at Offendene, the place chosen by her brother-in-law, Mr. Gascoigne, the group stood under the porch, the stone hall and staircase visible through the open door, warmed by a bright fire. Silence reigned as the eyes of the mother, sisters, and governess fixed on Gwendolen, awaiting her verdict. The girls ranged from sixteen-year-old Alice to ten-year-old Isabel, dressed in increasingly shabby black gowns. Miss Merry, the governess, was elderly and neutral. Mrs. Davilow's beauty, though worn, seemed pathetic in her appeal to Gwendolen, who surveyed the landscape and entrance hall with rapid, judging glances. Like a young racehorse amidst untrimmed ponies, Gwendolen stood apart.

"Well, dear, what do you think of the place?" Mrs. Davilow asked gently.

"I think it is charming," Gwendolen replied quickly. "A romantic place; anything delightful may happen in it. No one need be ashamed of living here."

"There is certainly nothing common about it."

"Oh, it would do for fallen royalty or grand poverty. We ought properly to have been living in splendor." Gwendolen's tone shifted to sharp surprise. "But I thought my uncle and aunt Gascoigne would be here to meet us, and my cousin Anna!"

"We are early," Mrs. Davilow said, entering the hall and greeting the housekeeper. "You expect Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne?"

"Yes, madam," the housekeeper replied, noting the state of the fires and the dusting of the brasses. "They'll be here at five."

Satisfied, Gwendolen tripped up the matted stone staircase to inspect the rooms: a dining chamber of dark oak and worn red satin damask adorned with a painting of snarling dogs; a library smelling of old leather; and finally, a drawing-room crowded with venerable knick-knacks.

"Mamma, pray come here!" Gwendolen cried. "Here is an organ. I will be Saint Cecilia!" She threw off her hat and gloves, seating herself before the instrument in an admirable pose, hair loose. Miss Merry, obediently dubbed Jocosa, combed out Gwendolen's hair, letting it fall in a smooth, light-brown stream.

"A charming picture, my dear!" Mrs. Davilow smiled, pleased by the display.

"What a queer, quaint, picturesque room!" Gwendolen exclaimed, admiring the embroidered chairs and garlands. "That one with the ribs... I think it is Spanish."

"Oh, Gwendolen!" Isabel, a plain child with an alarming memory, gasped, holding open a hinged panel of the wainscot.

Everyone gathered to look. Inside lay the image of an upturned dead face, from which an obscure figure fled with outstretched arms. "How horrible!" Mrs. Davilow cried with disgust. Gwendolen shuddered silently. Isabel whispered, "You will never stay in this room by yourself, Gwendolen."

"How dare you open things which were meant to be shut up?" Gwendolen snapped, snatching the panel shut. "There is a lock—where is the key? Let the key be found, or let one be made, and let nobody open it again; or rather, let the key be brought to me."

Turning away, flushed with reaction, Gwendolen declared, "Let us go up to our own room, mamma." The housekeeper searched and found the key in a nearby cabinet drawer, handing it to Bugle, the lady's-maid, with significant instructions to deliver it to "Her Royal Highness."

Inside the black-and-yellow bedroom, where a white couch awaited beside the massive "catafalque" bed, Gwendolen's first move was to gaze at herself in the tall mirror.

"That is a becoming glass, Gwendolen," Mrs. Davilow observed. "Or is it the black and gold color that sets you off?"

"I should make a tolerable St. Cecilia with some white roses on my head," Gwendolen mused. "Only how about my nose, mamma? I think saint's noses never in the least turn up. I wish you had given me your perfectly straight nose; it would have done for any sort of character—a nose of all work. Mine is only a happy nose; it would not do so well for

The Reverend Mr. Gascoigne, the rector of Pennicote, viewed his niece Gwendolen through a lens that was undeniably practical, yet devoid of malice. As a man accustomed to weighing the pros and cons of existence, he naturally saw in her a candidate for a distinguished alliance. Was it not within the bounds of human nature for him to align himself with the prevailing hopes of his circle—that a charming maiden like Gwendolen deserved nothing short of a splendid match? One must give him credit for a benevolent spirit; his calculations were those of a rational man, avoiding the absurdities of romantic folly. He did not envision a scenario where she wore a tattered gown to lure a marquis, nor did he scheme to stage a horseback accident to secure a rescue by a wealthy suitor. His intentions were straightforward: he wished to see her presented to the finest circles of the neighborhood, polished and visible.

Yet, Gwendolen shared her uncle's vision of a brilliant future, though her internal landscape was far steeper than his. While she accepted the inevitability of matrimony and dismissed the idea of settling for a mediocre union, she harbored no illusions that marriage was the ultimate triumph. To her, the domestic cage of wedlock was a "vexatious necessity," a state where a woman forfeited her freedom to do as she pleased, burdened by unwanted progeny and submerged in the monotonous humdrum of daily life. Marriage was, in her estimation, merely a form of social promotion, akin to accepting a peerage in lieu of true leadership. Gwendolen, a delicate sylph of twenty, aspired to lead. Though her passions were confined to the realm of feminine elegance and lacked any grandiose intent to alter the balance of the nation, she possessed an unyielding desire to command admiration. Whatever struck her fancy with a dazzling brilliance, however fleeting, seemed to her the very essence of pleasure.

"Miss Gwendolen will not rest until the world lies at her feet," observed Miss Merry, the gentle governess. These were hyperbolic phrases, often used to describe the whims of the vain, yet they captured the vast, hazy horizon before Gwendolen. Where others drifted like empty ships, tossed by currents of circumstance, Gwendolen vowed to master her destiny through superior intellect. Settled at Offendene, surrounded by the attention of Lady Brackenshaw, the local archery club, and the elite Arrowpoint family, her position might have seemed modest to others. Yet, Gwendolen's confidence rested squarely on her own capabilities. She believed her education, though perhaps unconventional, had fortified her mind with the rigid structures of rules and facts, supplemented by a broad consumption of novels, plays, and poetry. Her French and music, the standard accreditations of a lady, were flawless. Coupled with an innate, almost magnetic self-assurance, she felt fully equipped to navigate the labyrinth of life.

However, Gwendolen found little genuine interest in the mundane subjects of the world, dismissing them as inherently stupid, much as light appears dull to the aged. Yet, she was never at a loss for words if confronted with such trivia. No one dared dispute her superiority. From the moment of her arrival at Offendene, the atmosphere shifted around her. Whether it was the heavy tread of a footman, the subpar laundry, or the smoke billowing from a fireplace, every minor inconvenience was instantly framed as a personal slight against her sensibilities. If she delayed breakfast, the focus turned immediately to ensuring her toast remained crisp. When she entered the room, her light-brown hair cascading behind her and her large, dark eyes gleaming like polished onyx beneath long lashes, the room bowed to her. It was always she who demanded tolerance, correcting the posture of her maid or silencing the inquiries of her cousin Isabel, treating everyone else as servants to her mood.

She lived as a princess in exile, entitled to breakfast rolls of the finest flour and silver forks reserved solely for her, even amidst scarcity. Why was this? The surface explanation lay in her beauty, her unusual demeanor, and a decisive will that animated her graceful movements. On a rainy, lethargic day, her presence alone seemed to justify the continuation of civilized rituals, prompting hotel waiters to scour crumbs and creases with frantic alacrity. Added to this was her status as the eldest daughter, treated with an apologetic deference by her mother, who blamed her stepfather for all her tribulations. This combination created an aura of domestic empire so complete that it seemed obvious why the household revolved around her.

Yet, caution dictates that one looks deeper than the surface. There were those, neither beautiful nor unusually situated, who commanded similar assiduous service. What bound them together was not grace, but a fierce, unscrupulous determination to possess what they desired, coupled with a terrifying fearlessness in becoming disagreeable or dangerous when denied. Gwendolen, too, possessed this iron core. She was not merely served by the weak women of the house due to pity, but because they feared the consequences of crossing her. Even those who loved her were caught between affection and dread, a tension heightened by the iridescent quality of her character. Like Macbeth, who argued that two opposites could not exist simultaneously, the world assumed Gwendolen was a singular force. But feelings, unlike actions, could hold contradictory weights. A moment was wide enough to harbor both loyalty and treachery, a murderous impulse and its sudden repentance. Gwendolen was not simply a spoiled child; she was a complex vessel of conflicting desires, capable of shifting from radiant charm to chilling indifference in the space of a breath, leaving those around her perpetually guessing at the next movement of her will.

"Gwendolen's arrival in the neighborhood proved exactly what her uncle had predicted. From the imposing Brackenshaw Castle down to the cozy Firs at Wanchester, where Mr. Quallon, the banker, maintained a hospitable household, she was greeted with a mixture of genuine admiration and social necessity. Even those who found her difficult to warm to felt a pragmatic comfort in hosting a new, dazzling figure; for hosts who entertain frequently must assemble their gatherings like political cabinets, prioritizing balance over personal affinity. To secure Gwendolen's presence, it was unnecessary to court anyone unpleasant, for Mrs. Davilow invariably served as a quiet, picturesque chaperon, and Mr. Gascoigne remained a constant draw wherever he went.

Yet, among the homes where Gwendolen was tolerated rather than loved, stood Quetcham Hall. An invitation to a large dinner there served as her formal introduction to the local elite. In a select gathering of thirty, carefully balanced across generations, it was impossible to exclude any respectable family. Nowhere else could a youthful figure rival Gwendolen's as she drifted through the long, flower-adorned suites. Visible at first as a slender silhouette floating in white drapery, she grew in definition as she passed from shadow into light. She had never experienced such a promenade before, and she felt a surge of exultation that it suited her perfectly; to any observer, the grand galleries and lacquered servants might have seemed as inevitable to her life as breathing. Meanwhile, her cousin Anna, who was genuinely more accustomed to such affairs, felt almost as bewildered as a rabbit suddenly dropped into a brightly lit cage.

"Who is that with Gascoigne?" asked the Archdeacon, abandoning a discussion of military maneuvers on which, as a clergyman, he was naturally expected to comment. Beside him, his son—a hopeful young scholar who had recently proposed "ingenious" emendations to Greek texts—exclaimed simultaneously, "By George! Who is that girl with the awfully well-set head and jolly figure?"

But for a mind of general benevolence, wishing everyone to appear their best, it was maddening to watch Gwendolen eclipse the rest. Even the handsome Miss Lawe, the daughter of Lady Lawe, seemed to shrink under her gaze, appearing suddenly broad, heavy, and lifeless. Similarly, Miss Arrowpoint, unfortunately clad in white, instantly resembled a *carte-de-visite* where the charge had been applied solely to the skirt. While Miss Arrowpoint was generally beloved for her amiable, unpretentious demeanor, which softened her mother's eccentricities, there appeared to be a distinct dissonance in Gwendolen projecting such an aura of social importance.

"She is not really so handsome if you examine her features closely," Mrs. Arrowpoint confided later to Mrs. Vulcany. "She possesses a certain style that creates a massive initial effect, but afterwards, she becomes far less agreeable."

Indeed, Gwendolen, without intending it, had offended her hostess. Though Mrs. Arrowpoint was neither splenetic nor vindictive, she possessed strong susceptibilities. Several elements converged in the Lady of Quetcham to create an impression that the neighbors' reasoning faculties found essential: she was the heir to a fortune amassed through dubious urban business, a fact cited to explain her squat figure, her harsh, parrot-like voice, and her systematically elevated head-dress. These external quirks naturally led observers to conclude she harbored literary tendencies. A closer scrutiny, however, would reveal that these traits existed independently; daughters of aldermen are often robust and beautiful, pretty women may possess husky voices, and the creation of mediocre literature is compatible with virtually any physical form.

Gwendolen, possessing a sharp eye for the absurd in others but a kind heart for anyone capable of making life pleasant, intended to win Mrs. Arrowpoint's favor. Yet, self-confidence often addresses an imaginary dullness in others. Just as the wealthy cajole the poor or the vigorous speak artificially to the elderly, believing them deaf or imbecile, Gwendolen, despite her cleverness and intent to please, fell into this trap. Unreflectingly, she concluded that because Mrs. Arrowpoint was ridiculous, she must also lack penetration. She proceeded through her little scenes unsuspecting that every nuance of her behavior was being meticulously observed.

"You are fond of books as well as music, riding, and archery, I hear," Mrs. Arrowpoint said, approaching her for a tête-à-tête in the drawing-room after dinner. "Catherine will be delighted to have so sympathetic a neighbor." This remark, delivered in a low, melodious tone, carried a fatal twang of patronization. Gwendolen responded gracefully, yet with the air of a benefactor:

"It is I who am fortunate. Miss Arrowpoint will teach me what good music is. I shall be entirely a learner. I hear that she is a thorough musician."

"Catherine has indeed enjoyed every advantage," Mrs. Arrowpoint replied. "We have a first-rate musician in the house now—Herr Klesmer; perhaps you know all his compositions? You must let me introduce him. You sing, I believe? Catherine plays three instruments but does not sing. I hope you will let us hear you. I understand you are an accomplished singer."

"Oh, no!" Gwendolen interrupted, quoting Mephistopheles. "'Die Kraft ist schwach, allein die Lust ist gross.'"

"Ah, you are a student of Goethe. Young ladies are so advanced now. I suppose you have read everything."

"No, really. I shall be so grateful if you tell me what to read. I have examined all the books in the library at Offendene, but nothing is readable. The pages stick together and smell musty. I wish I could write books to amuse myself, as you can! How delightful it must be to write books according to one's own taste instead of reading other people's! Home-made books must be so charming."

For an instant, Mrs. Arrowpoint's gaze sharpened, but the satirical edge of Gwendolen's final sentence dissolved into girlish simplicity when she added, "I would give anything to write a book!"

"And why not?" Mrs. Arrowpoint encouraged. "Pen, ink, and paper are within everyone's reach. But I will send you all I have written with pleasure."

"Thank you. I shall be so happy to read your works. Knowing an author must grant a unique understanding of their books; one could discern which parts are humorous and which serious. I am sure I often laugh in the wrong places." Gwendolen sensed danger and hurried to reassure her, "In Shakespeare, you know, and other great masters we can never fully see. But I always crave to know more than exists in the books."

"If you are interested in any of my subjects, I can lend you many extra sheets in manuscript," offered Mrs. Arrowpoint, as Gwendolen felt herself painfully positioned like the young lady who professes to love potted sprats.

"These are things I dare say I shall publish eventually," Gwendolen admitted. "Several friends have urged me, and one dislikes being obstinate. My Tasso, for instance—I could have made it twice the length."

"I dote on Tasso," Gwendolen declared.

"Well, you shall have all my papers if you wish. So many have written about Tasso, yet they are all wrong. Regarding the specific nature of his madness, his feelings for Leonora, the true cause of his imprisonment, and Leonora's character—who, in my view, was a cold-hearted woman, for otherwise she would have married him despite her brother—all current accounts are incorrect. I differ from everyone."

"How fascinating!" Gwendolen exclaimed. "I like to differ from everyone. It seems so tedious to agree. That is the worst aspect of expressing opinions; you force people to conform." This statement revived a slight suspicion in Mrs. Arrowpoint, whose gaze once more turned examining. However, Gwendolen appeared utterly innocent, continuing with a docile air, "I know nothing of Tasso save the *Jerusalem Delivered*, which we recited by heart at school."

"Ah, his life is more intriguing than his poetry. I have reconstructed his early years as a sort of romance. Considering his father Bernardo and the circumstances, there is much that must be true."

"Imagination is often truer than fact," Gwendolen stated decisively, though she could not explain these fluent words as easily as if they were ancient Egyptian. "I shall be so eager to learn all about Tasso—and his madness, particularly. I suppose poets are always a little mad."

"To be sure—" Mrs. Arrowpoint quoted, "'The poet's eye in a fine frenzy rolling.' And someone says of Marlowe, 'For that fine madness still he did maintain, Which always should possess the poet's brain.'"

"But it was not always discovered, was it?" Gwendolen asked innocently. "I suppose some rolled their eyes in private. Mad people are often very cunning."

A fleeting shadow crossed Mrs. Arrowpoint's face, but the arrival of the gentlemen precluded any immediate confrontation with this overly quick young lady, who had overplayed her *naïveté*.

"Ah, here comes Herr Klesmer," announced Mrs. Arrowpoint, rising. Soon she brought him to Gwendolen, leaving them to a dialogue that was agreeable on both sides. Herr Klesmer was a felicitous blend of the German, Slav, and Semitic, possessing grand features, artfully floating brown hair, and brown eyes behind spectacles. His English lacked foreignness save for its fluency, and

"Croyez-vous m'avoir humiliée pour m'avoir appris que la terre tourne autour du soleil? Je vous jure que je ne m'en estime pas moins."
—FONTENELLE: *Pluralité des Mondes*.

Those words of lofty criticism had cut a fresh, stinging wound in Gwendolen's pride. She refused to acknowledge the shame she felt regarding her lack of musical training compared to Miss Arrowpoint. It was beneath her dignity to confess that she could not confidently question Herr Klesmer's artistic judgment, nor to admit to herself that every meeting with Miss Arrowpoint stirred a sharp, unwanted jealousy. It was not merely because Miss Arrowpoint was an heiress; it was because a girl who appeared physically unremarkable—slight of figure, features unassuming, complexion sallow—possessed a mental superiority that defied explanation. There was an exasperating thoroughness in Miss Arrowpoint's musical command, a fastidious discrimination in her tastes that commanded awe and prevented genuine admiration. One might suspect this quiet young lady secretly regarded Miss Harleth's talents as mediocre, yet she masked this judgment behind a veil of perfect kindness.

Gwendolen avoided dwelling on facts that cast an unfavorable light upon her own standing. The "Musical Magus" who had recently broadened her horizons was frequently away, traveling between London and Quetcham, creating opportunities for his opinions to shift toward greater admiration. Meanwhile, the evident pleasure her singing garnered at Brackenshaw Castle, the Firs, and elsewhere restored her equanimity. She preferred trust in approval over the sting of objection, content not to possess the parching thirst for perfection that drove others. Perhaps it would have been rash to claim she was inwardly exceptional, save for her rare grace of movement and a certain daring that gave piquancy to a common egoistic ambition. Though the set of her head suggested a hunger for supremacy, it was merely a matter of how that supremacy was pursued. For Gwendolen, a girl whose passion for being remarkable adhered strictly to the bounds of highest breeding and freedom from sordid needs, her internal rebellion was real enough. Yet, she lacked the speculative theories of the reformist woman; she satirized them. Her horizon remained that of the genteel romance, where the heroine's soul pours out in journals filled with vague power and rebellion, while her life moves strictly within the sphere of fashion. If she wandered into a swamp, the pathos lay in her wearing satin shoes. Nature and society had provided wirework restraints sufficient to hold a soul burning with a sense of what the universe was not captive within the ordinary forms, ensuring she did nothing particular.

Even in the novelty of the first winter at Offendene, Gwendolen felt this commonplace result threatening her. She was clear that she did not wish to live like ordinary young ladies, but unclear on how to assert her freedom or what specific acts would define her alternative life. Offendene served as a good backdrop, but the neighborhood offered little excitement beyond her initial presentations. Between sallies of satire that had offended Mrs. Arrowpoint, she filled the days with girlish devices. Her strongest assertion of individuality involved omitting Alice's lessons—believing Alice would excel in ignorance—and employing her with Miss Merry and the housemaid to arrange dramatic costumes for charades and theatrical pieces she intended to produce by force of will. Having never truly acted beyond *tableaux vivans* at school, she felt assured of her ability, fueled by memories of the Théâtre Français and her mother's praise of Rachel. She often dreamed of becoming an actress like Rachel, convinced her superior beauty outweighed the thin Jewish actress's. During the wet days before Christmas, they prepared Greek, Oriental, and composite costumes. Gwendolen attitudinized and speechified before a domestic audience, even pressing the housekeeper to swell the applause, though the housekeeper wisely declined a second invitation, noting that Miss Harleth looked far more regal in her own dress than in a baggy outfit with bare arms.

"Do I look as well as Rachel, mamma?" Gwendolen asked one day, displaying herself in a Greek dress with tragic intent.

"You have better arms than Rachel," Mrs. Davilow replied. "Your arms would do for anything, Gwen. But your voice is not so tragic as hers; it is not so deep."

"I can make it deeper, if I like," Gwendolen said provisionally, then decided firmly, "I think a higher voice is more tragic: it is more feminine; and the more feminine a woman is, the more tragic it seems when she does desperate actions."

"There may be something in that," Mrs. Davilow said languidly. "But I don't know what good there is in making one's blood creep. And if there is anything horrible to be done, I should like it to be left to the men."

"Oh, mamma, you are so dreadfully prosaic!" Gwendolen exclaimed. "As if all the great poetic criminals were not women! I think the men are poor cautious creatures."

"Well, dear, and you—who are afraid to be alone in the night—I don't think you would be very bold in crime, thank God."

"I am not talking about reality, mamma," Gwendolen said impatiently.

Her mother leaving the room, Gwendolen turned quickly to her cousin Anna. "Ask my uncle to let us get up some charades at the rectory. Mr. Middleton and Warham could act with us—just for practice. Mamma says it will not do to have Mr. Middleton consulting and rehearsing here. He is a stick, but we could give him suitable parts. Do ask, or else I will."

"Oh, not till Rex comes," Anna interjected eagerly. "He is so clever, and such a dear old thing, and he will act Napoleon looking over the sea. He looks just like Napoleon. Rex can do anything."

"I don't in the least believe in your Rex, Anna," Gwendolen laughed. "He will turn out to be like those wretched blue and yellow water-colors of his which you hang up in your bedroom and worship."

"Very well, you will see," Anna said stubbornly. "It is not that I know what is clever, but he has got a scholarship already, and papa says he will get a fellowship, and nobody is better at games. He is cleverer than Mr. Middleton, and everybody but you calls Mr. Middleton clever."

"So he may be in a dark-lantern sort of way. But he *is* a stick. If he had to say, 'Perdition catch my soul, but I do love her,' he would say it in just the same tone as, 'Here endeth the second lesson.'"

"Oh, Gwendolen!" Anna cried, shocked. "And it is very unkind of you to speak so of him, for he admires you very much. I heard Warham say one day to mamma, 'Middleton is regularly spooney upon Gwendolen.' She was very angry with him; but I know what it means. It is what they say at college for being in love."

"How can I help it?" Gwendolen said, somewhat contemptuously. "Perdition catch my soul if I love *him*."

"No, of course; papa, I think, would not wish it. And he is to go away soon. But it makes me sorry when you ridicule him."

"What shall you do to me when I ridicule Rex?" Gwendolen asked wickedly.

"Now, Gwendolen, dear, you *will not*?" Anna said, her eyes filling with tears. "I could not bear it. But there really is nothing in him to ridicule. Only you may find out things. For no one ever thought of laughing at Mr. Middleton before you. Every one said he was nice-looking, and his manners perfect. I am sure I have always been frightened at him because of his learning and his square-cut coat, and his being a nephew of the bishop's, and all that. But you will not ridicule Rex—promise me." Anna ended with a beseeching look that touched Gwendolen.

"You are a dear little coz," she said, gently pinching the tip of Anna's chin. "I don't ever want to do anything that will vex you. Especially if Rex is to make everything come off—charades and everything."

When Rex finally arrived, the animation he brought to Offendene and the rectory, along with his ready partnership in Gwendolen's plans, dispelled any inclination for ridicule that wasn't open and flattering. He was a fine, open-hearted youth, with a handsome face resembling his father's and Anna's, yet softer in expression and larger in scale. He possessed a bright, healthy, loving nature that found joy in ordinary innocence, leaving vice no temptation. His knowledge of vice lay in the outer courts of his mind, inaccessible to serious repulsion. He viewed vicious habits as "stupid stuff" done by "some fellows," keeping them at a distance. He returned Anna's affection fully, as expected of a brother whose pleasures exceeded hers, and he had never known a stronger love.

The cousins spent their time together, chiefly at Offendene, where Gwendolen held sway. Whatever she wished became a ruling purpose for Rex. The charades proceeded according to plan, alongside impromptu scenes. It was at Offendene that rehearsals and presentations took place, with Mrs. Davilow seeing no objection to Mr. Middleton joining now that Rex was present, especially as his services were indispensable. Warham, studying for India under a "coach," was too overwhelmed by cramming for his examination to spare time, and his prospects seemed tied to a quotable

"Perigot." As the bonny lass passed by,
"Willie." Hey, ho, bonnilasse!
"P." She roode at me with glauncing eye,
"W." As clear as the crystal glass.
"P." All as the sunny beam so bright,
"W." Hey, ho, the sunbeam!
"P." Glaunceth from Phoebus' face forthright,
"W." So love into thy heart did stream."
—SPENSER: _Shepherd's Calendar_.

"The kindliest symptom, yet the most alarming crisis in the ticklish
state of youth..."

The first sign of the approaching storm was a transparent white cloud, setting off the deepening blue of the sky. Inside the rectory, Anna sat in the quiet of her own thoughts, yet the air around her crackled with unspoken tension. Rex had changed. For the first time, he had not spoken to her about what burned within him; he simply assumed she knew, a silent confidence that terrified her more than any open declaration. Conversely, Anna found herself unable to articulate the turmoil swirling in her heart. Was it a pain she ought to hide? That he cared for someone else so soon, with such intensity? Yet, this fear was drowned by a deeper, gnawing anxiety for Rex's safety.

She looked at her cousin, Gwendolen, with a mixture of awe and trepidation. Gwendolen was radiant, a creature of impossible beauty, but to Anna, she resembled a magnificent wild beast whose appetites were mysterious and potentially destructive. Anna knew Gwendolen as a girl of immense ambition, always striving to be "something extraordinary." Now, watching her cousin, Anna felt a chill conviction: Gwendolen would never truly care for Rex. Where Anna poured out tenderness and reverence, Gwendolen remained cool, perhaps even disdainful. To imagine Gwendolen scorning Rex seemed far more likely than imagining her accepting his love. And Rex... Papa would be furious if he knew. Rex was too young for such a tempest, wasn't he? Anna had expected years to pass before anything like this, expecting to remain his devoted housekeeper forever. But what a heart must beat that refused to return love? In the shadow of his potential suffering, Anna began to dislike the fascinating, terrifying allure of her cousin.

Time, it seemed, had swollen with a tumultuous life visible to everyone but the actors themselves. Rex, if pressed, would claim he was eager to announce an imminent engagement to his father. Yet, for the first time, he was guarded. Not only about his feelings, but about his actions—a reserve that unnerved Anna. On her side, she grew nervous whenever her parents or Aunt Davilow spoke privately, fearing a mention of Rex and Gwendolen. But the elders were oblivious to this pantomime, interpreting the young ones' trivialities with the casual indifference one gives to busy ants.

On a gray morning, with Papa departed in his carriage and Aunt Davilow beside him, Anna spotted Rex. He wore his antigropelos, the closest approximation to hunting gear he owned.

"Where are you going, Rex?" she asked softly.

"To see the hounds throw off at the Three Barns."

"And... are you taking Gwendolen?" she ventured, her voice trembling.

"She told you, did she?"

"No, but I thought—" Anna hesitated, looking down. "Does Papa know?"

"Not that I'm aware of. I shouldn't think he'd bother."

"You're going to use his horse?"

"He knows I do that whenever I can."

"Don't let Gwendolen ride after the hounds, Rex!" Anna pleaded, her fear granting her a sudden, piercing insight. "Papa, Mamma, and Aunt Davilow all forbid it. They think it's not right for her."

"Why should you suppose she intends to do what is not right?" Rex asked, a smug smile playing on his lips, provoking her further.

"Gwendolen minds nobody sometimes," Anna snapped, her anger emboldening her.

"Then she wouldn't mind me," Rex replied, treating her anxiety as a game.

"Oh, Rex, I cannot bear it. You will make yourself very unhappy."

And then, the dam broke. Anna burst into tears.

"Nannie, Nannie, what on earth is the matter?" Rex exclaimed, impatient, hat in hand, whip ready.

"She will not care for you one bit—I know she never will!" the poor child whispered, sobbing uncontrollably.

Rex flushed, hurried out of the hall, leaving Anna to the misery of having failed to soothe him. As he rode away, he did think of her words. They carried the unwelcome weight of prophecy, even when laughed at. Yet, he quickly dismissed them as the product of little Anna's excessive tenderness. A new determination took root in his chest: he would prove her wrong. This certainty, fueled by a hint of doubt and unease, spurred him toward a confession that might otherwise have waited.

At the gate, Gwendolen awaited him, already mounted. She had prepared herself for his tardiness, keeping the groom in readiness, but now the groom was dismissed. The two rode away together, enveloped in a delightful freedom. Gwendolen was in high spirits, and Rex found her more lovely than ever; her long white throat, the curve of her cheek, were framed perfectly by her simple riding dress. To a young lover like Rex, the qualities of goodness, truth, and beauty seemed instantly embodied in her. He could not imagine a more perfect girl.

The morning was exquisite, a calm gray canvas for the mild winter scene. The hedgerows sparkled with red berries, the elms stood bare and purple, and the horses' hooves chimed musically over the frost. Laughter bubbled between them, mixing with the crisp air. Rex was the antithesis of a dandy, and Gwendolen delighted in teasing him. Every glance, every sound from their throats, flowed from a spring of pure joy. One might be tempted to wish, futilely, that they could have pledged themselves then and there, sealing a bond that would never waver. Some of the goodness Rex believed in was indeed there. But goodness is a fragile thing, like a crop growing underground, vulnerable to the blights of the soil or the storms from afar.

"Anna had got it into her head that you'd want to ride today," Rex said later, the memory of Anna's warning lingering dangerously close to the surface.

"Did she?" Gwendolen laughed lightly. "What a little clairvoyant she is!"

"Shall you?" Rex asked, confident in her common sense regarding the objections of the elders.

"I don't know. I can't tell what I'll do until I get there. Clairvoyants are often wrong; they foresee what is likely. I am not fond of what is likely—it's always dull. I do what is unlikely."

"Aha," Rex grinned. "Once I know what people generally do, I know you'll do the opposite. You've rounded onto a likelihood of your own. I can calculate on you. You can't surprise me."

"Yes, I can. I'll turn round and do what's likely for people in general," Gwendolen teased, her laugh musical.

"You see, you can't escape some sort of likelihood. Contradictoriness makes the strongest likelihood. You must give up a plan."

"No, I won't. My plan is to do what pleases me." Her chin jutted out slightly, her neck straight, a posture that would have jarred sweet-natured Rex if it hadn't been offset by the sheer humor and charm of her delivery.

"Can you manage to feel only what pleases you?"

"Of course not; that comes from what others do. But if the world were pleasanter, one would only feel what was pleasant. Girls' lives are so stupid; they never do what they like."

"I thought that was more the case of men," Rex countered gently. "They're forced to do hard things, bored and broken. And if we love a girl dearly, we want to do as she likes, so in the end, you have your own way."

"I don't believe it. I've never seen a married woman who had her own way."

"What should you like to do?" Rex asked, genuinely curious, venturing beyond his curriculum.

"Oh, I don't know! Go to the North Pole, ride steeple-chases, or be a queen in the East like Lady Hester Stanhope," Gwendolen flitted, her words escaping her lips before she could find a deeper meaning.

"You don't mean you'd never be married?"

"No, I didn't say that. Only when I marry, I won't do as other women do."

"You might do just as you liked if you married a man who loved you more dearly than anything else," Rex said, his heart pounding. "I know one who does."

"Don't talk of Mr. Middleton, for heaven's sake!" Gwendolen gasped, a blush rushing over her face and neck. "That is Anna's chant. I hear the hounds. Let us go on."

She urged her chestnut into a canter, and Rex followed, feeling a strange mix of relief and foreboding. Gwendolen knew Anna loved him, but she viewed it as a harmless pastime to fill the time of his stay at Pennicote, avoiding any explanation that might end it prematurely. She physically recoiled from direct courtship, despite enjoying the fantasy of being adored. A fierce

A deeper, more enduring trouble had settled upon the rectory. Rex returned there only to collapse onto his bed, draped in an armor of apparent apathy that held firm until the following day, when it began to crack under the weight of positive sickness. The conversation regarding Southampton vanished entirely. Instead, the minds of his mother and Anna were consumed by a singular, desperate question: How does one tend a patient who refuses to heal? The boy who had once been the household's brightest, most grateful light, had metamorphosed into a dull-eyed, unresponsive creature. Affectionate advances were met only with a weary murmur, "Let me alone."

His father, Mr. Gascoigne, looked past the immediate crisis, viewing it as the shortest path to extricating himself from an unfortunate entanglement. Yet, despite his pragmatic outlook, he felt a pang for the inevitable suffering. He would occasionally sit by Rex's side in silence, pressing a gentle hand upon his son's blank brow before departing with a soft, "God bless you, my boy." Warham and the younger siblings often peeked around the doorframe, transfixed by the sight of their lively brother laid low, only to be shooed away by stern fingers. The constant guardian in this shadow was Anna. Her small hand rested within her brother's, though he offered no welcoming squeeze. Her soul was torn in twain: half in anguish for Rex, the other half burning with reproach for Gwendolen.

"Perhaps it is wicked of me, but I think I never *can* love her again," whispered Anna, the recurring burden of her inner lament. Even Mrs. Gascoigne harbored an angry sentiment toward her niece, which she expressed to her husband with an apology that did little to soften the blow.

"I know, of course, it is better, and we ought to be thankful she is not in love with the poor boy," Mrs. Gascoigne confessed. "But really, Henry, I think she is hard. She has the heart of a coquette. I cannot help thinking she must have led him on with false promises; otherwise, the disappointment would not have struck him with such devastating force. And some blame must attach to poor Fanny; she is utterly blind concerning that girl."

Mr. Gascoigne answered imperatively. "The less said on that point the better, Nancy. I ought to have been more awake myself. As for the boy, be thankful if nothing worse ever happens to him. Let the matter die out as quickly as possible. Especially regarding Gwendolen—let it be as if it had never existed."

The rector's dominant emotion was that of a man who had escaped disaster. If Gwendolen had loved Rex in return, it would have created a problem far harder to solve, one potentially beyond his control. However, he faced further difficulties.

On a fine morning, Rex requested his bath and prepared for the day as he usually did. Anna, filled with electric hope at this sign of change, listened intently for his descent. Finally, hearing his steps, she ran to the foot of the stairs to meet him. For the first time since his illness, he gave her a faint smile. It was so melancholy upon his pale face that she could barely suppress the urge to cry.

"Nannie!" he said gently, taking her hand and leading her slowly down to the drawing-room. His mother was present, and when she kissed him, he exclaimed, "What a plague I am!"

He then sat still, gazing out of the bow-window at the lawn and shrubs dusted with hoarfrost, across which the sun sent faint, occasional gleams—a reflection, Anna thought, of that sad smile on Rex's face. He felt as though he had been resurrected into a new world, leaving the old interests far behind, unsure of how to inhabit this strange space. Anna sat near him, pretending to work while secretly watching him with yearning eyes. Beyond the garden hedge, a road stretched where wagons and carts sometimes traveled during field-work. A railed opening cut through the hedge, revealing an upland pasture bordered by woods and a cluster of ash trees against the sky.

Soon, a wagon laden with lumber appeared. The horses strained their magnificent muscles, and the driver, cracking his whip, ran alongside to guide the lead horse's head, fearful of a sudden swerve. Rex seemed shaken from his lethargy by the motion. He rose and watched until the last quivering trunk of timber disappeared, then paced the floor once or twice. Mrs. Gascoigne had retired, and when Rex sat down again, Anna, sensing a return of speech in his eyes, could not resist bringing a small stool. She seated herself against his knee, looking up at him with an expression that silently pleaded, *"Do speak to me."* And he spoke.

"I'll tell you what I'm thinking of, Nannie," Rex said. "I will go to Canada, or somewhere of that sort." (Rex had not yet studied the complex character of colonial possessions).

"Oh, Rex, not for always!"

"Yes, to earn my bread there. I should like to build a hut, work hard at clearing the land, have everything wild around me, and enjoy a great, wide quiet."

"And not take me with you?" Anna asked, large tears rushing to her eyes.

"How could I?"

"I should like it better than anything. Settlers go with their families. I would sooner go there than stay here in England. I could make the fires, mend the clothes, cook the food; I could learn how to bake the bread before we depart. It would be nicer than anything—it would be like playing at life over again, as we used to do when we made our tent with the drugget and played with our little plates and dishes."

"Father and mother would not let you go."

"Yes, I think they would, if I explained everything properly. It would save money, and Papa would have more to support the boys."

Further conversations of this practical nature followed at intervals, ending with Rex consenting that Anna accompany him, pending his discussion with his father.

Of course, this plan required the rector's attention when he was alone in his study. Their mother, who would eventually reconcile to whatever decision he made, would have found the initial question distressing to hear.

"Well, my children!" said Mr. Gascoigne, cheerfully as they entered. It was a comfort to see Rex moving about again.

"May we sit down with you a little, papa?" said Anna. "Rex has something to say."

"With all my heart."

These three formed a striking group, each possessing a face of identical structural type: the straight brow, the nose abruptly straightened from an intention to be aquiline, the short upper lip, the short but strong and well-hung chin. There was also the same tone of complexion and the same set of the eye. The gray-haired father was both massive and keen-looking; a perpendicular line in his brow deepened whenever he spoke with force or interest, and the habit of ruling bestowed upon him an air of reserved authority. Rex would have seemed a vision of his father's youth, if one could imagine Mr. Gascoigne stripped of his distinct plans and command, smitten with heart-sorrow, and devoid of any notion of concealment other than that of a sick animal. Anna, meanwhile, was a tiny copy of Rex, her hair drawn back and knotted, her face mirroring every shift in his expression, as if they shared a single soul.

"You know all about what has upset me, father," Rex began, and Mr. Gascoigne nodded.

"I am quite done up for life in this part of the world. I am sure it will be useless for me to return to Oxford. I could not possibly do any reading. I should fail, and cause you expense for nothing. I want your consent to pursue another course, sir."

Mr. Gascoigne nodded more slowly; the vertical line on his brow deepened, and Anna's trembling grew.

"If you would allow me a small outfit, I should like to go to the colonies and work on the land there." Rex thought the vagueness of the phrase prudent; "the colonies" necessarily embraced broader advantages and were less susceptible to refutation on a single specific ground than any particular settlement.

"Oh, and with me, papa," said Anna, unable to bear even temporary exclusion from the proposal. "Rex needs someone to take care of him, you know—someone to keep house. And we shall never, either of us, be married. And I should cost nothing, and I should be so happy. I know it would be hard to leave you and Mamma, but there are all the others to raise, and the two of us would be no trouble to you anymore."

Anna had risen from her seat, employing the feminine argument of proximity as she spoke, drawing closer to her father. He did not smile, but he drew her onto his knee and held her there, as if to gently remove her from the equation while he addressed Rex.

"You will admit that my experience gives me some power of judging for you, and that I can probably guide you in practical matters better than you can guide yourself?"

Rex was obliged to say, "Yes, sir."

"And perhaps you will admit—though I do not wish to press that point—that you are bound in duty to consider my judgment and wishes?"

"I have never yet placed myself in opposition to you, sir."

"Tell me, Berthold, what hope looks like in men:
A trembling child, delirious with delight,
Who casts a toy-fishing line, baited with stone,
Into the salt sea, dreaming of a prize."

Eight months after the family's arrival at Offendene, during the heat of late June, a whisper rippled through the local countryside, sparking excitement among those who listened. It bore no connection to the wars across the Atlantic, yet it resonated deeply with everyone from the corn merchants and brewers to the humble blacksmith of Diplow. To the laborers' wives, it promised employment for their nimble sons; to the farmers, a chance for better trade; and to the gentry, it offered a vision of elevated status. Above all, it stirred the dormant dreams of matrimony. Just as a royal visit ignites fantasies of knighthood, so this news awakened a fluttering vision of marriage in the minds of the well-born.

The news centered on Diplow Hall, Sir Hugo Mallinger's estate, which had stood silently watching its elms and beeches with shuttered windows for nearly two years. Now, preparations were underway to welcome a tenant in a fitting style for the remainder of the summer and the hunting season. The guest was not Sir Hugo, but his nephew, Mr. Mallinger Grandcourt—the presumptive heir to the baronetcy, as Sir Hugo's marriage had yielded only daughters. Fortune smiled upon young Grandcourt doubly: the baronetcy hung in the balance through his father, while his mother possessed a baronial lineage. Should certain distant relatives pass away, he might ascend to the peerage. Nature, it seemed, reserved the tuft of feathers for the male bird alone, yet the philosophy of copying nature blindly was not yet embraced by the wise. If Grandcourt secured a title, his wife would share it—an advantage that made the prospect of finding a bride for him a matter of considerable interest to many.

Yet, it is hard for some to believe that people would build matrimonial futures on mere rumors of a wealthy bachelor's arrival, dismissing such thoughts as cynical gall. They claim their minds are not so unbridged, that human nature knows better than to speculate on fallacies. However, this history does not concern general human nature, but rather a specific corner of Wessex, where reputations were unimpeached and visitors mingled freely with the ranks.

Consider the Arrowpoints, residing at their beautiful home, Quetcham. Though no one could accuse them of sordid motives regarding their daughter Catherine—who had stubbornly refused Lord Slogan, an Irish peer whose estate lacked drainage and population—their hearts were filled with anxious rumination. They wondered if Mr. Grandcourt was handsome, of sound constitution, virtuous, or perhaps a reformed man of liberal-conservative leanings. Whether his succession to the title was an event to be desired, they hoped he would be fit to secure Catherine's happiness.

These thoughts were mirrored in Mr. Gascoigne, the clergyman, who, despite his vocation, felt the heavy burden of a parent and guardian. He and his wife, Mrs. Gascoigne, began to feel that the young creatures under their care were beyond the reach of any metaphorical bit or bridle. Naturally, people did not openly confess their deepest fears or calculations concerning Grandcourt. On no subject is total transparency prudent, not even regarding the mysteries of chemistry or astronomy. One might forestall another with superior insight, or worse, incur ill will due to differing opinions. Mr. Gascoigne did not ask Mr. Arrowpoint for sources on Grandcourt as a husband, nor did Mrs. Arrowpoint voice to Mrs. Davilow her expectation that the prospective peer would seek Catherine, adding that she would not accept him unless he proved worthy. Even to his wife, the rector remained silent about the possibility of matrimony, fearing the implications of Grandcourt meeting Gwendolen at the upcoming Archery Meeting, though Mrs. Gascoigne's mind raced along similar paths. She had once exclaimed, "It would be a mercy, Fanny, if that girl were well married!" To which Mrs. Davilow, sensing a critique of her darling, replied inwardly, "You will not get her to marry for your pleasure."

To her husband, Mrs. Gascoigne asked if she had heard what sort of young man Grandcourt was, hoping he would set a good example. Mr. Gascoigne admitted he knew little. His acquaintances may have gossiped, but he deemed it futile to inquire into the past of a young man whose wealth and leisure rendered many habits venial. If Grandcourt had gambled, leaving others ruined, he would be a reformed character indeed, for true reformation requires the strength to stop when ruin is near. Yet, Mr. Gascoigne suspected no gambling. A landed proprietor with noble blood need not be scrutinized like a reformed servant offering himself as a footman. Reformation, when affordable, is rarely anything but genuine. Moreover, there was no evidence suggesting Grandcourt needed reform more than other youths of twenty-five, and ultimately, his current character mattered more than his history.

Mrs. Davilow, though outwardly composed, harbored her own speculations. An image of a handsome, accomplished, and excellent young man formed instantly in her mind, suitable for Gwendolen. But soon, a second, darker thought intruded: *Would Gwendolen be satisfied?* There was no guarantee that excellence aligned with Gwendolen's tastes or affections. Perhaps it required something else entirely. Thus, the image of the perfect suitor dissolved into a fluctuating blend of qualities that might win Gwendolen's heart. In this difficulty, Mrs. Davilow whispered to herself, "It would not signify about her being in love, if she would only accept the right person." For herself, marriage had been a necessity born of misfortune, yet she dared not dwell on its desirability for Gwendolen, fearing a response akin to that of the future Madame Roland: "Yes, Mother, like you."

Least of all would Mrs. Davilow allow hints of her "aerial castle-building" to slip out, aware that such thoughts might poison Gwendolen's view of a desirable husband before she even met him. Since Rex's farewell, the mother had felt a new sense of peril in probing the mystery of her child's feelings, yet she could conceive of welfare only in the shape of marriage.

The conversation shifted to Gwendolen's attire for the Archery Meeting. Having decided on a pale green accent for her white cashmere, Mrs. Davilow felt her ears tingle when Gwendolen suddenly threw herself into the stance of drawing a bow, her expression one of comic enjoyment. "How I pity all the other girls at the Archery Meeting!" she declared. "All thinking of Mr. Grandcourt! And they have not a shadow of a chance."

Startled, Mrs. Davilow hesitated, but Gwendolen turned sharply, her tone wicked. "Now you know they have not, Mamma. You and my Uncle and Aunt—you all intend him to fall in love with me."

Piqued, Mrs. Davilow feigned surprise. "Oh, my dear, that is not so certain. Miss Arrowpoint has charms which you have not."

"I know," Gwendolen retorted, "but they demand thought. My arrow will pierce him before he has time for thought. He will declare himself my slave—I shall send him round the world to bring me back the wedding ring of a happy woman—in the meantime, all the men who stand between him and the title will die of different diseases. He will return as Lord Grandcourt, without the ring, and fall at my feet. I shall laugh at him; he will rise in resentment; I shall laugh more. He will summon his steed and ride to Quetcham, where he will find Miss Arrowpoint newly wed to a needy musician, Mrs. Arrowpoint tearing her cap off, and Mr. Arrowpoint standing by. Exit Lord Grandcourt, who returns to Diplow, and, like M. Jabot, *changes his linen*."

Had there ever been a young witch more like this? One might try to hide secrets from her, sit upon them looking innocent, only to realize she knew precisely how much money lay beneath the cushion by the corner of her eye. To turn the key against the damp would be futile. By dint of divination, she likely already knew more of Mr. Grandcourt than anyone else. Driven by this suspicion, Mrs. Davilow asked, almost without thinking, "What kind of a man do you imagine him to be, Gwendolen?"

"Let me see!" said the witch, pressing her forefinger to her lips with a thoughtful frown, then stretching it out decisively. "Short—just above my shoulder—trying to appear taller by turning up his mustache and keeping his beard long. A glass in his right eye to lend him an air of distinction. A strong opinion about his waistcoat, but uncertain and trim about the weather, on which he will try to draw me out. He will stare at me constantly, and that glass in his eye will make him pull horrible faces, especially when he smiles flatteringly. I shall cast down my eyes, and he will perceive I am not indifferent. I shall dream that night that I am gazing at the magnified face of an insect—and the next morning he will make an offer. The sequel, as before."
