"That is a portrait of someone you have seen already, Gwen," Mrs. Davilow said gently. "Mr. Grandcourt may prove a delightful young man."

"Oh, yes," Gwendolen admitted with

"Sir, let us consult the taste of marriageable men. Consider the world's store: iron, cotton, wool, chemicals—all rendered to our plastic skill. Every shape is wrought responsive to demand; the market's pulse sets the law, by some sublime rule. Our daughters must be wives, and to the wives must be whatever men will choose. A man's taste is a woman's test. Observe the phrasing? 'Tis good, I think? Well-winged and poised with those letters."

"Nay, turn it round," interjected the second gentleman. "Give us the test of taste. A fine *menu*—is it today what Roman epicures insisted a gentleman must eat to earn the dignity of dining?"

At Brackenshaw Park, where the archery meeting was held, the landscape unfolded from gentle heights overlooking the valley, stretching eastward toward the downs and westward into a broad, cultivated plain that hung like a vast curtain. Upon the highest platform of the clustered hills stood the castle, constructed of rough-hewn limestone, its surface textured with the dark dust of lichens and the washings of rain, casting a play of lights and shadows. Masses of beech and fir sheltered it on the north, spreading down the green slopes like flocks seeking the water that gleamed below. At the farthest end of the park lay the carefully kept archery ground, a table-land protected by tall elms and a thick screen of hollies, ensuring the gravel walks and the freshly mown turf where targets stood remained in agreeable afternoon shade. Ahead, the Archery Hall with its arcade rose like a white temple against the surrounding greenery.

What could serve as a finer backdrop for the flower-groups of ladies moving, bowing, and turning their necks with the leisurely grace of lilies given motion? Even after the military band from Manchester ceased playing, the atmosphere retained a pleasant resonance: musical laughter in all registers, a harmony of friendly speeches rising to mild excitement before sinking back into an agreeable murmur. No open-air amusement could claim such freedom from the noisy, crowding conditions that often spoil modern pleasures. This gathering was highly selective; the number of guests accompanying the members was restricted by ticket awards to ensure comfort for the ensuing dinner and ball within the castle. Within the enclosure, no plebeian spectators were admitted save Lord Brackenshaw's tenants and their families. Among these, it was chiefly the feminine members who exercised the privilege, bringing small children or younger siblings. While the young males relieved the slight insipidity of the event with imaginative betting—"anything you like"—being their stake, the young maidens applied a different principle of discrimination, deliberating over which of the sweetly dressed ladies they would choose to emulate if given the power of selection. Though perhaps the rustic souls would have striven for a tabernacle unlike Gwendolen's—one flushed with more pink cheeks and hair of the most fashionable yellow—the male judges surrounding her unanimously pronounced her the finest girl present.

No wonder she reveled in that July day. Pre-eminence is sweet to those who cherish it, even amidst mediocrity. Perhaps it was not purely mythical that a slave has been proud to be bought first; nor unlikely that a barn-yard fowl, though unaware of his condition, might possess a self-aware consciousness of his relative importance and strut accordingly. Yet for complete enjoyment, the outward and inward must concur. And for Gwendolen, this concurrence was absolute.

Who could deny that bows and arrows are among the fairest weapons in the world for feminine forms to wield? They invite attitudes of grace and power, liberating the fine concentration of energy inherent in marksmanship from bloody associations. The ancient British resource of "killing something" was no longer carried out with bow and quiver; bands defended their passes under shades other than clouds of arrows, and poisoned darts existed only as harmless survivors in rhetoric or distant regions. Archery possessed no ugly scent of brimstone, broke no shins, and bred no athletic monsters. Its sole peril was failure, a prospect sufficient to mold skilled action in generous hearts. Moreover, the prizes awarded by the Brackenshaw Archery Club were of the nobler symbolic kind: not parcels to be hoarded, degrading honor into gain, but the gold and silver arrows, the stars to be worn upon the breast for a long time before transfer to the next worthy achiever. These signs of pre-eminence offered the virtue of wreaths without their inconvenience, avoiding any melancholy effect in the heat of the ballroom. Indeed, the club was framed with impeccable taste, seemingly immune to ridiculous incidents.

Today, every incalculable element favored the occasion. The air was mildly warm, devoid of wind to disturb hair, drapery, or the flight of the arrow. All preparations met fair play, and when the general march to retrieve arrows commenced, the promenade of joyful young creatures, speaking lightly and laughing, moving gracefully toward a common object, formed a spectacle worth viewing. Here, Gwendolen seemed a Calypso among her nymphs. It was in her attitudes and movements that everyone was compelled to admit her surpassing charm.

"That girl is like a high-mettled racer," observed Lord Brackenshaw to young Clintock, one of the invited spectators.

"First chop! Tremendously pretty, too," remarked the elegant Grecian, who had paid her assiduous attention; "I never saw her look better."

Perhaps she had never looked so well. Her face beamed with young pleasure, free from any malignant ray of discontent. Being satisfied with her own chances, she felt kindly toward all and content with the universe. Lacking the highest distinction in rank or the spotlight of an heiress like Miss Arrowpoint granted her an added triumph in eclipsing those advantages. Regarding personal recommendation, she would not have traded her family group for any other: her mother's appearance suited an amiable duchess, while her Uncle and Aunt Gascoigne, alongside Anna, made equally gratifying figures. Gwendolen was so full of joyous belief in herself that she felt no jealousy whatsoever regarding Miss Arrowpoint, despite the latter's prowess as an archeress.

Even the unexpected reappearance of the formidable Herr Klesmer, causing surprise among the company, aligned perfectly with Gwendolen's inclination to be amused. Short of Apollo himself, what greater musical maestro could cut a figure at an archery meeting? A satirical light flickered in Gwendolen's eyes as she glanced toward the Arrowpoint party upon their entrance. The contrast between Klesmer and the average assembly of English country folk reached its zenith in close proximity to his hosts—or patrons, as Mrs. Arrowpoint would prefer them to be called, thereby denying the lingering notion of patronizing genius, whose royalty was universally acknowledged. Such a contrast might have amused a graver soul, but Gwendolen found it diverting. The English, a miscellaneous people, display varied architectures of animal and facial ornament in chance fifties; however, the prevailing expression of the pure English gentleman is the easy style of his figure and clothing. He objects to marked ins-and-outs in attire and, equally, to appearing inspired.

Imagine an assembly where men bore the ordinary stamp of the well-bred Englishman, watching the entrance of Herr Klesmer. His mane floated backward in massive inconsistency with a chimney-pot hat, seemingly donned for a joke atop his pronounced, well-modeled features and powerful, clear-shaven jaw. His tall, thin frame was clad in a manner that, lacking strict adherence to English norms, emphasized intention to an almost comical degree. Draped in a loose garment with a Florentine beretta, he would have stood beside Leonardo da Vinci; yet how did he fare presenting himself in trousers that defied English sensibilities regarding the knee? How did the fire evident in his glances and the curious movements of his head transform into comedy under the tyranny of a hat mandating well-cropped hair and staid demeanor, such as that of Mr. Arrowsmith, whose nullity of face and perfection of tailoring passed everywhere without ridicule? One understands why it is often better for greatness to be dead, shedding the outward man.

Many present knew Klesmer, or had heard of him, but only in candle-lit settings where he appeared merely as a musician. He had not yet attained that supreme, world-wide celebrity that renders an artist great to the most ordinary through knowledge of his expense. It was a novel light for them to perceive him in—an unexpected presentation on this July afternoon within an exclusive society. Some were inclined to laugh; others felt a pang of disgust at the lack of judgment displayed by the Arrowpoints in utilizing an introductory card.

"What extreme guises those artistic fellows usually adopt!" cried young Clintock to Gwendolen. "Behold the figure he cuts, bowing with hand upon heart to Lady Brackenshaw—and Mrs. Arrowpoint's feather just reaching his shoulder."

"You are one of the profane," retorted Gwendolen. "You are blind to the majesty of genius. Herr Klesmer smites me with awe; I feel crushed in his presence; my courage oozes away."

"Ah, you understand all about his music."

"No, indeed," Gwendolen replied with a light laugh. "It is he who understands all about mine and thinks it pitiable." Klesmer's verdict on her singing had become an easier jest since he had been struck by her *plastic*.

"It is not addressed to the ears of the future, I suppose. I'm glad of that: it suits mine."

"Oh, you are very kind. But how remarkably well Miss Arrowpoint looks today! She would make quite a fine picture in that gold-colored dress."

"Too splendid,

To begin knowing someone, or something, is first to carve a shape out of one's ignorance. For Gwendolen, the desire to meet Mr. Grandcourt had ripened slowly within her, like a seed waiting for spring. Yet, when Lord Brackenshaw stepped back to reveal the stranger, a sudden jolt struck her, flushing her cheeks with a vexatious heat. The shock lay not in his presence, but in his absence of expectation; he was the antithesis of every portrait she had painted in her mind.

He stood slightly taller than she, their eyes meeting on a level plane. There was no smile upon his lips, no flicker of self-consciousness or anxiety in his posture. As he raised his hat, it revealed an extensive baldness framed by a sparse, reddish-blonde fringe, yet his hands were perfect instruments, and his features, from brow to chin, possessed a handsome, unadorned symmetry. There was absolutely no grimace, no solicitous wiggle of the body. If a man could exist entirely free of animation, he did so now. While the "correct" Englishman draws himself up from a bow into a rigid stiffness, suggesting a suppressed vitality that might explode later, Grandcourt's bearing leaned toward the flaccid. His complexion held a faded fairness, like an actress stripped of artifice, and his long, narrow gray eyes expressed a profound, unyielding indifference.

Conversations are built on pauses, and here the silence was heavy. Grandcourt looked at Gwendolen with a persistent, slightly exploring gaze, his expression unmoving, while she offered him occasional flashes of observation, softened only by a touch of coquetry. After her answers, he allowed a longer silence before speaking again.

"I used to think archery was a great bore," Grandcourt began. His voice had a fine accent, though delivered with a broken drawl, as if he suffered from a distinguished cold.

"Are you converted today?" Gwendolen asked.

During the pause, Gwendolen imagined various opinions he might hold of her.

"Yes, since I saw you shooting. In things of this sort, one generally sees people missing and simpering."

"I suppose you are a first-rate shot with a rifle."

Another pause, during which Gwendolen mentally sketched a description of him for an indefinite listener.

"I have left off shooting."

"Oh, then you are a formidable person," Gwendolen replied, interpreting his words. "People who have done things once and left them off make one feel very contemptible, as if one were wearing cast-off fashions. I hope you have not left off all follies, because I practice a great many."

"What do you call follies?"

"Well, in general, I think, whatever is agreeable is called a folly. But you have not left off hunting, I hear."

Gwendolen recalled what she had heard of Grandcourt's station and decided he was the most aristocratic-looking man she had ever seen.

"One must do something."

"And do you care about the turf? Or is that among the things you have left off?"

"I run a horse now and then, but I don't go in for the thing as some men do. Are you fond of horses?"

"Yes, indeed! I never like my life so well as when I am on horseback, having a great gallop. I think of nothing. I only feel myself strong and happy."

"Do you like danger?"

"I don't know. When I am on horseback, I never think of danger. It seems to me that if I broke my bones, I should not feel it. I should go at anything that came in my way."

"You would perhaps like tiger-hunting or pig-sticking. I saw some of that for a season or two in the East. Everything here is poor stuff after that."

"You are fond of danger, then?"

Gwendolen wondered if a man of such cold manners could truly be dangerous, yet she felt the strength of her own insight regarding the nature of adventure.

"One must have something or other. But one gets used to it."

"I begin to think I am very fortunate, because everything is new to me: it is only that I can't get enough of it. I am not used to anything except being dull, which I should like to leave off as you have left off shooting."

"Why are you dull?"

"This is a dreadful neighborhood. There is nothing to be done in it. That is why I practiced my archery."

Gwendolen reflected that the life of an unmarried woman, with no command over her surroundings, must inevitably become dull.

"You have made yourself queen of it. I imagine you will carry the first prize."

"I don't know that. I have great rivals. Did you not observe how well Miss Arrowpoint shot?"

"Miss Arrowpoint. No—that is, yes."

"Shall we go now and hear what the scoring says? Every one is going to the other end now—shall we join them? I think my uncle is looking toward me. He perhaps wants me."

Gwendolen found relief in shifting the situation. The tête-à-tête was not entirely disagreeable, but she could not shake the unwonted flush in her cheeks or the sense of surprise that made her feel less mistress of herself than usual. This Mr. Grandcourt, who seemed to feel his own importance more than hers, must not assume that he was of great moment to her. How Grandcourt had filled the pauses would become clearer later.

"You have just missed the gold arrow, Gwendolen," said Mr. Gascoigne. "Miss Juliet Fenn scores eight above you."

"I am very glad to hear it. I should have felt that I was making myself too disagreeable—taking the best of everything," Gwendolen said easily.

It was impossible to be jealous of Juliet Fenn, a girl as middling as midday market in everything save her archery and plainness, in which latter trait she resembled her father: underhung jaw and a receding brow, like the more intelligent fishes. Surely, considering the weight placed on such accidents in female offspring, intending bridegrooms should look at themselves dispassionately, lest their natural selection of a prettier mate prove futile due to their own ugliness.

A lively movement swept through the mingling groups, carrying the talk with it. Everyone spoke to everyone else by turns. Gwendolen, choosing to observe the scene, noted that Grandcourt was having Klesmer presented to him by a middle-aged man with a dark, full face and fat hands, who seemed on easy terms with both. The stranger led them to join the Arrowpoints, whose acquaintance Grandcourt and Klesmer had already made. Gwendolen cared little for the stranger's identity but wished to watch Grandcourt's manner toward others. It was precisely the same as before, except that he did not look at Miss Arrowpoint; rather, he watched Klesmer, who spoke with animation—stretching long fingers, pointing downward, tossing his mane—while addressing himself to both men. Grandcourt listened with an impassive face and narrow eyes, his left forefinger in his waistcoat pocket and his right lightly touching his thin whisker.

"I wonder which style Miss Arrowpoint admires most," Gwendolen thought, a mocking expression gathering at her lips. But she restrained herself from indulging curiosity, giving all her animation to those immediately around her, determined not to care whether Mr. Grandcourt approached her again or not.

He did approach, however, at a moment when he could propose conducting Mrs. Davilow to her carriage. "Shall we meet again in the ball-room?" she said as he raised his hat. The reply was a slight drawl of "Yes" with perfect gravity.

"You were wrong for once, Gwendolen," said Mrs. Davilow during their short drive to the castle.

"In what, Mamma?"

"About Mr. Grandcourt's appearance and manners. You can't find anything ridiculous in him."

"I suppose I could if I tried, but I don't want to do it," Gwendolen said pettishly, and her mother dared say no more.

By custom, ladies and gentlemen dined apart, allowing for comparative ease. Indeed, the gentlemen had spun stories of the ladies' epicureanism, reporting revolting masculine judgments in venison, even requests for the fat—a proof of the frightful rate at which corruption might spread in women, were it not for severe social restraint. Every year, the amiable Lord Brackenshaw, somewhat of a gourmet, cited Byron's opinion that a woman should never be seen eating, introducing it with a confidential, "The fact is," as if admitting a secret concurrence.

In the ladies' dining-room, Gwendolen was not a general favorite. There were no intimacies forming with the other girls; in conversation, they noticed what she said rather than speaking freely to her. Perhaps it was that she was not much interested in them, and when left alone, she felt a sense of empty benches. Mrs. Vulcany once remarked that Miss Harleth was too fond of the gentlemen, but Gwendolen knew she was not fond of them; she was only fond of their homage, and women did not give her homage. The exception was Miss Arrowpoint, who often managed unostentatiously to be by her side, talking with quiet friendliness.

"She knows, as I do, that our friends are ready to quarrel over a husband for us," Gwendolen thought, "and she is determined not to enter into the quarrel."

"I think Miss Arrowpoint has the best manners I ever saw," said Mrs.

"O gentlemen, the time of life is short;
To spend that shortness basely were too long..."

These words from Shakespeare hung in the air, a faint echo over breakfast on the second day after the archery meeting. The room itself was a study in quiet, sober antiquity, standing in stark contrast to the restless vulgarity of modern furnishings. Through the open windows, the summer air drifted in, carrying the scent of the old green turf where the dogs roamed free. Beyond lay the park, painted in soft purples, stretching toward a bordering wood that seemed to hold the world at bay.

Inside, however, the atmosphere was charged with a subtle tension. Mr. Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt sat with his back to the window, facing the lawn, his left leg draped casually over another chair, his right elbow propped on the table. A large cigar burned steadily between his lips, smoke curling around him like a halo of indifference. Beside him, Mr. Lush was still engaged with his food, a man of practical habits who knew exactly where his place stood in relation to his master.

Between them moved a procession of six dogs, creatures of considerable leisure who played at hunger, accepting delicacies only to decline them. They vacillated in their attentions, shifting loyalty from one gentleman to the other, save for one exception. There was Fetch, a beautiful liver-colored water-spaniel whose forepaws were planted firmly on the floor, her expressive brown gaze fixed unswervingly on Grandcourt. In his lap, the master held a tiny Maltese named Fluff, adorned with a silver collar and bell. Whenever Grandcourt's hand was not occupied with cigar or cup, it rested possessively on this bundle of warmth.

It was clear, to any observer with a heart for animals, that Fetch felt a sting of jealousy. She waited, silent and dignified, until the neglect became unbearable. Then, with a gentle, deliberate motion, she placed her large, silky paw upon her master's leg.

Grandcourt regarded her with a face devoid of change for half a minute. Then, with a sigh of profound effort, he laid down his cigar. He lifted the unimpassioned Fluff close to his chin, bestowing upon it the requisite caresses, all the while maintaining a grave, scrutinizing stare at Fetch. Poor Fetch, interpreting this as a final dismissal, let out a whimper, attempting to suppress the sign of her discontent. She lowered her head beside the appealing paw, looking up with piteous beseechment. To a lover of dogs, this was a scene of devotion; Grandcourt, reputed to adore animals, certainly acted as if he understood the tragedy unfolding.

But when the amusing anguish of the neglected spaniel erupted into a howling bark, Grandcourt pushed Fetch down without a word. He deposited Fluff carelessly onto the table, where the puppy's black nose soon dominated a salt-cellar. Turning his attention back to his cigar, he discovered with some annoyance that the beast had extinguished it. As he sought to relight it, Fetch, having begun to wail, found, like many of her sex, that it was difficult to stop. Indeed, the second howl was louder, the third indistinguishable from it.

"Turn out that brute, will you?" Grandcourt said to Lush, without raising his voice or even glancing at him, as if he expected his companion to read the smallest flicker of irritation.

Lush immediately rose. Though he was not fond of stooping, especially when the subject was heavy, he lifted Fetch and carried her out, disappearing for a couple of minutes before returning. He then lit a fresh cigar, positioned himself at an angle where he could observe Grandcourt's face without turning his body, and broke the silence.

"Shall you ride or drive to Quetcham today?"

"I am not going to Quetcham."

"You did not go yesterday."

Grandcourt smoked silently for a moment, then replied, "I suppose you sent my card and inquiries."

"I went myself at four," Lush said smoothly. "I told them you were sure to be there shortly. They would suppose some accident prevented you from fulfilling the intention. Especially if you go today."

A silence stretched between them for several minutes. Finally, Grandcourt asked, "What men are invited here with their wives?"

Lush drew out a notebook. "The Captain and Mrs. Torrington come next week. Then there are Mr. Hollis and Lady Flora, and the Cushats and the Gogoffs."

"A rather ragged lot," remarked Grandcourt after a pause. "Why did you ask the Gogoffs? When you write invitations in my name, be good enough to give me a list, instead of bringing down a giantess on me without my knowledge. She spoils the look of the room."

"You invited the Gogoffs yourself when you met them in Paris."

"What has my meeting them in Paris to do with it?" Grandcourt snapped, his voice dropping suddenly. "I told you to give me a list."

There was a remarkable duality to Grandcourt's voice. Hitherto, he had spoken in a superficial, interrupted drawl suggestive chiefly of languor and *ennui*. But these last few words had been uttered in subdued, inward tones, sharp and distinct—a sound Lush had long learned to recognize as the expression of a peremptory will.

"Are there any other couples you would like to invite?" Lush asked carefully.

"Yes," said Grandcourt, "think of some decent people, with a daughter or two. And one of your damned musicians. But not a comic fellow."

"I wonder if Klesmer would consent to come to us when he leaves Quetcham. Nothing but first-class music will go down with Miss Arrowpoint."

Lush spoke carelessly, yet his eyes were fixed observantly on Grandcourt. For the first time, Grandcourt turned his gaze toward his companion, holding it slowly and without speech until he had given two long, luxuriant puffs of smoke. Then, in a lower tone than ever, with a perceptible edge of contempt, he said, "What in the name of nonsense have I to do with Miss Arrowpoint and her music?"

"Well, something," said Lush, jocosely. "You need not give yourself much trouble, perhaps. But some forms must be gone through before a man can marry a million."

"Very likely. But I am not going to marry a million."

"That's a pity—to fling away an opportunity of this sort, and knock down your own plans."

"_Your_ plans, I suppose you mean," Grandcourt retorted.

"You have some debts, you know, and things may turn out inconveniently, after all. The heirship is not _absolutely_ certain."

Grandcourt did not answer. Lush pressed on. "It really is a fine opportunity. The father and mother ask for nothing better, I can see, and the daughter's looks and manners require no allowances, any more than if she hadn't a sixpence. She is not beautiful; but equal to carrying any rank. And she is not likely to refuse such prospects as you can offer her."

"Perhaps not."

"The father and mother would let you do anything you like with them."

"But I should not like to do anything with them."

Here Lush paused, gathering his thoughts, before speaking again in a deep voice of remonstrance. "Good God, Grandcourt! After your experience, will you let a whim interfere with your comfortable settlement in life?"

"Spare your oratory. I know what I am going to do."

"What?" Lush put down his cigar and thrust his hands into his side pockets, as if he had to face something exasperating, though he meant to keep his temper. "I am going to marry the other girl."

"Have you fallen in love?" The question carried a strong sneer.

"I am going to marry her."

"You have made her an offer already, then?"

"No."

"She is a young lady with a will of her own, I fancy. Extremely well fitted to make a rumpus. She would know what she liked."

"She doesn't like you," said Grandcourt, with the ghost of a smile.

"Perfectly true," said Lush, adding again in a markedly sneering tone. "However, if you and she are devoted to each other, that will be enough."

Grandcourt took no notice of this speech. He sipped his coffee, rose, and strolled out onto the lawn, all the dogs following in his wake.

Lush glanced after him a moment, then resumed his cigar, lighting it slowly. He consulted his beard with inspecting eyes and fingers, stroking it with an air of having arrived at some conclusion, before saying in a subdued voice, "Check, old boy!"

Lush, a man of some ability, had not known Grandcourt for fifteen years without learning what sorts of measures were useless with him, though what sort might prove useful often remained dubious. In the beginning of his career, he had held a fellowship and nearly taken orders for the sake of a college living, but, not being fond of that prospect, he had accepted instead the office of traveling companion to a marquess, and afterward to young Grandcourt. Having lost his father early, Grandcourt had found Lush so convenient that he had allowed him to become prime minister in all his more personal affairs.

The habit of fifteen years had made Grandcourt increasingly dependent on Lush's handiness, while Lush grew increasingly dependent on the lazy luxury to which his transactions on behalf of Grandcourt made no interruption worth reck

"Philistia, be thou glad of me!"

Having settled his mind on marrying Miss Harleth, Grandcourt displayed a remarkable capacity for tailoring means to his ends. For the next fortnight, scarcely a day passed without some arrangement ensuring he saw her, or some emphatic attention proving she occupied his thoughts. With his cousin, Mrs. Torrington, now managing the hospitality of his household, Mrs. Davilow and Gwendolen were invited to a grand party at Diplow. There, amidst many witnesses, the host distinguished the dowerless beauty with singular favor, while showing no solicitude whatsoever regarding the heiress.

The world—at least Mr. Gascoigne and the families of consequence within visiting distance of Pennicote—felt a growing assurance on the subject. In the rector's mind, this conviction hardened into a resolution to do his duty by his niece and ensure the settlements were adequate. Indeed, the wonder to both him and Mrs. Davilow was that the offer, for which so many suitable occasions presented themselves, had not already been made; and in this wonder, Grandcourt himself shared a significant portion. When he had first told Lush of his resolution, he had thought the affair would conclude swiftly. To his own surprise, he had repeatedly promised himself in the morning that he would give Gwendolen the opportunity to accept him that day, only to find by evening that the necessary formality remained unaccomplished. This remarkable fact served to heighten his determination for another day. He had never admitted to himself that Gwendolen might refuse him, yet heaven help us all! We are often unable to act on our certainties. Our objection to a contrary issue—even if it were possible—is so strong that it rises like a spectral illusion between us and our certainty. We are rationally sure that the blind worm cannot bite us mortally, but the thought of being bitten is so intolerable, and the creature has such a biting look, that we decline to handle it.

He had requested permission to bring a beautiful horse for Gwendolen to ride. Mrs. Davilow was to accompany her in the carriage, and they were to travel to Diplow for lunch, with Grandcourt conducting them. It was a fine mid-harvest time, neither too warm nor too cool for a noonday ride of five miles to be delightful. Poppies glowed along the borders of the fields, and a gentle breeze moved softly among the uncut corn, winging the shadow of clouds across the soft gray downs. Here, the sheaves stood tall; there, horses strained their muscles under the last loads from wide spaces of stubble. Everywhere, the green pasture made a broader setting for the corn-fields, and cattle rested beneath wide branches. The road lay through a corner of country where dairy farms looked much as they did in the days of our forefathers—a place where peace and permanence seemed to find a home away from the busy changes sending railway trains flying in the distance.

Yet the spirit of peace and permanence failed to penetrate poor Mrs. Davilow's mind enough to overcome her habit of uneasy foreboding. Watching Gwendolen and Grandcourt canter ahead of her, then slackening their pace to a conversational walk until the carriage caught up, created a gratifying sight. However, it served chiefly to fuel the conflict of hopes and fears swirling in her heart regarding her daughter's fate. Here lay an irresistible opportunity for a lover to speak and end all uncertainties. Mrs. Davilow could only hope, with trembling, that Gwendolen's decision would be favorable. Certainly, if Rex's love had been repugnant to her, Mr. Grandcourt offered a complete contrast. That he had produced some quite novel impression on her seemed evident in her marked abstinence from satirical observations, nay, her total silence concerning his characteristics—a silence Mrs. Davilow dared not break. "Is he a man she would be happy with?" was the question that inevitably arose in the mother's mind. "Well, perhaps as happy as she would be with anyone else—or as most other women are"—was the answer with which she tried to quiet herself; for she could not imagine Gwendolen under the influence of any feeling that would make her satisfied in what we traditionally call "mean circumstances."

Grandcourt's own thoughts looked in the same direction: he wanted to be done with the uncertainty of not having spoken. As to any further uncertainty, well, it was something without any reasonable basis, some quality in the air which acted as an irritant to his wishes.

Gwendolen enjoyed the riding, but her pleasure did not break forth in girlish, unpremeditated chat and laughter as it had on that morning with Rex. She spoke a little, and even laughed, but with a lightness as of a far-off echo. For her, too, there was some peculiar quality in the air—not, she was sure, any subjugation of her will by Mr. Grandcourt, nor the splendid prospects he meant to offer. Gwendolen desired everyone, that dignified gentleman himself included, to understand that she was going to do just as she liked, and that they had better not calculate on her pleasing them. If she chose to take this husband, she wanted him to know she was not going to renounce her freedom, or, according to her favorite formula, "not going to do as other women did."

Grandcourt's speeches that morning were, as usual, of that brief sort which never fails to make a conversational figure when the speaker is held important in his circle. Stopping so soon, they gave signs of a suppressed and formidable ability to say more, and possessed the meritorious quality of allowing lengthiness to others.

"How do you like Criterion's paces?" he said after they had entered the park and were slackening from a canter to a walk.

"He is delightful to ride. I should like to have a leap with him, if it would not frighten mamma. There was a good wide channel we passed five minutes ago. I should like to have a gallop back and take it."

"Pray do. We can take it together."

"No, thanks. Mamma is so timid—if she saw me it might make her ill."

"Let me go and explain. Criterion would take it without fail."

"No—indeed—you are very kind—but it would alarm her too much. I dare take any leap when she is not by; but I do it and don't tell her about it."

"We can let the carriage pass and then set off."

"No, no, pray don't think of it any more: I spoke quite randomly," said Gwendolen. She began to feel a new objection to carrying out her own proposition.

"But Mrs. Davilow knows I shall take care of you."

"Yes, but she would think of you as having to take care of my broken neck."

A considerable pause ensued before Grandcourt said, looking toward her, "I should like to have the right always to take care of you."

Gwendolen did not turn her eyes on him. It seemed to her a long while that she was first blushing, and then turning pale, but to Grandcourt's rate of judgment she answered soon enough, with the lightest flute-tone and a careless movement of the head, "Oh, I am not sure that I want to be taken care of: if I chose to risk breaking my neck, I should like to be at liberty to do it."

She checked her horse as she spoke and turned in her saddle, looking toward the advancing carriage. Her eyes swept across Grandcourt as she made this movement, but there was no language in them to correct the carelessness of her reply. At that very moment, she was aware that she was risking something—not her neck, but the possibility of finally checking Grandcourt's advances—and she did not feel contented with that possibility.

"Damn her!" thought Grandcourt as he too checked his horse. He was not a wordy thinker, and this explosive phrase stood for mixed impressions which eloquent interpreters might have expanded into sentences full of an irritated sense that he was being mystified, and a determination that this girl should not make a fool of him. Did she want him to throw himself at her feet and declare that he was dying for her? It was not by that gate that she could enter on the privileges he could give her. Or did she expect him to write his proposals? Equally a delusion. He would not make his offer in any way that could place him definitely in the position of being rejected. But as to her accepting him, she had done it already in accepting his marked attentions: and anything which happened to break them off would be understood to her disadvantage. She was merely coquetting, then?

However, the carriage came up, and no further *tête-à-tête* could well occur before their arrival at the house, where there was abundant company. To whom Gwendolen, clad in riding-dress with her hat laid aside, and clad also in the repute of being chosen by Mr. Grandcourt, was naturally a centre of observation. Since the objectionable Mr. Lush was not there to look at her, this stimulus of admiring attention heightened her spirits and dispersed, for the time, the uneasy consciousness of divided impulses which threatened her with repentance of her own acts. Whether Grandcourt had been offended or not there was no judging: his manners were unchanged, but Gwendolen's acuteness had not gone deeper than to discern that his manners were no clue for her, and because these were unchanged she was not the less afraid of him.

She had not been at Diplow before except to dine. And since certain points of view from the windows and the garden were worth showing, Lady Flora Hollis proposed after luncheon

"I will not clothe myself in wreck—wear gems
Sawed from cramped finger-bones of women drowned;
Feel chilly vaporous hands of ireful ghosts
Clutching my necklace: trick my maiden breast
With orphans' heritage. Let your dead love
Marry its dead."

The following morning, Gwendolen emerged from her chamber radiant and robust, resembling a tall lily freshly unfurled by the sun. A surge of youthful vitality coursed through her, washing away yesterday's fleeting doubts as easily as a ripple vanishes on a swift river. The prospect of the archery match at Cardell Chase filled her with delight. She envisioned herself gliding beneath the beech trees like a woodland nymph, surrounded by appreciative admirers. Within this imagined tableau, Grandcourt's advances seemed not only acceptable but inevitable. She foresaw him approaching slowly, weaving a web of conversation leading inevitably to a declaration, and she prepared to receive it with the calculated grace that her uncle had advised.

Upon descending to breakfast, where everyone had departed save Mrs. Davilow, Gwendolen discovered a pile of letters on her plate. She read one with a gathering smile, passing it to her mother, who returned it with a renewed cheerfulness, delighted by her daughter's buoyant spirit. "You don't feel inclined to go a thousand miles away?" Mrs. Davilow asked gently.

"Not exactly so far," Gwendolen replied lightly.

"It was a regrettable oversight not to write again sooner," her mother noted. "Could you not write now, before we depart?"

"It is not urgent. Tomorrow will suffice. You see, they leave town today and will remain until Monday. I must send word to Dover."

"Shall I write for you, dear, if it burdens you?"

Gwendolen hesitated briefly before sipping her coffee, then answered brusquely, "Oh, no, let it be. I will write tomorrow." A flicker of guilt touched her heart, and she softened her tone, looking up with playful tenderness. "Dear, old, beautiful mamma!"

"Old, child, truly."

"Please don't, mamma! I meant 'old' for 'darling.' You are hardly twenty-five years older than I am. When you speak in that manner, my life seems to shrivel."

"One can find great happiness in twenty-five years, my dear," Mrs. Davilow murmured.

"I must lose no time in beginning," Gwendolen declared merrily. "The sooner I secure my palaces and coaches, the better."

"And a devoted husband who worships you, Gwen," Mrs. Davilow encouraged.

Gwendolen pouted saucily, offering no verbal response.

A minor shadow fell upon her joy when she learned that the rector was detained by magistrate's business and likely unable to attend Cardell Chase. While she cared little that Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna intended to stay behind, her uncle's presence would have given the decision a sense of inevitability. Decision itself, however, had begun to feel formidable. Having drawn close to accepting Grandcourt, Gwendolen sensed that this accumulation of desired outcomes was solidifying into something rigid, a form that threatened to exclude possibility. When one wishes intensely for much, whatever is obtained often becomes mere limitation. Yet, a reassuring thought persisted: marriage would serve as the gateway to a broader freedom.

The gathering place was a grassy clearing known as Green Arbor, sheltered by a canopy of hanging branches forming a natural amphitheater. Here, the coachload of servants prepared the picnic provisions, while the warden of the Chase would guide the roving archers, ensuring they stayed within the prescribed bounds defined by landmarks like the Double Oak, the Whispering Stones, and the High Cross. The plan called for a brief stroll before luncheon, saving the main expedition for the golden light of the afternoon. The muster proceeded swiftly, sparing anyone from the boredom of waiting. As the groups scattered through the dappled light and shade cast by the intermingled beeches and oaks, one might imagine a painter longing to capture the scene.

Roving archery possessed a distinct elegance compared to stationary target practice, though success required skill in hitting moving marks. Consequently, the hits were distributed unevenly among the volunteers. Perhaps owing to this unpredictability, compounded by Gwendolen's dual distraction of preoccupation and a desperate desire to hide it, she did not distinguish herself in these initial trials. She managed her comparative failures with lively grace, dressed in white and green as she had been during the fateful meeting where Grandcourt first approached her. Though he remained constantly by her side, their outward demeanor suggested little change since their first conversation, yet observers, and Gwendolen herself, concluded that they stood on the brink of engagement.

As the groups returned toward Green Arbor, chatting aimlessly rather than focusing on targets, significant words were exchanged. These remarks marked the true beginning of her acceptance. Grandcourt asked, "Do you know how long it has been since I first saw you in this dress?"

"The archery meeting was on the twenty-fifth, and this is the thirteenth," Gwendolen laughed. "I am not adept at calculation, but I venture to say it must be nearly three weeks."

"A little pause ensued, and then he remarked, 'That is a great loss of time.'"

"That your knowing me causes you to feel the loss?" Gwendolen countered playfully. "Pray, do not be uncomplimentary; I dislike it."

Another pause stretched between them. "It is because of the gain that I feel the loss," Grandcourt finally stated.

Gwendolen allowed a silence to follow, her mind racing. *He is truly ingenious,* she thought. *He never speaks foolishly.* Her prolonged silence seemed the strongest affirmative answer possible. Grandcourt pressed on, "The gain of knowing you makes me acutely feel the time I spend in uncertainty. Do *you* like uncertainty?"

"I think I do, rather," Gwendolen replied, suddenly beaming with a playful smile. "There is more in it."

Grandcourt met her laughing gaze with a slow, steady look that seemed to penetrate her soul, an abstraction of vision. "Do you mean more torment for me?"

Something profoundly strange occurred in that moment, shaking Gwendolen out of her usual self-consciousness. Blushing and averting her eyes, she whispered, "No, that would make me sorry."

Grandcourt would have pursued this answer, which her shift in manner rendered decisive, but he was acutely aware that they were descending into Green Arbor in plain view of everyone, specifically at a point where the slope became inconveniently steep. This necessitated his offering his hand to assist her. She took it, and they descended in silence, watched intently by those already on the level, including Mrs. Arrowpoint, who stood beside Mrs. Davilow. Mrs. Arrowpoint had resolved that Grandcourt's merits were insufficient to induce Catherine to accept him, given Catherine's high standards, having previously rejected Lord Slogan. Thus, she regarded the tenant of Diplow with detached scrutiny.

"Mr. Grandcourt is not equal as a man to his uncle, Sir Hugo Mallinger—he is too languid," Mrs. Arrowpoint observed aloud. "To be sure, Mr. Grandcourt is much younger, but I should not be surprised if Sir Hugo outlives him, despite the difference in years. Succession is a dangerous calculation."

"It is indeed," Mrs. Davilow assented quietly, her cheerful composure masking her habitual melancholy regarding life's unsatisfactory nature.

The narrator need not chronicle the sumptuous food consumed in that green refectory nor dwell on the tales of forest scenery spreading beyond the hollow's edge. Suffice it to say, the food and champagne were of the finest quality, and the conversation belonged to the highest circles, devoid of invidious displays. Some gentlemen strolled and smoked cigars, enjoying a leisurely interval before four o'clock, when the roving would resume. Strangely, Grandcourt was among them, yet not Mr. Lush, who seemed to be serving everyone with excessive generosity, becoming increasingly conspicuous as a blot on the scene to Gwendolen, though he maintained an amiably aloof posture, avoiding obvious eye contact.

When the group prepared to depart, it transpired that the bows were under the charge of Lord Brackenshaw's valet. Mr. Lush offered to retrieve the ladies' bows from the carriage to spare them the trouble. He did not intend to fetch Gwendolen's bow, but fearing he might, she hurried ahead to collect it herself. Just as she approached, the valet intercepted her with the bow, handing it to her along with a letter addressed to her. She asked no questions, instantly recognizing the delicate, feminine handwriting of the past era. Moving away to avoid encountering Mr. Lush, who was arriving to fetch other bows, she turned her back toward the carriage stand and opened the letter. It read:

"If Miss Harleth is in doubt whether she should accept Mr. Grandcourt, let her break from her party after they have passed the Whispering Stones and return to that spot. She will then hear something to decide her; but she can only hear it by keeping this letter a strict secret from everyone. If she does not act according to this letter, she will repent, as the woman who writes it has repented. The secrecy Miss Harleth will feel herself bound in honor to guard."

Gwendolen experienced an internal shock, yet her immediate thought was, "It has arrived in time." Her youthfulness allowed her to absorb the idea of the impending revelation completely, suppressing any suspicion of

"Festina lente"—speed tempered with delay. So spoke Sir Thomas Browne, and the counsel seemed fitting for the tangled web of human fortunes. Gwendolen Harleth, having tasted the dizzying intoxication of foreign gambling, had returned to England with the delusion of an empress of luck. She clung to the chaotic philosophy that actions mattered less than amusement, a conviction born of her recent escapades. Yet, this philosophical detachment could not shield her from the brutal arithmetic of reality. Certain shadowy figures, operating under the mysterious banner of "Grapnell & Co.", had manipulated the realm of chance to alter her family's standing. Against her fierce reluctance, she had returned home bearing a heavy necklace—pawned in a fit of desperate pride, later redeemed by others.

As Gwendolen journeyed back to England, Lord Grandcourt was moving to intercept her. Yet, he approached the pursuit with the deliberate slowness of a predator who enjoys the hunt. He did not rush directly from Diplow to Leubronn; instead, he lingered in Baden-Baden, engaging in polite appointments with Russian acquaintances, only to shatter these social trappings when the necessity of reaching Leubronn became urgent. Grandcourt possessed a peculiar, intermittent vitality. His passions burned with a flickering, smoldering heat rather than open flame. Much of life, he observed, proceeded without such intense emotion: the careful tying of cravats, the attendance at dinners, the delivery of speeches honoring august personages—all performed with a veneer of the English gentleman, requiring little expenditure of vital energy. He could maintain the appearance of obstinacy and persistence, projecting sudden impulses that seemed daemonic, though often these were merely the result of unregulated souls lacking the ducts of habit to channel their nature, reducing them to spurs and puddles under pressure.

Lord Grandcourt harbored a quiet amusement regarding Gwendolen's flight. He viewed her departure not as a rejection, but as a spirited provocation. He suspected it stemmed from a stinging resentment of his perceived coolness in the Cardell Chase incident. To bring a woman so close to tender admission, only to walk away from the final opportunity of securing her consent, required a girl of genuine spirit to resist. And surely, if she meant him to follow, he intended to do so. For a full week, however, he took no action, nor did he inquire of Mr. Lush regarding Miss Harleth's whereabouts. Mr. Lush, the loyal steward, felt a mixture of triumph and deep suspicion. Grandcourt had remained curiously neutral, an expression like an alligator basking in the sun, leaving Lush unable to predict the slow-turning gears of his master's mind. Yet, this procrastination inevitably wasted Grandcourt's formidable energy.

At Diplow, the atmosphere shifted with the absence of the young lady. The guests, usually content, grew restless. Lady Flora Hollis, a woman of vibrant middle-age and insatiable curiosity, seized upon the mystery. She orchestrated a whirlwind of calls to Mrs. Torrington, visiting the rectory at Offendene and Quetcham, gathering gossip with the Arrowpoints. Through these networks, it became clear that Miss Harleth had departed for Leubronn to join the Baron and Baroness von Langen. The initial shock and disappointment of Mrs. Davilow and the Gascoignes had softened into a collective desire to frame Gwendolen's disappearance as nothing eccentric, ensuring it need not be a scandal. Even the rector, a man of practical sense, found his mind drifting toward poetic maxims. He recalled the myth of Amaryllis fleeing, hoping her hiding place would remain hidden, yet acknowledging that in an age of steam engines, love often finds a path "over the mountain and over the wave." He pictured Gwendolen as a creature of excellent sense and coquettish daring, wondering if her boldness had ultimately outstripped her wisdom.

Lady Flora, returning laden with news, saw no barrier to whispering it to Lord Grandcourt at dinner, hinting subtly at rumors of a disappointed suitor. Grandcourt listened with quiet attention, his features unreadable. The following day, he issued orders to Mr. Lush to arrange a plausible pretext for dissolving the party at Diplow within a week. He intended to depart for yachting voyages to the Baltic or elsewhere, feeling imprisoned by the company he did not desire. Lush, ever perceptive, recognized the command as a signal for Grandcourt's journey to Leubronn. Though Grandcourt might creep away like a billiard ball, sticking to the edge of the road, Lush planned to render himself indispensable, ensuring he accompanied his patron. Gwendolen's repulsion for him, a fact well known, only amused Grandcourt, reinforcing his resolve to have Lush constantly at hand.

Thus, it came to pass that Lord Grandcourt arrived at the *Czarina* hotel five days after Gwendolen's departure from Leubronn. There, he encountered his uncle, Sir Hugo Mallinger, along with the latter's family, including Daniel Deronda. It was rarely a joy for a reigning power and an heir presumptive to converge, especially when separated by a touch of gout in the elder and a streak of willfulness in the younger. Sir Hugo was a man of easy temper, tolerant of differences, yet the dispute over the settlement of the family estates gnawed at him. While he could forgive faults in the church or the ballot, the lack of a son to inherit his lands was a grievance that haunted his days. Under the ill-advised will of Sir Francis, even Diplow, the ancestral seat where Sir Hugo had spent countless hunting seasons, had been left to the same restrictive conditions as the vast Topping inheritance. He and his wife and daughters, who ought to have retired there, were barred from true ownership.

As years passed, this grievance deepened. Lady Mallinger, after three daughters who died in quick succession, had borne no children for eight years, remaining past forty barren. Sir Hugo, nearly twenty years her senior, felt the weariness of age, his hopefulness dimming until the concept of second childhood offered a fragile restoration. He had begun to despair of an heir, making the presence of Grandcourt, the presumptive successor, increasingly unwelcome. Yet, Sir Hugo exercised caution, driven by a pragmatic plan to secure Diplow as a future retreat for his wife and daughters, keeping the estate intact despite his childlessness. Knowledge of his nephew's disposition led him to believe Grandcourt might accept a transaction: a substantial sum of ready money in exchange for relinquishing his prospective interest in the domain. If, by some miracle, an heir were eventually born, the money would be lost, but Sir Hugo calculated this risk as negligible. Having wisely husbanded his fortune through mining ventures and lease sales, he was prepared for the outlay.

To avoid quarrels, Sir Hugo maintained a facade of perfect politeness. Years prior, during improvements at the Abbey involving the felling of obstructive timber, he had noted a distinct lack of active spite in Grandcourt's mind. Since then, no event had escalated their relationship beyond the bounds of mutual accommodation. On Grandcourt's side, his uncle was viewed as a superfluity, a bore to be expunged from existence. However, through Mr. Lush, the intermediary, Grandcourt had learned of his uncle's inclinations regarding Diplow. The prospect of cash was flattering to his sense of power, allowing him to refuse the offer if he chose, though the timing of the tenancy he requested had annoyed Sir Hugo, who feared Grandcourt might hold onto the property for its superior hunting grounds. Furthermore, Lush had whispered the rumor that Grandcourt sought to woo Miss Arrowpoint, a possibility that might make the money less tempting. Thus, at this unexpected reunion, Sir Hugo was eager to learn the state of affairs at Diplow, determined to be civil, and looking forward to private chats with Lush.

Between Deronda and Grandcourt lay a peculiar, faint connection, rooted in circumstances yet to unfold. At the first meeting around the *table d'hôte*, neither showed suppressed chagrin. Later, on the terrace, the four gentlemen—Sir Hugo, Grandcourt, Lush, and Deronda—moved together into the large salon.

"Did you play much at Baden, Grandcourt?" Sir Hugo asked.

"No; I looked on and betted a little with some Russians there."

"Had you luck?"

"What did I win, Lush?" Grandcourt replied, playing idly with his diamond ring and whisker, drawing stares from admiring foreigners.

"You brought away about two hundred," Lush said.

"You are not here for the sake of the play, then?" Sir Hugo queried.

"No; I don't care about play now. It's a confounded strain," Grandcourt muttered, noting the monotony that knitted the brain into meshes. "Somebody should invent a mill to do amusements for you, my dear fellow, as the Tartars get their praying done."

"But where's your gambling beauty, Deronda? Have you seen her lately?" Sir Hugo turned to the younger man.

"She's gone," Deronda answered curtly.

"An uncommonly fine girl, a perfect Diana!" Sir Hugo exclaimed, addressing Grandcourt. "Really worth a little straining to look at her. I saw her winning, and she took it as coolly as if she had known it all beforehand. The same day Deronda happened to see her losing like wildfire, and she bore it with immense pluck. I suppose she was cleaned out, or was wise enough to stop in time. How do you know she's gone?"

"Oh, by

Men, like planets, possess both a visible trajectory and an invisible history. An astronomer threads the void with rigid logic to account for every arc in a wanderer's path; similarly, a chronicler of human lives must weave the hidden corridors of emotion and thought that culminate in every action, including those moments of profound suffering that transform into deeds—akin to the cry of Prometheus, whose bound agony exerts a force greater than the heavens he calls upon or defies.

Daniel Deronda's life had indeed been marked by singular exceptions. One instant burned into his memory as the defining epoch: a day of July sunlight and large pink roses dropping their final petals onto a grassy court hemmed in by Gothic cloisters. Imagine him there, a boy of thirteen lying prone in the shade, his curly head resting on his arms over a book, while his tutor read nearby on a camp-stool. Daniel's volume was Sismondi's *History of the Italian Republics*; the lad possessed a burning passion for history, an insatiable curiosity about how time had filled the centuries since the Flood and how affairs continued during dull eras. Suddenly, he lowered his left arm and gazed at his tutor, speaking in pure, boyish tones, "Mr. Fraser, how was it that popes and cardinals always had so many nephews?"

His tutor, an able young Scot serving as Sir Hugo Mallinger's secretary, roused himself reluctantly from political economy, answering with the clear, emphatic cadence that makes a truth doubly resonant in Scottish speech, "Their own children were called nephews."
"Why?" asked Deronda.
"It was merely for propriety," the tutor replied, "because, as you know well, priests do not marry, and the children were illegitimate."
Mr. Fraser, thrusting out his lower lip with impatience, returned his gaze to his book. Meanwhile, Deronda, as if stung by a blow, sprang up in a sitting posture with his back to the tutor.

He had always addressed Sir Hugo Mallinger as his uncle. When he first dared to inquire about his father and mother, the baronet had responded, "You lost your father and mother when you were quite a little one; that is why I take care of you." Daniel, straining to see through that early twilight, retained only a dim sense of being kissed profusely and wrapped in thin, cloudy, scented drapery, until his fingers snagged on something hard that hurt, causing him to weep. All other memories were of the small, safe world in which he still lived. At that time, he had not minded learning more, for he was too devoted to Sir Hugo to regret the loss of unknown parents. Life had been delightful, sustained by an uncle who was perpetually indulgent and cheerful—a magnificent man in the bright noon of life whom Daniel considered perfect, residing in a place of grandeur that was simultaneously historical, romantic, and homelike: a picturesque architectural extension from an abbey retaining relics of old monastic trunks. Diplomay lay in another county, a relatively landless estate acquired through a wealthy lawyer on the maternal side who wore the powdered wigs of the Restoration; whereas the Mallingers held the grant of Monk's Topping from Henry VIII and traced origins to Hugues le Malingre, who arrived with William the Conqueror, allegedly correcting a sickly complexion inherited from the ancestor. Two rows of descendants, direct and collateral, stared down from the gallery above the cloisters as Daniel walked below: armored men with pointed beards and arched brows, pinched ladies in hoops and ruffs devoid of facial expression, grave men in black velvet with stuffed hips, and fair, frightened women clutching little boys' hands; smiling politicians in magnificent wigs, and ladies of the prize-animal variety with rosebud mouths and full eyelids, per Lely; followed by generations revised in the taste of Kneller and refined further through Reynolds and Romney, ending finally with Sir Hugo and his younger brother, Henleigh. The latter had married Miss Grandcourt, adopting her name and estates, thereby uniting two ancient families, impaling their heraldry and aligning their highest advantages in the prospects of Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt, who was at present more of an acquaintance to us than either Sir Hugo or his nephew, Daniel Deronda.

In Sir Hugo's youthful portrait, Sir Thomas Lawrence captured the agreeable alacrity of the baronet's expression and sanguine temperament, though he perhaps overdid justice by slightly elongating the nose, which was in reality shorter than expected in a Mallinger. Fortunately, the appropriate nose of the family reappeared in Henleigh, manifesting in all its refined regularity in his nephew, Mallinger Grandcourt. Yet in Daniel Deronda, the diverse family faces adorning the gallery walls found no reflection. Still, he was handsomer than any of them, and at thirteen, he might have served as a model for any painter seeking to capture the most memorable of boys. To behold his face thoroughly confronting one's own is difficult without believing that human beings had achieved nobility in times past and might achieve it even more nobly in the future. The finest childlike faces possess this consecrating power, making us shudder anew at the grossness and basely-wrought griefs of the world, fearing lest they enter here and defile.

However, amidst the rose-petals on the grass, Daniel Deronda was encountering those griefs for the first time. A novel idea had entered his mind, altering the aspect of his habitual feelings as happy, careless voyagers are transformed when the sky threatens and danger arises. He sat perfectly still, his back to the tutor, his face expressing a rapid internal transition. The deep blush that had risen when he first stood up gradually subsided, yet his features retained an indescribable look of subdued activity, often accompanying a new mental survey of familiar facts. Having read Shakespeare and vast amounts of history, his mind displayed a blend of childish ignorance and surprising knowledge, often seen in bright girls. He could have discoursed with the wisdom of a bookish child regarding men born out of wedlock, deemed unfortunate by the disadvantages requiring them to be heroes to equal their legally born brothers. But he had never associated such knowledge with his own lot, which had been too easy to contemplate—until this moment, when the possibility darted into his mind with the magic of quick comparison: that here lay the secret of his birth, and the man he called uncle was actually his father.

Some children, even younger than Daniel, recognize the first arrival of care like an ominous, irremovable guest when they discover their parents, whom they imagined could buy anything, were poor and in financial distress. Daniel felt the presence of a new guest who seemed to approach with an enigmatic, veiled face, carrying dimly conjectured, dreaded revelations. The ardor he had given to the imaginary worlds in his books suddenly rushed toward his own history, expending its pictorial energy there, explaining what he knew and representing the unknown. The uncle he loved so dearly took the aspect of a father who held secrets about him—one who had done him a wrong, yes, a wrong—and what had become of his mother, for whom he must have been taken away? These were secrets Daniel could never inquire about; speaking or being spoken to regarding these new thoughts seemed like falling flakes of fire to his imagination. Those who know an impassioned childhood understand this dread of utterance regarding any shame connected to one's parents. The impetuous advent of new images took possession of him with the force of fact, leaving him no immediate power to reflect that he might be trembling at a fiction of his own. The terrible sense of collision between a strong rush of feeling and the dread of its betrayal found relief eventually in big, slow tears, falling without restraint until the voice of Mr. Fraser was heard saying, "Daniel, do you see that you are sitting on the bent pages of your book?"

Daniel immediately moved the book without turning around, held it before him for an instant, then rose and walked away into the open grounds where he could dry his tears unobserved. Once the first shock of suggestion passed, he remembered he had no certainty of how things truly had been, having made conjectures about his own history just as he often invented stories about Pericles or Columbus to fill gaps before they became famous. Yet certain facts returned with an obstinate reality, almost like fragments of a bridge telling you unmistakably how the arches lay. Again, a mood arose in which his conjectures seemed like a doubt of religion, to be banished as an offense and a mean prying after what he was not meant to know; for there was hardly a delicacy of feeling this lad lacked. The summing-up of all his fluctuating experience at this epoch was that a secret impression had come to him, granting him something akin to a new sense in relation to all elements of his life. The idea that others probably knew things they chose not to mention set up a premature reserve within him, intensifying his inward experience. His ears were now open to words that previously would have passed unnoticed, and round every trivial incident imagination could connect with his suspicions, a newly roused set of feelings clustered eagerly.

One such incident, occurring a month later, wrought itself deeply into his life. Daniel possessed not only one of those thrilling boy voices that seem to bring an idyllic heaven and earth before our eyes, but a fine musical instinct, having early made out piano accompaniments while singing from memory. Since then, he had received some instruction, and Sir Hugo, who delighted in the boy, used to request his music in the presence of guests. One morning, after singing "Sweet Echo" before a small party of gentlemen detained indoors by rain, the baronet, shifting from a smiling remark to his next neighbor, called out,

"This is true the poet sings,
That a sorrow's crown of sorrow
Is remembering happier things."
—TENNYSON: *In Memoriam*.

On a fine evening near the end of July, Daniel Deronda rowed alone upon the Thames. It had been nearly a year since his return to England, a journey undertaken with the vague promise that his education was concluded and he was ready to assume his station in society. Though Sir Hugo had wished him to study law as a shield against idleness, the endeavor had served only to anchor his indecision more deeply. Now, surrounded by the Mallingers in town, his ancient passion for boating had resurfaced with renewed vigor. The river offered a solitude unavailable anywhere else, and whenever Sir Hugo did not require him, it became his primary sanctuary to row until the stars emerged.

Yet, Deronda was not lost in sentimentality. Rather, he harbored a contemplative mood, prevalent among thoughtful young men of his era, characterized by a profound questioning of whether participation in the world's struggles was truly worthwhile. Specifically, he questioned the value of joining a battle where the labor of inquiry was sustained by a mere fraction of capital amassed by others through prior struggle. Sir Hugo, a man of impeccable Whig sensibilities, found Deronda's hesitation perplexing; how could a youth who stood in such stark contrast to the sickly and sentimental be hamstrung by "spectral illusions"? Especially since Deronda rejected authorship, the profession supposedly designed to monetize idle musings.

Clad in a dark-blue shirt and skull-cap, with closely cropped curls and a mouth framed by abundant, soft waves of beard, Deronda carried only disguised traces of the seraphic boy who once trailed clouds of glory. To the uninitiated eye, he appeared merely as a handsome young man, though those who knew Gwendolen might recall the peculiar, "dreadful" gaze she attributed to him—a look that was, in truth, a mild and penetrating scrutiny. His voice, occasionally heard in subdued fragments of song, was a high baritone rather than the rare tenor nature sometimes sacrifices for beauty. One needed only to observe his lithe, powerful frame and the firm gravity of his countenance to understand that he possessed a strength grounded in earth, not ethereal flight.

His hands told a similar story: long, flexible, and firmly grasping, evoking the ideal of refinement combined with force as depicted by Titian. There was a striking resemblance between his face and hands—the uniform pale-brown skin, the perpendicular brow, and the calmly piercing eyes. He was no longer seraphic; he was thoroughly terrestrial and manly, a type capable of recognizing dignity even in poverty. Such figures appear intermittently among the average conditions of life, like a workman whistling over a measurement, yet the profound meanings of such faces often lie in the immediate impressions they evoke.

Deronda rowed beneath Kew Bridge, unaware that his appearance was destined to alter the course of an encounter. He vigorously rejected the notion, hinted at by others, that his looks naturally commanded attention. Compliments regarding his appearance resonated angrily within him, stemming from a complex history he could not yet articulate. For years, his reflection in the glass had haunted him with the unsettling realization that he resembled someone about whose character and fate he constantly wondered but dared not inquire.

As the hour approached seven, the river near Kew Bridge was no longer a solitude. People sauntered along the towing path, and boats plied the water. Deronda had rowed quickly to bypass this area, but upon seeing a great barge approaching, he guided his boat aside, resting his oar within yards of the bank. Unconsciously, he continued the low-toned chant that had haunted his throat since leaving Putney—the gondolier's song from *Otello*, setting Dante's immortal words to music: *"Nessun maggior dolore / Che ricordarsi del tempo felice / Nella miseria"* ("No greater sorrow / Than to remember happy times / In misery").

As he paused, the melodic wail of *"nella miseria"* drifted softly onto the water's edge. A few bystanders had stopped to watch the barge pass, and likely noted the young gentleman in the boat, but the sound held significance only to one ear. Turning his head toward the riverbank, Deronda spotted a figure that seemed an embodiment of the misery he voiced. It was a girl, hardly eighteen, of slender build and delicate features. Her dark curls were tucked behind her ears beneath a large black hat, and a long woolen cloak draped her shoulders. Her hands hung clasped before her, her eyes fixed on the river with an immovable, statue-like despair.

Her gaze arrested him completely, silencing his song. As his voice cut off abruptly, she shifted slightly, glancing around with a frightened look before meeting his face. Their对视 lasted only a couple of moments, yet it felt like an eternity. Her expression was akin to that of a fawn before it bolts—no blush, no panic, only a gentle timidity that could not stop her from holding his gaze for a moment longer. To Deronda, she seemed half-conscious of her surroundings, perhaps driven by hunger or bewilderment. A surge of interest and compassion overwhelmed him, but instantly, she turned and walked toward a bench under a nearby tree.

He had no right to linger. Poorly dressed, melancholy women were common sights; however, her delicate beauty and picturesque lines made her image exceptionally vivid in his mind. He began to row away, soon far up the river, yet the pale specter of the unhappy girl refused to fade. He speculated endlessly on the tragedy hidden behind her loneliness, smiled at his own prejudice that interesting faces must harbor interesting stories, and justified his sorrow for her plight.

"I should not have forgotten the look of misery if she had been ugly and vulgar," he murmured to himself. Yet, the attractiveness of the image ensured its persistence. It was clear to him as an onyx cameo: the brown-black drapery, the white face with minute features and long-lashed dark eyes. His mind wandered over the unseen tragedies playing out in the world, those hidden away like wounds in a copse where helpless creatures drag wounded wings and stain the moss with blood. Recently, his solitary excursions had been dominated by uncertainties regarding his own future, but these doubts, vast in scope, connected with all of life and history. Consequently, the new image of helpless sorrow blended seamlessly with his reasons for shrinking from the world's routines—the apologies for wrongdoing and the treatment of opinions as mere professional tools. Why should he not pull at any thread in the hopelessly entangled scheme?

Using his oars sparingly, content to drift with the tide and be returned by it, Deronda indulged in a solemn passivity born of lengthening shadows and mellow light. Thinking and desiring melted together imperceptibly, arguments transforming into passionate visions. By the time he returned past Richmond Bridge, the sun was nearing its setting. The approach of his favorite hour—the deepening stillness and darkening masses of trees and buildings caught between the double glow of sky and river—prompted him to linger.

Seeking a perfectly solitary spot to moor his boat, he chose a bend opposite Kew Gardens, where the water reflected the sky's glory while he remained in shadow. Lying on his back with his head propped on cushions, he watched the sunset and the emergence of the stars, answering God's call. He lay with his hands behind his head, propelling himself at a level with the boat's edge, able to see all around but unseen by anyone nearby. For a long while, he gazed fixedly ahead, forgetting everything else in a speculative identification with the landscape, imagining how far he might shift his center of consciousness until his personality existed outside him as purely as the scenery.

Then, sensing movement on the opposite bank, bordered by willow bushes, he turned his glance. A darting presentiment seized him. Soon, he could see the small face illuminated by dying sunlight. Fearing to startle her, he watched with motionless attention. She looked around, seemingly seeking security in the apparent solitude, hiding her hat among the willows, and immediately removing her woolen cloak. She seated herself and deliberately dipped the cloak into the water, holding it there briefly before pulling it out with evident effort, rising as she did so.

Deronda realized she intended to wrap the wet cloak around herself like a drowning shroud. There was no time to hesitate. Rising and seizing his oar, he moved swiftly across. Fortunately, her position lay slightly below him. Overcome with terror at the sight of the boatman, she sank back onto the brink, clutching the cloak half-out of the water. She crouched, covering her face, perhaps hoping the boatman was merely passing by accident.

Soon, however, he was close enough to steady his boat against the bank. Speaking very gently, he called out, "Don't be afraid. You are unhappy. Pray, trust me. Tell me what I can do to help you."

She raised her head and looked up at him. The light now fell upon his face, and she recognized him. Silence stretched between them for several moments, renewing their former gaze. Finally, in a low, sweet voice with an accent suggesting foreignness, she said, "I saw you before." After a pause, she added dreamily, "*nella miseria*."

Deronda, failing to grasp the connection, assumed her mind was weakened by distress and hunger. "It was you, singing?" she asked hesitantly—"*Nessun maggior dolore*." The mere words, uttered in her sweet undertones, seemed to impart a melody to his ear.

"Ah, yes,"

Life wears many masks: sometimes she glides through marble halls, crowned with jewels, never glancing down at those who serve her; other times, she trudges through shadowed alleys, reeking of cheap spirits, screaming amidst the chaos of the desperate. Yet tonight, she arrived not as either extreme, but as a practical, efficient matron, her mind sharp and cheerful, ready to find abundance within the smallest of things.

Mrs. Meyrick's home was quiet, a sanctuary separated from the city's roar. The front parlor overlooked the river, the rear garden offered green views, allowing the windows to stand open even as Mrs. Meyrick read aloud to her daughters. Inside the small, double room, a single lamp and two candles burned. One candle illuminated a separate table where Kate sat, sketching designs for a publisher; the lamp served not only the storyteller but also Amy and Mab, who were stitching satin cushions intended for "high society" gatherings.

From the outside, the house appeared narrow and worn, the bright light filtering through the holland blinds revealing a heavy, old-fashioned frame. Yet within these grim walls lay a culture untainted by vulgarity. In foggy London, poverty often stripped away the need for ostentatious display, leaving behind a purity that rich houses rarely achieved. Here, the grand spectacles of the world were merely distant curiosities, sparking neither envy nor futile striving.

The Meyrick household clung to this specific dwelling because every object remained exactly where it belonged. For Mrs. Meyrick, the furniture and engravings were relics of her marriage; for the girls, they were as familiar and essential as the stars of the Great Bear seen from their back windows. Mrs. Meyrick had sacrificed much comfort to preserve a few cherished prints her husband loved, filling the narrow walls with scenes and portraits that the children knew by heart. The chairs and tables were old friends, far superior to anything a broker might sell cheap. Despite their scarcity, the space allowed for a life of wide-ranging taste, open to the heights of music, painting, and poetry. Even during times of deepest want, before Kate earned her living, they likely managed without a live-in servant, yet they maintained a fastidious standard that refused to accept the coarse selfishness and petty squabbles depicted in the so-called "literary photographs" of fashionable life.

The family possessed unique quirks, inherited streaks of eccentricity from both parents, their minds resembling medieval houses with unexpected recesses and sudden staircases leading to new vistas. However, they were bound together by a triple knot: familial love, a shared admiration for noble works and actions, and a habit of diligent industry. Hans's desire to fund a more luxurious lifestyle for them had been firmly rejected, saving everyone from the regret of his artistic ambitions clashing with financial security—a triumph that would eventually force him to abandon his fellowship. Instead, they laughed at his caricatures and accepted his pursuit of art as a natural gift, unhindered by material needs. Their reward was a grand treat of opera-going whenever Hans visited.

That evening, observing the group, one could scarcely wish for any change. They were all petite, perfectly scaled to their miniature rooms. Mrs. Meyrick, reading from a French volume, was a lively little woman, half-French, half-Scotch, with a speech so articulate it seemed to illuminate the listener's understanding.
