Though nearing fifty, her hair, mostly gray and tucked beneath a Quaker-style net cap, rippled softly above brown eyebrows that matched her bright eyes. Her black dress, buttoned in rows like a priest's cassock, fitted a neat, barely five-foot figure.

Her daughters mirrored her spirit, though Mab possessed Hans's light hair and fair skin, marked by a bossy, irregular brow and other peculiarities that recalled him. From the tight coils of their hair, pinned *à la Chinoise*, to their gray skirts—a Puritan defiance of the era's fashion which demanded fuller silhouettes—they were compact and distinct. If they were wax figures, they might fit easily into a fashionable lady's travel trunk. Their faces seemed packed with words, as if their minds had been shelled like horse-chestnuts to reveal a bright core. The only large entity in the room was Hafiz, the Persian cat, lounging regally on a brown leather chair back, blinking occasionally to ensure the smaller creatures caused no trouble.

Mrs. Meyrick held Erckmann-Chatrian's *Histoire d'un Conscrit*. Having just concluded the reading, Mab dropped her embroidery, leaning forward with intense focus.

"I believe that is the finest story in the world," she declared.

"Of course, Mab!" Amy replied. "It is the last one you've heard. Whatever pleases you is the best, right now."

"It isn't really a story," Kate corrected gently. "It's a piece of history magnified by a powerful telescope. We see the soldiers' faces... no, it's more than that. We can hear everything. We can almost feel their hearts beating."

"I don't care what you label it," Mab said, tossing her thimble aside. "Call it a chapter in Revelation. It makes me want to do something good, something magnificent. It fills me with sorrow for everyone. It reminds me of Schiller—I want to wrap the world in my arms and kiss it. I must kiss you instead, little mother!" She flung her arms around her mother's neck.

"When you're in that mood, Mab," Amy chided, "your work suffers. Finishing your cushion without staining it would count as doing something good."

"Oh—oh—oh!" Mab groaned, stooping to retrieve her tools. "I wish I had three wounded conscripts to nurse."

"You'd spill their beef tea while talking," Amy teased.

"Poor Mab! Don't be too harsh," the mother interceded. "Hand me the embroidery, child. You continue with your zeal, and I'll resume the pink and white poppies."

"Well, Ma, I suppose you're more biting than Amy," Kate remarked, tilting her head to study her drawing.

"Oh—oh—oh!" Mab cried again, rising and stretching. "I wish something wonderful would happen! I feel like the Deluge. The waters of the deep have burst, and the windows of heaven are open. I must sit down and play the scales."

As Mab began opening the piano, the others laughed at her dramatic climax. Suddenly, a cab halted before the house, followed immediately by a sharp knock at the door.

"My goodness!" Mrs. Meyrick started, rising quickly. "It is past ten, and Phoebe is already asleep." She hurried out, leaving the parlor door ajar.

"Mr. Deronda!" The girls caught the exclamation from their mother. Mab clasped her hands, whispering loudly, "There now! Something *is* happening." Kate and Amy abandoned their tasks in stunned silence. But Deronda's voice was so low that his words remained unheard, and Mrs. Meyrick swiftly closed the parlor door.

"I know I am relying on your kindness in an extraordinary manner," Deronda continued once inside, after offering a brief, urgent summary. "You can imagine how helpless I feel with a young creature like this on my hands. I cannot leave her alone with strangers, and in her nervous state, I would dread bringing her into a house full of servants. I have placed my trust in your mercy. Please, do not consider my action unwarranted."

"On the contrary," Mrs. Meyrick responded warmly. "You have honored me by trusting me. I understand your difficulty. Please, bring her in. I will prepare the girls."

As Deronda returned to the cab, Mrs. Meyrick re-entered the parlor. "Here is someone to care for instead of your wounded conscripts, Mab," she announced. "A poor girl who was planning to drown herself in despair. Mr. Deronda found her only moments ago. He brought her along in his boat and didn't know what else to do safely, so he trusts us with her. She seems to be a Jewess, yet quite refined—he says she knows Italian and music."

The three girls, curious and expectant, moved forward, standing close in a silent unity of feeling. Mab looked awestruck, as if this fulfillment of her dreams were supernatural.

Meanwhile, Deronda approached the cab door where a pale face gazed out, alert and observant. "I have brought you to some of the kindest people in the world," he told her. "These are daughters like you. It is a happy home. May I take you to them?"

She stepped out obediently, slipping her hand into his and forgetting her hat. As Deronda led her into the warm light of the parlor, where the four little women awaited, she became a vision that would have moved even colder hearts. Initially dazed by the brightness, she focused her gaze, and instantly Deronda placed her hand in her mother's. He felt a quiet joy that the Meyricks were so small; her dark-curled head was the tallest among them. The wandering girl could not fear these gentle faces so close to hers. Now, she looked at each of them in turn as the mother said, "You must be weary, poor child."

"We will take care of you—we will comfort you—we will love you," Mab shouted, unable to hold back anymore, and took the small right hand tenderly between both of her own. This warmth penetrated the bewildered stranger. She hesitated just enough to scrutinize the four faces, whose goodwill was reflected back in her own eyes—not in smiles, but in that indefinable shift where anxiety melts into contentment. For an instant, she looked up at Deronda

"I pity the man who travels from Dan to Beersheba and declares it all barren,' and yet, for those unwilling to nurture its gifts, the entire world remains desolate.' — STERNE: *A Sentimental Journey*

To label Daniel Deronda as merely "romantic" would be a profound misunderstanding of his nature. Beneath a surface of quiet composure and self-restraint burned a fierce, internal fire that allowed him to discover the poetry of everyday existence where others saw only routine. Perhaps true romance and poetic wonder are as abundant today as they ever were, vanishing only from the hearts of those whose spirits are too heavy, too indifferent to perceive them. They exist effortlessly beside the cold precision of science and within the clatter of railway cars, waiting only for a soul capable of awe to awaken them. Consider the vast machinery of the heavens above and the tender, nurturing breast of a mother below; how could these create beauty for a mind devoid of reverence and compassion? For Deronda, however, the chance encounter with Mirah felt as stirring as any mythic reunion of Orestes or Rinaldo. He sat awake for nearly the entire night, reliving the moment he first saw her by the riverbank with a startling, fresh vividness. When he attempted to read a book to quiet his racing thoughts, the ink seemed to dissolve, leaving behind a transparent screen through which he could see and hear every detail of those two hours anew. He saw not only what had happened, but endless possibilities of what might follow, fueled by a warm blood of hope and terrifying anticipation.

Something deep within his own history caused Mirah's desperate search for her mother to grip his imagination with unusual force. His initial urge was pure altruism: if living relatives existed in London, there were methods, subtle as a scientific experiment, to locate them. Yet, as he thought, the shadows of his own past bled into his concern for her. The desire to know his own mother was always haunted by a paralyzing dread, and now he imagined that Mirah's discovery could bring exactly that kind of horror. In the boat, she had spoken of her mother and brother with innocence, calling them "good"; but Deronda feared that ten or twelve years of separation could have twisted that goodness into something unrecognizable. Worse still, his ignorance of the Jewish community weighed heavily on him. Though he generally sided with the oppressed, his practical knowledge of Jews was limited to the ugliest stereotypes—the thieves lurking in alleys, the hypocrites in fine clothes. He assumed the educated had abandoned their faith to blend into society, and he felt a natural aversion to the idea that Mirah's lineage might drag her down into the squalor he associated with her people. He pictured, with chilling clarity, the reality behind her sweet, purified face: a dingy street, a hawk-eyed, unkempt woman haggling over a starving girl's clothes, or a slick, familiar young Jew eager to please gentlemen with his "knowledge." These were not insults born of malice, but the products of a mind governed by dread. If Mirah's family had been Christian, his fears would likely have been grounded in broader knowledge, but the unknown magnified the terror.

And what, then, was to be done for Mirah? She needed shelter and protection, and his chivalric spirit demanded that he secure allies beyond himself immediately. Yet, a strange contradiction held him back. The sheer power of the impression she made upon him led him to believe she must remain entirely independent of him. Vague, fantastical visions of their future lingered, fueling an anxiety stronger than any logical motive: he felt compelled to ensure that anyone observing his actions understood, from the start, the full history of his relationship with her. Secrecy regarding life's great ties and obligations repelled him; he hated the way a spell of intertwined sensitivities prevented him from revealing the truth. He had vowed to himself that since the truths that disgrace humanity are rarely of our own making, he would never use them to shame another person. This promise terrified him now, lest he falter into the philosophy that excuses the world by claiming it contains nothing better than one's own conduct.

For a fleeting moment, he resolved to pour out the entire adventure to Sir Hugo and Lady Mallinger at breakfast the following day. But the possibility that a new revelation might emerge during his next visit to Mrs. Meyrick silenced the impulse. Instead, he let sleep claim him, deciding to wait until he had seen her again before speaking a word to anyone else.

"It will hardly be denied that even in this frail and corrupted world, we sometimes meet persons who, in their very mien and aspect, as well as in the whole habit of life, manifest such a signature and stamp of virtue, as to make our judgment of them a matter of intuition rather than the result of continued examination."

Mirah claimed she had slept soundly. When she descended in Mab's black gown, her dark hair drying into fresh curls from its recent bath, she appeared as someone beginning to heal after long sorrow. Her cheeks, previously pale, were regaining color, though faint blue circles lingered beneath her eyes. It was Mab who brought her breakfast and led her down, proud of the tiny felt slippers she had hurried to purchase, as the house lacked shoes small enough for Mirah. Mirah's borrowed dress ended at her ankles, revealing cheap garments that molded to her feet like the protective sheaths of budding flowers. To Mab, the farthing buckles were jewels.

"Oh, if you please, mamma?" cried Mab, clasping her hands and bowing toward Mirah's feet as they entered the parlor. "Look at the slippers, how beautifully they fit! I declare she resembles Queen Budoor—"reciting the verse about delicate feet supported by the Creator."

Mirah glanced down at her own feet with a childlike wonder before smiling at Mrs. Meyrick. The older woman, inwardly thinking, *One could hardly imagine this creature harboring an evil thought,* returned the smile gently. "I fear the feet have sustained a heavy burden recently," she said. "But today, she shall rest and be my companion."

"And she will tell you so many things, and I shall miss them!" grumbled Mab, feeling relegated to the sidelines of a romantic tale she desperately wished to inhabit fully, despite her obligations to pupils.

Kate was already sketching by the river, and Amy was out on business. This solitude, desired by the mother, allowed her to be alone with this stranger, whose story was undoubtedly tragic yet necessary to unfold.

The small front parlor felt like a sanctuary that morning. Sunlight spilled onto the river, drawing in soft air through the open window. The walls, adorned with paintings, seemed to host a silent assembly of witnesses: the Virgin soaring with her cherubs, Grand Melancholia with her solemn universe, the Prophets and Sibyls, the School of Athens, the Last Supper, and various groups from far-off ages rendered in monochrome. There were portraits of grave artists, the Tragic Muse, children musings, and Italian poets. The neat mother, having weathered troubles with a cheerful countenance, sorted colored wools for her embroidery. Hafiz purred on the windowsill; the clock on the mantelpiece ticked steadily, undisturbed by the distant rumble of wheels outside. Mrs. Meyrick believed this profound quiet was the perfect invitation for speech and refrained from disturbing it.

Mirah sat opposite in her accustomed posture, hands clasped on her lap, ankles crossed. Her eyes traveled slowly over the surroundings until they rested on Mrs. Meyrick with a placid reverence. Finally, she began to speak softly.

"I remember my mother's face better than anything else," Mirah said. "Yet I was not seven when I was taken away, and I am nineteen now."

"There are some earliest impressions that endure the longest," Mrs. Meyrick replied.

"Yes, it was the beginning. I think my life started with waking up and loving my mother's face. It was so close to mine; her arms were round me, and she sang. One hymn she repeated endlessly, teaching me to join her. It was the first song I ever knew. They were always Hebrew hymns, and because I did not understand the words, they seemed filled entirely with our love and happiness. Lying in my little bed, with white above me, she would bend over, singing in a sweet, low voice. I can dream myself back to that time when I am awake, and it returns to me in sleep—my hand was so small then, reaching up to her face, and she kissed it. Sometimes in my dreams, I tremble, thinking we are both dead. But then I wake, and my hand lies there, and for a moment, I hardly know myself. Yet, if I could see my mother again, I would know her instantly."

"You must expect changes after twelve years," Mrs. Meyrick said gently, indicating her own grey hair, which had been bright brown a decade prior. "Days and months pass over us like restless little birds, leaving marks of their flight backward and forward. Especially when they are birds with heavy hearts, they tread heavily."

"Ah, I am certain her heart was heavy with want for me. But to feel her joy if we met again, to make her know my love and bring deep comfort after all her mourning! If that were possible, I would mind nothing. I would be glad to have survived my trouble. I did despair. The world seemed miserable and wicked; no one helped me to bear their looks and words. I felt my mother was dead, and death was the only mercy. But yesterday, in the last moment when I longed for the water to close over me, thinking death the best image of mercy, goodness came to me living. I felt trust in the living. And strangely, I began to hope she was living too. Now, here with you this morning, peace and hope have flooded into me. I want nothing; I can wait. Because I hope, I believe, and I am so grateful. You have not thought evil of me; you have not despised me."

Mirah spoke with low-toned fervor, sitting as still as a painting throughout.

"Many others would feel as we do, my dear," Mrs. Meyrick said, a mist rising in her eyes as she looked at her work.

"But I did not meet them—they did not come to me."

"How was it that you were taken from your mother?"

"It takes me a long time to reach that point," Mirah hesitated. "It is dreadful to speak of, yet I must tell you everything. My father took me away. I thought we were going on a short journey, and I was pleased. There was a box containing all my little things. But we boarded a ship and drifted farther from the land. Then I became ill; I thought it would never end. It was the first misery, seeming endless. At last, we landed. I knew nothing then and believed my father. He comforted me, telling me I would return to my mother. But we had reached America, and it was many years before we returned to Europe. At first, I often asked my father when we would go back. I tried to learn writing quickly so I could write to my mother. But one day, when he found me attempting to write a letter, he took me on his knee and told me that my mother and brother were dead—that was why we did not return. I remember my brother slightly; he carried me once, but he was not always home. I believed my father. I visualized them under the earth, eyes forever closed. I never suspected it was untrue. I used to cry every night in my bed for a long time. Then, when she visited me so often in my sleep, I thought she must be living nearby, though I could not always see her. That comforted me. I was never afraid of the dark because of that belief. Often during the day, I would shut my eyes, bury my face, and try to see and hear her singing. Eventually, I learned to do this without closing my eyes."

Mirah paused, her face holding a sweet content, as if viewing a happy vision, while gazing out toward the river.

"Still, your father was not unkind to you, I hope?" Mrs. Meyrick asked after a moment, anxious to redirect the conversation.

"No," Mirah answered. "He petted me and took pains to teach me. He was an actor. Later, I discovered that the 'CoBURG' he mentioned as going to at home was a theater. However, he had more involvement with the theater than merely acting. He had not always been an actor; he had been a teacher and knew many languages. His acting was not particularly good, I think, but he managed the stage, wrote, and translated plays. An Italian lady, a singer, lived with us for a long time. Both she and he taught me, and I also had a master who made me memorize and recite. I worked quite hard, though I was so young. I was not nine when I first stepped onto the stage. I could learn things easily and was not afraid. But from then on, I hated our way of life. My father had money, and we possessed finery in a disorderly fashion. Men and women constantly came and went; there was loud laughter, disputing, strutting, finger-snapping, jeering, and faces I did not like to look at—though many petted and caressed me. Yet, I remembered my mother. Even at first, when I understood little, I shrank away from those external things into a companionship with thoughts unlike them. I gathered thoughts rapidly because I read many things—plays and poetry, Shakespeare and Schiller—and learned of evil and good. My father began to believe I might become a great singer; my voice was considered wonderful for a child. He secured the best teaching for me. But it was painful that he boasted of me, setting me to sing for display at any moment, as if I were a musical box. Once, at nine, I played the part of a little girl who had been forsaken and did not realize it, sitting and

Knowledge is often called power, yet few have truly grasped the terrifying magnitude of Ignorance. While wisdom slowly constructs worlds through patience and accumulation, Ignorance can dismantle them in a heartbeat. It burns the records of ages to fuel a single meal, and when it arrives drunk on the seventh day of creation, wielding a match and a jar of oil, it shrivels the vibrant tapestry of life into a charred ruin. True knowledge operates with a conscience, understanding the limits of what *must* be and what *may* be; Ignorance, however, is a blind giant. Left unchecked, it would tear down the pillars supporting human goodness and plunge joy into the darkness of a buried Babylon. In the growth of any individual soul, a lack of insight into the bonds between events—the false confidence that one can compel outcomes through sheer will—is like a visual defect that sees distant mountains as steps away. Such blindness precipitates the soul onto a cliff of destruction.

It was past ten o'clock when Gwendolen Harleth arrived at the station for Offendene, her gloomy journey from Leubronn finally complete. No carriage or friend awaited her; in her telegram from Dover, she had requested a later train, unable to bear the lingering wait at the London terminus. Now, she stood alone, deposited as a *femme sole* amidst her large trunks, forced to wait for a vehicle summoned from the "Railway Inn"—a sprawling establishment that felt more like a prison cell than a lodging. The waiting room was a study in dreariness: dirty paint, a dusty decanter of flat water, and large, moralizing texts urging repentance. These were merely the opening notes of her family's tragedy, and Gwendolen hurried to the outer door, seeking the lane and fields. Yet, even the sunshine seemed melancholy. Autumn leaves and grass shivered in the wind, while a pair of old hens, having lost their chicks, cackled uselessly, turning up their feathers in confusion. Nearby, a railway official observed her with a look of innocent condescension, his gaze heavy with the judgment of a man who recognized her poverty. Beside him, a laborer prepared a dirty, ancient barouche. To Gwendolen, these trivial details—the grime, the isolation, the lack of funds—accumulated with crushing force, shaping a mood that felt like a grim theory. Solitary at this ugly, remote spot, stripped of her expected income, she felt the sting of a new consciousness: poverty. What use was charm, intelligence, or resolve if they could not buy a decent carriage? As the vehicle lumbered toward home, she ruminated on her misery, convinced that life itself was a series of humiliations.

Her driving vision had been one of escape: the family moving abroad, finding some sliver of income, living quietly in a dull foreign land while she aged gracefully away from her mother's increasing melancholy. But now, staring at the empty road ahead, the illusion shattered. Weariness and disgust replaced her dreams. She felt a strange, hollow futility in being "anything" at all. Events spun wildly, and men, she realized with a sudden, bitter clarity, were hateful. Yet, beneath this hatred lay a shift in her feelings toward Grandcourt. It was one thing to hate stolen goods; it was another to hate them because they blocked one's path. Anger flared within her—not just at the theft, but at Grandcourt for being the obstacle that prevented her marriage, the architect of her current desolation.

As the barouche crested the hill and approached Offendene, the windows framed the house, and a figure appeared beneath the portico. Gwendolen's heart leaped. It was her mother, Mrs. Davilow, her face lined with fresh, deep sadness. Gwendolen rushed from the carriage and threw her arms around her mother's neck, momentarily drowning her own sorrows in the weight of her mother's grief. Behind them followed the four younger sisters, their faces etched with sorrow, each carrying a small, isolated world that mattered only to herself. Yet, strangely, Gwendolen's presence acted as a balm to their weakness; her quick, authoritative dismissal—"Now go away, girls!"—offered them a semblance of order. Miss Merry, usually so meek, busied herself with the trunks and the coachman's pay, while Mrs. Davilow and Gwendolen ascended the stairs, shutting themselves inside the black-and-yellow bedroom.

"My dear, never mind," Gwendolen whispered, pressing her handkerchief to her mother's weeping cheeks. "I will do something. I will be someone. Things will come right."

She spoke with a conviction that stirred a wave of compassionate tenderness, transforming vague hopes into concrete resolve. Her mother, forgetting the depths of their plight, gazed at her daughter with a sort of adoration. "Bless you, my good, good darling! I can be happy, if you can!"

Yet, by afternoon, the tide receded. The comforting illusions vanished, revealing the old, slippery rocks and weedy realities of their situation. The "announcement" of misfortune had become a tyrannical inhabitant. For now, however, Gwendolen clung to the comforts of the house: the roomy silence, the ready-to-hand luxuries of her toilette, and a private tray bearing her favorite foods. She had insisted on solitude, wishing to be alone with her mother.

When Gwendolen descended to the drawing-room, fresh as a swan, she leaned against the cushions beside her mother, ready to confront the facts. "Mamma, what exactly have you thought of doing?"

"Oh, my dear," Mrs. Davilow replied, "the first step is to leave this house. Mr. Haynes is delighted to have it back, and Lord Brackenshaw's agent, Bazley, will arrange the terms."

"But surely, Mamma, Lord Brackenshaw would let you stay rent-free?" Gwendolen asked, her instincts failing her in matters of finance.

"My love, Lord Brackenshaw is in Scotland and knows nothing of us. Nor would your uncle wish to approach him. Besides, how could we manage without servants or money to heat the house? We must move quickly. We have nothing but our clothes."

"And where shall we go?" Gwendolen asked, a sharp edge returning to her voice. Fear, cold and new, coursed through her veins.

"It is decided," Mrs. Davilow said, her voice trembling. "We shall go to Sawyer's Cottage."

Gwendolen went silent, her face paling with a justifiable rage. "That is impossible! Something else must have been arranged. My uncle will not allow it. I will not submit!"

"My sweet child, what else could be done? Your uncle is kind, but he suffers too, and he has his own family to raise. Do you understand? We have absolutely nothing, save what your uncle and aunt provide. They have been wise and active, but we must try to earn something. I and the girls intend to work a table-cloth border for the Ladies' Charity at Winchester, and a communion cloth for Pennicote Church."

Mrs. Davilow spoke timidly, dreading the shock she delivered to her daughter, yet hoping Gwendolen would accept the necessity. Gwendolen, however, refused to listen to deprecation. "Sawyer's Cottage? Where an exciseman lived? Surely some other place exists!"

"No, indeed, dear. Houses are scarce. We must be thankful for privacy. It has two parlors and four bedrooms. You may sit alone whenever you wish."

Gwendolen, her sympathy for her mother exhausted, paid no heed. "I cannot believe all your property has vanished so quickly, Mamma. It hasn't been a week since you wrote!"

"The news came much earlier," Mrs. Davilow admitted, hesitating. "But I wished to spare you the pain until it was unavoidable."

"Oh, how vexatious!" Gwendolen cried, flushing with anger. "If I had known, I could have brought home the money I won! Because I stayed, I lost it. I had nearly two hundred pounds!" She paused, her voice rising impetuously. "Everything goes against me. People come near me only to blight me."

In referring to "people," she included Daniel Deronda. Had he not intervened, she would have returned to the gaming tables with a few napoleons and likely recovered her losses. Instead, fate had intervened.

"We must resign ourselves to the will of Providence, my child," Mrs. Davilow murmured, startled by the mention of gambling but afraid to press further. She suspected "people" referred to Grandcourt, a name her lips dared not utter.

"But I do not resign myself!" Gwendolen snapped. "I will fight it. Why do you call people's wickedness 'Providence'? You wrote it was Mr. Lassman's fault. Did he run away with all the money?"

"No, dear, you misunderstand. There were vast speculations—he intended to profit. It involved mines and risky ventures. He simply risked too much."

"I don't call that Providence! It was his improvidence with *our* money, and he must be punished! Can we not sue to recover our fortune? My uncle must take action!"

"My dear child, the law cannot restore money lost in such games. Your uncle says it is milk spilled upon the ground. Moreover, one needs a fortune to hire lawyers; there is no law for the ruined. Our money has mingled with others'; many suffer alongside us."

"But I refuse to live

"We weave ideal webs to please our fancy, but meanwhile, life moves in the loom where busy passion drives the shuttle back and forth, weaving the familiar patterns of our actions."

Gwendolen's note, arriving "just in time, yet too soon," slipped into Klesmer's hands precisely as he was departing Quetcham. Driven by a desire to honor her plea for kindness, despite the inconvenience to himself, he spent the night in Wanchester. There were compelling reasons why he could not remain at Quetcham.

That magnificent mansion, built with extravagant expense, had become stiflingly hot for him, largely due to its owners' reaction to an uprising against the established order. Like certain politicians who are astounded by rebellions they failed to foresee, the Arrowpoints had been blindsided by a shift in the local dynamic that ordinary observers could plainly see brewing beneath their noses.

As usual, the house was filled with guests. Among them stood a political man of good family, Mr. Bult, whom Miss Arrowpoint anticipated as a new contender for her hand. A man who confidently expected a peerage and believed, on sound public principles, that a title required a substantial fortune to support properly. Heiresses vary greatly in temperament and appearance—too pale or too flushed, tall and ungainly or short and square, volatile or dull—but it is generally assumed that they will view themselves merely as appendages to their wealth and marry where suitors dictate. However, Nature, accommodating herself poorly to our favored practices by creating "only children," occasionally endows the misplaced daughter with a clear intellect and a formidable will. The Arrowpoints had grown increasingly anxious regarding Catherine's refusal to accept the standard view of her social duty, specifically the requirement to marry a needy nobleman or a commoner climbing the ladder toward nobility. They were uneasy about her persistence in declining suitable offers. Yet, regarding the possibility that Catherine might be in love with Klesmer, they were remarkably unconcerned—a very common form of parental blindness. Generally, mortals are astonishingly surprised by effects toward which they have labored diligently, while remaining oblivious to the absence of effects toward which they have done nothing but hope. Parents marvel at their sons' ignorance despite investing vast sums in education; spouses are shocked by the loss of affection they neglected to nurture; and everyone is prone to wondering why neighbors do not admire them. Through this mechanism, truth appears highly improbable. Truth is distinct from the lazy, wish-born combinations we habitually construct. Finally, the Arrowpoints' moment of astonishment had arrived.

When a passion exists between an heiress and a proud, independent spirit, reaching an understanding proves difficult. However, these obstacles are often surmounted unless the proud man secures himself through a constant alibi. Brief meetings following studied absences are powerful agents of revelation; yet far more potent is frequent companionship marked by full sympathy in taste and admirable qualities on both sides. This dynamic is legendary in history, possessing a charm today no less profound than it held in the days of Abelard.

Nevertheless, such comparisons had not crossed the minds of the Arrowpoints when they first invited Klesmer to Quetcham. Having a first-rate musician in one's household is a privilege of wealth; Catherine's musical talents demanded every advantage, and she particularly sought to utilize her quiet country hours for rigorous study. Klesmer was not yet considered a Liszt, a figure universally adored by ladies across Europe (save for Lapland). Even with that reputation, it was not assumed he would propose to an heiress. Furthermore, no musician of honor would do so. Still less could it be conceived that Catherine would provide him the slightest pretext for such audacity. The large check Mr. Arrowpoint intended to draw in Klesmer's name appeared to render him as safe an inmate as a footman. Where marriage is deemed inconceivable, a girl's sentiments are considered secure.

Klesmer was a man of eminent honor, yet marriages seldom commence with formal proposals. Moreover, Catherine's limits of the conceivable did not perfectly align with her mother's. Outsiders might have perceived Klesmer's position as perilous if Miss Arrowpoint had been an acknowledged beauty, failing to recognize that the most powerful beauty is that which reveals itself only after sympathy has been established. There is a charm of eye and lip accompanying every phrase that certifies delicate perception or fine judgment, every unostentatious word or smile indicating a heart awake to others. No sweep of garment or turn of figure is more satisfying than that which restores confidence, signaling the presence of someone on whom no intention will be lost. What dignity of meaning accumulates in frowns and laughter never observed in the wrong place? What suffused adorableness resides in a human frame housing a mind capable of flashing comprehension and hands skilled in fine execution? The more obvious beauty, though sometimes adorable, begins as an apology for folly and eventually becomes tiresome through repetition. Therefore, Klesmer's passionate attachment to Miss Arrowpoint, despite his susceptibility to conventional beauty, was no paradox compared to any other triumph of manifold sympathy over monotonous attraction. We object less to being accused of enslaving excess in our passions than to our deficiencies in wider compassion; yet if the truth were known, our reputed intensity is often the dullness of not knowing what else to do with ourselves. Tannhäuser, one suspects, was a knight of ill-furnished imagination, scarcely possessing broader discourse than a heavy Guardsman; Merlin had surely seen his best days, merely repeating himself, when he fell into that hopeless captivity; and we know that Ulysses felt such manifest ennui under similar circumstances that Calypso herself furthered his departure. Though there is a report that he later left Penelope, given her habitual absorption in woolen work and the likelihood that Telemachus derived his mean, pettifogging disposition from her, no inference can be drawn from this dubious scandal regarding the relationship between companionship and constancy.

Klesmer proved as versatile and fascinating as a young Ulysses upon sufficient acquaintance—a soul whom Nature seemed to create generously first, then endowed with music as a dominant power utilizing all abundant rest. Finding expression not only in the highest finish of execution but in a fervor of creative work and theoretic belief that pierced the future of a life with the delight of congruous devotion, he mirrored Mendelssohn. His foibles of arrogance and vanity did not exceed those found in the best English families, and Catherine possessed no corresponding restlessness to clash with his. Despite her native kindliness, she was perhaps too coolly firm and self-sustained. Yet she belonged to those satisfactory creatures whose interaction holds the charm of discovery; whose integrity of faculty and expression generates a desire to know what they will say on all subjects or how they will perform whatever they undertake. Thus, they raise not only a continual expectation but a continual sense of fulfillment—the systole and diastole of blissful companionship. In such cases, the outward presentation easily becomes what the image is to the worshipper. It was not long before the two became aware that each was interesting to the other, though the extent of their mutual interest remained a matter of doubt. Klesmer did not conceive that Miss Arrowpoint viewed him as a potential lover, nor was she accustomed to thinking of herself as likely to stir more than a friendly regard, nor to fear the expression of more from any man who was not enamored of her fortune. Each was content to endure some unshared sense of denial for the sake of loving the other's society a little too well. Under these conditions, no need had arisen to restrict Klesmer's visits for the past year, whether in country or town. He knew very well that if Miss Arrowpoint had been poor, he would have made ardent love to her instead of sending a storm through a piano or folding his arms to pour out a hyperbolic tirade about something as impersonal as the North Pole. Nor was she less aware that if it had been possible for Klesmer to wish for her hand, she would have found overmastering reasons for granting it. Here lay the safety of full cups, which are as secure from overflow as the half-empty ones, provided no disturbance occurs. Naturally, silent feelings had not remained static, any more than the stealthy dial-hand. During this current visit to Quetcham, Klesmer had begun to think he would not return, while Catherine grew more sensitive to his frequent brusqueness, which she resented as a needless effort to assert his footing of superiority in every sense save the conventional.

Meanwhile entered the expectant peer, Mr. Bult, an esteemed party man who, though neutral in private life, held strong opinions concerning the districts of the Niger, the Brazils, and affairs in the South Seas. He spoke decisively of Parliamentary and itinerant speeches, possessing the general solidity and suffusive pinkness of a healthy Briton residing on the central table-land of life. Catherine, aware of the tacit understanding that he was an undeniable husband for an heiress, had nothing to say against him save that he was thoroughly tiresome. Mr. Bult was amiably confident, harboring no idea that his insensibility to counterpoint could ever be reckoned against him. He hardly regarded Klesmer in the light of a serious human being entitled to a vote, and he did not mind Miss Arrowpoint's addiction to music any more than her probable expenses in antique lace. Consequently, he was somewhat amazed by an after-dinner outburst from Klesmer regarding the lack of idealism in English politics. To Klesmer, all mutuality between distant races was determined simply by the need of a market; the Crusades, in his view, at least possessed the excuse of a banner of sentiment around which generous feelings could rally—even if the scoundrels rallied too, they did so

Among the inheritors of Art, as with the ancient division of the Promised Land, every share must be seized through fierce contention. Bestowal follows the pattern of prophecy; it grants a title to a realm not yet possessed. To carry a map of an untamed estate in one's pocket is merely a precarious form of tenancy, and in fantasy to cast a shoe across Eden offers no guarantee that a man will ever plant his boot on a single acre of his own soil.

The most stubborn delusions mortals harbor regarding themselves rest on no evidence beyond a constant, spontaneous throbbing of self-satisfaction—an invisible seed of madness, a blind faith that they can shift the world without understanding where they stand or what leverage to apply.

"Please go to church, Mamma," Gwendola said the following morning. "I prefer seeing Herr Klesmer alone." (He had replied to her note stating he would meet her at eleven.)

"That hardly seems correct, I think," Mrs. Davilow said, her brow furrowed with anxiety.

"Our affairs are too weighty for us to concern ourselves with such absurd rules," Gwendola retorted, her tone dripping with disdain. "They are insulting as well as ridiculous."

"You wouldn't mind Isabel accompanying you? She could read quietly in a corner."

"No, she couldn't. She'd gnaw at her nails and stare. It would be far too irritating. Trust my judgment, Mamma; I must be alone. Send them all to church."

Gwendola secured her wish, naturally. Only Miss Merry and two younger girls remained at home, sitting at the dining-room windows to lend the house an illusion of habitation.

It was a delectable Sunday morning. The melancholy, fading sunlight of autumn settled upon the half-scattered grass and filtered gently through the windows in oblique bands of brilliance, illuminating the antique furniture and the glass panel that mirrored it. Light fell upon the tapestried chairs adorned with faded floral wreaths, the dark, enigmatic paintings, and the superannuated organ where Gwendola had delighted in playing Saint Cecilia upon her first joyful arrival. Through the open doors of the antechamber, a multitude of pallid, dusty knick-knacks was visible—the backdrop where she had achieved the wearing of her Greek costume as Hermione. That specific memory was vividly active in her mind now. Had not Klesmer then been struck by admiration for her pose and expression? Whatever he had said, whatever she imagined him to have thought, was currently scrutinized with her keenest interest. Perhaps she had never in her life felt so inwardly dependent, so acutely aware of needing another person's opinion. A new fluttering of spirit vibrated within her, introducing a fresh element of deliberation into her self-assessment, which had previously been a blissful gift of pure intuition. Yet, the recurring burden of her internal soliloquy remained: Klesmer had seen but little of her, and any unfavorable conclusion he reached would surely rest on a foundation too narrow. She genuinely felt clever enough for any undertaking.

To occupy the time, she gathered her volumes and musical scores, laying them atop the piano, and set about classifying them. Catching the reflection of her movements in the glass panel, she was diverted to contemplating her own image and walked toward it. Dressed in black, devoid of ornament, with the warm whiteness of her skin set off between her light-brown crown of hair and her square-cut bodice, she might have tempted an artist to attempt again the Roman trick of a statue carved from black, white, and tawny marble. Seeing her image advance slowly, she thought, "I *am* beautiful"—not with exultation, but with grave decision. Being beautiful was, after all, the condition upon which she most desperately needed external testimony. If anyone objected to the turn of her nose or the form of her neck and chin, she lacked the sense that she could demonstrate her power of attainment in these branches of feminine perfection.

There was little time left to fill in this manner before the clatter of wheels, the loud ringing of a bell, and the opening of doors assured her that she would not, by any accident, be disappointed. This slight increase in her inner fluttering served to heighten her dread of Klesmer. Despite her self-confidence, she feared him as part of that unmanageable world which existed independently of her wishes—something vitriolic that would not cease to burn simply because one smiled or frowned at it. Poor creature! She was at a higher crisis point in her woman's fate than during her last experience with Grandcourt. The questioning then had been whether she should take a particular man as a husband. The inmost fold of her questioning now was whether she needed to take a husband at all—whether she could not achieve substantial independence for herself and know gratified ambition without bondage.

Klesmer made his most deferential bow in the wide doorway of the antechamber, displaying the deference of the finest gray kerseymere trousers and perfect gloves (for the "masters of those who know" are happily, altogether, human beings). Gwendola met him with unusual gravity, holding out her hand and saying, "It is most kind of you to come, Herr Klesmer. I hope you have not thought me presumptuous."

"I took your wish as a command that did me honor," Klesmer replied, answering gravity. He was indeed setting aside his own affairs to give his utmost attention to whatever Gwendola might have to say; yet his temperament was still in a state of excitation from the events of yesterday, likely enough to lend his expressions a more than usually biting edge.

Gwendola, for once, was under too great a strain of feeling to remember formalities. She stood near the piano, and Klesmer took his stand near the other end, his back to the light and his terribly omniscient eyes fixed upon her. No affectation was of use, and she began without delay.

"I wish to consult you, Herr Klesmer. We have lost all our fortune; we have nothing. I must earn my own bread, and I desire to provide for my mother, saving her from any hardship. The only way I can think of—and I should like it better than anything else—is to become an actress—to go on the stage. But, of course, I should like to take a high position, and I thought—if you thought I could"—here Gwendola became slightly more nervous—"it would be better for me to be a singer—to study singing as well."

Klesmer placed his hat upon the piano and folded his arms as if concentrating his thoughts.

"I know," Gwendola resumed, turning from pale to pink and back again—"I know that my method of singing is very defective; but I have been ill-taught. I could be better taught; I could study. And you will understand my wish: to sing and act, like Grisi, occupies a much higher position. Naturally, I should wish to take as high a rank as I can. And I can rely on your judgment. I am sure you will tell me the truth."

Gwendola somehow held the conviction that now, in making this serious appeal, the truth would prove favorable.

Yet Klesmer spoke not a word. He quickly removed his gloves, tossed them into his hat, rested his hands on his hips, and walked to the other end of the room. He was filled with compassion for this girl; he wanted to put a guard around his speech. When he turned again, he looked at her with a mild frown of inquiry and said, with gentle though rapid utterance, "You have never seen anything, I think, of artists and their lives?—I mean of musicians, actors, artists of that kind?"

"Oh, no," Gwendola said, unperturbed by a reference to this obvious fact in the history of a young lady who had hitherto been well-provided for.

"You are—pardon me," Klesmer said, pausing again near the piano—"in coming to a conclusion on such a matter as this, everything must be taken into consideration—you are perhaps twenty?"

"I am twenty-one," Gwendola said, a slight fear rising within her. "Do you think I am too old?"

Klesmer pouted his lower lip and shook his long fingers upward in a manner totally enigmatic.

"Many persons begin later than others," Gwendola said, betrayed by her habitual consciousness of possessing valuable information to bestow.

Klesmer paid no notice, but said with more studied gentleness than ever, "You have probably not thought of an artistic career until now: you did not entertain the notion, the longing—what shall I say?—you did not wish yourself an actress, or anything of that sort, until the present trouble?"

"Not exactly: but I was fond of acting. I have acted; you saw me, if you remember—you saw me here in charades, and as Hermione," Gwendola said, truly fearing that Klesmer had forgotten.

"Yes, yes," he answered quickly, "I remember—I remember perfectly," and again walked to the other end of the room. It was difficult for him to refrain from this kind of movement whenever he was engaged in any argument, whether audible or silent.

Gwendola felt that she was being weighed. The delay was unpleasant. But she did not yet conceive that the scales could tip on the wrong side, and it seemed to her only graceful to say, "I shall be very much obliged to you for taking the trouble to give me your advice, whatever it may be."

"Miss Harleth," Klesmer said, turning toward her and speaking with a slight increase

"I question things but do not find
One that will answer to my mind:
And all the world appears unkind."

Gwendolen breathed a sigh of relief that the interview with Klesmer was concluded before she faced her uncle and aunt. With a resolve forged in anticipation, she had convinced herself that whatever horrors awaited her lay ahead, and she felt a grim, dogged calm settle over her, ready to weather any humiliation.

The meeting did not occur until Monday. Gwendolen accompanied her mother to the rectory, having stopped first at Sawyer's Cottage by the way. They had inspected every inch of the narrow rooms bathed in a harsh midday sun, unfiltered by blinds or curtains, for the furnishings meant to be scavenged from the rectory had not yet arrived.

"How *shall* you endure it, mamma?" Gwendolen asked as they walked away, her voice tight. She had remained silent during the tour of the bare walls, the scrubbed floors, and the tiny garden choked with cabbage stalks and a yew arbor thick with dust and cobwebs. "You and the four girls squeezed into that closet of a room, with that garish green and yellow wallpaper staring you in the eye? And without me?"

"It will be a comfort, dear, that you do not have to share that burden."

"If it weren't for the money I must earn, I'd sooner be there than go to be a governess."

"Do not set yourself against it in advance, Gwendolen. If you go to the palace, you will have every luxury surrounding you. You know how deeply you have always cherished that. You won't find it nearly as hard as climbing those steep, narrow stairs, listening to crockery clatter through the house, and overhearing the girls talking."

"It feels like a bad dream," Gwendolen exclaimed impulsively. "I cannot believe Uncle will let you go to such a place. He should have taken other steps."

"Don't be unreasonable, dear child. What else could he have done?"

"That was for him to figure out. This world seems extraordinarily strange to me if people in our position must sink like this all at once," Gwendolen said, her mind drifting to other worlds she knew, constructed with a perfect logic that arranged her future agreeably.

It was her temperament that shaped her sentences under this crushing new pressure. She could have spoken more wisely of vicissitudes affecting others, though it was never her aspiration to sound virtuous so much as clever—a flaw that must be considered when judging her words, which were often worse than her intentions. Despite the sharp sting of her own humiliation, she felt a flicker of guilt when her uncle and aunt received her with a warmth and affection they had rarely shown before. She was struck by the dignified cheerfulness with which they discussed the necessary economies in their living arrangements and the education of the boys. Mr. Gascoigne's inherent worth of character, slightly veiled by worldly distractions but shining brightly now, revealed itself under this sudden financial reduction. Prompt and methodical, he had already dismantled his carriage, scrutinized his worn suits, eliminated breakfast meat, abandoned periodicals, withdrawn Edwy from school to manage the boys' studies personally, and ordered the entire household onto the sparest footing imaginable. For healthy souls, economy holds its pleasures; the rector's spirit had permeated the house. Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna, who always modeled their behavior on their father, genuinely missed nothing they truly cared for, and sincerely believed the saddest part of their losses was the displacement of Mrs. Davilow and her children.

For the first time, Anna could channel her resentment regarding Rex into sympathy for Gwendolen. Mrs. Gascoigne hoped trouble might prove salutary for her niece, refusing to add any bitterness to the remedy. Both women had been busily devising ways to procure blinds and curtains for the cottage from the household stores, yet with delicate tact, they kept these practicalities in the background. Instead, they focused initially on Gwendolen's journey and the comfort it brought her mother to have her back home.

There was nothing for Gwendolen to latch onto that justified extending her discontent to the people around her. She felt shaken into a hyper-alert state, as if summoned to a drill everyone else was obediently following, when her uncle began, in a voice of firm kindness, to outline the efforts he had made to secure her a situation offering maximum advantage. Mr. Gascoigne had not forgotten Grandcourt, but the possibility of further advances from that quarter was too vague for a man of his good sense to rely upon; uncertainties of that nature could not paralyze his actions in securing immediate aid for his niece under current conditions.

"I felt there was no time to lose, Gwendolen," the rector continued. "A position in a good family where you will receive some consideration cannot be obtained at a moment's notice. However long we wait, we could hardly find one where you would be better off than at Bishop Mompert's. I am known to both him and Mrs. Mompert, which is an obvious advantage. Our correspondence has proceeded favorably, but I cannot be surprised that Mrs. Mompert wishes to see you before making an absolute engagement. She intends to arrange for you to meet her at Wanchester when she travels to town. I daresay you will find the interview somewhat trying, my dear, but you will have a little time to prepare your mind."

"Do you know *why* she wants to see me, uncle?" Gwendolen asked, her mind rapidly cycling through various reasons an imaginary Mrs. Mompert with three daughters might entertain—all reasons of a distinctly disagreeable nature for someone presenting herself for inspection.

The rector smiled gently. "Do not be alarmed, my dear. She desires a more precise impression of you than my report can convey. A mother is naturally scrupulous about choosing a companion for her daughters. I have told her you are very young. However, she exercises close supervision over her daughters' education, which makes her less anxious regarding age. She is a woman of taste and strict principle, and she objects to having a French person in the house. I am certain she will judge your manners and accomplishments as good as she is likely to find, and regarding the religious and moral tone of the education, she, and indeed the bishop himself, will preside."

Gwendolen dared not answer, but the suppression of her decided dislike for the entire prospect sent a deep, unusual flush over her face and neck, receding as swiftly as it arrived. Anna, filled with tender fears, interlaced her little hand with her cousin's, and Mr. Gascoigne, a kindly man, could not fail to perceive the trial this sudden change entailed for a girl like Gwendolen. Determined to present a cheerful view of affairs, he continued in an easy, conversational tone, as if addressing no objections, "I hold this position in such high regard that I should have been tempted to strive for it for Anna, had she been at all likely to meet Mrs. Mompert's expectations. It is truly a home, with a continuation of education in the highest sense: 'governess' is a misnomer. The bishop's views are decidedly Low Church compared to my own—he is a close friend of Lord Grampian's—but though privately strict, he is not narrow-minded in public matters. Indeed, he has aroused as little dislike in his diocese as any bishop on the bench. He has remained friendly to me, though before his promotion, when he was an incumbent of this diocese, we had a minor controversy concerning the Bible Society."

The rector's words were laden with satisfactory meaning for himself, leading him to assume they would produce similar effects in his niece's mind. "Continuation of education"—"bishop's views"—"privately strict"—"Bible Society"—these phrases sounded to him like a few harmless snakes released for the instruction of ladies who viewed all reptiles as equally equipped with poison sacs, stinging or biting merely for convenience. To Gwendolen, already recoiling from the opening prospect, such phrases resembled the intensifying heat of a burning glass—not at all the links of reflective persuasion they formed for the benevolent uncle. Desperately, she began to search for an alternative.

"There was another situation, I think, mamma mentioned?" she said, exercising determined self-mastery.

"Yes," the rector replied in a deprecating tone. "But that is in a school. I would not derive the same satisfaction from your taking that post. It would involve much harder work, as you are aware, and offer fewer benefits in other respects. Furthermore, you do not have an equal chance of obtaining it."

"Oh, dear no," said Mrs. Gascoigne. "It would be much harder for you, my dear—and much less appropriate. You might not even have a bedroom to yourself." Gwendolen's memories of school life conjured other particularities that forced her to admit internally that this alternative offered no relief. Turning back to her uncle, she feigned acceptance of his ideas, "When is Mrs. Mompert likely to send for me?"

"That is somewhat uncertain, but she has promised not to entertain any other proposal until she has seen you. She has entered deeply into your position. It will likely be within the next fortnight. But I must depart now. I am going to lease part of my glebe."

The rector ended with great cheerfulness, leaving the room with the satisfying conviction that Gwendolen was adapting herself to circumstances like a sensible girl. Having spoken appropriately, he naturally presumed the effects would be appropriate; accustomed, as a household and parish authority, to "speaking to" refractory persons with the understanding that the measure was

To map the contours of a mind stripped bare by the sterility of a fastidious egoism is a difficult task. In such a psyche, all direct desires have dulled, shrinking from active motives into a wavering anticipation of what others might desire. It is a landscape composed entirely of shifting moods, where a fitful, rank impulse sprouts here and there amidst a sea of general, overgrown weeds. Such a condition often befalls a life lived too freely, unshaped by the heavy, molding pressure of obligation. As the old maxim suggests, "As you like" is a treacherous signpost when the terrain is so vast and empty.

Powerful figures announce their intentions with minimal effort, affecting significant shifts in fortune for the price of a few casual words. When Lord Grandcourt learned that Gwendolen Harleth had fled Leubronn, he casually remarked that the fashionable resort was a "beastly hole," inferior even to Baden. To Mr. Lush, this offhand comment was a definitive signal that his patron intended to return immediately to Diplow. Yet, the execution of this plan lagged far behind the speed of the intention. For the following day, Grandcourt loitered, issuing no distinct orders for departure. Perhaps he sensed Lush waiting, perhaps he simply relished the delay. He lingered over his grooming, descending finally with a faded, yet perfectly distinguished countenance that made the fresh complexions of lesser men appear as signs of raw vulgarity. He drifted through the terraces, the gambling rooms, and the reading-room, cultivating an air of profound indifference toward everyone and everything around him.

Only when he encountered Lady Mallinger did he exert any effort. He raised his hat, paused, and feigned attention to her recommendation of the local waters, replying, "Yes; I heard somebody say how providential it was that there always happened to be springs at gambling places."

"Oh, that was a joke," said innocent Lady Mallinger, misled by Grandcourt's languid seriousness, "an imitation of the old saying about towns and rivers, you know."

"Ah, perhaps," said Grandcourt, his expression unchanged. Lady Mallinger deemed this conversation worthy of sharing with Sir Hugo, who chuckled softly. "Oh, my dear, he is not a fool," the baronet assured her. "Do not suppose he cannot see a joke. He can play his cards as well as most of us."

"He has never seemed to me a very sensible man," Lady Mallinger murmured, offering an excuse for her own unease. She harbored a secret aversion to meeting Grandcourt; to her, he was little more than a looming monument to her failure as a wife—a failure defined by her inability to bear Sir Hugo a son. Her constant reflection was that her husband might fairly regret his choice, and if he were not a saint, he might treat her with some roughness, for gentlemen naturally dislike being disappointed.

Deronda, too, received a nod of recognition from Grandcourt, a gesture for which he felt no gratitude, though he returned it with perfect civility. No amount of reasoning regarding the foundations of custom could erase the early-rooted conviction that his birth had been attended with injury for which his father was to blame. Seeing that but for this injury, Grandcourt's prospects might have been his own, Deronda was proudly resolute not to behave in any way that could be interpreted as irritation. He saw a very easy path into mean, unreasoning rancor and triumph in another's frustration, and being determined not to descend into that ugly pit, he turned his back on it, clinging to the kinder affections within him as a possession. Pride, indeed, served him well—the pride of not acknowledging a disadvantage for oneself that vulgar minds are prone to exaggerate, such as the shabby trappings of poverty. He would not allow a man like Grandcourt to suppose himself envied by him. But there is no guarding against interpretation. Grandcourt believed that Deronda, the poor devil whom he was certain was his cousin by the father's side, inwardly winced under their shared position. Therefore, the presence of that less fortunate person was more agreeable to Grandcourt than it would otherwise have been. An imaginary envy, the notion that others feel their comparative deficiency, is the ordinary escort of egoism; and his pet dogs were not the only creatures he delighted in making jealous. Consequently, he was civil enough to exchange several words with Deronda on the terrace about the hunting around Diplow, even suggesting, "You had better come over for a run or two when the season begins."

Mr. Lush, not displeased by the delay, amused himself well, partly by gossiping with Sir Hugo and answering his questions about Grandcourt's affairs as far as they might affect his willingness to sell his interest in Diplow. Regarding Grandcourt's personal entanglements, the baronet knew enough already for Lush to feel released from silence on a sunny autumn day, when there was nothing more agreeable in the lounging promenades than to speak freely of a tyrannous patron behind his back. Sir Hugo willingly inclined his ear to a little good-humored scandal, which he affectionately called *traits de mœurs*; but he was strict in keeping such communications from hearers who might take them too seriously. Whatever knowledge he held of his nephew's secrets, he had never spoken of it to Deronda, who considered Grandcourt a pale-blooded mortal but was far from wishing to learn how the red corpuscles had been washed out of him. It was Lush's policy and inclination to gratify everybody when he had no reason to the contrary, and the baronet always treated him well, as one of those easy-handled personages who, frequenting the company of gentlemen without being exactly gentlemen themselves, can be the more serviceable, like the second-best articles of our wardrobe, which we use with a comfortable freedom from anxiety.

"Well, you will let me know the turn of events," said Sir Hugo, "if this marriage seems likely to come off after all, or if anything else happens to make the want of money pressing. My plan would be much better for him than burdening Ryelands."

"That's true," said Lush, "but it must not be urged upon him—simply placed in his way so that the scent may tickle him. Grandcourt is not a man to be always led by what makes for his own interest; especially if you let him see that it makes for your interest too. I'm attached to him, of course. I've given up everything else for the sake of keeping by him, and it has lasted a good fifteen years now. He would not easily get anyone else to fill my place. He's a peculiar character, is Henleigh Grandcourt, and it has been growing on him of late years. However, I'm of a constant disposition, and I've been a sort of guardian to him since he was twenty; an uncommonly fascinating fellow he was then, to be sure—and could be now, if he liked. I'm attached to him; and it would be a good deal worse for him if he missed me at his elbow."

Sir Hugo did not think it needful to express his sympathy or even assent, and perhaps Lush himself did not expect this sketch of his motives to be taken as exact. But how can a man avoid himself as a subject in conversation? And he must make some sort of decent toilet in words, as in cloth and linen. Lush's listener was not severe: a member of Parliament could allow for the necessities of verbal toilet; and the dialogue went on without any change of mutual estimate.

However, Lush's easy prospect of indefinite procrastination was cut short the next morning by Grandcourt saluting him with the question, "Are you making all the arrangements for our starting by the Paris train?"

"I didn't know you meant to start," said Lush, not exactly taken by surprise.

"You might have known," said Grandcourt, looking at the burned length of his cigar and speaking in that lowered tone which was usual with him when he meant to express disgust and be peremptory. "Just see to everything, will you? And mind no brute gets into the same carriage with us. And leave my P.P.C. at the Mallingers'."

In consequence, they were in Paris the next day; but here Lush was gratified by the proposal or command that he should go straight on to Diplow and see that everything was right, while Grandcourt and the valet remained behind. It was not until several days later that Lush received the telegram ordering the carriage to Wanchester station.

He had used the interim actively, not only in carrying out Grandcourt's orders about the stud and household, but in learning all he could of Gwendolen and how things were going on at Offendene. What was the probable effect that the news of the family misfortunes would have on Grandcourt's fitful obstinacy, he felt to be quite incalculable. So far as the girl's poverty might be an argument that she would accept an offer from him now in spite of any previous coyness, it might remove that bitter objection to risk a repulse which Lush divined to be one of Grandcourt's deterring motives; on the other hand, the certainty of acceptance was just "the sort of thing" to make him lapse hither and thither with no more apparent will than a moth. Lush had had his patron under close observation for many years and knew him perhaps better than he knew any other subject; but to know Grandcourt was to doubt what he would do in any particular case. It might happen that he would behave with an apparent magnanimity, like the hero of a modern French drama, whose sudden

"I bring white asses laden with the freight of Tyrian vessels—purple, gold, and balm—to bribe your will!" Daniel Deronda recited the lines aloud, his voice echoing with a theatrical intensity that belied the quiet room around him. "I'll bid them chase him forth, nor let him breathe the taint of his surmise on my secure resolve. Ay, 'tis secure! And therefore let him come to spread his freight. For firmness hath its appetite and craves the stronger lure, more strongly to resist. Would know the touch of gold to fling it off; scent wine to feel its lip the soberer; behold soft byssus, ivory, and plumes to say, 'They're fair, but I will none of them,' and flout Enticement in the very face."

His recitation was a performance of a role he had recently assumed, a mask of ironclad resolve worn to hide the churning turmoil within. Yet, as the words faded, the reality of the situation intruded upon the stage of his mind. The "secure resolve" he had just championed was already cracking under the weight of impending choices.

In the parlor below, the air was heavy with a mixture of dust and suppressed emotion. Gwendolen sat alone, the afternoon light casting long, gray shadows across the floorboards. The news from Mr. Gascoigne had settled over her like a wet woolen blanket; it was a suffocating warmth that offered no comfort. Her aunt, Mrs. Mompert, had finally scheduled an interview for the following week, a formal step that signaled the end of her sheltered existence at Offendene. The prospect of moving to Wanchester, of becoming a governess to the Mompert children, was a catastrophe disguised as duty.

Gwendolen stared at her hands, which lay limp in her lap. She had tried to convince herself, as her uncle had urged, that this was a trial of character, a chance to grow strong. "You will find an interest in varied experience," he had said, his voice smooth and reassuring. "And I have no doubt you will be a more valuable woman for having sustained such a part." But the truth gnawed at her gut. She could not pretend to believe she would like it. The idea of serving others, of being subservient to women who held her in contempt, filled her with a cold, creeping horror.

"You cannot pretend to believe that I shall like it," she had muttered when he last visited, her voice trembling with a petulance she usually kept hidden. "But I am quite aware that I am obliged to bear it."

Now, alone, the bitter tears began to rise, hot and stinging, blurring the edges of the room. Her heart rebelled against the notion that youth made suffering easier. *When,* she wondered, staring blankly at the wallpaper, *am I to have any happiness, if it does not come while I am young?* The world, once a playground of endless possibility, now seemed a barren landscape enclosed by a sterile distance. She felt a profound forlornness, a sense that she was not made for easy pre-eminence. The "spoiled child," as she sometimes felt herself, was being stripped bare, her illusions shattered by the harsh light of necessity.

She rose from the low ottoman, pacing the drawing-room restlessly. Her movements were jerky, driven by an inner restlessness. She leaned forward, resting her cheek on her palm, a single tear tracing a path down her cheek. *I have always felt that Mamma was not a happy woman,* she thought, the image of her mother surfacing unbidden. *And now I dare say I shall be more unhappy than she has been.*

The thought of her mother aged her instantly. Gwendolen imagined her mother growing old and white, herself fading into the background, two weary faces meeting in a future of shared misery. It was a vision so terrifying that it broke her composure. She sobbed, not in anger, but with a tender, crushing grief, burying her face in her hands.

The front door opened, and the sound of footsteps echoed through the hall. Gwendolen barely registered her mother entering before the woman's presence brought her crashing back to reality. Her mother, Mrs. Davilow, stood in the doorway, her face pale and tremulous, holding something wrapped in paper.

"It was nothing, Mamma," Gwendolen gasped, pressing her handkerchief hastily to her eyes, trying to regain some semblance of control. "It is all over now."

Mrs. Davilow stepped closer, withdrawing her arms gently. Gwendolen saw then that her mother held a letter. The sight sent a fresh jolt of panic through her. "What is that letter?" she asked, her voice thin with fear. "Worse news still?"

"I don't know what you will think it, dear," Mrs. Davilow said softly, clutching the envelope as if it were a live coal. "You will hardly guess where it comes from."

"Don't ask me to guess anything," Gwendolen snapped, the frustration rising in her throat like bile. As if a bruise were being pressed, she felt the need to defend her fragile emotional state.

"It is addressed to you, dear."

Gwendolen gave a slight, imperceptible toss of her head. "It comes from Diplow," Mrs. Davilow whispered, handing over the letter.

Gwendolen recognized the indistinct, sharp handwriting immediately. Her mother watched her closely, seeing the blood drain from Gwendolen's face as she read. The colors fled from her lips, leaving them pale and drawn. The words were few, formal, and chilling:

*Mr. Grandcourt presents his compliments to Miss Harleth, and begs to know whether he may be permitted to call at Offendene tomorrow after two and to see her alone. Mr. Grandcourt has just returned from Leubronn, where he had hoped to find Miss Harleth.*

Mrs. Davilow read the note, then looked at her daughter with wide, inquiring eyes. Gwendolen let the letter fall to the floor, turning away as if struck. The silence that followed was deafening.

"It must be answered, darling," Mrs. Davilow said, her voice trembling with timidity. "The man waits."

Gwendolen sank onto the settee, clasping her hands together, staring straight ahead. She looked like someone who had been startled by a gunshot and was frozen, listening to the echoes determine if more were to come. The situation had shifted violently. Moments ago, she had been resigned to a life of repulsive monotony, a path laid out for her by fate and circumstance. Now, a moment of choice had arrived.

Yet, triumph or terror? It was impossible to tell. There was a thrill in the tribute to her power, a fleeting sense that she was regaining an empire over her own life. But how to use it? The terror was immediate and visceral. Flashbacks hit her like a series of images in a book beaten open in haste: the allurements, the vacillations, the resolve to accede, the final repulsion. She saw the incisive face of the dark-eyed lady with the lovely boy, the scene of disclosure that had broken her faith in men and things. That unalterable experience made her shrink from the vision of what was coming.

Where was the good of this choice? What did she wish? Anything different? No! And yet, in the dark seed-growths of her consciousness, a new wish was forming—one so desperate it terrified her. *I wish I had never known it!* Something, anything, that would save her from the dread of letting Grandcourt come.

Minutes stretched into an eternity for Mrs. Davilow, who waited anxiously. Finally, she ventured gently, "It will be necessary for you to write, dear. Or shall I write an answer for you—which you will dictate?"

"No, Mamma," Gwendolen said, drawing a deep, shuddering breath. "But please lay me out the pen and paper."

Time. That was what she needed. Time to delay, to hesitate, to avoid the absolute decision. Was she to decline Grandcourt's visit? Close the shutters forever? Though the assurance remained that she would remain exactly where she was, trapped in her servitude. A strange, warm current of activity surged within her, stirring toward something that would be an event—an opportunity where she could look and speak with former effectiveness. The interest of the morrow was no longer at a deadlock.

"There is really no reason on earth why you should be so alarmed at the man's waiting a few minutes, Mamma," Gwendolen said, her voice dropping to a remonstrant whisper. "Servants expect nothing else than to wait. It is not to be supposed that I must write on the instant."

"No, dear," Mrs. Davilow said, her tone corrected, turning to sit down and pick up a piece of needlework that lay nearby. "He can wait another quarter of an hour, if you like."

It was simple speech, yet it felt subtly calculated. Gwendolen felt a contradictory desire to be hastened; hurry would save her from deliberate choice, from facing the abyss of what she truly wanted.

"I did not mean him to wait long enough for that needlework to be finished," she said, lifting her hands to stroke the backward curves of her hair. She rose from her seat and stood still, the room spinning slightly.

"But if you don't feel able to decide?" Mrs. Davilow asked sympathetically.

"I *must* decide,"

Desire trims the sails, yet circumstance alone provides the wind to fill them.

Outside, Grandcourt rode his magnificent black stallion, Yarico, accompanied by his groom on *Criterion*, enjoying the leisurely journey from Diplow to Offendene. Inside, however, the air was thick with a quiet, internal storm. Gwendolen sat before the mirror, her mother gently gathering the long, light-brown tresses she had just brushed into a soft coil.

"Simply gather it loosely, Mamma," Gwendolen instructed softly. "Make a single coil."

Mrs. Davilow offered to bring earrings, her gaze lingering on the glass where they both appeared. It was impossible to miss the transformation in her daughter's face; the eyes burned with a brightness unseen for days, a shadow lifted from the features to reveal lines of placid youthfulness. A cheerful note slipped into her mother's voice. "You truly desire those earrings?"

"No, Mamma," Gwendolen replied, offering one of her practiced, enigmatic smiles. "I shall wear none. I intend to dress in my black silk. Black is the appropriate attire for refusing an offer." She rose, shedding her dressing gown with a fluid motion.

"And suppose the offer is never made?" Mrs. Davilow ventured, a sly glint in her eye.

"Then it will simply be because I have refused it beforehand," Gwendolen countered, her chin lifting with a proud, defiant toss. "It amounts to the same result."

As Gwendolen descended the stairs in her long, somber robes, her posture was impeccable—a firm poise and an elastic grace that had recently been absent, like sap returning to a parched plant. Her mother watched her pass, thinking, *She is restored. It must be the joy of his arrival. Has she really resolved against him?*

If that thought had been spoken aloud, Gwendolen would have bristled. Indeed, for the last twenty hours, her mind had been a battleground of alternating images and arguments regarding Grandcourt. The resolution she had formed had evaporated, dissolved by the oscillation of counterbalancing desires. She would have maintained her stance, but it felt hollow, drained of blood, no more vital to her quivering existence than a passive prayer for divine will. She did not intend to accept him; since the letter arrived, she had intended to reject him. Yet, this resolve forced her to confront the unwelcome truths head-on, stripping away the veil of ignorance. By staring intently at a dubious future with a constructive imagination, one could conjure twenty different shapes. The vague hesitations she had felt before the meeting at the Whispering Stones now counted for nothing, submerged beneath a final, overwhelming repulsion.

"If it had not been for that day at Cardell Chase," she told herself, "there would be no barrier to our union." That day, and the ones following, had shifted her from reasoning to action. An impulsive force, born not merely of maidenly pride or jealousy, but of a profound, vague dread of wrongdoing, had driven her. Whatever aligned with the external image of a "Lady" she embraced without scruple; but from the shadow realm of disgrace, guilt, and sin, she shrank with a mixture of terror and pride. She placed any deliberate injury to another firmly within the category of guilt.

Yet, a nagging question persisted: Did she truly know the extent of Mrs. Glasher's plight? She had promised not to interfere, but was a second marriage to Grandcourt truly the fatal blow to that promise? Could it not be better for Grandcourt to marry? What limits could a woman not break if she learned to assert herself? Here, her mind wandered into constructive fantasy.
