Gwendolen possessed as accurate a concept of marital dynamics—as of magnetic fields and storm laws—as she did of reality. "Mamma managed poorly," she summarized her mother's failures. "I shall manage otherwise."

Her inner debate swirled with hypotheticals: *What would Mamma and Uncle say of Mrs. Glasher? What would anyone think if Mr. Grandcourt married someone else and fathered other children?* Considering "what anyone would say" freed her from the paralysis of obscurity. Memories confirmed that society viewed illegitimate children as more shameful than illegitimate fathers. The verdict of the crowd reassured her: she had no business worrying deeply for Mrs. Glasher.

However, there was another burden. Beyond public opinion lay a personal feeling of indignation and loathing—to be expected to wed an "outworn" life, heavy with backward secrets, felt like an affront to her own identity. Though the question of love for Grandcourt had played a minor role, the idea of being "enamored" belonged to him. She had found his approach acceptable until the glimpse of his past, which she resented as a calculated insult. His advances to her were deliberate, and now, viewing them retrospectively, they tasted of disgust. These feelings had sunk deep, dulled by recent troubles into a steady, firm conviction: she would not accept Grandcourt.

Had anything altered this, it would have been the prospect of saving her mother—"poor Mamma"—but no! She was going to refuse. Meanwhile, the knowledge that he was coming to be rejected invigorated her. She held the white reins again. A new current flowed through her, reviving her from the crushed consciousness left by her interview with Klesmer. She would no longer seek validation of her capabilities; she would exercise her power.

Was this why her heart hammered anxiously when she heard the horse's hooves on the gravel? When Miss Merry announced Grandcourt's presence in the drawing-room, Gwendolen realized her preparations were futile. It mattered little whether he arrived suddenly amidst despair or after careful planning.

Walking into the room, she concentrated every ounce of energy into self-control, projecting a grave, gracious demeanor. She handed him her hand, answering his inquiry about her health with a voice low and languid, mirroring his own. Seated opposite on two wreath-painted chairs, Gwendolen sat upright with downcast lids, while Grandcourt leaned back, hat in hand, gazing at her. To an observer, they appeared suspended in a stage of romantic tension. The courtship had indeed begun; she felt herself being wooed by this silent man, enveloped in an atmosphere of subtle roses and undivided attention. He, too, felt himself courting, delighted by the challenge of a girl who remained somewhat uncalculable.

"I was disappointed not to find you at Leubronn," he began, his broken drawl laced with amorous laziness. "The place was unbearable without you. A mere kennel. Do you not agree?"

"I cannot judge what it would be without me," Gwendolen replied, turning her eyes to him with a flicker of mischief. "With me, I like it well enough to stay longer, if I could. But I was forced home by family troubles."

"It was cruel of you to leave," Grandcourt said, ignoring the troubles, though Gwendolen wished to clarify them immediately. "You must have known it would ruin everything. You were the heart and soul of it all. Are you quite reckless about me?"

A "yes" would not be taken seriously; a "no" was impossible. Turning down her eyelids, she blushed from cheek to neck. Grandcourt read this as a sign of inclination and decided to push further.

"Perhaps there is some deeper interest? Some attraction—an engagement—that it would have been fair to disclose? Is there a man standing between us?"

An answer formed internally: *No; but there is a woman.* Yet, how could she speak this? Even if she had promised silence, entering the subject with Grandcourt felt forbidden. How to arrest his wooing with a formal speech? A fish invited to dinner knows how to decline, but what if it swims against a net? Gwendolen had no time to decide. Compelled by silence, she waited. After a pause, Grandcourt pressed, "Am I to understand that someone else is preferred?"

Impatient with her embarrassment, Gwendolen seized the difficulty, freeing herself. Raising her eyes, she spoke with renewed clarity and defiance, "No." She wished him to understand: *What then? I may not be ready to take you.* Nothing could escape Grandcourt's perception if it affected his ego.

"The last thing I would do is importune you," he declared smoothly. "I would not hope to win you by becoming a bore. If there were no hope, I would ask you to tell me so, and I would ride away to—well, anywhere."

To Gwendolen's own shock, the image of Grandcourt riding away sent a sudden alarm through her. What would remain? Only the former dreariness. She needed him there. Snatching at a delaying tactic, she spoke of her circumstances.

"I fear you are unaware of what has befallen us. I have lately been consumed with my mother's troubles, pushing other matters aside. She has lost her entire fortune, and we must leave this house. I ask you to pardon my preoccupation."

By evading a direct appeal, Gwendolen regained some composure. She spoke with dignity, meeting his gaze. His long, narrow, impenetrable eyes locked onto hers, arresting them mysteriously. The drama between them defied simple description, a complex interplay that words like "Love" or "Thought" could not capture. Who held the stronger influence? At that moment, his strongest wish was to master this creature—a piquant blend of maidenliness and mischief. He knew she harbored secrets that made her recoil, spurring him to triumph over her repugnance, and

"The general man is easier to know than the particular man." — La Rochefoucauld.

An hour after Grandcourt had departed, the news of Gwendolen's engagement rippled through the rectory. Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne, accompanied by Anna, spent the evening at Offendene. The atmosphere was thick with a mixture of relief and speculative calculation.

"My dear, let me congratulate you on having secured a powerful attachment," the rector observed, his tone warm yet analytical. "You look serious, and I do not wonder at it; a lifelong union is a solemn undertaking. Yet, from the way Mr. Grandcourt has conducted himself and spoken, I believe we may already perceive some good emerging from our adversity. It has afforded you an opportunity to observe your future husband's delicate liberality."

Mr. Gascoigne nodded sagaciously, referring to Grandcourt's subtle implication regarding provisions for Mrs. Davilow—a detail Gwendolen had previously recounted to her mother with precise accuracy.

"But I have no doubt that Mr. Grandcourt would have behaved quite as generously had you not fled to Germany," Mrs. Gascoigne interjected, sensing the necessity of this diplomatic reassurance. "Had you been engaged to him a month ago, as you might have been, surely he would have acted handsomely. Now, however, there is no room for caprice; indeed, I trust you harbor no inclinations toward any. A woman owes a profound debt of gratitude to a man who persists in offering himself despite obstacles. No doubt you feel properly."

"I am not at all certain that I do, Aunt," Gwendolen replied with a gravity that bordered on the saucy. "I do not know precisely what feelings are proper to entertain upon being engaged."

The rector patted her shoulder, smiling at what he deemed innocent naughtiness, while his wife interpreted his demeanor as permission not to be displeased. Anna, meanwhile, approached Gwendolen with a tender kiss, whispering, "I do hope you will be happy," before sinking quietly into the background, fighting back tears. In recent days, she had nurtured a private romance involving Rex—a fantasy wherein, if he still longed for Gwendolen, hardship might soften her heart until they could marry. That dream had transmuted into a prayer for her role as a faithful sister, useful only so long as Rex remained poor. Now, however, she required grace to celebrate something else.

The evening gathered around the honor and glory of Gwendolen. Miss Merry and four younger girls—Alice with her high shoulders, Bertha and Fanny the whisperers, and Isabel the listener—were present, rendering real life as absorbing as a novel like *Sir Charles Grandison*. The conversation flowed chiefly through the rector's decisive remarks, answering the conjectures of the two elder ladies. According to him, the matter of financial settlements need not be mentioned; everything must, and would undoubtedly, safely be left to Mr. Grandcourt.

"I should like to know exactly what sort of places Ryelands and Gadsmere are," Mrs. Davilow asked curiously.

"Gadsmere, I believe, is a secondary residence," Mr. Gascoigne offered. "But Ryelands is one of our finest seats. The park is extensive, the woods of rare value. The house itself was built by Inigo Jones, with ceilings painted in the Italian style. The estate yields twelve thousand a year, and there are two livings attached, including a rectory, in the gift of the Grandcourts. There may be some burdens on the land, yet Mr. Grandcourt is an only child."

"It would be most remarkable," Mrs. Gascoigne mused, counting off items on her fingers, "if he were to become Lord Stannery in addition to everything else. Consider: the Grandcourt estate, the Mallinger estate, _and_ the baronetcy, _and_ the peerage"—she paused on the fourth digit, adding, "though they say there will be no land accompanying the peerage." It seemed a pity there was nothing for the fifth finger.

"The peerage," the rector noted judiciously, "must be regarded as a remote chance. There are two cousins between the present peer and Mr. Grandcourt. It is certainly a serious reflection how death and other causes sometimes concentrate inheritances on a single man. But an excess of that kind is to be deprecated. To be Sir Mallinger Grandcourt Mallinger, with corresponding properties, is a valuable talent enough for any man entrusted with it. Let us hope it will be wisely used."

"And what a position for the wife, Gwendolen!" Mrs. Gascoigne exclaimed. "A great responsibility indeed. But you must lose no time in writing to Mrs. Mompert, Henry. It is a good thing you have an engagement to offer as an excuse; otherwise, she might feel offended. She is rather a formidable woman."

*I am rid of that horror,* Gwendolen thought internally, finding the name of Mompert akin to Mumbo-Jumbo. She remained remarkably silent throughout the evening. That night, she could hardly sleep in her little white bed. It was a rarity in her robust youth to be wakeful, and perhaps an even greater anomaly for her to conceal her restlessness from her mother. Her state of mind was entirely new: she who had always felt secure in managing others had just taken a decisive step she had previously vowed never to take—or perhaps, one she was bound not to take. She could not retreat now; she found much allure in what lay ahead, and returning would leave nothing of value.

Yet, her resolution was haunted by the shadow of a previous resolve, once the undoubted movement of her entire being. Lying on her pillow with wide-open eyes, watching a darkness visible only to the blind, she was appalled by the realization that she was proceeding with what she had initially fled from with repugnance. It was new to her that a question of right or wrong in her conduct should rouse such terror; she had never known compunction that atoning caresses and gifts could not quell. But here stood a moment when a new consciousness was awakened. She seemed poised to adopt deliberately, for the rest of her life, what she had rashly declared in her bitterness during her flight to Leubronn: that it did not signify what she did, so long as she amused herself.

That lawlessness, that casting aside of all concern for justification, suddenly terrified her. It arrived accompanied by a shadowy array of possible calamities behind it—calamities that had ceased to be merely abstract names. All the infiltrated influences of disregarded religious teaching, alongside the deeper impressions of something awful and inexorable enveloping her, concentrated in the vague conception of avenging power. The brilliant position she had longed for, the imagined freedom of marriage, the deliverance from the dull insignificance of girlhood—all stood immediately before her, yet they tasted like sacrilegious food, which she must snatch with trembling.

In the darkness and loneliness of her bed, her more resistant self could not act against the first onslaught of dread following her irrevocable decision. Images of that unhappy-faced woman and her children—Grandcourt and his relations with her—repeated themselves in her imagination like a clinging memory of disgrace, gradually erasing all other thoughts. Only the consciousness remained that she had dragged those scenes into her life. Her long wakefulness seemed a delirium. A faint, pale light penetrated beside the window curtain; the chill intensified. She could bear it no longer and cried, "Mamma!"

"Yes, dear," Mrs. Davilow responded instantly, her voice waking.

"Let me come to you."

Soon Gwendolen fell asleep on her mother's shoulder, sleeping until late. When she opened her eyes, her mother stood by the bedside holding a small packet.

"I am sorry to wake you, darling, but I thought it better to give you this at once. The groom has brought the Criterion; he has come on another horse and says he is to stay here."

Gwendolen sat up, opening the packet. Inside was a delicate enameled casket containing a splendid diamond ring, alongside a letter enclosing a folded piece of colored paper bearing these words:

*"Pray wear this ring when I come at twelve in sign of our betrothal. I enclose a check drawn in the name of Mr. Gascoigne for immediate expenses. Of course Mrs. Davilow will remain at Offendene, at least for some time. I hope, when I come, you will have granted me an early day, when you may begin to command me at a shorter distance.—Yours devotedly,*

The check amounted to five hundred pounds. Gwendolen turned it toward her mother along with the letter.

"How very kind and delicate!" Mrs. Davilow said, her voice filled with emotion. "But I truly prefer not to be dependent on a son-in-law. My daughters and I could get along very well."

"Mamma, if you say that again, I will not marry him," Gwendolen snapped angrily.

"My dear child, I trust you are not marrying solely for my sake," Mrs. Davilow pleaded, deprecatingly.

Gwendolen tossed her head away from her mother and let the ring lie. Irritated by this attempt to strip away a motive, perhaps the deeper cause of her agitation was the consciousness that she was not marrying solely for her mother's sake—that she was drawn toward this marriage in ways that stronger reasons than her mother's renunciation could not hinder. She had awakened to the signs that she was irrevocably engaged, and all the

"Now my cousins are at Diplow," said Grandcourt, "will you go
there?—to-morrow? The carriage shall come for Mrs. Davilow. You can
tell me what you would like done in the rooms. Things must be put in
decent order while we are away at Ryelands. And to-morrow is the only
day."

He sat sideways on a sofa in the drawing-room at Offendene, propped up with one arm, his legs crossed, watching her with the intense, still scrutiny of a man deeply absorbed in his subject. Gwendolen, who had once despised needlework, now held a piece of white embroidery with apparent fervor, though a closer inspection would have revealed many false stitches. For the past week, their days had been filled with horseback riding, yet they always carved out time for this quieter, more intimate companionship, which Gwendolen surprisingly did not find tiresome. She was thoroughly satisfied with Grandcourt. His responses to her lively inquiries about his travels and adventures were delivered with a languid, rolling cadence that suited her perfectly. From the start, she had noted his uncanny ability to speak; he possessed nothing of the fool, and through some subtle alchemy, he managed to convince her that all foolishness belonged to everyone else, save himself. A man who commands the best possesses a sovereign power to diminish everything else. His courtship had barely exceeded the bounds of obtrusive affection, lingering like the scent of roses, leaving only a gratified vanity in its wake. On one occasion, however, he had kissed not her cheek but the skin beneath her ear. Startled, Gwendolen had jumped up, visibly agitated, causing him to rise and ask, "I beg your pardon—did I annoy you?" "Oh, it was nothing," Gwendolen had replied, trembling slightly, "only I cannot bear—to be kissed under my ear." She had sat down again with a feigned laugh, yet her heart hammered with a vague, primal fear. She was no longer free to mock him as she had mocked Rex. This agitation seemed flattering to him, and he had contentedly refrained from pushing further.

Today, a light rain prevented riding, but a parcel arrived from London, and Mrs. Davilow had just left, displaying the exquisite items Grandcourt had selected for the house. Gwendolen basked in the scenery of her life. Letting her hands fall to her lap, she adopted a prettier, perverse air.

"Why is to-morrow the only day?"

"Because the next day is the first with the hounds," Grandcourt replied calmly.

"And after that?"

"After that I must go away for a couple of days—it's a bore—but I shall go one day and come back the next." Grandcourt caught the flicker of anxiety crossing her face. Releasing his hand from beneath his knee, he covered it with his own and said softly, "You object to my going away?"

"It's no use objecting," Gwendonen said, her voice cool. She fought desperately against the urge to confess her suspicion regarding his destination, to speak freely without restraint.

"Yes it is," Grandcourt said, drawing her hand closer. "I will postpone the trip. And I will travel at night, so as to be away only one day." He believed he understood the source of what he internally termed this fleeting temper, and at this precise moment, she fascinated him profoundly.

"Then don't postpone going, but travel at night," Gwendolen insisted, feeling a surge of power over him, a small vent for her irritation within his peremptory control.

"Then you will go to Diplow to-morrow?"

"Oh, yes, if you wish it," Gwendolen agreed in a high, careless tone. Her preoccupation with deeper emotions had blinded her to the warmth of his hand holding hers.

"How you treat us poor devils of men!" Grandcourt murmured, lowering his voice. "We are always getting the worst of it."

"_Are_ you?" Gwendolen asked, looking at him with naive inquiry. She longed to believe this common banter as the solemn truth about her lover; if so, she, too, was justified. If she knew everything, Mrs. Glasher would seem far more blameworthy than Grandcourt. "_Are_ you always getting the worst?"

"Yes. Are you as kind to me as I am to you?" Grandcourt gazed into her eyes with his narrow, piercing stare.

Gwendolen felt struck dumb. Conscious of the vast amount she had received, her sense of command faltered and sank away. It was as if she had consented to mount a chariot where another held the reins, and it was not in her nature to leap out before the world. She had not consented in ignorance, and all she could utter now would be a confession that she had not been ignorant. Her right to explanation had vanished. All she could do was adjust herself so that the spikes of this unwilling penance, imposed by conscience, would not wound her spirit. With a mental shiver, she resolved to shift her perspective. After a brief pause, during which she had not averted her eyes, she broke into a sudden smile.

"If I were as kind to you as you are to me, that would spoil your generosity: it would no longer be as great as it could be—and it is that now."

"Then I am not to ask for one kiss," Grandcourt said, content to pay a high price for this new mode of courtship, which introduced marriage by the finest contrasts.

"Not one?" Gwendolen asked, growing saucy, nodding defiantly.

He lifted her little left hand to his lips, then released it respectfully. To call him not disgusting was faint praise; he was almost charming. At this moment, she felt unlikely to ever love another man better than this one. His reticence gave her an inexplicable, delightful consciousness.

"Apropos," she said, picking up her embroidery again, "is there anyone besides Captain and Mrs. Torrington at Diplow? Or do you leave them _tête-à-tête_? I suppose he converses in cigars, and she answers with her chignon."

"She has a sister with her," Grandcourt said, a shadow of a smile touching his lips, "and there are two men besides—one of them you know, I believe."

"Ah, then, I have a poor opinion of him," Gwendolen shook her head.

"You saw him at Leubronn—young Deronda—a young fellow with the Mallingers."

Gwendolen felt as if her heart were performing a sudden gambol, and her fingers, striving to maintain a firm grip on her work, grew cold.

"I never spoke to him," she said, fearing any discernible change in herself. "Is he not disagreeable?"

"No, not particularly," Grandcourt said in his most languid manner. "He thinks a little too much of himself. I thought he had been introduced to you."

"No. Someone told me his name the evening before I left. That was all. What is he?"

"A sort of ward of Sir Hugo Mallinger's. Nothing of any consequence."

"Oh, poor creature! How very unpleasant for him!" Gwendolen murmured, speaking without real sarcasm. "I wonder if it has stopped raining!" she added, rising to look out the window.

Fortunately, the rain ceased the following day, and Gwendolen rode to Diplow on Criterion, as she had done previously when returning with her mother. She always felt bolder in her riding dress, bolstered by the agreeable belief that she looked her best—a sustaining illusion in any encounter that seemed formidable. Her anger toward Deronda had transmuted into a superstitious dread, perhaps due to the coercion he had exerted over her thoughts. Lest his first interference in her life foreshadow some future influence, she feared him. Superstitions are often born of intense self-feeling, making the evening star shine with a threat or a beggar's blessing encouraging. Such beliefs carry consequences that often verify their hopes or forebodings.

Before luncheon, Gwendolen toured the rooms with Mrs. Torrington and Mrs. Davilow, convinced that if she saw Deronda, a mere bow would suffice. She intended to notice him as little as possible. Yet, ultimately, an inward compulsion stronger than her pride seized her. From the first moment they shared the room, she seemed to do nothing but observe him; everything else became an automatic performance of an habitual role.

When he took his place at lunch, Grandcourt remarked, "Deronda, Miss Harleth tells me you were not introduced to her at Leubronn?"

"Miss Harleth hardly remembers me, I imagine," Deronda replied, looking at her quite simply as they bowed. "She was intensely occupied when I saw her."

Did he suppose she had not suspected him of redeeming her necklace?

"On the contrary. I remember you very well," Gwendolen said, feeling nervous but governing herself, examining him anew. "You did not approve of my playing at roulette."

"How did you come to that conclusion?" Deronda asked gravely.

"Oh, you cast an evil eye on my play," Gwendolen said, turning her head with a smile. "I began to lose as soon as you came to look on. I

Picture a rambling, haphazard house, its finest section constructed of grey stone and topped with red tiles, featuring a round tower that jutted from a corner, its conical roof crowned by a weather-vane that cast a pleasing silhouette against the summer glimmers or winter snows. Deep shadows spread beneath sprawling trees; a magnificent oak flourished on one side, while behind it, broken banks revealed the naked roots of Scotch firs, overlooking a distant rookery. On the opposite side lay a pool draped in foliage, where waterfowl flitted and squawked. Surrounding it all was a vast meadow, effectively a park, hemmed in by ancient plantations and guarded by stone lodges resembling miniature prisons. Beyond the gate, the countryside, once purely pastoral and charming, was now choked with coal mines, inhabited chiefly by men and brothers wearing candles in their hats, their skin possessing a diabolish hue that made them appear uniquely suspect to the children at Gadsmere—Mrs. Glasher's four stunning offspring, who had resided there for roughly three years.

Now, in November, as flower beds lay bare and trees stood leafless, the pool shimmered black, one might have declared the estate somberly aligned with the black roads and black mounds that seemed to mourn the district. Yet, whenever the children played on the gravel accompanied by the dogs, the gloom broke. Under her current circumstances, Mrs. Glasher found Gadsmere as agreeable as any home; the complete seclusion ensured by the area's unattractiveness suited her perfectly. When she drove her two ponies with a wagonette full of children, no gentry in carriages appeared, only businessmen in gigs. At church, she faced no eyes she needed to dodge, for the curate and his wife were either ignorant of anything damaging or simply ignored it. To them, she was merely a widowed lady, the tenant of Gadsmere; the name of Grandcourt held little weight there compared to the lessees of the collieries, Fletcher and Gawcome.

A decade had passed since the elopement of an Irish officer's beautiful wife with young Grandcourt, followed by a duel where bullets wounded only the air. Those who remembered the affair now marveled at the fate of Mrs. Glasher, whose beauty and brilliance had once marked her abroad, living openly with young Grandcourt. That he had extricated himself from that entanglement seemed natural and desirable. As for her, it was assumed a woman who had abandoned her child alongside her husband had sunk further. Grandcourt, naturally, grew weary of her; he was devoted to the chase of women, and a man of his station would surely seek a suitable match with a noble daughter. No one spoke of Mrs. Glasher anymore, not as they did of a manslaughter victim ten years prior. She was a lost vessel, after whom no expedition sailed, while Grandcourt remained in harbor, colors flying, registered seaworthy.

Yet, truthfully, Grandcourt had never truly disentangled himself from Mrs. Glasher. His passion for her had been the strongest and most enduring of his life. Though it was now as dead as the music of a cracked flute, it left behind a dull disposition that, following the death of her husband three years prior, prompted a wavering notion of marrying her, an idea often expressed during his first ardor. Back then, Grandcourt would have willingly paid for the freedom won by divorce, but the husband refused, unwilling to remarry himself or have his domestic habits printed in evidence.

The shift in Mrs. Glasher's poise was diametrically opposed. Initially, she was relatively careless about marriage; escaping a disagreeable husband and finding bliss with a lover who completely fascinated her—young, handsome, amorous, and living in high style—was sufficient. She was impassioned and vivacious, craving adoration and exasperated by five years of marital rudeness. The sense of release stilled her anxieties; an equivocal position mattered not to her then. She envied no dull, disregarded wife. The sole blemish on her vision of this new pleasant world was the knowledge that she had left her three-year-old boy, who died two years later. His first utterance of "mamma" had sounded distinct from the children who followed.

However, years had wrought many changes beyond the contours of her cheek and throat. For Grandcourt to marry her had become her dominant desire. The equivocal position she had once shrugged off now weighed heavily upon her through her children, whom she loved with a devotion charged with the passion of atonement. She possessed no repentance save in this direction. If Grandcourt married her, the children would fare no worse; they would see their mother in a dignified position, free from worldly disadvantage. Her son could be made his father's heir. This yearning granted supreme importance to Grandcourt's feelings for her. Her love for him had long resolved into an anxious plea that he grant her the unique, permanent claim of a wife. She expected no other happiness than the satisfaction of her maternal love and pride—including her pride in herself before her children. For this result, she was prepared to endure anything quietly in marriage with tragic firmness. She possessed enough acumen to cherish Grandcourt's flickering purpose negatively, avoiding passionate appeals or scenes. In her, as in everyone seeking something from him, his incalculable turns and tendency to harden under beseeching had bred a reasonable dread—a slow discovery born of no presentiment in the bearing of a youthful lover with a fine facial line and softest manners. Yet, reticence had cost this impassioned woman dearly, making her bitterer. There is no quailing, even that forced upon the helpless and injured, without an ugly obverse; the withheld sting gathered venom.

She was absolutely dependent on Grandcourt. Though he had always been liberal with expenses, everything remained voluntary on his part. With the goal of marriage before her, she sought nothing less. He had stated he would never settle anything except by will. Often, when contemplating her future, it occurred to her that even if she did not become Grandcourt's wife, he might never have a son with a legitimate claim, meaning her son could eventually inherit the bulk of his estates. No son of that early age could promise greater resemblance to his father's physique. Becoming Grandcourt's wife was far from an extravagant fantasy; even Lush had entertained it, stating he would sooner bet on it than any other likelihood regarding his familiar companion. Indeed, Lush, inferring Grandcourt intended to use his residence at Diplow to win Miss Arrowpoint, thought it wise to fan that project, viewing it as a tacit renunciation of the marriage with Mrs. Glasher, which had long hovered like a moth around Grandcourt's caprice. However, both prospects were negatived by Gwendolen's arrival. It was natural for Mrs. Glasher to eagerly join Lush's plan to hinder this new danger by erecting a mental barrier in the girl's mind. She entered into it with an eagerness blending passion and purpose, some of her stored-up venom delivering itself through this channel.

Subsequently, she heard from Lush of Gwendolen's departure and the probability that all danger from her had ceased. Yet, no letter informed her that the danger had returned and become a certainty. Since then, she had written to Grandcourt as she habitually did, and he had been unusually slow to answer. She inferred he might intend visiting Gadsmere precisely when he was en route, harboring hope—that construction of another's mind wherein strong wishing equals reality—that a certain sickness from that frustrated courtship might dispose him to slip more easily back onto the old track of intention.

Grandcourt had two grave purposes in coming to Gadsmere: to convey news of his approaching marriage in person, rendering this first difficulty final; and to retrieve from Lydia his mother's diamonds, which he had long ago entrusted to her and desired her to wear. Her person suited diamonds, making them appear as though they were worth the money spent. These particular jewels were not mountains of light; they were mere peas and beans for ears, neck, and hair, yet worth several thousand pounds, and Grandcourt necessarily wished to possess them for his wife. Previously, when he requested Lydia return them to his keeping solely on grounds of safety and banking deposit, she had quietly but absolutely refused, declaring them quite safe. Finally, she had asked, "If you ever marry another woman I will give them up to her: are you going to marry another woman?" At that time, Grandcourt had no motive urging persistence, and he possessed the grace that his disposition to exercise power—whether by cowering, disappointing, or exciting rage—had always lain in abeyance before Lydia. A severe interpreter might claim the mere facts of their relation, the melancholy position of this woman dependent on his will, constituted a standing banquet for his delight in domination. Yet, something else existed in his forbearance: the surviving, though metamorphosed, effect of the power she had once held over him. It was this effect, the fitful dull lapse toward solicitations lacking zest, that had repeatedly inclined him to espouse a familiar past rather than rouse himself to novel expectations. But now, novelty had seized him, urging him to maximize it.

Mrs. Glasher sat in the pleasant room where she usually spent her mornings with her children surrounding her. A square projecting window overlooked broad gravel and grass sloping toward a little brook entering the pool. The tops of a low, black cabinet, an old oak table, and chairs in tawny leather were littered

"A wild dedication of yourselves
To unpath'd waters, undreamed shores."

On the morning that Gwendolen Harleth became Mrs. Grandcourt, the air was crisp with a slight frost, though the sun hung low and bright in a clear sky. The procession moving toward the church was a spectacle, drawing crowds from nearly every corner of Pennicote. A close friend of the rector officiated, while the clergyman himself served as the father, lending a dignified gravity to the parade. Yet, amidst the brilliance, two faces bore the shadow of sorrow: Mrs. Davilow's and Anna's. Mrs. Davilow's eyes were swollen and pink, betraying nights of weeping, and no one blamed her for grief at the departure of her beloved daughter. It was harder to understand why Anna, adorned in her sister's honor, wore such a troubled countenance, but the villagers' curiosity quickly turned to gossip regarding the union itself.

Of the couple, Gwendolen was the undeniable star. Her carriage and figure bespoke a "lady o' title," though perhaps her complexion lacked the rosy bloom such status usually commanded. Grandcourt, however, shared her pallor—a trait noted by the miller's wife, who found comfort in their similarity. The villagers, innocent of deeper truths, assumed he loved her deeply, hoping he would never resent her past as a governess or her mother's humble cottage at Sawyer's. The miller's fourteen-year-old daughter, listening to tales of high gentry treating wives poorly, received stern correction from her mother, who spoke of Squire Pelton's brutal habits. The tailor, overhearing this, wisely warned Mrs. Girdle that quarrels often begin with the tongue, not the whip. "The Lord gave it 'em to use," Mrs. Girdle replied, noting that men rarely intended for women to wield it. They speculated that Grandcourt, known for his silence, was a tyrant who demanded everything be done "dummy-like" without question.

As the pair emerged together, the tailor marveled at Gwendolen's long, pointed eyes, remarking how they made people feel comical when she looked at them. In truth, Gwendolen displayed an electric vitality, her gaze shining with the lustre of intense excitement. She felt no pain, but rather a thrilling intoxication akin to standing at a gambling table after a massive loss. Whatever uneasy conscience she harbored was drowned out by the gratification of her ambition and desire for luxury. She did not repent accepting Grandcourt; instead, she faced the world with a defiant exultation, daring to win or lose with equal éclat. She believed she was stepping into a realm of full power, armed with the official strength of marriage. The intoxicating fumes of youthful egoism, previously shaken by shame and guilt, had returned stronger than before. She walked among illusions, unaware that she was already a little drunk on the prospect of greatness.

Back at home, Mrs. Davilow helped Gwendolen remove her bridal white for traveling clothes. "Thank God you bear it so well, my darling!" the mother whispered, her trembling hands showing her deep anxiety. Gwendolen laughed, pressing her hands to her mother's cheeks with affectionate mockery. "If I were going to Mrs. Mompert's, you would have said that, dear, sad, incorrigible mamma!" she declared, spreading her arms to display herself as "Mrs. Grandcourt." She teased her mother about being ready to die of vexation if Gwendolen refused the match, promising that her future included splendid houses, horses, diamonds, and court life. She vowed to love her mother better than anyone else, though she dismissed the notion of her husband expecting primacy with a pretty grimace, claiming she wouldn't treat him ill unless he deserved it.

The journey to Ryelands, fifty miles by rail, left the sky veiled in twilight. As they drove through the park, Gwendolen chatted incessantly, ignoring any shift in their dynamic, while Grandcourt remained ecstatically quiet. She seized his hand with renewed vivacity, like a kitten refusing to be petted quietly. Her heart beat fast, not merely from the novelty of the estate, but from the terrifying fulfillment of her girlish dreams. She imagined herself the heroine of an admired play, surrounded by lackeys who revered her insolence, living behind corridors of immense height where painted springs bloomed. Yet, beneath this exultation lay a sharp infusion of dread, ready to curdle at any sign.

As they approached the gates, Gwendolen fell silent. When Grandcourt kissed her, she barely registered it, lost in the absorbing show of the estate. Inside, the hall glowed with warmth, mats, carpets, and statues, staffed by few but efficient servants. Led by Grandcourt down a scented corridor, she entered an ante-room where an open doorway spilled rich light. "These are our dens," he said, kissing her hand before departing to prepare for dinner. More in love than he had ever expected, he left her to solitude.

Gwendolen threw herself into a chair by the glowing hearth, admiring her reflection in glass panels amidst her faint-green satin. Impatient for privacy, she dismissed the housekeeper, instructing her to leave everything until Hudson arrived. Moments later, the housekeeper returned, holding a packet wrapped carefully. "I was ordered to give this into nobody's hands but yours when you are alone," she explained, citing Grandcourt's specific instructions. "It is a present particularly ordered by him, but he was not to know of its arrival until you wore it."

Gwendolen took the packet, letting it rest on her lap as the doors closed. She suspected it contained the diamonds Grandcourt had mentioned. In a moment of confused feeling and creeping luxury, she welcomed the diversion. Inside the sealed covering was a box, and within it, a jewel-case. As soon as she opened it, she saw the diamonds gleaming—but instantly, she noticed a letter lying atop them. Recognizing the handwriting immediately, she felt a chill as if an adder had coiled over the jewels. Her heart leaped with a shock that drained her strength, and her hands trembled violently as she unfolded the thin paper.

*"These diamonds, which were once given with ardent love to Lydia Glasher, she passes on to you,"* the letter read, *"You have broken your word to her, that you might possess what was hers. Perhaps you think of being happy, as she once was, and of having beautiful children such as hers, who will thrust hers aside. God is too just for that. The man you have married has a withered heart. His best young love was mine: you could not take that from me when you took the rest. It is dead: but I am the grave in which your chance of happiness is buried as well as mine. You had your warning. You have chosen to injure me and my children. He had meant to marry me. He would have married me at last, if you had not broken your word. You will have your punishment. I desire it with all my soul."*

*"Will you give him this letter to set him against me and ruin us more—me and my children? Shall you like to stand before your husband with these diamonds on you, and these words of mine in his thoughts and yours? Will he think you have any right to complain when he has made you miserable? You took him with your eyes open. The willing wrong you have done me will be your curse."*

At first, Gwendolen stared at the words, spellbound by the horror of them, as if reading a sentence of penance. Suddenly, a fresh spasm of terror seized her. She leaned forward, stretching the paper toward the fire, desperate to destroy the evidence before anyone could see it. The letter flew like a feather from her trembling fingers, caught instantly in a great draft of flame. In her frantic movement, the casket fell from her lap, scattering the diamonds across the floor. She paid no attention to the glittering stones; instead, she collapsed back into her chair, utterly helpless. For a long time, she sat in a stupor, feeling ill, haunted by the repeating words of the letter. Truly, these were poisoned gems, and the poison had already seeped into her blood.

Finally, a tap at the door announced Grandcourt's return, dressed for dinner. The sight of him sent a new, violent shock through her, causing her to scream again and again with hysterical force. He had expected her dressed and smiling, ready to descend for the meal. Instead, he found her pale, shrieking as if possessed by terror, with the jewels scattered around her on the floor. It was as if the furies themselves had crossed his threshold.

In every age, it has been a favored maxim that a mighty love possesses the nature of an isolated fate, to which the mind's usual opinions and accustomed resolutions are wholly strangers. Consider Daphnis's madness, where conviction of Heraclitus's doctrines would have availed him little; or Tristan, bred by a philtre, who, though as profound as Duns Scotus, would have had his reasoning shattered by that single cup; or Romeo, seized suddenly for Juliet, where any objections he might have raised against Ptolemy would have made no difference to his discourse beneath the balcony. Yet not all love is thus severed from reason, even when potent. Nay, this passion holds a vast capacity for alliance with every operation of the soul, acknowledging effects from the imagined light of unproven firmaments and scaling itself to the grander orbits of what has been and shall be.

Upon returning to town, Daniel Deronda could assure Sir Hugo that he had lodged in Grandcourt's mind a clear understanding: fifty thousand pounds could be obtained by surrendering a prospect that was likely distant and by no means absolute. Yet, regarding Grandcourt's actual disposition, Deronda saw nothing more than an evident inclination to maintain friendly communications.

"And what did you think of the future bride upon a closer inspection?" Sir Hugo asked.

"I thought better of her than I did at Leubronn," Deronda replied. "Roulette was not a fitting setting for her; it revealed something of the demon within. At Diplow, she seemed far more feminine and attractive—less hard and self-possessed. I perceived a different expression in her mouth and eyes."

"Do not flirt with her too much, Dan," Sir Hugo said, intending a pleasant jest. "If you manage to make Grandcourt savage when they arrive at the Abbey at Christmas, it will interfere with my affairs."

"I can remain in town, sir."

"No, no. Lady Mallinger and the children cannot do without you at Christmas. Only do not make mischief—unless you can engineer a duel and manage to shoot Grandcourt, which might be worth a slight inconvenience."

"I do not think you have ever seen me flirt," Deronda said, unmoved.

"Oh, haven't I, though?" Sir Hugo countered provocatively. "You are perpetually gazing tenderly at women and conversing with them in a Jesuitical manner. You are a dangerous young fellow—a sort of Lovelace who will cause Clarissas to chase you rather than vice versa."

What use was it to be exasperated by a tasteless joke? Only the exasperation precedes reflection on utility. Few friendly remarks are more irritating than the information that we constantly *seem* to do what we never intend to do. Sir Hugo's conception of flirting was, it was hoped, rather peculiar; for Deronda was certain he had never flirted. Nevertheless, he was relieved that the baronet lacked knowledge of the repurchase of Gwendolen's necklace, which might otherwise have fed his appetite for such banter.

From now on, he would exercise vigilance. Specifically, in his conduct at Mrs. Meyrick's, where he was about to pay his first visit since arriving from Leubronn. For Mirah was undoubtedly a creature in whom it was difficult not to display a tender kind of interest, both in gaze and speech.

Mrs. Meyrick had not failed to send Deronda a report of Mirah's well-being. "We are growing fonder of her every day," she had written. "At breakfast, we all look toward the door with expectation to see her enter, watching and listening to her as if she were a native from a new land. I have heard not a word from her lips that causes me doubt. She is quite contented and full of gratitude. My daughters are learning from her, and they hope to secure her other pupils, for she is anxious not to eat the bread of idleness but to work, like my girls. Mab says our life has become like a fairy tale, and all she fears is that Mirah will turn into a nightingale again and fly away from us. Her voice is simply perfect—not loud and strong, but searching and melting, like the thoughts of what has been. That is how old people like me feel a beautiful voice."

However, Mrs. Meyrick did not delve into particulars requiring her to admit that Amy and Mab, who had accompanied Mirah to the synagogue, found the Jewish faith less reconcilable with their wishes in Mirah's case than in Scott's Rebecca. They remained silent out of delicacy, for Mirah's religion was too tender a subject to be touched lightly. Yet eventually, Amy, a practical reformer, could not restrain a question.

"Excuse me, Mirah, but *does* it seem quite right to you that women should sit behind rails in a gallery apart?"

"Yes, I never thought of anything else," Mirah answered with mild surprise.

"And you prefer to see the men with their hats on?" Mab cautiously proposed the smallest point of difference.

"Oh, yes. I like what I have always seen there, because it brings back to me the same feelings—the feelings I would not part with for anything else in the world."

After this, any criticism, whether of doctrine or practice, would have seemed to these generous little people an inhospitable cruelty. Mirah's religion was of one fiber with her affections and had never presented itself to her as a set of propositions.

"She says herself she is a very bad Jewess and does not half know her people's religion," Amy remarked when Mirah retired to bed. "Perhaps it would gradually melt away from her, and she would pass into Christianity like the rest of the world, if she grew to love us very much and never found her mother. It is so strange to be of the Jews' religion now."

"Oh, oh, oh!" cried Mab. "I wish I were not such a hideous Christian. How can an ugly Christian, who is always dropping her work, convert a beautiful Jewess, who has not a fault?"

"It may be wicked of me," said shrewd Kate, "but I cannot help wishing that her mother may not be found. There might be something unpleasant."

"I do not think so, my dear," said Mrs. Meyrick. "I believe Mirah is cut out after the pattern of her mother. And what a joy it would be to her to have such a daughter brought back again! But a mother's feelings are not worth reckoning, I suppose"—she shot a mischievous glance at her daughters—"and a dead mother is worth more than a living one?"

"Well, and so she may be, little mother," said Kate, "but we would rather hold you cheaper, and have you alive."

Not only the Meyricks, whose varied knowledge had been acquired by the irregular foraging to which clever girls are often reduced, but Deronda himself, despite all his masculine instruction, had been awakened by this apparition of Mirah to the consciousness of knowing hardly anything about modern Judaism or its inner history. The Chosen People have commonly been treated as a people chosen for the sake of somebody else; and their thinking as something (whatever it may be) that ought to have been entirely otherwise. Deronda, like his neighbors, had regarded Judaism as a sort of eccentric, fossilized form which an accomplished man might dispense with studying and leave to specialists. But Mirah, with her terrified flight from one parent and her yearning for the other, flashed upon him the hitherto neglected reality that Judaism was something still throbbing in human lives, still providing them with the only conceivable vesture of the world. On the idle excursion he immediately undertook with Sir Hugo, he began to seek out the exteriors of synagogues and the titles of books concerning the Jews.

This awakening of a new interest—this transition from the supposition that we hold the right opinions on a subject we neglect, to a sudden care for it and a realization that our opinions amount to ignorance—is an effective remedy for ennui, which, unfortunately, cannot be secured by a physician's prescription. Yet Deronda had carried it with him, enduring his weeks of lounging all the better. It was during this journey that he first entered a Jewish synagogue—at Frankfurt, where his party rested on a Friday. Exploring the Juden-gasse, which he had seen long before, he recalled well enough its picturesque old houses; what his eyes chiefly dwelt upon now were the human types there. His thoughts, busily connecting them with the past phases of their race, stirred that fiber of historic sympathy which had helped to determine in him certain traits worth mentioning for those interested in his future.

True, when a young man possesses a fine person, no eccentricity of manners, the education of a gentleman, and a present income, it is not customary to feel a prying curiosity about his way of thinking or his peculiar tastes. He may very well settle into life as an agreeable, clever young fellow without passing a special examination on those heads. Later, when he becomes somewhat slovenly and portly, his peculiarities are more distinctly discerned, and it is taken as a mercy if they are not highly objectionable. But anyone wishing to understand the effect of later events on Deronda should know a little more of what he was at twenty-five than was evident in ordinary intercourse.

His early-woken sensibility and reflectiveness had developed into a many-sided sympathy, threatening to hinder any persistent course of action. As soon as he took up any antagonism, though only in thought, he seemed to himself like the Sabine warriors in the memorable story—with nothing to meet his spear but flesh of his flesh, and objects that he loved. His imagination had so wrought itself to the habit of seeing things as

"No man," declared a Rabbi, offering an undeniable example, "shall fashion the bones of his father and mother into spoons"—assured that his listeners understood the ethical barriers to such financial prudence. Yet the market for spoons had never widened sufficiently to prompt anyone to ask, "Why not?" or to claim that human advancement relies on repurposing human remains. The sole restraint was a sentiment, a principle that holds sway only over those who believe feelings constitute the true currency of the world.

Deronda, however, chose a less fashionable mode of exercise than riding along Rotten Row. He frequently wandered through the districts of London most densely populated by common Jews. He strolled to the synagogues during services, peered into shop windows, and studied the countenances of passersby—a pursuit unlikely to yield immediate results. Why, he mused, did he not seek out an influential Rabbi or a prominent community member to inquire about a mother named Cohen, a son named Ezra, and a lost daughter named Mirah? He considered approaching someone after Christmas. Yet, despite his appreciation for poetry in everyday details, Deronda found that when a sharp personal interest was ignited, he could not, any more than others, permanently shield himself from the crushing weight of the harsh, unyielding Reality. This Actual world never consults our tastes; it offers no selection. We know that enthusiasm finds comfort among abstract ideas, tolerating the smell of garlic from medieval times and ignoring the drab uniforms of classic processions. But when ideals confront us as warm, tangible flesh, enthusiasm grows squeamish and struggles to stand upright. Lying dreamily in a boat, imagining oneself searching for a maiden's kin in Cordova amidst Jews of Ibn-Gebirol's era, one bears all physical inconveniences without shock. Even if the grim scenes of St. Mary Axe and Whitechapel were magically transported to the Rhine's banks in the late eleventh century—where the cries of Crusaders sounded like bloodhounds' bays and devilish missionaries wielded swords and torches before the stoic, heroic figure of the reviled Jew—the squalor of dingy shops and plain faces would vanish beneath the thrill of contemplative emotion. Yet the fervor we feel for a grandiose martyrdom pales beside the enthusiasm that sustains itself where there is no danger, no challenge, only the impartial noon sun falling upon commonplace, perhaps slightly repulsive, objects that are truly beloved ideas made manifest. Here lies the essence of poetic power: the imagination's strength to pierce or elevate solid fact, rather than drifting among clouds. To glory in a prophetic vision of knowledge covering the globe is an easier exercise for the believing mind than witnessing its dawn in newspaper placards staring from a bridge beyond the cornfields. For those of us, delicate souls, it might well happen that we stood in the midst of Armageddon, unaware save for the annoyance of explosive smoke and bodies scrambling on the ground around us.

It was customary for Deronda to despise the weak, fastidious sympathy that recoils from the broad sweep of human life. But now, with Mirah standing before him as a living reality, whose fate he felt compelled to safeguard, he viewed every common Jew and Jewess through the lens of comparison with her. A presentiment troubled him: a collision between her idealized notion of an unknown mother and brother and the stark, discovered facts. This foreboding burned hotter within him because of a suppressed awareness that a similar, terrifying possibility of collision might be hidden within his own destiny. Did he not harbor a secret grievance against the way others had shaped his life, lacking the open daylight of full relations and the guiding clarity of primary duties that others possessed?

He did not eagerly seek to find any specific individual. When, as was his habit, he glanced at the sign above a shop door, he was relieved it did not read "Ezra Cohen." Indeed, he wished, almost desperately, that Ezra Cohen did not own a shop. Wishes are often deemed ominous; according to the superstitious view, if one harbors an impious objection to a squint, one's offspring is more likely to be born with one, and conversely, if one desires a squint, it shall not come to pass. This despondent calculus of probability is wholly rejected by the hopeful, who take their wishes as sufficient guarantees of fulfillment.

One morning, Deronda turned into a quiet side street to escape the noise and obstructions of Holborn, and the scales tipped toward the despondent view. He was weary of the streets and paused to hail a passing cab when his eye was caught by a pair of magnificent, antique clasps in chased silver displayed in a window to his right. His first thought was that Lady Mallinger, with her staunch Protestant taste for such Catholic relics, might wish to wear them as a bracelet. Then his gaze swept over the window's contents, revealing that this was a pawnbroker's establishment specializing in jewelry, lace, and all equivocal objects introduced as *bric-à-brac*. A placard in the corner announced: *Watches and Jewelry exchanged and repaired*.

His scrutiny had not gone unnoticed. A figure emerged from the doorway, regarding him with a tone of cordial encouragement. "Good day, sir," he said. In that fleeting instant, Deronda recognized the unmistakably Jewish face of a young man, roughly thirty, who winced under the shopkeeper's persuasive gaze. Before the persuasion could take root, Deronda stepped to the other side of the street and beckoned the cabman to draw up nearby. From there, he read the name above the window: *Ezra Cohen*.

There might be a hundred Ezra Cohens inscribed above shop fronts, yet Deronda had not encountered them. Presumably, the young man engaging a potential customer was Ezra himself, an age appropriate for Mirah's brother, who had grown to manhood while she remained a child. Yet, as he drove homeward, Deronda's first endeavor was to convince himself that there was no warrantable presumption that this Ezra was Mirah's brother. Next, he reasoned that even if, against all logic, he proved to be that brother, and inquiries revealed the mother to be deceased, it was not his duty to disclose this discovery to Mirah. An inconvenient thought disturbed this conclusion: Mirah would possess a religious longing to know of her mother's death and to ascertain whether her brother lived. How far was he justified in determining another's life by his own notions? Was it not his private complaint that others had arranged his life without open daylight, denying him the full guidance of primary duties that others enjoyed?

Immediate relief from this internal turmoil came from the reflection that he had made no real discovery yet. By investigating the facts more closely, he would surely certify that no decision was demanded of him. He intended to return to the shop soon to purchase the clasps for Lady Mallinger. However, he was hindered for several days by Sir Hugo, who, preparing for an after-dinner speech on a burning topic, required Deronda to research the legal aspects of the issue. Their daily arguments invariably ended in a drawn battle. On many subjects, they held opposing views, yet Sir Hugo cared little for this and, when Deronda articulated his point well, remarked with a mixture of satisfaction and regret:

"Confound it, Dan! Why don't you seize an opportunity to speak these things in public? You're wrong, you know. You won't succeed. You've got the massive sentiment—the heavy artillery of the country—against you. But it's excellent ground for a young man to display himself. When I was your age, I would have taken it. It would throw you into greater relief. If you seized such an occasion to make an impression, you might be in Parliament in no time. And you know that would gratify me."

"I am sorry not to do what would gratify you, sir," said Deronda. "But I cannot persuade myself to look at politics as a profession."

"Why not? If a man is not born into public life by his position in the country, there is no other path but to embrace it through his own efforts. The business of the nation must be conducted—Her Majesty's Government carried on, as the old Duke said. And it never could be, my boy, if everyone regarded politics as prophecy, demanding an inspired vocation. If you are to enter Parliament, you cannot sit still waiting for a call, either from heaven or constituents."

"I do not wish to make a living out of opinions," Deronda replied. "Especially out of borrowed opinions. Not that I intend to blame other men. I dare say many better fellows than I do not mind climbing onto a platform to praise themselves, and giving their word of honor for a party."

"I'll tell you what, Dan," said Sir Hugo, "a man who sets his face against every sort of humbug is simply a three-cornered, impracticable fellow. There is a bad style of humbug, but there is also a good style—one that oils the wheels and makes progress possible. If you are to rule men, you must rule them through their own ideas. I agree with the Archbishop at Naples who organized a St. Januarius procession against the plague. It is useless to have an Order in Council against popular shallowness. No action is possible without a little acting."

"One may be obliged to yield to occasional necessities," said Deronda. "But it is one thing to say, 'In this particular case, I am forced to put on this foolscap and grin,' and another to buy a pocket foolscap and practice grinning. I cannot see any real public expediency that does not maintain an ideal before it, establishing a limit of deviation from the direct path. But if I were to set

"His name is Israel.
A witch's spell has turned him into a dog.
*       *       *       *       *
But every Friday evening,
In twilight hour, suddenly
The magic fades, and the dog
Becomes anew a human being."
—HEINE: _Princess Sabbath_.

At five o'clock, the shop had already closed, and the Christian servant admitted Deronda. She led him into a chamber behind the counter, revealing a scene of unexpected charm. Though the house was old and the rear extension likely gloomy by day, the interior now glowed warmly under a magnificent antique brass lamp with seven oil-flames suspended above a table draped in snow-white linen. The air within was heavy with the smoke of age, rendering the walls and ceiling dim, yet this shadow played beautifully against the figures gathered there, giving them a luminous, Venetian quality.

The grandmother was dressed in shades of yellow-brown, adorned with a heavy gold chain serving as a necklace; beneath the soft light, her yellow complexion, framed by dark eyebrows and a roll of gray hair, possessed a picturesque beauty. Beside her sat young Mrs. Cohen in red and black, her throat encircled by a thick strand of large artificial pearls. Between them, a baby slept peacefully under a scarlet coverlet. Adelaide Rebekah wore braided amber silk, and Jacob Alexander sat in black velveteen with scarlet stockings. As the four pairs of dark eyes brightened with welcome, Deronda felt a sudden shame for the haughty disdain they had provoked in him during daylight hours. The atmosphere was impossibly cordial; both mother and grandmother seemed to derive a heightened sense of dignity from displaying such warm hospitality on their private hearth.

Deronda glanced around with wonder at the antiquated furnishings. An oak bureau and a high side-table stood nearby, appearing less like objects of taste and more like acquisitions born of necessity. On the side-table rested a large dish of blue and yellow ware, flanked by two tarnished silver vessels. Before them lay a massive volume bound in darkened vellum with a deeply ribbed spine. At the far end, an open door revealed a sliver of light from an inner room.

While absorbing these details through fleeting glances, Deronda focused his attention on Jacob's earnest concern regarding the knife. The boy had gone to some trouble to procure one featuring a hook and a white handle, producing it upon request.

"Is this the sort you wanted, Jacob?" Deronda asked.

The instrument underwent a rigorous inspection; the hooks and blades were tested, and the article intended for barter—a corkscrew—was produced for comparison.

"Why prefer a hook to a corkscrew?" Deronda queried.

"'Cause I can grab things with a hook," Jacob explained seriously. "A corkscrew won't fit anything but corks. But it works better for you, because you can pull corks."

"You agree to trade, then?" Deronda asked, noting the grandmother watching with delighted interest.

"What else do you have in your pockets?" Jacob pressed, his expression grave.

"Hush, Jacob, darling," the grandmother chided gently. Mindful of propriety, Deronda replied, "I suppose I cannot tell you that. Our business concerns the knives."

Jacob gazed up into his face, searching for answers, then, seemingly satisfied, declared solemnly, "I'll swap," passing the corkscrew-knife to Deronda, who tucked it away with equal gravity.

Instantly, the youngest son of Shem darted into the adjacent room, his voice rising in rapid chatter, before scambling back. Spotting his father, he snatched a small velvet hat from a chair and donned it to greet him. Mr. Cohen, however, retained his own hat, ignoring the gesture, and stood still as the two children climbed onto his knees. He laid his hands upon each, uttering a Hebrew benediction. Then, having recently lifted the sleeping infant from the cradle, Mrs. Cohen brought the child forward, holding it beneath her husband's outstretched hands to receive the blessing even in dreams. For a moment, Deronda perceived that this pawnbroker, proud of his calling, was not entirely devoid of spiritual depth.

"Well, sir, you found your welcome in my household, I trust," Cohen said, removing his hat and resuming his usual demeanor. "And you were punctual. There is nothing quite like a bit of pressure here," he added, tapping his side pocket as he sat. "It benefits us all in turns. I remember when I had to arrange payments; I began fitting boxes of every kind. It is invigorating to the mind. Now then! Let us see, let us see."

"That is the ring I mentioned," Deronda said, lifting it from his finger. "I believe it cost a hundred pounds. It should serve as ample security for fifty, I think. I expect to redeem it within a month."

Cohen's glistening eyes seemed to narrow slightly as he met the innocent gaze of this somewhat rough young gentleman, who apparently believed redemption offered a unique satisfaction to pawnbrokers. Taking the ring, he examined it closely before returning it with indifferent grace. "Good, good. We shall discuss it after our meal. Perhaps you will join us, if you have no objection. My wife and I shall feel honored, and so will Mother; do you, Mother?"

The invitation was enthusiastically echoed, and Deronda readily accepted. Everyone turned toward the table. Aside from a single dish concealed beneath a napkin, no food was visible, though Mrs. Cohen had placed a porcelain bowl near her husband for him to wash his hands. However, after putting on his hat again, Cohen paused, then called out loudly, "Mordecai!"

Could this be part of a religious rite? wondered Deronda, unsure what role the ancient hero might play. Yet he heard a "Yes" from the next room, prompting him to look toward the open door. To his astonishment, there stood the enigmatic Jew he had encountered that morning in the bookshop. Their eyes met, and Mordecai appeared as surprised as Deronda; neither offered any sign of recognition. But as Mordecai took his seat at the far end of the table, he merely bowed his head to the guest in a cold, distant manner, as if the disappointment of the morning lingered as an unpleasant association.

Now, Cohen washed his hands, reciting Hebrew words throughout the ritual. He removed the napkin to reveal two long, flat loaves sprinkled with seeds—the remembrance of the manna that sustained the wandering ancestors. Breaking off small portions, he distributed them to each member of the family, including Adelaide Rebekah, who stood on a chair, her entire length displayed in her amber gown, her little Jewish nose comically elongated by the compression of her lips in an attempt to maintain a proper countenance. Cohen then pronounced another Hebrew blessing. Following this, the men uncovered their heads, all seated themselves, and the meal commenced without any peculiarity that particularly engaged Deronda.

He was barely conscious of the dishes before him, preoccupied with a desire to steer the conversation toward a leading question, and constantly thinking of Mordecai, between whom and himself lay an exchange of fascinated, half-furtive glances. Mordecai lacked a handsome Sabbath garment; instead of the threadbare, rusty black coat worn that morning, he wore a loose coat of light drab, which appeared to have once been a handsome paletot now shrunk by frequent washing. This change in attire only accentuated his dark-haired, eager visage, which might have belonged to the prophet Ezekiel—itself likely unfashionable in contemporary eyes. It was noticeable that the thin tails of the fried fish were reserved for Mordecai, a share typically allotted to a poor relative, undoubtedly a "survival" of prehistoric custom, rarely acknowledged today as superstitious.

Mr. Cohen maintained the conversation with lively spirit, introducing subjects always treated with decorum (for the Jew takes pride in his loyalty), namely the Queen and the Royal Family, the Emperor and Empress of the French—topics into which both grandmother and wife entered with genuine zest. Young Mrs. Cohen demonstrated an accurate memory of distinguished birthdays, while the elder assisted her son in recounting events when the Imperial couple visited England and toured the city a decade prior.

"I dare say you know all about it better than we do, sir," Cohen repeated often, prefacing his full information. These interesting statements continued in a spirited trio.

"Our baby is named Eugenie Esther," announced young Mrs. Cohen vivaciously.

"It is wonderful how the Emperor resembles a cousin of mine in the face," remarked the grandmother. "It struck me like lightning when I first saw him. I never could have imagined it."

"Mother and I went to see the Emperor and Empress at the Crystal Palace," Mr. Cohen recounted. "I had a difficult task to ensure Mother's safety; she might have been crushed flat—though she was nearly as robust then as she is now. I said that if I had a hundred mothers, I would never take one of them to see the Emperor and Empress at the Crystal Palace again. You may think a man cannot afford it when he possesses but one mother—not even if he holds a vast insurance policy on her." He stroked his mother's shoulder affectionately, chuckling softly at his own wit.

"Your mother has been a widow for a long time, perhaps," Deronda observed, seizing an opportunity. "That has made your care for her all the more essential."

"Ay, ay, it has been many *yore-zeit* since I managed for her and myself,"

The history of mankind, if guilt were weighed solely by the scale of the sin committed, would surely reveal a different order. The architects of greedy wars and the ruthless predators of finance markets would not march in separate ranks from the petty thief and the butcher who slaughters with his own hands. They would all gather in a single procession of self-flagellating penitents. While wickedness does dispense rewards, the victors in this devilish game inevitably prove themselves baser, crueller, and brutish than the fabric of our world permits for the vast majority of humanity. Most humans carry a conscience—a shadow of justice born of fear, and a shadow of love born of pity—that prevents them from achieving the serenity of total depravity, a state too fragile to sustain itself within the complex nature of flesh.

On the twenty-ninth of December, Daniel Deronda knew the Grandcourts had arrived at the Abbey, yet he had not caught a glimpse of them until he retired to dress for dinner. A magnificent snowfall had blanketed the grounds, offering the children a rare joy in snowball fights and castle building. During the Christmas holidays, the Mallinger girls were content with no amusement that "Cousin" Deronda did not join and manage. Following this outdoor exertion, he had played billiards, and the hours slipped by without him dwelling on the prospect of meeting Gwendolen at dinner. Yet, the anticipation remained. As he retired to his room, slightly tired and flushed, he speculated on the influence her marriage to Grandcourt would exert on her. He wondered if there would be discernible shifts in her demeanor compared to her time at Diplow or her first vision at Leubronn.

"I fancy there are some natures one could see growing or degenerating every day, if one watched them closely," he mused. "Some move faster than others. I am sure she retains strong traces of whatever once impressed her deeply. That little affair with the necklace, the scandal regarding her gambling, had clearly bitten into her soul. But such impressibility cuts both ways; it may drive a person to desperation as swiftly as to virtue. Whatever fascinations Grandcourt holds for her capricious tastes—who can believe he possesses the capacity to summon tender affection in daily companionship? One might be tempted to strike him with a whip just to provoke a shred of passion from his face. I fear she married him out of ambition, to escape poverty. But why did she initially avoid him? The poverty came later, though. Poor thing! She may have been urged into it. How can one feel anything but pity for a young creature, full of unused life, ignorantly rash, hanging all her blind expectations on what remains of a human being?"

Deronda acknowledged that applying these meditations to the groom inevitably cast pity onto the bride. His notion of Grandcourt as a "remnant" sprang not from specific knowledge, but from the impression of ordinary polite intercourse that Grandcourt had worn out all natural interest in life. In marriages of consequence, male acquaintances tend to pity the bride, assuming she could do better, while females pity the bridegroom, viewing his situation as retributive justice. Perhaps Deronda was excused for withholding pity for Grandcourt, who had never struck acquaintances as a man likely to suffer more than he inflicted. Conversely, Gwendolen—youthful, headlong, eager for pleasure, fed by flattery that convinced her of a divine right to rule—risked a swift turn from expectancy to a bitter realization of the irremediable. After what he had witnessed, he must have had rather dull feelings not to look forward with interest to her entrance. However, with the honeymoon three weeks past and Gwendolen already enthroned at Ryelands and Diplow, she was likely to compose her countenance, masking her feelings behind a suitable facade.

A various party had gathered in the drawing-room before the new couple appeared. The aristocracy was represented by Lord and Lady Pentreath; the old gentry by the young Mr. and Mrs. Fitzadam; politics and the cider interest by Mr. Fenn and his daughters; Lady Mallinger's family by her brother, Mr. Raymond, and his wife; and the useful bachelor element by Mr. Sinker and Mr. Vandernoodt, whom Sir Hugo had found pleasant at Leubronn. Time passed chiefly in observing the children—various little Raymonds and Lady Mallinger's three girls. The scene was delightful, framed by full-length portraits and illuminated by the red fire of oak-boughs and pale wax-lights, stilled by deep carpets and high English breeding. Lady Mallinger, with her fair matronly roundness and mildly prominent blue eyes, moved about in black velvet, carrying a tiny white dog. The children were scattered among the ladies, while the gentlemen stood aloof, conversing with moderate vivacity. Deronda stood somewhat apart in a dialogue fixed upon him by Mr. Vandernoodt, a man of Dutch blood who was industrious in gathering personal details but silent on philosophy.

"Men who have seen a good deal of life don't always choose their wives well," Mr. Vandernoodt remarked, implying he had learned much about Grandcourt since Leubronn. "He has had rather an anecdotic history... gone deep into pleasures."

"No, really," Deronda replied in an indifferent tone. "I know little more of him than that he is Sir Hugo's nephew."

Just then, the door opened. The scene set off any figure of distinction that entered, and certainly, when Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt stepped in, their figures commanded attention. The bridegroom wore an easy perfection of costume, his face an impassible mask. It was to be expected that he would accept nothing less than the best in outward equipment, including his wife. The bride was what he might have been expected to choose. "By George, I think she's handsomer, if anything!" said Mr. Vandernoodt. Deronda agreed silently. White silk and diamonds flashed around her neck, ears, and hair, enhancing her new imposingness. Some faces of peculiar beauty are like original artworks, always greeted with questions. At Diplow, Deronda had discerned a tender, appealing charm in Gwendolen, a womanly allure beyond expectation. Was there any new change? Distrusting his impressions, he noted her receiving greetings with a proud, cold quietude and a superficial smile. Within her, the same demonic force seemed to work that had possessed her when she took his resolute glance at the gaming table and turned away a loser. There was no time for further conclusion before the summons to dinner.

Seated not far from her, Deronda could sometimes hear her answers to Sir Hugo, who was at his liveliest. Though he intended to bow, she gave him no opportunity for some time. Finally, Sir Hugo, seemingly unaware they had not spoken, addressed him: "Deronda, you will like to hear what Mrs. Grandcourt tells me about your favorite Klesmer."

Gwendolen's eyelids had lowered. Deronda, already looking at her, thought he discovered a quivering reluctance as she raised them to return his unembarrassed bow and smile, hers being merely a smile of the lip. Sir Hugo continued promptly, "The Arrowpoints have condoned the marriage, and he is spending the Christmas with his bride at Quetcham."

"I suppose he will be glad of it for the sake of his wife, else I dare say he would not have minded keeping at a distance," Deronda said.

"It's a sort of troubadour story," said Lady Pentreath, an easy, deep-voiced old lady. "I'm glad to find a little romance left among us. I think our young people now are getting too worldly wise."

"It shows the Arrowpoints' good sense to adopt the affair after the fuss in the papers," Sir Hugo agreed. "Disowning your own child because of a *mésalliance* is something like disowning your one eye: everybody knows it's yours, and you have no other to make an appearance with."

"As to *mésalliance*, there's no blood on any side," Lady Pentreath countered. "Old Admiral Arrowpoint was one of Nelson's men, a doctor's son. And we all know how the mother's money came."

"If they were any *mésalliance*, I should say it was on Klesmer's side," Deronda interjected.

"Ah, you think it is a case of the immortal marrying the mortal. What is your opinion?" Sir Hugo asked, looking at Gwendolen.

"I have no doubt that Herr Klesmer thinks himself immortal. But I dare say his wife will burn as much incense before him as he requires," Gwendolen replied, recovering her composure.

"Don't you approve of a wife burning incense before her husband?" Sir Hugo asked with jocoseness.

"Oh, yes," said Gwendolen, "if it were only to make others believe in him." She paused, then added with more gaiety, "When Herr Klesmer admires his own genius, it will take off some of the absurdity if his wife says Amen."

"Klesmer is no favorite of yours, I see," Sir Hugo noted.

"I think very highly of him, I assure you," Gwendolen insisted. "His genius is quite above my judgment, and I know him to be exceedingly generous." She spoke with the sudden seriousness often meant to correct an indiscreet sally, harboring a bitterness against Klesmer in her secret soul that she knew she could not justify. Deronda wondered what he would have thought of her if he had never heard of her before; probably that she put on hardness and
