"Nothing weighs so heavily as a secret;
To carry it far is difficult for ladies;
And I know, indeed, upon this fact,
Good many men who are women."

Meanwhile, Deronda had been whisked away by Mr. Vandernoodt, who desired a brisker pace, a cigar, and a morsel of gossip. Since a man cannot fully confess his own secrets to another, the restraint of keeping them often breeds a desire to pair off in conversation with someone less informed. Mr. Vandernoodt, therefore, launched into the subject with characteristic ease.

"What a washed-out piece of cambric Grandcourt is!" the older gentleman remarked, eyeing the groom of the chambers. "But if he is a favorite of yours, I withdraw the remark."

"Not the least in the world," Deronda replied.

"I thought not. One wonders how he came to reignite such a great passion, and he must have had—to marry in this way. Though Lush, his old chum, hints that he married this girl out of obstinacy. By George! it was a very accountable obstinacy. A man might make up his mind to marry her without the stimulus of contradiction. But he must have made himself a pretty large drain of money, eh?"

"I know nothing of his affairs."

"What! Not of the other establishment he keeps up?"

"Diplow? Of course. He took that of Sir Hugo. But merely for the year."

"No, no; not Diplow: Gadsmere. Sir Hugo knows, I'll answer for it."

Deronda remained silent. He felt a stirring of curiosity, yet he foresaw that he would hear what Mr. Vandernoodt had to tell without the condescension of asking.

"Lush would not altogether own to it, of course. He's a confident and go-between of Grandcourt's. But I have it on the best authority. The fact is, there's another lady with four children at Gadsmere. She has had the upper hand of him these ten years and more, and by what I can understand has it still—left her husband for him, and used to travel with him everywhere. Her husband's dead now; I found a fellow who was in the same regiment with him, and knew this Mrs. Glasher before she took wing. A fiery dark-eyed woman—a noted beauty at that time—he thought she was dead. They say she has Grandcourt under her thumb still, and it's a wonder he didn't marry her, for there's a very fine boy, and I understand Grandcourt can do absolutely as he pleases with the estates. Lush told me as much as that."

"What right had he to marry this girl?" Deronda asked, his voice tinged with disgust.

Mr. Vandernoodt, adjusting the end of his cigar and shrugging his shoulders, offered a nonchalant smile.

"She can know nothing of it," Deronda stated emphatically. Yet, immediately following this positive declaration, an inward query arose: *Could she have known anything of it?*

"It's rather a piquant picture," said Mr. Vandernoodt. "Grandcourt between two fiery women. For depend upon it this light-haired one has plenty of devil in her. I formed that opinion of her at Leubronn. It's a sort of Medea and Creüsa business. Fancy the two meeting! Grandcourt is a new kind of Jason: I wonder what sort of a part he'll make of it. It's a dog's part at best. I think I hear Ristori now, saying, 'Jasone! Jasone!' These fine women generally get hold of a stick."

"Grandcourt can bite, I fancy," said Deronda. "He is no stick."

"No, no; I meant Jason. I can't quite make out Grandcourt. But he's a keen fellow enough—uncommonly well built too. And if he comes into all this property, the estates will bear dividing. This girl, whose friends had come to beggary, I understand, may think herself lucky to get him. I don't want to be hard on a man because he gets involved in an affair of that sort. But he might make himself more agreeable. I was telling him a capital story last night, and he got up and walked away in the middle. I felt inclined to kick him. Do you suppose that is inattention or insolence, now?"

"Oh, a mixture. He generally observes the forms: but he doesn't listen much," Deronda replied. Then, after a moment's pause, he ventured, "I should think there must be some exaggeration or inaccuracy in what you have heard about this lady at Gadsmere."

"Not a bit, depend upon it; it has all lain snug of late years. People have forgotten all about it. But there the nest is, and the birds are in it. And I know Grandcourt goes there. I have good evidence that he goes there. However, that's nobody's business but his own. The affair has sunk below the surface."

"I wonder you could have learned so much about it," Deronda said, rather drily.

"Oh, there are plenty of people who knew all about it; but such stories get packed away like old letters. They interest me. I like to know the manners of my time—contemporary gossip, not antediluvian. These Dryasdust fellows get a reputation by raking up some small scandal about Semiramis or Nitocris, and then we have a thousand and one poems written upon it by all the warblers big and little. But I don't care a straw about the faux pas of the mummies. You do, though. You are one of the historical men—more interested in a lady when she's got a rag face and skeleton toes peeping out. Does that flatter your imagination?"

"Well, if she had any woes in her love, one has the satisfaction of knowing that she's well out of them."

"Ah, you are thinking of the Medea, I see."

Deronda then chose to point to some giant oaks worth looking at in their bareness. He also felt an interest in this piece of contemporary gossip, but he was satisfied that Mr. Vandernoodt had no more to tell about it.

Since the early days when he tried to construct the hidden story of his own birth, his mind had perhaps never been so active in weaving probabilities about any private affair as it had now begun to be about Gwendolen's marriage. This unavowed relation of Grandcourt's—could she have gained some knowledge of it, which caused her to shrink from the match—a shrinking finally overcome by the urgency of poverty? He could recall almost every word she had said to him, and in certain of these words he seemed to discern that she was conscious of having done some wrong—inflicted some injury. His own acute experience made him alive to the form of injury which might affect the unavowed children and their mother. Was Mrs. Grandcourt, under all her determined show of satisfaction, gnawed by a double, a treble-headed grief—self-reproach, disappointment, jealousy? He dwelt especially on all the slight signs of self-reproach: he was inclined to judge her tenderly, to excuse, to pity. He thought he had found a key now by which to interpret her more clearly: what magnifying of her misery might not a young creature get into who had wedded her fresh hopes to old secrets! He thought he saw clearly enough now why Sir Hugo had never dropped any hint of this affair to him; and immediately the image of this Mrs. Glasher became painfully associated with his own hidden birth. Gwendolen knowing of that woman and her children, marrying Grandcourt, and showing herself contented, would have been among the most repulsive of beings to him; but Gwendolen tasting the bitterness of remorse for having contributed to their injury was brought very near to his fellow-feeling. If it were so, she had got to a common plane of understanding with him on some difficulties of life which a woman is rarely able to judge of with any justice or generosity; for, according to precedent, Gwendolen's view of her position might easily have been no other than that her husband's marriage with her was his entrance on the path of virtue, while Mrs. Glasher represented his forsaken sin. And Deronda had naturally some resentment on behalf of the Hagars and Ishmaels.

Undeniably Deronda's growing solicitude about Gwendolen depended chiefly on her peculiar manner toward him; and I suppose neither man nor woman would be the better for an utter insensibility to such appeals. One sign that his interest in her had changed its footing was that he dismissed any caution against her being a coquette setting snares to involve him in a vulgar flirtation, and determined that he would not again evade any opportunity of talking to her. He had shaken off Mr. Vandernoodt, and got into a solitary corner in the twilight; but half an hour was long enough to think of those possibilities in Gwendolen's position and state of mind; and on forming the determination not to avoid her, he remembered that she was likely to be at tea with the other ladies in the drawing-room. The conjecture was true; for Gwendolen, after resolving not to go down again for the next four hours, began to feel, at the end of one, that in shutting herself up she missed all chances of seeing and hearing, and that her visit would only last two days more. She adjusted herself, put on her little air of self-possession, and going down, made herself resolutely agreeable. Only ladies were assembled, and Lady Pent

"Pardon, my lord—I speak for Sigismund."

"For him?" cried Fronsberg, his countenance lighting with a mischievous glint. "Ay, for him I always hold a pardon safe in bank, sure he will draw sooner or later on me. What is his need? Some mad project broken? Fine mechanic wings that would not fly? Durance, assault on watch, a bill for Epernay, not a crust to eat?"

"Oh, none of these, my lord," Aspern replied smoothly. "He has escaped from Circe's herd and seeks to win the love of your fair ward, Cecilia. But he would win first your consent."

"You frown," observed Aspern.

"Distinguish words," Fronsberg retorted sharply. "I said I held a *pardon*, not *consent*."

***

In spite of Deronda's urgent reasons for wishing to return to town—motives inextricably blended with his deepening anxiety for Mirah and a burning curiosity regarding the enigmatic Mordecai—he did not manage to ascend before Sir Hugo, who preceded his family to ensure readiness for Parliament's opening on the sixth of February. Instead, Deronda took up his quarters in Park Lane, fully aware that his chambers were sufficiently tenanted by Hans Meyrick. This arrangement, he had anticipated, yet he discovered circumstances not altogether according to his expectations.

Most of us recall Retzsch's drawing of Destiny in the guise of Mephistopheles playing chess with Man for his soul, wherein the clever adversary feints unintended moves to lure the beguiled mortal away from the true point of attack. The Fiend mocks preparation above all, fatally persuading us to forego waterproofs when the sky is destined to clear, foreseeing that the imbecile will transform this delusion into a prejudice against rain-coats rather than studying weather signs. It is a peculiar test of a man's mettle when, after painfully adjusting to what seems a wise provision, he finds all his mental precautions slightly astray, his excellent intentions reduced to miscalculated dovetails cut from a wrong starting-point. His magnanimity prepares to meet misbehavior, only to find a quite different summons. Something akin to this befell Deronda.

His first impression was one of pure pleasure and amusement at discovering his sitting-room transformed into an *atelier*. It was strewn with miscellaneous drawings and the contents of two chests brought from Rome; the lower half of the windows were darkened with baize, and the blonde Hans, in his weird youth, presided over the littered space as its genius. Hans's hair was longer than of old, his face more whimsically creased, and his high voice rose higher under the excitement of rapid talk. The friendship between the two had been maintained warmly since their memorable days at Cambridge, sustained not merely by correspondence but by little episodes of companionship abroad and in England. The original relation of confidence on one side and indulgence on the other had, as is wont to be the case where such spiritual borrowing begins well, been further developed in practice.

"I knew you would like to see my casts and antiquities," Hans announced after the first hearty greetings and inquiries, "so I didn't scruple to unload my chests here. But I've found two rooms at Chelsea, not many hundred yards from my mother and sisters, and I shall soon be ready to hang out there—once they've scraped the walls and put in some new lights. That is all I'm waiting for. But you see, I don't wait to begin work: you can't conceive what a great fellow I'm going to be. The seed of immortality has sprouted within me."

"Only a fungoid growth, I dare say—a growing disease in the lungs," Deronda remarked, treating Hans with his customary brotherly fashion. He walked toward some drawings propped on the ledge of his bookcases: five rapidly sketched heads, different aspects of the same face. He stood at a convenient distance, making no immediate remark. Hans, equally silent for a minute, took up his palette and began touching the picture on his easel.

"What do you think of them?" Hans asked finally.

"The full face looks too massive; otherwise, the likenesses are good," Deronda said, more coldly than was usual with him.

"No, it is not too massive," Hans decided. "I have noted that. There is always a little surprise when one passes from the profile to the full face. But I shall enlarge her scale for *Berenice*. I am making a *Berenice* series—look at the sketches along there—and now, thinking of it, you are just the model I want for the *Agrippa*." Hans, still holding pencil and palette, had moved to Deronda's side as he spoke, but he added hastily, as if conscious of a mistake, "No, no, I forgot; you don't like sitting for your portrait, confound you! However, I've picked up a capital *Titus*. There are to be five in the series. The first is *Berenice* clasping the knees of Gessius Florus and beseeching him to spare her people; I've got that on the easel. Then, this, where she stands on the Xystus with *Agrippa*, entreating the people not to injure themselves by resistance."

"*Agrippa*'s legs will never do," Deronda interjected.

"The legs are good realistically," Hans replied, his face creasing drolly. "Public men are often shaky about the legs—'Their legs, the emblem of their various thought,' as somebody says in the *Rehearsal*."

"But these are as impossible as the legs of Raphael's *Alcibiades*," Deronda countered.

"Then they are good ideally," Hans insisted. "*Agrippa*'s legs were possibly bad; I idealize that and make them impossibly bad. Art, my Eugenius, must intensify. But never mind the legs now: the third sketch in the series is *Berenice* exulting in the prospects of being Empress of Rome, when the news has come that Vespasian is declared Emperor and her lover *Titus* his successor."

"You must put a scroll in her mouth, else people will not understand that. You can't tell that in a picture."

"It will make them feel their ignorance then—an excellent aesthetic effect. The fourth is *Titus* sending *Berenice* away from Rome after she has shared his palace for ten years—both reluctant, both sad—*invitus invitam*, as Suetonius hath it. I've found a model for the Roman brute."

"Shall you make *Berenice* look fifty? She must have been that."

"No, no; a few mature touches to show the lapse of time. Dark-eyed beauty wears well, hers particularly. But now, here is the fifth: *Berenice* seated lonely on the ruins of Jerusalem. That is pure imagination. That is what ought to have been—perhaps was. Now, see how I tell a pathetic negative. Nobody knows what became of her—that is finely indicated by the series coming to a close. There is no sixth picture." Hans pretended to speak with a gasping sense of sublimity, drawing back his head with a frown as if seeking a like impression on Deronda. "I break off in the Homeric style. The story is chipped off, so to speak, and passes with a ragged edge into nothing—*le néant*; can anything be more sublime, especially in French? The vulgar would desire to see her corpse and burial—perhaps her will read and her linen distributed. But now, come and look at this on the easel. I have made some way there."

"That beseeching attitude is really good," Deronda said after a moment's contemplation. "You have been very industrious in the Christmas holidays; for I suppose you have taken up the subject since you came to London." Neither of them had yet mentioned Mirah.

"No," Hans said, applying touches to his picture, "I made up my mind to the subject before. I take that lucky chance for an augury that I am going to burst on the world as a great painter. I saw a splendid woman in the Trastevere—the grandest women there are half Jewesses—and she set me hunting for a fine situation of a Jewess at Rome. Like other men of vast learning, I ended by taking what lay on the surface. I'll show you a sketch of the Trasteverina's head when I can lay my hands on it."

"I should think she would be a more suitable model for *Berenice*," Deronda said, struggling to express his discontent.

"Not a bit of it. The model ought to be the most beautiful Jewess in the world, and I have found her."

"Have you made yourself sure that she would like to figure in that character? I should think no woman would be more abhorrent to her. Does she quite know what you are doing?"

"Certainly. I got her to throw herself precisely into this attitude. Little mother sat for Gessius Florus, and Mirah clasped her knees." Hans stepped back to consider the effect of his touches.

"I dare say she knows nothing about *Berenice*'s history," Deronda said, feeling more indignation than he could justify.

"Oh, yes, she does—the ladies' edition. *Berenice* was a fervid patriot, but was beguiled by love and ambition into attaching herself to the arch-enemy of her people. Whence the Nem

There are those who believe that the deepest tragedy lies not in Prometheus bound *after* he has stolen the celestial fire, but *before*. Before he secures the torch in his staff to bring it to mortals, he is thrust by the brute forces of rigid systems into a solitude of despised ideas. Fastened by the crushing weight of poverty and sickness, he exists in a lonely realm where many pass by, yet none truly look.

"Second sight" is a banner flying over contested terrain. Yet, it is undeniable knowledge that some souls possess yearnings and visions that materialize as images with prophetic power. To them, the deeds they intend to perform rise before them in complete form, casting a coercive shadow; the events they crave or fear grow rapidly from a tiny seed, fed by countless impressions. These individuals are not necessarily less logical or saner than the ordinary calculators of commerce; indeed, their natures may harbor manifold openings, like the hundred gates of Thebes, allowing a greater and more varied influx than through a single, guarded door. Of course, there exist abject specimens of the visionary, as there is a tiny mammal you might trap inside the finger of your glove. This small relative of the elephant causes no harm, but what great mental or social archetype remains entirely free from specimens whose insignificance is both ugly and harmful? One hesitates to consider everything the word "patriot" encompasses, or the jostling that might occur at the final judgment for those who claimed authorship, carrying volumes in their hands or on carts.

This defense of inevitable kinship serves to introduce facts concerning Mordecai. A figure had bitten itself into Daniel Deronda's mind as a new, compelling question, though the interest felt was nothing more than a vague, expectant suspension. The consumptive-looking Jew, seemingly a fervid scholar earning his bread through quiet handicraft, fit into none of Deronda's anticipations.

For Mordecai, however, the effect of their meeting was profound. For many winters, as his physical life ebbed and spiritual loneliness widened, his entire passion had concentrated on finding a young ear into which he could pour his mind as a testament. He sought a soul kindred enough to accept the spiritual product of his brief, painful life as a mission to be executed. Remarkably, the hopefulness that often serves as a beneficent illusion for consumptive patients was in Mordecai wholly diverted from the prospect of bodily recovery. Instead, it flowed entirely into this yearning for transmission. The desperate panting of his desire, rising from overwhelming discouragement, had hardened into a hope, which in turn became a confident belief. Rather than checking his resolve, the clear conception of his hastening decline intensified his expectant faith in a prophecy destined to fulfill itself in a brief span.

Several years had passed since Mordecai first began measuring men with a keen, searching glance, looking for a possibility that gradually sharpened into a distinct concept. At first, this distinctness was achieved chiefly through contrast: he sought a man who differed from himself. Tracing the reasons for his rebuffs and the hindrances that beset him, he imagined a counterpart who would possess all the elements necessary for sympathy but embodied them differently. This man must be a Jew, intellectually cultured and morally fervid—a nature ready to be filled from Mordecai's—but his face and frame must be beautiful and strong. He must be accustomed to the refinements of social life, his voice flowing with a full and easy current, his circumstances free from sordid need. He must glorify the possibilities of the Jew, not sit and wonder as Mordecai did, bearing the stamp of his people amidst the signs of poverty and failing breath. Sensitive to physical characteristics, Mordecai, both abroad and in England, studied pictures as well as people. In idle hours, he lingered in the National Gallery seeking paintings that might feed his hopefulness with grave and noble types of the human form, suitable to men of his own race. Yet, he returned in disappointment. The instances scattered thinly across European galleries, where the fortune or selection of the chief masters had gifted art with a face at once young, grand, and beautiful, failed to meet his standard. If any melancholy existed in these portraits, it was not a feeble passivity, but entered into a foreshadowed capability of heroism.

Observant persons may recall his emaciated figure and dark eyes deep in their sockets as he stood before a picture that touched him to new or habitual meditation. He commonly wore a cloth cap with black fur around it, which no painter would have asked him to remove. Spectators likely viewed him as an odd-looking Jew, perhaps extracting money from the gallery, and Mordecai, when he looked at them, was perfectly aware of the impression he made. Experience had rendered him morbidly alive to the effect of poverty and other physical disadvantages in cheapening ideas, unless those ideas were akin to Peter the Hermit's alarm bells for the rabble. However, he was too sane and generous to attribute his spiritual banishment solely to the excusable prejudices of others; certain incapacities of his own had sealed the sentence of exclusion. Hence, his imagination constructed another man—one who would be something more ample than the "second soul" bestowed, according to Cabalist notions, to aid the insufficient first. This imagined man would be a blooming human life, ready to incorporate all that was worthiest in an existence whose visible, palpable part was burning itself fast away. His inward need for this conception of an expanded, prolonged self was reflected as an outward necessity. The thoughts of his heart, that ancient phrase best shadows the truth, seemed too precious, too closely interwoven with the growth of things to lack a further destiny. As the more beautiful, stronger, and more executive self took shape in his mind, he loved it beforehand with an affection half-identifying, half-contemplative and grateful.

Mordecai's mind worked so constantly in images that his coherent trains of thought often resembled the significant dreams attributed to sleepers by waking persons in their most inventive moments. Nay, they often resembled genuine dreams in their way of breaking off the passage from the known to the unknown. For a long while, he habitually thought of the Being answering to his need as one distantly approaching or turning his back, darkly painted against a golden sky. The reason for the golden sky lay in one of Mordecai's habits. He was keenly alive to some poetic aspects of London, and a favorite resort, when strength and leisure allowed, was some bridge, especially about sunrise or sunset. Even when bending over watch-wheels and trinkets or seated in a small upper room looking out on dingy bricks and cracked windows, his imagination spontaneously planted him on a spot with a far-stretching scene. His thoughts traveled in wide spaces, and whenever possible, he tried to experience the influences of a large sky. Leaning on the parapet of Blackfriars' Bridge and gazing meditatively, the breadth and calm of the river, with its long vista half-hazy, half-luminous, along with the grand, dim masses of tall buildings marking world-commerce, and the oncoming of boats and barges from the distant silence into sound and color, entered his mood. They blended indistinguishably with his thinking, acting like a fine symphony that bears up our spiritual wings. Thus, the figure representing Mordecai's longing was mentally seen darkened by the excess of light in the aerial background. But in the inevitable progress of his imagination toward fuller detail, he ceased to see the figure with its back turned. It began to advance, and a face became discernible. The attributes of youth, beauty, refinement, Jewish birth, and noble gravity turned into hardly individual but typical form and color, gathered from his memories of faces seen among the Jews of Holland and Bohemia, and from the paintings that revived those memories.

Reverently, it must be said of this mature spiritual need that it was akin to the boy's and girl's picturing of the future beloved. Yet, the stirrings of such young desire are feeble compared with the passionate current of an ideal life straining to embody itself, made intense by resistance to imminent dissolution. The visionary form became a companion and auditor, keeping a place not only in the waking imagination but in those lighter dreams of slumber where it is truest to say, "I sleep, but my heart waketh"—when the disturbing, trivial story of yesterday is charged with the impassioned purpose of years.

Of late, the urgency of irremediable time, measured by the gradual choking of life, had turned Mordecai's trust into an agitated watch for a fulfillment that must be at hand. Was the bell on the verge of tolling, the sentence about to be executed? The Deliverer's footsteps must be near—the Deliverer who was to rescue Mordecai's spiritual travail from oblivion and grant it an abiding-place in the best heritage of his people. An insane exaggeration of his own value, even if his ideas were as true and precious as those of Columbus or Newton, many would count this yearning as the sublimer part for a man to declare: "If not I, then another," holding cheap the meaning of his own life. But the fuller nature desires to be an agent, to create, not merely to look on. Strong love hungers to bless, not merely to behold blessing. While there is warmth enough in the sun to feed an energetic life, there will still be men who feel, "I am lord of this moment's change, and will charge it with my soul."

Yet, with that mingling of inconsequence which belongs to us all, and not unhappily, since it saves us from many effects of mistake, Mordecai's confidence in the friend to

Before venturing into the circle of the initiated, safety lies in every case; if you struggle long enough, he will surely find where you falter. Yet hope for applause remains, for he knows precisely where you have struck the mark.

—Goethe, *West-Eastern Divan*

Significant shifts in Daniel Deronda's fortunes began that same evening following his visit to Chelsea regarding Mirah's public identity. However, for the Meyrick household, the true turning point arrived two days later. At four o'clock, the familiar clatter of wheels halted before the door, followed by a knock and ring that resonated with the weight of social ceremony in a neighborhood usually animated only by the muffin man. Inside, the girls were assembled; the two rooms had been merged to accommodate Kate's latest drawing and a sprawling piece of embroidery that had displaced the satin cushions, serving as a centerpiece for needlework sessions. Mrs. Meyrick leaned over one corner of this tapestry, Mab occupied the center, and Amy worked at the far end. Mirah, whose stitching skills resembled those of a tailor-bird building a nest, acted as the reader. Seated on a camp-stool, she simultaneously served as Kate's model for a title-page vignette depicting a fair public engrossed in the family's literary offerings. As she read Charles Lamb's "Essay on Chimney-Sweepers" with charming distinctness, the room filled with smiles over the "innocent blackness" until the imposing knock shattered the atmosphere.

"My goodness!" exclaimed Mrs. Meyrick. "Is it Lady Mallinger? Does a grand carriage await?"

"No, merely a hansom cab," Kate replied dryly. "Hans insists that the greatest man in London can fit into one."

"Oh, perhaps it is Lord Russell!" cried Mab.

Five pairs of bright eyes turned toward the parlor door, where the old maid-servant emerged carrying a card, leaving it slightly ajar. There stepped a figure markedly unlike the dignified Prime Minister—tall, physically imposing, dressed in kid gloves and kerseymere, with a massive face, flamboyant hair, and gold spectacles. As Mrs. Meyrick glanced at the card, the visitor was revealed: Julius Klesmer.

Even embarrassment could scarcely have rendered the "little mother" so flustered; perceptive as she was, she instantly grasped the situation. She felt a surge of satisfaction that this towering figure had sought her out rather than summoning Mirah to him, interpreting it as a sign of profound, active interest. Yet, the moment Klesmer entered, the cozy rooms seemed to contract into closets. To Mab, the cottage piano became a ridiculous toy, and the entire domestic existence felt as trivial and private as a mouse colony in the Tuileries. Klesmer's persona, particularly his sweeping glance around the room, evoked vast horizons and a multitude of unseen audiences. Perhaps this was indeed the landscape of his consciousness, for everyone carries their thoughts within a habitual locus populated by the presence of others, and those who perceive on a broader scale often appear vain and affected. Klesmer was certainly vain, yet no more so than many heavy-set contemporaries whose vanity springs forth startlingly, like a spear from a cane. His carriage and gestures were as natural to him as the length of his fingers; the most affectation he could display would likely be feigned diffidence. While his grandiose manner made Mab feel like a doll beside the miniature piano, he observed the surroundings with a keen, thoroughly kind sensitivity. Having known a home no larger than this on the outskirts of Bohemia, and having experienced the romance and variety inherent in small incomes, he approached Mrs. Meyrick with utmost deference.

"I hope I have not intruded too freely. Being in the neighborhood, I ventured to save time by calling. My friend, Mr. Deronda, mentioned that I should have the honor of meeting a young lady here—Miss Lapidoth."

Klesmer had indeed spotted Mirah instantly, yet with subtle politeness, he circled the room, bowing at the three sisters as if unsure which one was the intended guest.

"These are my daughters," Mrs. Meyrick indicated, waving her hand toward Mirah. "And this is Miss Lapidoth."

"Ah," Klesmer murmured, his voice brimming with gratified anticipation. He turned a radiant smile and a deep bow toward Mirah. Unlike the others, she was not taken aback; a calm pleasure softened her features. She liked the look in his eyes, sensing that he would treat her with the stern kindness of a great musician.

"You will not mind if we begin our acquaintance with a song?" he asked, aware that preliminaries could be dispensed with quickly.

"I shall be delighted. It is generous of you to listen," Mirah replied, moving toward the piano. "Shall I play my own accompaniment?"

"Certainly," Klesmer agreed, settling himself where Mrs. Meyrick had invited him, ensuring a clear view of the singer. The astute mother did not acknowledge her own vulnerability in wishing to be seen, yet she thought to herself, *"He will appreciate her singing far more if he watches her perform."*

While all the feminine hearts save Mirah's beat rapidly with anxiety, viewing Klesmer as a terrifying judge, Mirah felt strangely at ease. She chose a fine setting of Leopardi's *Ode to Italy*. First came the recitative, *"O patria mia, vedo le mura..."*, followed by the mournful melody *"Ma la gloria... non vedo"*, leading to a climax of devout triumph. From the subdued adoration of the Andante—"Beatissimi voi, che offriste il petto alle nemiche lance"—she moved to the joyful outburst of an exultant Allegro: *"Oh viva, oh viva!"*

When she concluded, Klesmer waited a moment before remarking, "That is Old Leo's music."

"Yes, he was my last master at Vienna. So fierce and so good," Mirah answered with a melancholy smile. "He prophesied that my voice would never suit the stage. And he was right."

"*Con*tinue, if you please," Klesmer urged, puckering his lips and shaking his long fingers, though his articulation remained unintelligible to the listeners.

The three girls detested him unanimously for withholding praise. Mrs. Meyrick grew alarmed. Determined to fulfill Klesmer's request, Mirah imagined he wished to hear German, and she proceeded through Prince Radзивill's arrangements of Gretchen's songs from *Faust*, one after another, without waiting for interrogation. Upon finishing, Klesmer rose and paced to the far end of the room before returning to the piano. Mirah stood near it, hands clasped, awaiting judgment. Suddenly, his brow unknitted, his eyes beamed, and he extended his hand. "Let us shake hands," he declared abruptly. "You are a musician."

Mab felt tears rising, and the girls held Klesmer in awe. Mrs. Meyrick drew a long breath. But instantly, the frown returned. The long hand, palm upturned, reached out again, this time with a fingertip gently touching the back of Mirah's hand, accompanied by a protruded lip. "Not for great tasks. No high roofs. We are no skylarks. We must be modest." He paused. And Mab's adoration vanished; it was as if Mirah had displayed the slightest hint of conceit!

Mirah fell silent, sensing a verdict awaited. Klesmer continued, "I would not advise—and I repeat, I would not encourage you to sing in any space larger than a private drawing-room. But you will excel there. Here in London, that is one of the finest careers available. Lessons will follow. Will you join me for a private concert at my house on Wednesday?"

"Oh, I shall be so grateful," Mirah said, pressing her hands together devoutly. "I would prefer earning my living in that way to anything more public. I will strive to improve. What should I focus on most?"

Klesmer offered a preliminary response in sounds that seemed like words bitten short and swallowed, shaking his fingers rhythmically, before speaking clearly: "I shall introduce you to Astorga. He is the foster-father of good singing and will guide you." Turning to Mrs. Meyrick, he added, "Mrs. Klesmer will call before Wednesday, with your permission."

"We shall consider that a great kindness," Mrs. Meyrick responded.

"You will sing to her," Klesmer instructed, turning back to Mirah. "She is a thorough musician, possessing a soul with more ears than you will often encounter in a professional. Your singing will satisfy her." Quoting Goethe again, he intoned, "'Vor den Wissenden sichstellen.' Do you know the rest?"

"'Sicher ist's in allen Fällen,'" Mirah recited promptly. Klesmer nodded approvingly, "Schön!" and extended his hand once more for farewell.

He had certainly chosen the most delicate method of praise, and the Meyrick sisters now bestowed their highest esteem upon him. Yet, imagine Mab's shock when Klesmer suddenly fixed his gaze upon her and decided, "That young lady is musical, I see!" A flush of burning shame swept over her.

"Yes," Mirah spoke for her. "And she has a touch."

"Oh, please, Mirah—it's a scramble, not a touch!" Mab cried in anguish, terrified that this terrible diviner, seemingly Satan in gray trousers, might command her to sit at the piano. Her heart melted like wax. This discomfort proved

"The soul possesses a faculty that, through interpositions capable of veiling and obscuring, transforms shadows into contingencies of grandeur; thus, it serves to heighten its innate radiance. Like the vast moon, rising behind a dense, towering forest in the deep stillness of a summer eve, it burns within the green canopy like an unconsumed fire, kindling the surrounding foliage and turning the dark veil into a substance as glorious as itself, wholly incorporated by a power both capacious and serene."
— WORDSWORTH: *Excursion*, Book IV.

Daniel Deronda emerged from the cramped dwelling at Chelsea in a frame of mind that craved vigorous physical exertion to dispel what he privately termed the "fumes" of his irritation. Heading toward the city, the sight of the Chelsea Stairs and the idling boats instantly resolved his dilemma: he would avoid the stifling inertia of a carriage, opting instead to hire a wherry and row himself.

His mission was twofold. First, he intended to visit Ram's bookshop, where he had arrived too late the previous day to catch Mordecai during his midday vigil, having been informed that the scholar invariably returned between five and six. Deronda deeply desired further acquaintance with this enigmatic inhabitant of the Cohens' household, viewing it as a prerequisite for redeeming his stolen ring. He dreaded that their conversations would repeatedly terminate swiftly with that specific, fleeting spark of Mordecai's interest, akin to a drawbridge retracting to cut off easy communication forever. As he warmed to the rhythm of the oar, focusing his mind on this errand and the goals he pursued regarding Mirah, he experienced a familiar, rapid shift in his perspective. He mentally shifted his viewpoint from seeing Mordecai merely as a useful instrument for his own designs to considering the other man's humanity, chiding himself for resembling an enlistment sergeant who secures recruits without caring for the drama that brings them.

"I suppose, if I obtain the information I most urgently seek from this man," Deronda mused, "I would be content even if he showed no inclination to reveal more of himself, or explain why he seems to harbor expectations of me that remain unfulfilled. Such curiosity would eventually fade; yet, perhaps, we have approached and parted like two ships, each carrying an exile who would recognize the other if they could look face-to-face. While there is little chance of a peculiar tie between me and this poor fellow, whose journey, I suspect, is nearly concluded, I wonder if there exists any significant mutual absence between people who exchange blank glances, or even long for one another's departure in a crowded space. Nevertheless, one often creates opportunities for missing by adopting the recruiting sergeant's strategy."

As the wherry neared Blackfriars Bridge, where Deronda planned to disembark, the clock struck half-past four. The grey day was dying in glorious fashion, its western clouds shattered into narrowing purple strata before a wide, spreading saffron clarity. Above, the sky held a monumental calm, but upon the river, it manifested as luminous movement—the alternating flash of ripples and currents, the sudden glow of a brown sail, the passage of laden barges shifting from blackness into color. These elements formed an active response to that brooding splendor.

Feeling thoroughly warmed, Deronda set down the oar and draped his Inverness cape over himself. As he raised his head to fasten the final button, his eyes caught a familiar face gazing down from the bridge parapet. Illuminated by the western light into startling distinctness and brilliance, it was an illuminated type of bodily emaciation and spiritual urgency. It was Mordecai. Having spotted the approaching boat while watching westward, Mordecai had kept his gaze fixed upon it, first simply noting its approach, then recovering impressions that caused him to shiver with a presentiment. Finally, as the nearing figure lifted its face toward him—the face of his visions—he immediately beckoned with an uplifted white hand, again and again.

Anxious that Mordecai should recognize and await him, Deronda had not delayed in signaling, and the answer came instantly. Mordecai lifted his cap and waved it, feeling in that instant that his inward prophecy was fulfilled. Obstacles, incongruities, and all barriers melted into the sense of completion flooding his soul with outward satisfaction. His exultation mirrored that of an experimenter witnessing the first stirrings of change corresponding to what concentrated prevision had foreshadowed. The prefigured friend had emerged from the golden background to signal him; this was actuality; the remainder was destiny.

Within three minutes, Deronda had landed, paid the boatman, and joined Mordecai, whose instinct was to stand perfectly still and wait.

"I was very glad to see you standing here," said Deronda, "for I intended to proceed to the bookshop to find you again. I was there yesterday—perhaps they mentioned it?"

"Yes," said Mordecai, with simple gravity, "that was the reason I came to the bridge."

To Deronda, this answer was startlingly mysterious. Were the peculiarities of this man truly associated with some form of mental alienation, as Cohen had hinted?

"You knew nothing of my presence at Chelsea?" Deronda asked after a moment.

"No," Mordecai replied. "But I expected you to come down the river. I have been waiting for you these five years." Mordecai's deep-set eyes were fixed upon Deronda's with a look of affectionate dependence, at once pathetic and solemn. Deronda's sensitivity responded not less intensely because he could not but believe this strangely disclosed relation rested on an illusion.

"It will be a satisfaction to me if I can be of any real use to you," Deronda answered earnestly. "Shall we take a cab and drive to—whichever you wish? You have likely walked enough with your short breath."

"Let us go to the bookshop. It will soon be time for me to be there. But now, look upriver," Mordecai said, turning back toward the water and speaking in undertones of what might be called an excited calm—so absorbed by a sense of fulfillment that he felt no barrier to a complete understanding between them. "Observe the sky; it fades slowly. I have always loved this bridge; I stood upon it when I was a child. It is a meeting-place for spiritual messengers. It is true, as the Masters said, that each order of things has its angel: meaning the full message of each from afar. Here, I have listened to the messages of earth and sky. When I was stronger, I used to linger and watch for the stars in the deep heavens. Yet, this time, just about sunset was what I loved best. It sank into me and dwelled with me—fading, slowly fading: it was my own decline. It paused, it waited, until finally it brought me my new life, my new self, who shall live when this breath is all spent."

Deronda remained silent. He felt strangely wrought upon. The first-prompted suspicion that Mordecai might be prone to hallucinations, or perhaps a monomaniac obsessed with some subject that had strained his diseased organism too severely, yielded to a more submissive expectancy. His nature was too broad, too ready to conceive realms beyond his own experience, to settle immediately on the easy explanation of "madness" whenever a consciousness displayed such fullness and conviction where his own was blank. It accorded with his habitual disposition to meet rather than resist claims arising from another's need; and this claim brought with it a sense of solemnity, appearing as a radiation from Mordecai that utterly nullified his outward poverty, lifting him into authority as if he were that preternatural guide seen in universal legends, who suddenly drops his mean disguise to stand as manifest Power. This impression was sanctioned by a sort of resolved quietude produced in Mordecai's manner by the persuasion of fulfillment. After a moment of silence, Mordecai said, "Let us go now."

Once in the cab, Mordecai added, "We will alight at the end of the street and walk to the shop. You may examine the books, and Mr. Ram will be departing shortly, leaving us alone."

It seemed this enthusiast was just as cautious, just as alert to judgments in other minds, as if he were the antipode of all enthusiasm, known as "a man of the world."

As they rattled along, Mirah was still present with Deronda in the midst of this strange experience, though he foresaw that the course of conversation would be dictated by Mordecai, not by himself. He was no longer confident of the questions he could pose. With a reaction on his own mood, he thought inwardly, "I suppose I am in a state of complete superstition, as if awaiting a destiny that could interpret the oracle. Yet, some strong relation must exist between me and this man, since he feels it so acutely. Great heaven! What relation has proven more potent in the world than faith, even when mistaken? Than expectation, even when perpetually disappointed? Will my side of the relation prove disappointing or fulfilling? Well, if it is ever possible for me to fulfill, I will not disappoint."

In ten minutes, the two men, possessing an intensity of consciousness as if they were two undeclared lovers, found themselves alone in the small, gas-lit bookshop and turned face to face. Each bare his head from an instinctive feeling that they wished to see the other fully. Mordecai stepped forward to lean his back against the little counter, while Deronda stood against the opposite wall, scarcely four feet away.

*I wish I could perpetuate

"It is a part of probability that many improbable things will happen." So Aristotle spoke in the *Poetics*, and though the words were ancient, they echoed sharply in Daniel Deronda's mind on the evening following his strange encounter with Mordecai. To a youth of ordinary temperament, such an adventure—a sudden collision with a fanatic Jew on a London bridge—might have served merely as a fleeting diversion, a curious episode to be filed away and forgotten. But Deronda was not of dull susceptibility. The event had struck him with a violence that demanded immediate scrutiny. As the usual reaction of his intellect set in, he began to dissect the roots of his turmoil, wrestling with the question of whether he must yield to the currents Mordecai had set in motion.

There was a distinct alarm within him. He felt, with a sharp pang, that he was being half-dominated by Mordecai's energetic certainty, and even more so by the fervent, almost desperate trust the old man placed in him. This consciousness roused his defenses. It was his nature to recoil from the "moral stupidity" of undervaluing what stood close at hand, blind to the monumental crises unfolding in his own life that history would later recognize as sacred. If he had read of this scene as a historical account from Rome, Greece, or Palestine, involving a young man dissatisfied with neutrality and seeking a deeper purpose, it would have appeared entirely natural for that figure to be profoundly moved. Why, then, should he feel shame for his own agitation simply because he dressed for dinner, wore a white tie, and inhabited a circle where possessing a serious conscience was often mocked as the solemn folly of taking oneself too seriously? That bugbear of the fashionable world—the lack of grave emotion passing for wit—made him shrink. Yet, he also feared allowing his course to be dictated by mere contagion, without the consent of his own reason, or letting a reverential pity for spiritual struggle sweep him along a dimly lit path before he understood where it led.

"What, after all, had really happened?" he asked himself.

He knew quite accurately what Sir Hugo would have answered: A consumptive Jew, driven mad by obstacles and hastening death, had latched onto Deronda as the living embodiment of some visionary image born of wedded hope and despair. Despair over his own failing life, coupled with an irrepressible hope in spreading his fanatical beliefs. The instance might appear odd or exceptional in form, but substantially, it was not rare. Fanaticism was not uncommon, though perhaps less visible than bankruptcy; it was abundant enough. While Mordecai waited on the bridge for the fulfillment of his visions, countless other men were convinced they held the mathematical key to the universe to supersede Newton, believing all physicists conspired to silence them; others claimed the metaphysical key, differing from the old wards by only a hair's breadth. Scattered everywhere, one might find a "terrible person"—some with a glittering eye, others with a preternaturally dull gaze—seeking the man who must hear them. Most possessed volumes difficult to print, or impossible to read. Mordecai, however, possessed a more pathetic aspect, a more passionate and penetrative speech than the average monomaniac. He was more poetical than a social reformer drawing diagrams of a new moral world, or an enthusiast preaching about sewage systems. Still, he fell under the same class. A man of the world would know what to think beforehand: to indulge him a little, perhaps, offering practical comfort, but regarding the likelihood of his notions holding the value he ascribed to them as nil. And concerning Mordecai's conviction that he had found a new executive self, it might well be preparing for him the worst of disappointments—that final, crushing disillusionment.

Deronda's ear caught these negative whispers, and he repeated them distinctly to himself. It was not the first time he had faced this question of the "family likeness" among the heirs of enthusiasm, whether prophets or dreamers, from the first believer in his own unmanifested inspiration down to the last inventor of a perpetual motion machine. The kinship of human passion, the sameness of mortal scenery, inevitably filled fact with burlesque and parody. Error and folly had their hecatombs of martyrs. If one reduced the grandest type of man known to history to an abstract statement of his qualities and efforts, he appeared in dangerous company: like Copernicus and Galileo, immovably convinced in the face of hissing incredulity. But so was the contriver of perpetual motion. One could not fairly test spirits by this sort of trial. If the goal was to avoid administering hemlock or sentences of banishment in the wrong case, nothing sufficed but a capacity to understand the subject-matter on which the immovable man was convinced, and a fellowship with human travail, both near and afar, to prevent scanning any deep experience lightly. Should we say, "Let the ages try the spirits"? Alas, we are the beginning of the ages, and justice depends solely on just judgments formed in separate human breasts—separate yet combined. Even steam-engines could not have been made without this condition; they must have remained in the mind of James Watt.

This train of thought was familiar enough to Deronda to save him from any contemptuous prejudgment of Mordecai, even if their communication had been free from that peculiar claim strangely ushered in by some long-growing preparation in the Jew's agitated mind. Considered rationally, this claim might seem dismissible as illusory and preposterous; but it was precisely what turned Mordecai's hold on him from an appeal to easy sympathy into a clutch on his struggling conscience. Consciences are not all cast in the same mold; they are the voice of sensibilities as various as our memories. And Deronda's conscience included sensibilities beyond the common, enlarged by his early habit of imagining himself into the experiences of others.

What was the claim this eager soul made upon him? "You must believe my beliefs—be moved by my reasons—hope my hopes—see the vision I point to!" To take such a demand as a direct obligation would have been preposterous, bordering on dishonesty. Deronda, looking back at the agitation of those moments, felt thankful that amidst his compassion he had preserved himself from the bondage of false concessions. The claim, moreover, hung on a supposition that might well be in discordance with the full fact: the supposition that Deronda himself was of Jewish blood. Was there ever a more hypothetical appeal?

Yet, since the age of thirteen, Deronda had associated the deepest experiences of his affections with what was a pure supposition: that Sir Hugo was his father. That hypothesis had been the source of passionate struggle within him; by its light, he had learned to subdue feelings and to cherish them. He was well accustomed to finding a motive in a conception that might be disproved, and to anticipating revelations that would alter his view of duty. To exist in a state of suspense, which was also a state of emotive activity and scruple, was a familiar attitude of his conscience.

Now, suppose that wish-begotten belief in his Jewish birth, and that extravagant demand for discipleship, were the foreshadowing of an actual discovery and a genuine spiritual result. Suppose Mordecai's ideas made a real conquest over Deronda's conviction? Nay, it was conceivable that as Mordecai needed and believed this, he had found an active replenishment of himself, so Deronda might receive from Mordecai's mind the complete ideal shape of that personal duty and citizenship which lay in his own thought like sculptured fragments, certifying some beauty yearned after but not traceable by divination.

As this possibility presented itself in his meditations, he was aware it would be called "dreamy," and he began to defend it. If the influence he imagined submitting to had been that of some honored professor, some authority in a seat of learning, some philosopher accepted as a voice of the age, would thorough receptiveness toward direction have been ridiculed? Only by those who hold it a sign of weakness to be obliged for an idea, preferring to hint that they have implicitly held in a more correct form whatever others have stated with sad shortcoming. After all, what was there but vulgarity in taking the fact that Mordecai was a poor Jewish workman, perhaps to be met on a sanded floor in the parlor of the *Hand and Banner*, as a reason for deciding beforehand that no spiritual force within him could determine a white-handed gentleman? There was a legend of Emperor Domitian, who, hearing of a Jewish family of the house of David from whom the ruler of the world was to spring, sent for them in alarm but quickly released them upon observing they had the hands of workmen. Being of the opposite opinion to the Rabbi who stood waiting at the gate of Rome, confident the Messiah would be found among the destitute. Both Emperor and Rabbi were wrong in trusting outward signs: poverty and poor clothes are no sign of inspiration, said Deronda to his inward objector, yet they have accompanied it in remarkable cases. To regard discipleship as out of the question because of these trappings would be mere dullness of imagination.

A more plausible reason for dismissing discipleship was the strain of visionary excitement in Mordecai, which turned his wishes into overmastering impressions, making him read outward facts as fulfillments. Was such a temper of mind likely to accompany the wise estimate of consequences, the only safeguard from fatal error, even for an ennobling motive? But it remained to be seen whether that rare conjunction existed in Mordecai: perhaps his nature was one where a wise estimate of consequences was fused in the fires of passionate belief, thereby determining the consequences he believed in. The

If there are hierarchies of suffering, the Jewish people occupy the summit; if endurance elevates the spirit, they stand alongside the nobility of every land. Consider literature: if a few classical tragedies constitute richness, what of a national tragedy sustained for fifteen hundred years, performed by the heroes themselves? This reflection, drawn from Zunz, lingered in Daniel Deronda's mind as he approached the Cohen household. To him, the Cohens presented a jarring contrast to that sublime archetype of the Jewish martyr. Ezra Cohen, a pawnbroker, wore no pallid vestments of penitence, nor did he embody the tragic weight of his ancestry. Instead, he was animated by a voracious appetite for profit, a trait that seemed to defy the historical stoicism of his race. Yet, as Deronda watched, he sensed a deeper irony: nestled within this "self-gratulating" prosperity was Mordecai, a fragile vessel of the national conscience, struggling to breathe amidst the mundane.

When Deronda returned, the gloom lifted instantly. The Cohens' faces glowed with a radiant, almost theatrical joy. Ezra declared that the diamond ring, however lucrative, mattered less than the sheer delight of the women and children in welcoming a young gentleman whose visit had become the topic of endless chatter. Young Mrs. Cohen expressed regret that the infant was asleep but delighted that Adelaide remained awake, urging Deronda to bypass the shop entirely and proceed directly to the parlors to meet "mother and the children." Deronda accepted, carrying a set of paper figures for Adelaide and an ivory cup and ball for Jacob.

Inside, the grandmother presided over a game of "plates," tossing cards that miraculously remained whole mid-air. Jacob, eager to impress, ran to Deronda, begging him not to step on his card before watching it return to perfection. The game eventually collapsed into fragments, and Deronda sat down, noting quietly that the door leading to Mordecai's workroom was now sealed. He wished to establish a rapport with the family before revealing his urgent concern for their troubled lodger.

Only after settling Adelaide on his knees and arranging her toys did Deronda ask, "Is Mordecai in?"

Ezra Cohen, seizing a brief lull in business, explained the situation. Mordecai had recently been "a little touched," lacking the sharp rationality of the others, yet industrious and devoted to the boy. Lately, however, he had moved like a somnambulist or sat statue-still, staring into space. The grandmother whispered gently of illness, fearing he could not withstand it much longer, while the younger Mrs. Cohen attributed his condition to a preoccupation with writing, noting his delayed responses. Despite acknowledging their own limitations, Ezra insisted on their principle: they would never abandon Mordecai, regardless of his burden, distinguishing themselves from those who profited from ignorance. "Mordecai carries a blessing inside him," the grandmother affirmed, a sentiment Jacob quickly corrected, citing Mordecai's refusal to eat a bun.

Deronda, however, felt a resonance with Jacob's concern. He revealed that he had visited Mordecai at Ram's bookshop and promised to return, an assurance that seemed to stir something within the lodger. As Jacob offered to fetch Mordecai, his mother silenced him, asking only for the door to be opened quietly. Jacob obeyed with practiced stealth, and Deronda followed him into the dim workroom, lit by a dying fire and a single shaded candle. Jewelry and books cluttered the boards, but Mordecai sat alone at a high chair, hands clasped over a watch on a stand, vibrating with the agonizing suspense of a prisoner awaiting release.

"I am come for you. Are you ready?"

Mordecai turned instantly, grabbing his fur cap and joining Deronda. As they emerged into the sitting room, Jacob attempted to cheer him with a toss of the ivory ball, which elicited a startled smile and a "Fine, fine!" from Mordecai. Young Mrs. Cohen retrieved his greatcoat, and Ezra, returning to an undertone, admitted he had predicted Mordecai's reaction.

Outside, Deronda remarked on the Cohens' apparent affection. Mordecai replied swiftly, "And I for them," describing them as possessing the heart of Israel despite lacking the understanding of the broader world. Apologizing for his delay, Deronda explained his previous absence. Mordecai, agitated by the stirring of his youth, described his physical weakness as a cage for a bird seeking flight, his joy threatening to shatter his frail frame. Deronda advised caution regarding the cold air, suggesting they proceed to *The Hand and Banner*, assuming privacy there. Mordecai hesitated, fearing the crowds of the club, but Deronda assured him he would accompany him.

They arrived at "The Philosophers," a modest gathering of impoverished men who met to discuss profound ideas. The room, barely fifteen feet square, was filled with smoke and the hazy glow of gaslights. Several men, shabbily dressed, listened intently to a speaker quoting Shelley. Upon entering, the attention shifted to Mordecai, who introduced Deronda as a traveler and student of their subjects.

"My name is Daniel Deronda," he announced simply, earning a murmur of approval. Seated opposite Mordecai, Deronda observed the group with quiet respect. Among them were Miller, a second-hand bookseller of German extraction; Buchan, a Scottish saddler; Pash, a vivacious Jewish watchmaker; and Gideon, a rational Jew who advocated for assimilation. The conversation, initially focused on the laws of progress and statistics, quickly turned to the nature of ideas and social change.

Miller argued that ideas acted as a parliament distinct from the "commonwealth" of material actions, while Goodwin countered that ideas gained power only when intimately mixed with the realities of life. Pash, with a mischievous grin, suggested that the idea of nationality was spreading like a scent among wild asses, a notion Mordecai gently refuted by emphasizing nationality as a feeling rather than a concept.

The debate deepened when Pash claimed that the nationalist sentiment was destined to die out in Europe, replaced by a universal progress. Buchan challenged the definition of progress itself, questioning whether change inherently implied betterment. Deronda intervened, arguing against the fatalism of accepting trends as inevitable, warning against mistaking dangerous tendencies for laws of nature.

Mordecai, however, seized the floor. He spoke of the "seed of fire" within the Jewish soul, a growth knit together by joy and sorrow, absorbing the thoughts of other nations while enriching the world. He painted a picture of a people whose spirit, though scattered and oppressed, refused to dry up. "Who shall say the fountain of their life is dried up?" he asked passionately. "Not he who feels the life of his people stirring within his own."

Gideon, the rational voice, pushed back, advocating for the shedding of superstition and exclusivity, suggesting that Jews should merge into the surrounding populations and adopt the maxim, "A man's country is where he's well off." Pash supported this, mocking the idea of a Jewish homeland as impractical. But Mordecai rose, his voice gaining a feverish brilliance. He rejected the notion of assimilation, declaring that to lose the specific "sap of special kindred" was to lose the essence of humanity.

"For the same reason that the cleverest men in the country don't get themselves into Parliament," Pash quipped, dismissing the Jewish contribution to the world. Mordecai countered, "Each nation has its own work... Israel is the heart of mankind." He described the history of his people not as a static relic but as a dynamic, living organism that had preserved its identity through millennia of persecution, turning scattered misery into a reservoir of wisdom.

"The night is unto them, that they have no vision," Mordecai proclaimed, his eyes burning with a hectic light. "But the sun is gone down over the prophets, and the day is dark above them." He envisioned a revival, a "living warmth" spreading to the weak extremities of the race, transforming superstition through the illumination of great facts. He spoke of a future where the Jewish spirit would manifest in a new order, a republic of justice and equality, serving as a neutral ground between East and West.

"And the world will gain as Israel gains," he concluded, his voice sinking to a whisper as exhaustion overtook him. "The vision is there; it will be fulfilled."

Silence fell over the room. Mordecai sank his chin onto his breast, his eyelids falling as if in a trance. The others, including Gideon and Pash, looked at him with a mixture of awe and unease. Deronda, however, felt a profound connection to the man's vision. He saw in Mordecai not merely a suffering individual, but a conduit for a vast, invisible current of hope and memory. The talk had ended, but the impact lingered, leaving the room empty save for the two friends, bound by a shared, silent understanding of the struggle and the dream.

"My spirit grows too thin for earth's heavy hold;
Like an eagle stricken, gazing at the sky,
I feel the weight of dying draw me nigh."

For several moments, the silence between them hung heavy and unbroken, a profound stillness that seemed to settle deep within Mordecai's consciousness. Finally, he raised his gaze to look at Deronda. There was no confusion, no startled surprise in his eyes, only a serene, resting satisfaction, as if a long journey had finally found its harbor. Deronda rose and shifted his chair closer, ensuring his voice would carry gently over the distance. To Mordecai, this simple act felt like the softness of a feather pillow easing aching bones.

"In the ancient teachings of the Kabbalah," Mordecai began, his voice a low murmur, speaking as much to himself as to Deronda, "souls are born anew, again and again, seeking perfection. When a soul is freed from a body grown weary, it joins the soul that needed it most, so they may complete their work together and ascend. Only then do they leave the mortal realm, making space for new souls to enter from the eternal store. It is the lingering imperfections of those still bound here that delay the coming of the Messiah; thus, the mind gives shape to what is hidden, casting shadows of truths long known. When my wandering soul is finally liberated from this tired vessel, it will join yours, and our shared work will be finished."

Mordecai fell silent, his words hanging in the air like a question. Deronda felt compelled to answer, driven by a deep, unspoken bond. "I promise you," Deronda said, his voice steady, "everything within my conscience allows me to do to make your life meaningful, I will do."

"I know," Mordecai replied, his tone calm and certain, needing no further reassurance. "I heard you. You see everything clearly—you stand beside me on the mountain of vision, showing me the paths others refuse to walk."

A moment passed, heavy with reflection, before Mordecai continued, his eyes distant. "You will take up where my life broke. I feel myself transported back to that terrible day when it shattered. The bright sun was shining on the quay at Trieste; the clothes of travelers from every nation glittered like gems, and the boats pushed out to sea. The Greek ship bound for Beirut was to depart in an hour. I was traveling with a merchant as his clerk, dreaming of vast horizons. 'I shall see the lands and peoples of the East,' I told myself, 'and speak with a fuller vision.' I breathed easily, unburdened, with the light step and enduring spirit of youth. I knew how to fast, how to sleep on the hard ground. I had married poverty, and I loved my bride—for to me, poverty was freedom. My heart swelled with a joy akin to that of Moses ben Maimon, strong and knowing the tasks ahead. It was the first time I had traveled south; the soul within me felt the return of a forgotten sun. Standing there on the quay, where the ground seemed to glow with light and shadows danced with an azure brilliance, spirits becoming visible, I felt swept into a flood of glorious life. My own short, numbered existence melted away, unnoticed, and a great sob rose within me, like rushing waters too strong to resist—a bliss overwhelming in its intensity. I waited for my companion, and saw him not until he spoke: 'Ezra, I have been to the post; here is your letter.'"

"Ezra!" Deronda gasped, unable to hold back his shock.

"Ezra," Mordecai repeated, lost in the vividness of the memory. "I was expecting a letter; I wrote constantly to my mother. That sound of my name was like a wand touching me, recalling me from the ocean of existence where I had briefly floated, free from the pressure of individual bonds. I opened the letter, and the name cried out again—a scream that would have disturbed me in heaven, driving me to the source of that sorrow. 'Ezra, my son!'"

Mordecai paused, his imagination frozen by the grip of that long-past moment. Deronda held his breath, waiting. A strange possibility had just unfolded. Mordecai's eyes dropped, lost in abstract thought, before rising again slowly.

"She was a mother deserving of blessing. In her, I understood the Master who, hearing his mother's footsteps, rose and declared, 'The Majesty of the Eternal draws near!' That letter was her cry from the depths of anguish and desolation—the scream of a mother robbed of her children. I was her eldest. Death had taken four babies one after another. Then came my little sister, late and beloved, the very desire of her eyes. The letter was a piercing cry: 'Ezra, my son, I am robbed of her. He has taken her away, leaving disgrace behind. They will never return.'"

Here, Mordecai lifted his eyes suddenly, laying his hand firmly on Deronda's arm. "Mine was the lot of Israel. For the father's sin, my soul must go into exile. For the father's sin, the work was broken, the day of fulfillment delayed. She who bore me was desolate, disgraced, destitute. I turned back. On that instant, her spirit and the spirit of her fathers, those with worthy Jewish hearts, moved within me, drawing me forward. God, who holds the universe, was within me as the strength of obedience. I turned and traveled with hardship—to save the scant money she would need. I left the sunshine and walked into freezing cold. In the final stage of my journey, I spent a night exposed to snow and bitter wind. And there, in that freezing darkness, began this slow death."

Mordecai let his gaze wander again, removing his hand. Deronda resolutely suppressed the storm of questions raging within him. While Mordecai was in this state of raw emotion, no other confession could be sought than what came spontaneously. Yet, Deronda himself felt a kindred emotion, dreading his own words as too momentous.

"But I worked. We were destitute; everything had been seized. And she was ill; the clutch of anguish was too strong, working with some lurking disease. At times, she could not stand for the pounding of her heart, and the images in her brain became chambers of terror, where she saw me rearing my sister in evil. In the dead of night, I heard her crying for her child. Then I rose, and we stretched forth our arms together and prayed. We poured our souls into a desperate desire that Mirah might be delivered from evil."

"Mirah?" Deronda repeated, wishing to ensure his ears had not betrayed him by a feverish imagination. "Did you say Mirah?"

"That was my little sister's name." After the prayers, his mother would rest awhile. It lasted hardly four years, and in the minute before she died, we prayed the same prayer—I aloud, she silently. Her soul flew out upon its wings."

"Have you never since heard of your sister?" Deronda asked, keeping his voice quiet.

"Never. Never have I heard if she was delivered as we prayed. I know not, I know not. Who shall say where the paths lie? The poisonous will of the wicked is strong. It poisoned my life—it is slowly stifling this breath. Death delivered my mother, and I felt it a blessing to be alone in those winter months of suffering. But what are the winters now? They are far off." Here, Mordecai again rested his hand on Deronda's arm, looking at him with a joy that pierced the heart with sadness—"there is nothing to wail in the withering of my body. The work will be the better done. Once I said the beginning of this work was mine, I am born to do it. Yes, I shall do it. I shall live in you. I shall live in you."

His grasp grew convulsive, and Deronda, agitated beyond measure, felt the certainty that this was Mirah's brother suffuse his own strange connection with Mordecai in a new solemnity and tenderness. His young heart beat faster, his lips turning pale. He shrank from speech. He feared that in Mordecai's current state of exaltation—an alarming strain on his fragile frame—he might utter a word of revelation about Mirah that would shatter the delicate balance. He feared to answer below the high pitch of expectation, which resembled a flash from a dying fire, causing watchers to fear it would fade all the quicker. His dominant impulse was to do as he had done before: he laid his firm, gentle hand over the hand that gripped him. Mordecai's hand, as if it had a soul of its own—for he was not distinctly willing to release it—relaxed under Deronda's pressure and turned upward. As the two palms met and pressed together, Mordecai regained some sense of his surroundings and whispered, "Let us go now. I cannot talk any longer."

And indeed, they parted at Cohen's door without another word, merely sharing one last, pressing of hands.

Deronda felt a weight upon him, half joy, half anxiety. The joy of discovering in Mirah's brother a nature even more worthy of her devotion carried the weight of solemnity and sadness; the reunion of brother and sister was, in reality, the first stage of a supreme parting—like a farewell kiss that resembles a greeting, or a last glance of love that becomes the sharpest pang of sorrow. There remained the heavy burden of anxiety regarding the revelation of this fact on both sides, and the careful arrangements necessary beforehand.

Fairy folk listening, hear the seed sprout in the spring;
For music to their dance, hear the hedgerows wake from trance;
Sap that trembles into buds sends little rhythmic floods
Of fairy sound in fairy ears.
Thus all beauty that appears has birth as sound to finer sense
And lighter-clad intelligence.

And Gwendolen? She was thinking of Deronda much more than he was thinking of her—often wondering what were his ideas "about things," and how his life was occupied. Yet a lap-dog would be necessarily at a loss in framing to itself the motives and adventures of doghood at large; and it was as far from Gwendolen's conception that Deronda's life could be determined by the historical destiny of the Jews, as that he could rise into the air on a brazen horse, and so vanish from her horizon in the form of a twinkling star.

With all the sense of inferiority that had been forced upon her, it was inevitable that she should imagine a larger place for herself in his thoughts than she actually possessed. They must be rather old and wise persons who are not apt to see their own anxiety or elation about themselves reflected in other minds; and Gwendolen, with her youth and inward solitude, may be excused for dwelling on signs of special interest in her shown by the one person who had impressed her with the feeling of submission, and for mistaking the color and proportion of those signs in the mind of Deronda.

Meanwhile, what would he tell her that she ought to do? "He said, I must get more interest in others, and more knowledge, and that I must care about the best things—but how am I to begin?" She wondered what books he would tell her to take up to her own room, and recalled the famous writers that she had either not looked into or had found the most unreadable, with a half-smiling wish that she could mischievously ask Deronda if they were not the books called "medicine for the mind." Then she repented of her sauciness, and when she was safe from observation carried up a miscellaneous selection—Descartes, Bacon, Locke, Butler, Burke, Guizot—knowing, as a clever young lady of education, that these authors were ornaments of mankind, feeling sure that Deronda had read them, and hoping that by dipping into them all in succession, with her rapid understanding she might get a point of view nearer to his level.

But it was astonishing how little time she found for these vast mental excursions. Constantly she had to be on the scene as Mrs. Grandcourt, and to feel herself watched in that part by the exacting eyes of a husband who had found a motive to exercise his tenacity—that of making his marriage answer all the ends he chose, and with the more completeness the more he discerned any opposing will in her. And she herself, whatever rebellion might be going on within her, could not have made up her mind to failure in her representation. No feeling had yet reconciled her for a moment to any act, word, or look that would be a confession to the world: and what she most dreaded in herself was any violent impulse that would make an involuntary confession: it was the will to be silent in every other direction that had thrown the more impetuosity into her confidences toward Deronda, to whom her thought continually turned as a help against herself. Her riding, her hunting, her visiting and receiving of visits, were all performed in a spirit of achievement which served instead of zest and young gladness, so that all around Diplow, in those weeks of the new year, Mrs. Grandcourt was regarded as wearing her honors with triumph.

"She disguises it under an air of taking everything as a matter of course," said Mrs. Arrowpoint. "A stranger might suppose that she had condescended rather than risen. I always noticed that doubleness in her."

To her mother most of all Gwendolen was bent on acting complete satisfaction, and poor Mrs. Davilow was so far deceived that she took the unexpected distance at which she was kept, in spite of what she felt to be Grandcourt's handsome behavior in providing for her, as a comparative indifference in her daughter, now that marriage had created new interests. To be fetched to lunch and then to dinner along with the Gascoignes, to be driven back soon after breakfast the next morning, and to have brief calls from Gwendolen in which her husband waited for her outside either on horseback or sitting in the carriage, was all the intercourse allowed to her mother.

The truth was, that the second time Gwendolen proposed to invite her mother with Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne, Grandcourt had at first been silent, and then drawled, "We can't be having *those people* always. Gascoigne talks too much. Country clergy are always bores—with their confounded fuss about everything."

That speech was full of foreboding for Gwendolen. To have her mother classed under "*those people*" was enough to confirm the previous dread of bringing her too near. Still, she could not give the true reasons—she could not say to her mother, "Mr. Grandcourt wants to recognize you as little as possible; and besides it is better you should not see much of my married life, else you might find out that I am miserable." So she waived as lightly as she could every allusion to the subject; and when Mrs. Davilow again hinted the possibility of her having a house close to Ryelands, Gwendolen said, "It would not be so nice for you as being near the rectory here, mamma. We shall perhaps be very little at Ryelands. You would miss my aunt and uncle."
