And all the while this contemptuous veto of her husband's on any intimacy with her family, making her proudly shrink from giving them the aspect of troublesome pensioners, was rousing more inward inclination toward them. She had never felt so kindly toward her uncle, so much disposed to look back on his cheerful, complacent activity and spirit of kind management, even when mistaken, as more of a comfort than the neutral loftiness which was every day chilling her. And here perhaps she was unconsciously finding some of that mental enlargement which it was hard to get from her occasional dashes into difficult authors, who instead of blending themselves with her daily agitations required her to dismiss them.

It was a delightful surprise one day when Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne were at Offendene to see Gwendolen ride up without her husband—with the groom only. All, including the four girls and Miss Merry, seated in the dining-room at lunch, could see the welcome approach; and even the elder ones were not without something of Isabel's romantic sense that the beautiful sister on the splendid chestnut, which held its head as if proud to bear her, was a sort of Harriet Byron or Miss Wardour reappearing out of her "happiness ever after."

Her uncle went to the door to give her his hand, and she sprang from her horse with an air of alacrity which might well encourage that notion of guaranteed happiness; for Gwendolen was particularly bent to-day on setting her mother's heart at rest, and her unusual sense of freedom in being able to make this visit alone enabled her to bear up under the pressure of painful facts which were urging themselves anew. The seven family kisses were not so tiresome as they used to be.

"Mr. Grandcourt is gone out, so I determined to fill up the time by coming to you, mamma," said Gwendolen, as she laid down her hat and seated herself next to her mother; and then looking at her with a playfully monitory air, "That is a punishment to you for not wearing better lace on your head. You didn't think I should come and detect you—you dreadfully careless-about-yourself mamma!" She gave a caressing touch to the dear head.

"Scold me, dear," said Mrs. Davilow, her delicate worn face flushing with delight. "But I wish there was something you could eat after your ride—instead of these scraps. Let Jocosa make you a cup of chocolate in your old way. You used to like that."

Miss Merry immediately rose and went out, though Gwendolen said, "Oh, no, a piece of bread, or one of those hard biscuits. I can't think about eating. I am come to say good-bye."

"What! going to Ryelands again?" said Mr. Gascoigne.

"No, we are going to town," said Gwendolen, beginning to break up a piece of bread, but putting no morsel into her mouth.

"It is rather early to go to town," said Mrs. Gascoigne, "and Mr. Grandcourt not in Parliament."

"Oh, there is only one more day's hunting to be had, and Henleigh has some business in town with lawyers, I think," said Gwendolen. "I am very glad. I shall like to go to town."

"You will see your house in Grosvenor Square," said Mrs. Davilow. She and the girls were devouring with their eyes every movement of their goddess, soon to vanish.

"Yes," said Gwendolen, in a tone of assent to the interest of that expectation. "And there is so much to be seen and done in town."

"I wish, my dear Gwendolen," said Mr. Gascoigne, in a kind of cordial advice, "that you would use your influence with Mr. Grandcourt to induce him to enter Parliament. A man of his position should make his weight felt in politics. The best judges are confident that the ministry will have to appeal to the country on this question of further Reform, and

A carriage, drawn by polished bays and attended by a powdered lackey, halted on the street. Inside, Lady Mallinger swept off her hat, stepping onto the crimson carpet with a determined grace, her sole purpose "to be there." Like Sirius rising silently above the darkened sea, she concealed her restless heart beneath a glittering exterior.

Soon enough, the Grandcourts arrived at Grosvenor Square, summoned by a card for the musical gathering. Sir Hugo knew beforehand that his ill-favored nephew, Daniel Deronda, would be attending, though he offered no warning. It had been barely three evenings since their arrival, and Gwendolen wandered through the unfamiliar grandeur of the room with a distracted air. Her thoughts were fixed on Deronda, and on Mirah Lapidoth, whom she recalled as someone capable of enduring "anything in the form of duty." Yet Gwendolen clung to a bitter memory of Deronda's words regarding Mirah. She replayed them in her mind, tasting their sting, aware with a vague, gnawing shame that her own submission to Sir Hugo was no act of duty at all. It was a yoke forced upon her by her own actions, worn with a heavy, selfish burden that left no room for virtue.

The drawing-room, bathed in shades of white, gold, and pale crimson, was sparsely populated until the Grandcourts entered. For over half an hour, the air had hummed with instrumental music, broken only by murmurs of conversation. Herr Klesmer stood at the piano, his wife beside him, intending to sing Leo's "O Patria Mia" accompanied by Mirah. Klesmer had urged Mirah to choose this piece for its distinctiveness, a choice that now placed her conspicuously in the center of attention. Just then, Gwendolen, radiant in pale green velvet and jewels that seemed poisoned by their cold brilliance, was ushered to a seat of honor directly in view of the performers. With her sharp eyes and iron self-control, she instantly surveyed the room, exchanging a swift, electric bow with Klesmer as she passed. A flash of lightning sparked within her mind, recalling that morning when she had dreamed of standing *above* the "little Jewess," surveying a vast audience from the heights of her own talent. Now, however, she was merely part of the crowd in silk and gems, whose highest achievement was to applaud or criticize. "He believes I am finally on the right path," whispered a resentful thought deep inside her.

Gwendolen had not yet spotted Deronda. As she settled into polite chatter with Sir Hugo, she scanned the room with practiced ease, bowing to acquaintances, terrified that any anxious, searching gaze aimed at finding Deronda would be noticed by her husband and labeled "damnably vulgar." Yet, the journey of a single eye around a crowded hall often leads to unwanted discoveries. Among those who met her gaze—and compelled a slight, automatic bow—were the eyes of Mr. Lush, the "amateur too fond of Meyerbeer," whom Sir Hugo currently deemed useful as a gentleman of mixed blood. He stood near her husband, who, however, turned a shoulder away to listen to Lord Pentreath. Suddenly, a disagreeable sensation pierced Gwendolen's composure: the realization that this man knew everything about her husband's life. She had successfully exiled him from her sight, satisfied by the silence surrounding him, yet here he stood, resurrected at her husband's elbow. An instantaneously forged memory, like a phantom in a dream, surged within her: the knowledge that he was connected to the secrets that made her miserable. She strained to turn her head away, feigning a wandering survey of the walls as if nothing mattered, until her eyes inevitably drifted toward Deronda. But he did not look at her. She pulled her gaze back, comforting herself with the lie that he must have seen her entrance. In truth, he stood near the door with Hans Meyrick, both men uneasy lest Mirah's performance suffer. Deronda, too, hovered on the brink of emotion; Mirah's presence triggered a flood of memories, all converging on the brother he was soon to reveal to her. He had slipped away from Lady Pentreath, whose violoncello voice had mocked Mirah's silence. "Well, your Jewess is pretty—there's no denying that," she had sneered. "But where is her Jewish impudence? She looks as demure as a nun. I suppose she learned that on the stage."

Deronda felt a surge of indignation, mirroring the seraphic protectiveness he once felt for himself. He winced at the casual dismissal of Mirah as an imported commodity, acutely aware that Mordecai would interpret the slur "Jewess" as a damning brand. As the Grandcourts entered, Hans immediately hailed Deronda about "that Vandyke duchess of a beauty." Deronda felt a sudden, renewed repulsion toward Gwendolen, viewing her beauty and flaws as complicit in Mirah's undervaluation—a sentiment akin to class hatred. To Hans's exaggerated admiration, Deronda replied with a sarcasm that lacked warmth: "I thought you could admire no style of woman but your Berenice." Hans, mistaking this for playful rivalry, declared, "That is the style I worship—not admire... Other styles I might make myself wicked for, but for Berenice I could make myself—pretty good." Deronda cut the conversation short, claiming the singing was about to begin, relieved when Hans moved away.

For the first time, Deronda heard Mirah sing "O Patria Mia." Knowing Leopardi's ode intimately, he felt the weight of the omitted lines echoing in his soul: *"Do none of thy children defend thee? Arms! bring me arms! Alone I will fight, alone I will fall."* These words vibrated like a heroic, tragic passion, the sound of a man dying helplessly far from the battlefield. Mirah sang with a purity that touched his heart. While the room erupted in applause, Klesmer leaned close to whisper, "Good, good—the crescendo better than before." But Mirah's true anxiety lay in satisfying Mr. Deronda. Every prospect she had was owed to him, and performing in his home demanded a special standard. She looked across the room, seeking his approval, but he remained distant, watching the stream of admirers close around her. Soon, the crowd parted to make way for Gwendolen, who was escorted by Mrs. Klesmer.

Now, Daniel softened toward Gwendolen. His fleeting harshness gave way to penitence; he recalled the many signs that she, too, needed a rescue, though hers seemed far more impossible than Mirah's. A silent question formed in his mind: *Is it not cowardly to use fear as an excuse for turning away?* This became his resolve to approach her the next moment, honoring her past trust despite Sir Hugo's subtle warnings.

As Gwendolen approached, Klesmer smiled, a look that dwelt in his eyes rather than on his lips, observing the striking contrast between the two young women on the red divan. Gwendolen expressed her gratitude for the treat of hearing Mirah sing, admitting her ignorance of such greatness. Mirah, her mind fixated on Gwendolen, regarded her with a vague awe, sensing these grand ladies emerged from some unknown, perhaps tragic, drama. "We shall all want to learn of you," Gwendolen said, glancing archly upward at Klesmer. "I sing very badly... I have been rebuked for not liking to be middling." She paused, looking at Klesmer, who quickly assured her that studying further would indeed be worthwhile, and that Mirah would be happy to assist. Before leaving, Mirah, taking everything with naive seriousness, promised, "If you think I could teach you, I shall be very glad. I am anxious to teach... remembering how my master taught me."

Gwendolen, usually so certain of herself, felt a flicker of uncertainty. Desperate to shift the subject, she asked if Mirah had been long in London and if she had known Deronda abroad. "No," Mirah replied simply, "I never saw him before I came to England in the summer." Gwendolen pressed, noting that Deronda had spoken of her with high praise, implying he knew her well. "Oh, I was poor and needed help," Mirah explained, her voice shifting to a tone of profound feeling. "Mr. Deronda has given me the best friends in the world. That is the only way he came to know anything about me—because he was sorry for me. I had no friends when I came. I owe everything to him."

Gwendolen, who had once wished to be a struggling artist herself, felt a sting of humiliation at the condescending pity directed at the "Jewess." To Mirah, however, the mention of Deronda stirred only reverential gratitude and a fierce desire to prove her debt. Both Deronda and Hans, watching from afar, would have been indignant if they knew Mirah portrayed herself in this light of neediness. Yet, in her movement, there lay an exquisite delicacy—a refusal to allow anyone to assume a relationship of equality or lesser generosity than actually existed. Her answer delighted Gwendolen, who saw only the compassion she had trusted in the past, unaware that this "protégé" would soon alter her life far beyond improving her singing.

Seeking solitude, Gwendolen moved from her original seat to a settee with only one neighbor, placing her closer to Deronda. Almost immediately, he approached to shake hands. As he stood by the empty end of the sofa, the swelling music of the orchestra seemed

"If anyone asks me why I loved him, I have no answer save to say, 'Because it was he, because it was I.' There exists a power beyond words that drew us together." —Montaigne, *On Friendship*.

The time had arrived for Mordecai to prepare for the revelation of his long-lost sister and the impending move that would bring Mirah to England. Mrs. Meyrick, to whom Daniel Deronda had entrusted every secret save Mordecai's unique bond with himself, had tirelessly assisted in securing a charming lodging in Brompton, mere minutes' walk from her own home. This proximity ensured that Mirah would remain within the gentle, watchful circle of her "motherly" care. With a blend of Scottish fervor and Gaelic vivacity, Mrs. Meyrick had kept the news strictly hidden from the younger girls and from Hans, fearing that any premature leak might spark agitation and ruin the delicate groundwork essential for Mirah's newfound independence. Both Mrs. Meyrick and Deronda harbored a profound, unspoken desire for Mirah to stand on her own feet. While Mrs. Meyrick, the "little mother," may have harbored a faint, dubitable hesitation regarding the extraordinary brother described to her—perhaps viewing his intense devotion to Judaism as a form of stubborn pertinacity akin to the Covenanters—she ultimately trusted Deronda's judgment above all. Though the reality of Mirah's frailty, marked by consumption, tugged at her tender heart, she hoped that Mirah's fierce attachment to her Jewish identity would eventually soften, merging into the warmer, reciprocal currents of love flowing through her new friendships. Indeed, Mrs. Meyrick's private fantasy had not centered on a dramatic discovery of Jewish lineage, but rather on a brighter future for Hans; now, however, a brother stood poised to plunge Mirah's spirit once more into the deepest dyes of ancestral sentiment.

"It is a comfort," Mrs. Meyrick confided to Deronda in the quiet of the newly rented room, "that the pawnbroker is not her brother. There are countless Ezras in the world, and truly, it is relief to know that not all Jews resemble those shopkeepers who refuse to let you depart. Besides, his words about his mother and sister make me bless him. He seems so kind. Yet, I confess, I have never held a fondness for anything fanatical. Perhaps I heard too much preaching in my youth and lost my appetite for it."

"I do not believe you will find Mordecai obtruding any preaching," Deronda replied gently. "To me, fanaticism implies an enthusiasm that is narrow and blinded, lacking a sense of proportion, rendering a man unjust and unsympathetic to those outside his own circle. Mordecai is an enthusiast, indeed, but I reserve that title for the highest orders of mind—those who care supremely for grand and universal benefits for humanity. He is not a strictly orthodox Jew, and his conformity in many matters is simply an allowance for the conditions of other Jews. Those who live with him adore him, though they cannot fully comprehend his inner world."

"Oh, well," Mrs. Meyrick smiled, smoothing the curtains, "I can live up to the level of the pawnbroker's mother, and appreciate whatever virtue I perceive. For what I cannot see, I shall take your word. By your definition, perhaps one could be fanatical in worshipping common-sense? My late husband often mused that the world would be a poor place if it contained nothing but common sense. However, Mirah's brother shall have the finest bedding I have procured, and I shall paste up this extra window to stave off the drafts. It is a solace to know the household folk are no strangers to me, no hypocritical harpies. Once the children know the truth, we shall be able to adorn the rooms much more prettily."

"The next stage," Deronda noted, looking toward the door, "is to inform Mordecai and arrange his relocation. That may prove the more difficult endeavor."

"And will you tell Mirah before I say a word to the children?" Mrs. Meyrick asked, her tone shifting to one of deliberate persuasion. "No, I think not. Let me tell Hans and the girls tonight, and they will be gone tomorrow morning."

"Yes, that is best," Deronda agreed, offering a soft smile. "But do justice to my account of Mordecai—or 'Ezra,' as Mirah will surely wish to call him. Do not feed their imaginations with tales of Habakkuk Mucklewrath."

"Trust me, trust me," the little mother insisted warmly. "I shall have to persuade them so desperately to be glad that I shall convert myself. When I am frightened, it is a good thing to have someone to be angry with for not being brave; it warms the blood."

Though Deronda could have engaged in further debate regarding the nature of Mordecai's beliefs, his thoughts were preoccupied with a more pressing task: he wished to approach the Cohens without wounding their sensibilities. Following a memorable exchange where Mordecai had signaled his acute sensitivity to any inadvertence concerning their feelings, Deronda had met with the family at the *Hand and Banner*. Now, after careful reflection, he had written to Mordecai requesting the honor of sitting in his workroom for an hour, framing it as a personal request that would vanish if any objection arose. He hoped to cultivate a sense of anticipation that would serve as a gentle preparation.

Upon arriving, Deronda was greeted with the usual warmth, accompanied by subtle shifts in the women's and children's attire, and a lingering, silent wonder from the elders—a mystery surrounding Deronda's relationship with Mordecai that they were too humble to articulate aloud. When Deronda announced, "I suppose Mordecai is at home and expecting me," Jacob, who had caught wind of the family whispers, trotted over to kneel beside him. "What do you want to talk to Mordecai about?" the boy asked eagerly.

"Something very interesting to him," Deronda said, playfully pinching the lad's ear, "but you cannot understand."

"Can you say this?" Jacob challenged, launching into a string of rote-learned Hebrew verses with a wonderful, throaty resonance, nodding solemnly as if presenting formidable evidence capable of altering their mutual standing.

"No, really," Deronda kept grave, "I cannot say anything like that."

"I thought not!" Jacob declared, performing a triumphant dance with his small scarlet legs, emptying and refilling the deep pockets of his knickerbockers to display his resources. He then dashed to the workroom door, flung it wide, leaned back against it, and shouted, "Mordecai, here's the young swell"—a borrowed phrase from his father that seemed to him the perfect capstone to his recitation.

He was recalled with hushed warnings from his mother and grandmother. As Deronda entered and closed the door behind him, he noticed a piece of carpet laid down, a chair positioned, and the fire and lights tended to, all signs of the Cohens' deep respect. As Mordecai rose to greet him, Deronda was struck by the solemn expectancy in his face—an expression that would have seemed entirely natural if his letter had hinted at a revelation regarding a lost sister. Silence fell between them until Deronda, with his customary tenderness, drew a vacant chair from the opposite side of the hearth and seated himself near Mordecai.

"You are coming to tell me something that my soul longs for," Mordecai said in a tone of fervid certainty.

"It is true I have something very weighty to tell you—something I trust you will rejoice in," Deronda responded, guarding himself against the probability that Mordecai expected something entirely different.

"It is all revealed—it is made clear to you," Mordecai pressed, leaning forward with clasped hands. "You are even as my brother that sucked the breasts of my mother—the heritage is yours—there is no doubt to divide us."

"I have learned nothing new about myself," Deronda admitted quietly. The disappointment was inevitable, yet it was kinder not to strain the moment with prolonged false hope.

Mordecai sank back in his chair, momentarily unable to care what was truly coming. His entire day had existed in a state of tension toward a single fulfillment; the reaction was sickening, and he closed his eyes.

"Except," Deronda went on gently after a pause, "except that I had recently come into another sort of hidden connection with you, besides what you have spoken of as existing in your own feeling."

The eyes remained shut, but there was a fluttering in the lids.

"I had made the acquaintance of one in whom you are deeply interested."

"One who is closely related to your departed mother," Deronda continued, striving to make the disclosure gradual. Noticing a shrinking movement in Mordecai, he added softly, "Whom she and you held dear above all others."

Mordecai gave a sudden start, laying a spasmodic, desperate grasp on Deronda's wrist. A great terror seized him. Deronda divined it instantly. A tremor vibrated through his clear tones as he spoke, "What was prayed for has come to pass: Mirah has been delivered from evil."

Mordecai's grip loosened slightly, but he panted with a tearless sob.

"Your sister is worthy of the mother you honored," Deronda affirmed.

He waited. Mordecai, throwing himself backward in his chair, closed his eyes again, uttering himself almost inaudibly in Hebrew for several minutes before subsiding into a happy-looking silence. Watching the expression in Mordecai's uplifted face

"And you must love him ere to you / He will seem worthy of your love."

There was a certain charm in watching Daniel arrange fresh garments for Mordecai, a quiet satisfaction akin to an artist sketching a vision. He imagined the crisp gray flannel shirts and a dressing gown that evoked the humble robes of a Franciscan monk, framing Mordecai's slender frame. A significant part of his delight stemmed from seeing Mirah's brother anew, hoping to shield her from any jarring reality and preserve the intensity of her joy. Yet, as he finished preparing everything, a shadow of uncertainty crept in. Was he perhaps misreading her nature? Did he mistake the superficial appeal of poverty for something deeper? Had he not recognized that Mirah, like himself, possessed a capacity to find profound dignity even within the starkness of deprivation, viewing it not merely as a barrier but as a sacred testament to her brother's history? There was, however, the practical necessity of appeasing the Meyricks, a family prone to skepticism regarding Mordecai's Jewish background. Daniel found himself acutely aware of the need to hide anything that might trigger their immediate aversion, craving instead to bathe every object in the solemn, golden light of a sunset born from deep, reverent love.

His doubts would have grown stronger had he listened to the conversation around Mrs. Meyrick's hearth later that evening, after Mirah retired. Hans, now residing in Chelsea, had stayed on, and as Mrs. Meyrick stoked the fire into a warm blaze, she announced, "Now, Kate, extinguish your candle, and let us gather close. Hans, dearest, please cease your laughter at those poems for the ninety-ninth time and join us. I have something marvellous to share."

Kate, watching the others climb onto the fender, retorted, "As if I didn't know already, Mother! I've caught glimpses of it in your eyes and noticed your feigned errands for weeks."

"Well, since you're so perceptive," Mrs. Meyrick declared, "perhaps you realize that Mirah's brother has been found!"

"Oh, bother!" Hans exclaimed instantly.

"Hans, that is cruel," Mab chided. "What if we had lost you?"

"I simply cannot help feeling somewhat relieved," Kate admitted. "And where is her mother?"

"She is deceased."

"I trust the brother is not a bad man," Amy offered cautiously.

"Nor a dandy wrapped in jewels, a 'Crystal Palace Assyrian' with a top hat," Hans muttered in a sour mood.

"Were there ever such unfeeling children?" Mrs. Meyrick pressed, emboldened by their lack of sympathy. "Do you not care at all about Mirah's happiness?"

"You know, Ma, Mirah barely remembers her brother," Kate noted.

"People missing for twelve years should never return," Hans insisted, pacing the room with his hands deep in his velvet pockets. "They only stand in the way."

"Hans!" Mrs. Meyrick scolded gently. "If I had been absent for twenty years, I imagine—"

"I spoke of twelve years," Hans interrupted sharply. "Any relative lost for roughly that duration belongs to the shadows."

"But it is lovely to find people again; there is so much to say," Mab interjected, clutching her knees. "Did Prince Camaralzaman discover him?"

Mrs. Meyrick then recounted everything she knew without pause, concluding, "Mr. Deronda holds the utmost respect for him. He seems almost to worship him, and he believes Mirah is precisely the kind of sister needed to understand this brother."

"Deronda is becoming utterly absurd about these Jews," Hans sneered, rising and banging his chair aside. "He intends to indulge Mirah in whatever delusions she harbors."

"Oh, shame on you, Hans!" Mab cried. "To speak of Mr. Deronda that way!" Mrs. Meyrick's face betrayed a subtle, suppressed emotion beneath her composed exterior.

"Now we shall never be a complete circle again," Hans rambled, "yet we must entertain this Prophet Elijah, and Mirah will spend all her time dwelling on the ruins of Jerusalem. She'll be ruined as an artist—you mark my words, she'll become as narrow-minded as a nun. Everything will be undone—our home, our peace. I shall resort to drinking."

"Oh, truly, Hans," Kate snapped, exasperated. "Men are the most contemptible creatures. They demand the world bend to their whims, otherwise they become insufferable."

"Oh, oh, oh, it's dreadful!" Mab wailed, mimicking ancient Nineveh collapsing.

"If all that knowledge from university means you remain so childish, Hans, what was the point?" Amy reasoned. "You should learn to accept kindness from those Providence sends you. *We* will have to accept him."

"I pray you all enjoy the new *Lamentations of Jeremiah*—'to be continued in our next,'" Hans dismissed, grabbing his wide-awake. "There is no virtue in being superior if you must tolerate men with fixed ideas staring blankly at you, expecting your entire conversation to serve as mere footnotes to their texts. If you are trapped behind a petrifying wall, you'd best be an old boot. I refuse to be an old boot." With a hurried, desperate gesture, he kissed his mother's brow and offered a condescending farewell to the girls before departing.

"Suppose Mirah knew how you behave," Kate murmured, though the answer was merely the slamming of the door. "I should like to witness Mirah when Mr. Deronda tells her," she told her mother, "for I know she will look beautiful."

However, upon reflection, Daniel had decided against a direct confrontation. Instead, he wrote a letter for Mrs. Meyrick to deliver the following morning. He requested she reveal the news rather than waiting for him, omitting his true motive—he dreaded revisiting a narrative where he appeared as the central benefactor—preferring to stay with Mordecai while Mrs. Meyrick brought Mirah ostensibly on a visit. This arrangement would create a brief respite before the inevitable relocation Mirah would soon propose.

Secretly, Daniel worried about Mordecai. After years of solitary contemplation, his physical frailty had sharpened his mental focus, potentially rendering him exclusive in his views. Would Mordecai allow space for Daniel's tenderness toward his sister, beyond the obligatory piety? While Mordecai's feelings for the Cohens, particularly young Jacob, remained active and evident, Daniel observed a strange, heavy silence from Mordecai regarding Mirah. The old man had accepted the new clothes with obedient quietude, only to whisper afterward, with a faint smile, "I must keep my old garments by me for a remembrance." Seated together, awaiting Mirah, Mordecai said nothing, his eyelids lowered, yet his face and hands betraying a restless agitation. It was a specific nervous disturbance known to those whose minds, driven long by a single powerful current, are suddenly forced into a new or reopened channel. For susceptible souls whose strength has been drained by dormant biases, an interview that violently resurrects the past feels like a terrifying illness. Even joy, in this context, carries a terrible weight.

Daniel caught the contagion of this excitement. When the bell rang, he stepped outside, unsure why, simply wishing to greet Mirah before the formal introduction. To his startlement, she wore the hat and cloak in which he had first encountered her—the memorable cloak that had once served as a makeshift shroud, wetted by rain. She had descended prepared for this encounter. When Mrs. Meyrick asked, "You wish to go in that dress, dear?", Mirah replied, "My brother is poor, and I want to resemble him, lest he feel distant from me"—presuming she would meet him in his workman's attire. Daniel could offer no comment, feeling a secret shame for his own fastidious preparations. They shook hands silently; Mirah's countenance was pale and awestruck.

As Daniel held the door open, Mordecai rose, his gaze turning eagerly toward the entrance. Mirah took only a few steps before halting. They gazed at one another, frozen in time. It was less their physical presence that overwhelmed them than a spectral presence, a meeting of spirits prior to the touch of flesh. Mirah broke the silence first.

"Ezra," she called out, using the exact tone she employed when recounting her mother's summons.

With a sudden movement, Mordecai stepped forward, placing his hand gently on her shoulder. He towered over her, looking down with tender regard. "That was Mother's voice. Do you recall her calling me?"

"Yes," Mirah whispered, tears welling. "And how you answered, 'Mother!' I knew then that you loved her." She threw her arms around her brother's neck, claspng her small hands tightly behind it, drawing his face down to hers with a lavish, childlike kiss. Her hat tipped backward, revealing her tousled curls.

"Ah, the dear head, the dear head!" Mordecai murmured, his thin hand caressing the curls in a low, loving hum.

"You are very ill, Ezra," Mirah said, her expression softening with sorrowful observation.

"Yes, my dear child, I shall not long remain in this body," he answered quietly.

"Oh, I will love you, and we shall speak," Mirah poured forth, her words flowing spontaneously like bird-song. "I will tell you everything, and you will teach me. You will instruct me on how to be a good Jew

'Tis a heavy and poorly rewarded burden to try to arrange all things ahead of time according to the rules of our own security, as Machiavelli hints regarding Cesare Borgia. He, it is said, considered every contingency following his father's death and guarded against every misfortune, save one: it never crossed his mind that when his father died, his own death would swiftly follow.

Grandcourt's significance in this kingdom was of a grandly passive sort, derived solely from inheriting land. Political and social currents brushed against him only through the thread of his income. Even a meticulous chronicler would not need to study Schleswig-Holstein, Bismarck's policies, trade unions, household suffrage, or recent commercial panics. He skimmed the leading newspaper columns on these matters, and his views lacked breadth, for he lumped all Germans, all businessmen, and all voters prone to using the wrong soap under the single label "brutes." Yet, he took no action on these agitated issues beyond peering from beneath his eyelids at anyone who mentioned them, maintaining a silence that shook the convictions of timid thinkers.

Within his own sphere, however, Grandcourt displayed qualities akin to the triumphalist diplomacy of the wildest continental nations.

Not a movement of Gwendolen in relation to Deronda escaped him. He would deny being jealous, for jealousy implies a doubt of one's own power to prevent what one has resolved against. It pained him not that his wife might incline toward another man's society; what he required was that she remain as fully aware as if locked in handcuffs, understanding that her inclination was powerless to contradict his resolve. Though his entry into matrimony might have been filled with vacillating whims, his interpretation of the bond showed none. He had not repented of his marriage; it had injected aim into his life, offering new objects for his will. He had not repented of his choice. His tastes were fastidious, and Gwendolen satisfied them. He would not accept a wife who had not received an elevation of rank from him, nor one who did not command admiration through her bearing and beauty, nor one whose nails were not shaped correctly, nor one whose ear lobes were too large and red. Nor could he tolerate a ninny, unable to make spirited replies. These requirements may not seem excessive to refined contemporaries whose ability to fall in love hangs suspended due to a lack of essential details. Fewer, however, may follow him in his contentment that his wife possessed a temperament inclined to fly out if she dared, and that she had been urged into marrying him by feelings other than passionate attachment. Still, for those who prefer command to love, one wonders why the habit of mind should shift precisely at the point of matrimony.

Grandcourt did not feel he had chosen the wrong wife, and having assumed the role of husband, he refused to be fooled or appear pitiable. This was his state of mind—not jealousy, yet his behavior resembled jealousy as yellow resembles yellow, a hue produced by vastly different causes.

He had arrived in town earlier than usual to consult on legal arrangements for his will, mortgage transfers, and the transaction with his uncle regarding the succession to Diplow, secured by the bait of ready money. Another welcome accompaniment to his presence in town was the presentation of himself alongside the beautiful bride he had chosen despite expectations. True, Grandcourt moved with the sense that he cared not a languid curse for anyone's admiration; yet this state of not caring, just as much as desire, required its object: a world of admiring or envious spectators. For if one delights in staring stonily at smiling persons, those persons must smile, a rudimentary truth often forgotten by those who complain of mankind's general contemptibility, since any other aspect of the race disappoints the voracity of their contempt. In town for the first time with his wife, Grandcourt's abstention from cursing enlarged and diversified through splendid receptions, conspicuous rides, and presentations on all distinguished occasions. He wished her to be sought after; he liked that "fellows" should be eager to talk with her and escort her within his observation. There was even a certain lofty coquetry on her part that he would not have objected to. But what he disliked were her ways in relation to Deronda.

After the musical party at Lady Mallinger's, when Grandcourt observed the dialogue on the settee as keenly as Hans did, it was characteristic of him to name Deronda for an invitation along with the Mallingers, tenaciously avoiding any suggestion that Deronda's presence or absence mattered to him. He made no direct observation to Gwendolen on her behavior that evening, lest the expression of his disgust be too strong to satisfy his pride. A few days later, however, he remarked, without regard for *à propos*, "Nothing makes a woman more of a gawky than looking out after people and showing tempers in public. A woman ought to have good manners. Else it's intolerable to appear with her."

Gwendolen made the expected application and felt alarm at the notion of being a gawky. Like her, she preferred that her distaste include admirers. But the sense of overhanging rebuke intensified the strain of expectation toward any meeting with Deronda. The novelty and excitement of her town life were like the hurry and constant change of foreign travel; whatever inward despondency existed, there was a program to fulfill, yielding gratification to her many-sided self. Yet, as always happens with deep interests, the comparatively rare occasions on which she could exchange words with Deronda had a diffusive effect in her consciousness, magnifying their communication and enlarging the place she imagined it held in his mind. How could Deronda help this? He certainly did not avoid her; rather, he wished to convince her by every delicate indirect means that her confidence in him had not been indiscreet, as it had not lowered his respect. Moreover, he liked being near her—how could it be otherwise? She was something more than a problem: she was a lovely woman, for whose turn of mind and fate he had a care which, however futile, solicited him as a responsibility. Perhaps all the more so, when he dared think of his own future, he saw it lying far away from this splendid, sad-hearted creature, who, because he had once been impelled to arrest her attention momentarily, as he might have seized her arm to warn her from stepping where there was danger, had turned to him with a beseeching, persistent need.

One instance in which Grandcourt stimulated a feeling in Gwendolen that he would have liked to suppress without seeming to care related to Mirah. Gwendolen's inclination lingered over the project of singing lessons as a sort of obedience to Deronda's advice, but day followed day with that want of perceived leisure belonging to lives where no work marks off intervals. The continual liability to Grandcourt's presence and surveillance seemed to flatten every effort to the level of the boredom his manner expressed; his negative mind was as diffusive as fog, clinging to all objects and spoiling all contact.

But one morning, while breakfasting, Gwendolen, in a recurrent fit of determination to exercise the old spirit, said, dallying prettily over her prawns without eating them, "I think of making myself accomplished while we are in town, and having singing lessons."

"Why?" said Grandcourt, languidly.

"Why?" echoed Gwendolen, playing at sauciness, "because I can't eat *pâté de foie gras* to make me sleepy, and I can't smoke, and I can't go to the club to make me like to come away again—I want a variety of *ennui*. What would be the most convenient time, when you are busy with your lawyers and people, for me to have lessons from that little Jewess, whose singing is getting all the rage?"

"Whenever you like," said Grandcourt, pushing away his plate and leaning back in his chair, looking at her with his most lizard-like expression, while he played with the ears of the tiny spaniel on his lap (Gwendolen had taken a dislike to the dogs because they fawned on him).

Then he said, languidly, "I don't see why a lady should sing. Amateurs make fools of themselves. A lady can't risk herself in that way in company. And one doesn't want to hear squalling in private."

"I like frankness: that seems to me a husband's great charm," said Gwendolen, with her little upward movement of her chin, as she turned her eyes away from his, and lifting a prawn before her, looked at the boiled ingenuousness of its eyes as preferable to the lizard's. "But;" she added, having devoured her mortification, "I suppose you don't object to Miss Lapidoth's singing at our party on the fourth? I thought of engaging her. Lady Brackenshaw had her, you know: and the Raymonds, who are very particular about their music. And Mr. Deronda, who is a musician himself and a first-rate judge, says there is no singing in such good taste as hers for a drawing-room. I think his opinion is an authority."

She meant to sling a small stone at her husband in that way.

"It's very indecent of Deronda to go about praising that girl," said Grandcourt in a tone of indifference.

"Indecent!" exclaimed Gwendolen, reddening and looking at him again, overcome by startled wonder, and unable to reflect on the probable falsity of the phrase—"to go about praising."

"Yes; and especially when she is patronized by Lady Mallinger. He ought to hold his tongue about her. Men can see what is his relation

Within the quiet corridors of his own spirit, a vast and unyielding justice reigns supreme. It is the kind of grace that allows gratitude to bloom even where resentment once festered, a testament to souls born of nobler blood and broader capacity for joy. These are not the clever minds that twist truth until goodness dissolves into mush, mere excuses for "thank you" that mask a selfish heart; no, they are the devoted servants who, though bound by vows of poverty in love and trust, still beg from a world that hoards its humanity.

As Daniel parted from Gwendolen, he carefully withheld the heavy truth of his departure. To say, "I shall not see you again for a long time," would have given Lord Grandcourt the satisfaction of knowing it mattered deeply to her. Instead, he left her with a polite distance, masking the storm within.

Yet, fate was already turning. Daniel was leaving soon enough, driven by circumstances so profound that his return to call upon Gwendolen felt like a step back into a dream already fading. As he prepared to keep his word, a solemn emotion settled over him, reviving the deepest currents of his existence.

Sir Hugo had summoned him, sending a note that cut through the air like a bell: *"Come immediately. Something has happened."* When Daniel entered the baronet's study, expecting a scene of distress, he found instead a grave affection. Sir Hugo received him with a warmth that belied the weight in the room.

"It is nothing to grieve you, sir?" Daniel asked, his voice steady, taking the hand extended to him. He felt a sudden surge of restored confidence, yet he saw an unusual depth in Sir Hugo's eyes, a suppressed tremor in his throat.

"No, Dan, no," Sir Hugo replied softly. "Sit down. I have something to say."

Daniel obeyed, sensing the gravity of the moment. It was rare for Sir Hugo to wear such serious feeling, a somber mask that spoke of a secret finally breaking open.

"Not to grieve me, my boy, no," Sir Hugo continued, his gaze searching Daniel's face. "At least, if there is nothing in it that will grieve you too much. But I hardly expected that *this*—just *this*—would ever come to pass. There have been reasons why I have never prepared you for it. There have been reasons why I have never told you anything about your parentage. But I have striven in every way not to make that an injury to you."

Sir Hugo paused, the air thick with unspoken history. Daniel sat silent, unable to speak. He could not say, "I have never felt it an injury," because those words felt false in the light of this revelation. Far more hung in the balance than anyone else could know. Sir Hugo had never seen the great face he loved so dearly grow so pale, nor the lips pressed together with such a look of pain. With a tenderness that bordered on anxiety, fearing he might wound the boy further, he reached into his pocket.

"I have acted in obedience to your mother's wishes," Sir Hugo whispered. "The secrecy was her wish. But now she desires to remove it. She desires to see you. I will put this letter into your hands, which you can look at by-and-by. It will merely tell you what she wishes you to do, and where you will find her."

A letter, written on foreign paper, was placed in Daniel's palm. He thrust it into his breast pocket, grateful for the reprieve from reading it immediately. The emotion on Daniel's face had grown, shaking Sir Hugo's composure. The baronet struggled to find words, while Daniel's entire soul was consumed by a single, terrifying question—the hardest in the world to utter.

And yet, he could not bear to delay. This was a sacramental hour; if he let it slip, he could never again stand under these influences and hear the answer. For several moments, their eyes were cast downward, and it seemed as though invisible threads of thought wove between them. Finally, Daniel lifted his gaze, speaking with a tremulous reverence, dreading the indirect reproach that years of stifled affection might carry.

"Is my father also living?"

The answer came instantly, low and emphatic. "No."

Joy and pain mingled in the aftermath, indistinguishable, swirling like a tempest in the small room. A new light had fallen upon the past for Sir Hugo as well. After a silence so deep it felt like standing on the edge of a new faith, the baronet spoke in a tone of confession.

"Perhaps I was wrong, Dan, to undertake what I did. And perhaps I liked it a little too well—having you all to myself. But if you have had any pain which I might have helped, I ask you to forgive me."

"The forgiveness has long been there," Daniel answered gently. "The chief pain has always been on account of someone else—someone whom I never knew, whom I am now to know. It has not hindered me from feeling an affection for you which has made a large part of all the life I remember."

Seized by a singular, powerful impulse, the two men clasped hands, holding onto the fragile bridge of understanding they had just built across the chasm of secrets.

"If some mortal, born too soon,
Were laid away in some great trance—the ages
Coming and going all the while—till dawned
His true time's advent; and could then record
The words they spoke who kept watch by his bed,
Then I might tell more of the breath so light
Upon my eyelids, and the fingers warm
Among my hair. Youth is confused; yet never
So dull was I but, when that spirit passed,
I turned to him, scarce consciously, as turns
A water-snake when fairies cross his sleep."
—BROWNING: *Paracelsus*.

These lines framed the letter Sir Hugo pressed into Deronda's hand, a document that, despite its plain, almost colorless phrasing, promised a revelation of profound weight.

My good friend and yours, Sir Hugo Mallinger, will have told you that
I wish to see you. My health is shaken, and I desire there should be
no time lost before I deliver to you what I have long withheld. Let
nothing hinder you from being at the *Albergo dell' Italia* in
Genoa by the fourteenth of this month. Wait for me there. I am
uncertain when I shall be able to make the journey from Spezia, where
I shall be staying. That will depend on several things. Wait for
me—the Princess Halm-Eberstein. Bring with you the diamond ring that
Sir Hugo gave you. I shall like to see it again.—Your unknown mother,

To Deronda, this epistle offered no clues as to the specific content of the withheld secret, yet it rendered resistance impossible. He accepted Sir Hugo's reticence, sensing an implicit pledge not to preface the mother's disclosures, and found his lifelong conjectures regarding his parentage abruptly stilled. Though his imagination soared quickly over a landscape of possibilities, he rigorously refused to privilege any single scenario. To do so would be to nurse a desire or repugnance that would cloud his judgment; instead, he resolved to prepare himself with a brave neutrality to face whatever truth awaited.

In this guarded state of mind, Deronda could not articulate the reason for his sudden absence to those around him, particularly Mordecai, whom the news would strike with a power akin to his own internal turmoil, albeit differently. If he confessed, "I am going to learn the truth about my birth," Mordecai's hope would ignite a dangerous, painful excitement. With careful exclusion of suppositions, Deronda framed his journey as undertaken solely by Sir Hugo's wish, masking his uncertainty with feigned indifference. He suggested the trip might be brief, leaving the duration vague.

"I will ask to have the child Jacob to stay with me," Mordecai comforted himself, offering a small measure of relief after the initial, mournful glances exchanged between them.

"I will drive round and ask Mrs. Cohen to let him come," Mirah offered gently.

"The grandmother will deny you nothing," Deronda replied, a smile softening his features. "I'm glad you were a little wrong as well as I," he added, looking kindly at Mordecai. "You thought that old Mrs. Cohen would not bear to see Mirah."

"I undervalued her heart," Mordecai admitted, his tone respectful. "She is capable of rejoicing that another's plant blooms though her own be withered."

"Oh, they are dear good people; I feel as if we all belonged to each other," Mirah said, a tinge of merriment coloring her smile.

"What should you have felt if that Ezra had been your brother?" Deronda asked, his voice tinged with mischief, though a flicker of provocation remained—he chafed slightly at how readily she had embraced people who stood to cause him significant annoyance.

Mirah regarded him with a momentary surprise before speaking softly. "He is not a bad man—I think he would never forsake any one." As the words left her lips, she blushed deeply, casting a timid glance at Mordecai before turning away to some quiet occupation. Her father lingered in her mind, a presence that cast a shadow over her conversation with her brother. To Mirah, the thought of him arriving to find them made the daylight streets seem as shadowy as a haunted forest, where every corner concealed an imaginary apparition.

Deronda perceived her involuntary allusion and understood the blush. How could he be slow to recognize feelings that now seemed closer to his own than ever? For the words of his mother's letter implied that his filial bond was not to be severed from painful conditions; indeed, singularly, the letter that had made his mother a living reality for the first time had paradoxically thrown her into greater remoteness for his affections. The tender yearning for a being whose life might have been marred by a lack of his care, the image of a mother who had received neither full reverence nor compassion, had long hovered secretly in his observations of the women he met. Yet, now, it appeared that picturing his mother according to this ideal fit the facts no better than his former conceptions about Sir Hugo. He wondered how, when his mother's very handwriting arrived bearing words of her actual feeling, his affections had instantly contracted into a state of comparative neutrality. A veiled figure with enigmatic speech had pushed aside the image he had, in spite of uncertainty, gradually molded and claimed as the possessor of his tenderness and dutiful longing.

As he prepared to depart for Genoa, the interest truly uppermost in his mind related less to his mother than to Mordecai and Mirah.

"God bless you, Dan!" Sir Hugo had said, shaking his hand. "Whatever else changes for you, it can't change my being the oldest friend you have known, and the one who has all along felt the most for you. I couldn't have loved you better if you'd been my own—only I should have been better pleased with thinking of you always as the future master of the Abbey instead of my fine nephew; and then you would have seen it necessary for you to take a political line. However—things must be as they may." It was a defensive movement on the baronet's part, mingling purposeless remarks with the expression of serious feeling.

When Deronda finally arrived at the *Italia* in Genoa, no Princess Halm-Eberstein was present. On the second day, however, a letter reached him stating that her arrival might occur within a week or be deferred by a fortnight and more. She was under circumstances rendering it impossible to fix her journey precisely, and she entreated him to wait as patiently as he could.

Faced with this indefinite prospect of suspense concerning matters of supreme moment, Deronda undertook the difficult task of seeking amusement on philosophic grounds, hoping to quiet his excited feelings and grant patience a lift over a weary road. His previous visit to this superb city had been cursory, leaving much to explore beyond the prescribed rounds of sight-seeing. He spent the cooler hours wandering observantly through the streets, the quay, and the environs, often taking a boat to enjoy the magnificent view of the city and harbor from the sea. Every sight, every subject—even the anticipated meeting with his mother—found a central union in Mordecai and Mirah, and the ideas immediately associated with them.

Amidst the thoughts that most filled his mind while his boat drifted within view of the grand harbor, rose the vision of the multitudinous Spanish Jews centuries ago, driven destitute from their homes, allowed land from crowded ships only for a brief rest on this grand quay of Genoa. They overspread it with a pall of famine and plague—dying mothers clutching dying children at their breasts, fathers and sons gazing at each other's haggardness, like groups from a hundred Hunger-towers turned out beneath the midday sun. Inevitably, dreamy constructions of a possible ancestry for himself wove themselves with these historic memories, which had begun to hold a new interest since his discovery of Mirah, and now, under Mordecai's influence, had become irresistibly dominant. He would have sealed his mind against such constructions if it had been possible, and he had never fully admitted to himself that he wished the facts to verify Mordecai's conviction. He inwardly repeated that he had no choice in the matter, and that wishing was folly; nay, on the question of parentage, wishing seemed part of that meanness which disowns kinship: it was a disowning by anticipation. What he had to do was simply to accept the fact; and he had really no strong presumption to go upon, now that he was assured of his mistake about Sir Hugo. There had been a resolved concealment which made all inference untrustworthy, and the very name he bore might be a false one. If Mordecai was wrong—if he, the so-called Daniel Deronda, were held by ties entirely aloof from any such course as his friend's pathetic hope had marked out?—he would not say "I wish"; but he could not help feeling on which side the sacrifice lay.

Across these two importunate thoughts, which he resisted as much as one can resist anything in that unstrung condition which belongs to suspense, there came continually an anxiety which he made no effort to banish—dwelling on it rather with a mournfulness, which often seems to us the best atonement we can make to one whose need we have been unable to meet. This anxiety was for Gwendolen. In the wonderful mixtures of our nature, there exists a feeling distinct from that exclusive passionate love of which some men

She held the spindle as she sat,
Errina with the thick-coiled mat
Of raven hair and deepest agate eyes,
Gazing with a sad surprise
At surging visions of her destiny—
To spin the byssus drearily
In insect-labor, while the throng
Of gods and men wrought deeds that poets wrought in song.

When Daniel Deronda knocked upon the door of his mother's apartment in the *Italia*, a familiar chill of boyhood agitation seized him, stirring memories of early, unearned anxieties. Two servants in the antechamber regarded him with marked surprise; the striking young gentleman who appeared to be the doctor his mistress sought was a startling deviation from expectation, his evening attire lending an accidental grandeur to the severe lines of his countenance. Yet Deronda paid them no heed until the second door swung open, revealing a figure standing at the far end of the vast room, awaiting his approach.

She was draped in black lace, falling loosely from the summit of her whitening hair to a long train that trailed behind her tall form. Her arms, bare to the elbow save for rich bracelets, were folded before her, and the fine poise of her head rendered her visage somehow more handsome than reality warranted. But before Deronda could truly observe her, he stood before her, taking the hand she offered and pressing it to his lips. She retained his grasp, studying him intently, while his chief awareness was struck by the piercing quality of her eyes and the mobile nature of her face, which seemed poised to transform into a stranger in the next instant. Even as she examined him, a play across her brow and nostrils formed a silent, tacit language. Deronda dared not move, unable to fathom what emotional manifestation she required; he felt his cheeks flushing like a girl's, yet marveled at his own lack of profound emotion. After so many idealized imaginings of his mother, this encounter felt curiously unreal. He could not conjecture the language she would use. Suddenly, she released his hand and laid both palms upon his shoulders, her face flashing with an admiration so radiant that every trace of weariness vanished, restoring a youthful bloom.

"You are a beautiful creature!" she said, her voice low and melodious, the syllables possessing a foreign, agreeable outline. "I knew you would be." She kissed him on each cheek, and he returned them, yet the exchange felt akin to a greeting between sovereigns.

She paused as the lines returned to her face, then spoke in a colder tone, "I am your mother. But you can have no love for me."

"I have thought of you more than of any other being in the world," Deronda replied, his voice trembling with nervous intensity.

"I am not like what you thought I was," she stated decisively, withdrawing her hands to fold her arms again, inviting him to scrutinize her. He had often pictured her face as bearing a likeness to his own; he saw traces of it now, but amidst striking differences. She was a remarkable being. What caused his painful sense of aloofness? Her worn beauty possessed a strangeness, as if she were not quite a human mother, but a Melusine bound to some realm independent of theirs.

"I used to think that you might be suffering," Deronda said, driven above all by a fear of wounding her. "I used to wish that I could be a comfort to you."

"I *am* suffering. But with a suffering that you can't comfort," the Princess said, her voice hardening as she moved to a sofa where cushions were carefully arranged. "Sit down." She indicated a seat near her, and sensing distress in his expression, softened, "I am not suffering at this moment. I am at ease now. I am able to talk."

Deronda seated himself, waiting for her to continue. It seemed he stood before a Mysterious Fate rather than the longed-for mother. He watched her with wonder, drawn to the spiritual distance she had erected between them.

"No," she began. "I did not send for you to comfort me. I could not know beforehand—I don't know now—what you will feel toward me. I have not the foolish notion that you can love me merely because I am your mother, when you have never seen or heard of me in all your life. But I thought I chose something better for you than being with me. I did not think I deprived you of anything worth having."

"You cannot wish me to believe that your affection would not have been worth having," Deronda said, noting her pause as if she awaited an answer.

"I don't mean to speak ill of myself," the Princess declared with proud impetuosity, "but I had not much affection to give you. I did not want affection. I had been stifled with it. I wanted to live out the life that was in me, and not to be hampered with other lives. You wonder what I was. I was no princess then." Rising with a sudden movement, she stood as she had before; Deronda rose instantly, feeling breathless.

"No princess in this tame life that I live in now. I was a great singer, and I acted as well as I sang. All the rest were poor beside me. Men followed me from one country to another. I was living a myriad lives in one. I did not want a child."

A passionate self-defense vibrated in her tone. She had discarded precedent entirely, seeking justification only in the most intense words available for her experience. She seemed to fling her final words against any possible reproach in her son's mind, who stood clutching his coat-collar as if clinging to a lifeline, his blood churning with a commotion akin to witnessing a sacred rite of criminality. What else could she tell him? She continued with the same intensity, a pale illumination in her face.

"I did not want to marry. I was forced into marrying your father—forced, I mean, by my father's wishes and commands; and besides, it was my best way of getting some freedom. I could rule my husband, but not my father. I had a right to be free. I had a right to seek my freedom from a bondage that I hated."

Seating herself again, a subtle movement played through her eyes and closed lips, suggesting a suppressed continuation of speech. Deronda remained standing. After a moment, she looked up with a less defiant plea.

"And the bondage I hated for myself I wanted to keep you from. What better could the most loving mother have done? I relieved you from the bondage of having been born a Jew."

"Then I *am* a Jew?" Deronda burst out with deep-voiced energy that made his mother shrink slightly backward against her cushions. "My father was a Jew, and you are a Jewess?"

"Yes, your father was my cousin," she said, watching him with a change in her look, as if she sensed a shadow looming.

"I am glad of it," Deronda exclaimed impetuously, in a voice veiled with passion. He could not have anticipated how he would arrive at such a statement, nor dreamed it would oppose his mother so sharply. He was shaken by a mixed anger, unchecked by reflection, against this mother who seemed to have borne him unwillingly, willingly made herself a stranger, and—perhaps—was now revealing herself unwillingly. This last suspicion flashed an explanation over her speech.

But the mother was equally shaken by an anger differently mixed, her frame unequal to any repression. The shaking visible in her was physical, her eyes appearing larger due to her pallid excitement as she cried violently,

"Why do you say you are glad? You are an English gentleman. I secured you that."

"You did not know what you secured me. How could you choose my birthright for me?" Deronda asked, throwing himself sideways into his chair, leaning his arm over the back, his gaze averted.

An intolerant fire seemed foreign to him, yet he struggled desperately to master himself and remain silent. A horror swept over him, lest he utter something too harsh in this epoch-making moment. A pause ensued before his mother spoke again, her voice becoming more firmly resistant in its finely varied tones:

"I chose for you what I would have chosen for myself. How could I know that you would have the spirit of my father in you? How could I know that you would love what I hated?—if you really love to be a Jew." The last words held such bitterness that any listener might have suspected a hatred arising between mother and son.

Yet Deronda had recovered his fuller self. He recalled his sensibilities to what life had been and was for her, whose best years were gone and whose frame bore signs of suffering as she exerted herself to recount a past that belonged not only to him but to her as well. His habitual shame at accepting events as solely his burden aided him here. As he gazed at his mother silently after her words, his face regained a penetrative calm, yet it exerted a strangely agitating influence over her: her eyes were fixed upon him with a sort of fascination, devoid of maternal delight.

"Forget me, if I speak hastily," he said with diffident gravity. "Why have you resolved now on disclosing to me what you took care to have me brought up in ignorance of? Why—since you seem angry that I should be glad?"

"Oh—the reasons of our actions!" the Princess said, with a ring of sarcastic scorn. "When you are as old as I am, it will not seem so simple a question—'Why did you do this?' People talk of their motives in a cut-and-dried way. Every woman is supposed to have the same set of motives, or else to be a monster. I am not

"The same firmness that serves to resist love also renders it violent and enduring; and weak persons, who are perpetually agitated by passions, are rarely truly filled with them."

Among Deronda's correspondence the following morning was a lengthy missive from Hans Meyrick, four quarto pages penned in the small, exquisite hand distinctive to his family.

MY DEAR DERONDA,—In exchange for your sketch of Italian movements and your broader views on worldly affairs, I report from home that the most prudent local sentiment regarding current causes is simply "time will show." As for the origins of past effects, the infamous swindling telegrams have now been linked to last year's cattle plague—a refutation of false philosophy and a justification for farmer compensation. My own theory, that a murrain will soon afflict the commercial class and reveal itself via the sudden sale of rejected paintings, has been dismissed as an unsound analogy. Yet, there are minds unwilling to deprive a neglected painter of his solace. To my sensibilities, there is profound beauty in the notion that some bad judge might bid high for my *Berenice* series, thereby punishing the men of the City for my ill-merited luck.

Meanwhile, I console myself for your absence by discovering advantages therein—shining like Hesperus when Hyperion departs. I sit with our Hebrew prophet, studying the contours of his head in the hours formerly occupied by you, earning credit as a learned young Gentile who would have been Jewish if circumstances allowed. I agree with him in the general principle that whatever is best is, by definition, Jewish. Though I lack the *forte* for severe reasoning, I see the logic: if the best is A, and B happens to be best, then B must be A. On this principle, I could have endorsed a pamphlet proving all good art is Protestant. However, our prophet is an uncommonly interesting sitter—a superior model to Rembrandt's Rabbi—and I depart from him with new discoveries. It constantly amazes me that, despite his fiery devotion to his race and traditions, he is not a rigid, spittle-spewing Jew anticipating the roasted Leviathan to dry up the Gentile tongue, nor sending up endless plates *ad libitum*. (One perceives that my studies prepare one for the Orthodox Jew.) I confess I have always treated your account of Mordecai as somewhat apologetic, a mere antediluvian point of view designed to spare the *megatherium* from injustice. But hearing him in his proper person, I find him a philosophical-allegorical-mystical believer, yet possessing a sharp dialectic point capable of silencing any argumentative rattler of peas in a bladder. This mixture may be a Jewish prerogative. Indeed, his mind seems so broad that my own correct opinions lie within it comfortably; how to align them with the vast remainder is his affair, not mine. I leave the settlement of our basis to him, for I have never encountered a basis that was not a world-supporting elephant, costly and cumbersome to maintain. My finances preclude keeping a private elephant, so I retreat into mystery—a cheap, lasting gas derived from decomposing elephants. If I favor an opinion, I treat it civilly, sparing suspicious inquiry. I harbor a genuine fondness for Mordecai's notion that a whole Christian is three-quarters a Jew, and that since Alexandria, the most comprehensive minds have been Jewish. I intend to point out to Mirah that, aside from Arabic and other incidental differences, there is little distinction between me and Maimonides.

However, I have recently observed that your shallow lover cannot help making declarations. If Mirah's ways were less distracting, and her presence less of a heaven to behold, I would long have thrown myself at her feet, begging her to tell me, with less indirectness, whether she wished me to blow my brains out. I possess a knack of hope, equivalent to an estate in reversion, provided one resists the temptation of transforming it into certainty, which may ruin everything. My Hope wanders among orchard blossoms, feels warm snow falling upon it in the sunshine, and doubts nothing. But when Certainty appears in the distance, revealed as an ugly, Janus-faced deity offering a dubious wink, I turn away swiftly. You, with your supreme reasonableness, self-nullification, and preparation for the worst, know nothing of this immortal, delicious maiden, forever courted and forever propitious, whom fools have called deceitful, as if Hope carries the cup of disappointment. It is her deadly enemy, Certainty, whom she escapes only through transformation. (Do you observe my new vein of allegory?) Seriously, however, I must allege that truth prevails, prejudice melts before it, diversity accompanied by merit becomes fascinating, and no virtuous aspiration fails—all doctrines of the schools implying that the Jewess I prefer will prefer me. Any blockhead cites generalities, but the mind-master discerns the particular cases they represent.

I am less convinced that my company compensates Mordecai for your absence, but occasionally Jacob Cohen intrudes as a substitute. It is worthwhile to catch our prophet's expression when this remarkable young Israel sits on his knee, pouring forth Semitic inspiration with a sublime look of melancholy patience and devoutness. Sometimes Jacob considers Hebrew more edifying if he plugs his ears and imitates the muffled sounds, or if he holds his fists tight. When Mordecai gently draws down the little fists, Jacob's features erupt in extraordinary activity, resembling a man walking through a menagerie, succeeding best with the owl and the peccary. You have surely seen this. He treats me with familiar ease, generally viewing me as a second-hand Christian commodity likely to depreciate, remarking on my disadvantages with a frankness implying thoughts of future purchase. It is charming, however, to see the change in him if Mirah enters. He instantly becomes a child—though his age usually resembles ancient Israelitish garments, perhaps nearing forty, he retains an air of recent production. With Mirah, he reminds me of dogs raised by women, manageable only by them. Yet, the dog is fond of Mordecai, bringing sugar-plums to share, filling his own mouth to an embarrassing extent while watching how Mordecai handles a smaller supply. Judging from this modern Jacob at six, I am astonished that his race has not purchased us all long ago, pocketing our feeble generations as stock and scrip, akin to slave property. There is one Jewess I should not mind being enslaved to. Yet I wish I did not imagine that Mirah grows a little sadder, striving to hide it. It is natural, considering she watches the slow death of this brother, whom she worships with looks of loving devoutness, leading me to wish myself in his place.

For the rest, we are slightly merrier than usual. Rex Gascoigne, whom you recall admiring in my sketches, a fellow with a fine upper lip studying law, now occupies rooms nearby, hosting a neat sister (also with a good upper lip). I have introduced them to my mother and the girls, who discovered from Miss Gascoigne that she is a cousin to your Vandyke Duchess!!! I marked the exclamation points to denote the initial shock of this information upon my feeble understanding. Upon reflection, however, I found no ground for surprise, unless I previously believed that no one could be anyone's cousin without my knowledge. This specific surprise, I conclude, depends on a lively spine coupled with a variable nullity of the brain. I recall a fellow I met in Rome, effervescent with surprise at the simplest information. Tell him you are fond of easy boots; he will invariably reply, "No! Are you?" with the same energetic wonder. He is the very fellow of whom pastoral Browne prophesied: *"A wretch so empty that if e'er there be / In nature found the least vacuity / 'Twill be in him."* I have accounted for it all—he possessed a lively spine.

Nevertheless, this cousinship with the Duchess emerged by chance one day when Mirah visited, and they discussed the Mallingers. *Apropos*; I am becoming so important that I have rival invitations. Gascoigne invites me to his father's rectory in August to view the countryside. But self-interest, well understood, directs me to Topping Abbey, for Sir Hugo has invited me, proposing—with God bless him for his rashness!—that I paint his three daughters sitting on a bank, in the Gainsborough style. He visited my studio recently, recommending I apply myself to portraiture. Of course, I know what he means. "My good fellow, your attempts at the historic and poetic are simply pitiable. Your brush is that of a successful portrait-painter—it possesses a little truth and a great facility in falsehood—your idealism will never suffice for gods and heroes, but it may fetch a high price as flattery. Fate, my friend, has made you the hind wheel—*rota posterior curras*—run behind, because you cannot help it." What effort it costs our friends to offer such candid opinions! I have even known a man summon me specifically to declare that I had irretrievably exposed my want of judgment, suggesting that if I had asked, we would have lent him our own. Such was my ingratitude and readiness at composition that, even as he spoke, I inwardly sketched a Last Judgment featuring that candid friend's physiognomy on the left. Yet, all this pales beside Sir Hugo, whose manner of implying one's gifts are not of the highest order is so exceedingly good-natured and comforting that I begin to feel it an advantage not to be among those poor fellows at the tip-top. His kindness tastes all

"My desolation does begin to make / A better life."
—SHAKESPEARE: *Antony and Cleopatra.*

A single day intervened between the summons to a second audience and the arrival itself. During that time, the Princess sent no further word than a brief note stating she was not yet well enough to receive him. But on the third morning, a fresh letter arrived, commanding: "I leave to-day. Come and see me at once."

Deronda was ushered into the same chamber as before, yet the atmosphere had shifted profoundly. Blinds and heavy curtains darkened the room until it resembled a twilight sanctuary. The Princess was absent at first, but soon she entered, wrapped in a loose gown of soft, dusky orange silk. Black lace floated about her head, framing features that appeared even more striking in the somber gloom; her eyes seemed larger, her lines more vigorous. In the dim light, she possessed the air of a sorceress who might stretch forth a magical hand to brew potions for others, yet who scorned to mix them for herself, having already drunk her fill of youth.

As Deronda stepped forward, she threw her arms around his shoulders, kissing him on both cheeks with a sudden, fierce warmth. Then, seating herself among cushions with an air of assured firmness and regal dignity—a stark contrast to the erratic volatility of their first meeting—she gestured for him to sit beside her.

"You are quite relieved now, I trust?" he asked softly.

"Yes, I am at ease again," she replied, her tone carrying the matter-of-fact gravity of a queen rather than the intimacy of a mother. "Is there anything more that you would like to ask me?"

"Can I find the house in Genoa where you used to live with my grandfather?" Deronda ventured.

"No," she answered, waving her arm dismissively. "It is pulled down—not to be found. As for the details of our family, and where my father lived at various times, you will find everything among the papers in the chest. It is better than I can tell you. My father was a physician. My mother was a Morteira. I used to hear all those stories without truly listening. You will find them all. I was born amidst them against my will, but I banished them the moment I could."

Deronda struggled to conceal the pang that struck his heart. "Anything else that I should desire to know from you could only be what it is some satisfaction to your own feelings to tell me."

"I think I have told you everything that could be demanded of me," the Princess said, her gaze turning cold and meditative. It seemed she believed she had exhausted every emotional reservoir during their previous encounter. In truth, she had told herself, *"I have done it all. I have confessed all. I will not go through it again. I will save myself from agitation."* And thus, she played out this rigid script.

Yet, for Daniel Deronda, the moment was cruel. It transformed his lifelong filial yearning into a disappointed pilgrimage to a shrine stripped of its sacred symbols. It felt as though all the woman lacking within her mother now dwelt entirely within him. With a tremor in his voice, he asked, "Then are we to part, and I never be anything to you?"

"It is better so," the Princess said, her voice suddenly softening to a mellower hue. "There could be nothing but hard duty for you, even if it were possible for you to take the place of my son. You would not love me." She cut him off abruptly, raising a hand to forestall denial. "Don't deny it. I know what is the truth. You don't like what I did. You are angry with me. You think I robbed you of something. You are on your grandfather's side, and you will always harbor a condemnation of me in your heart."

Deronda felt placed under a ban of silence. Rising from his seat, he preferred to stand, obeying whatever imperious prohibition forbade any display of tenderness. But then his mother looked up at him, her gaze shifting to a new, startled admiration.

"You are wrong to be angry with me," she said. "You are the better for what I did." After a pause, she added sharply, "And now tell me what you shall do?"

"Do you mean now, immediately," Deronda asked, "or as to the course of my future life?"

"I mean in the future. What difference will it make to you that I have told you about your birth?"

"A very great difference," Deronda affirmed emphatically. "I can hardly think of anything that would make a greater difference."

"What shall you do then?" the Princess pressed, her tone sharpening. "Make yourself just like your grandfather—be what he wished you—turn yourself into a Jew like him?"

"That is impossible," Deronda replied, his voice gaining tenacity. "The effect of my education can never be undone. The Christian sympathies in which my mind was reared can never die out of me." He continued, his conviction unshaken, "But I consider it my duty—it is the impulse of my feeling—to identify myself, as far as possible, with my hereditary people. And if I can see any work to be done for them to which I can give my soul and hand, I shall choose to do it."

The Princess fixed her eyes on him with a wondering speculation, examining his face as if trying to read a difficult, ancient language. He met her gaze steadily, sustained by a resolute opposition that expressed his fullest self. She leaned closer, speaking with decisive emphasis, "You are in love with a Jewess."

Deronda blushed. "My reasons would be independent of any such fact."

"I know better. I have seen what men are," she said peremptorily. "Tell me the truth. She is a Jewess who will not accept anyone but a Jew. There *are* a few such," she added, touching her lips with a hint of scorn.
