Deronda faced the difficulty of answering those who interpret reality with such absolute certainty that enlightenment becomes impossible. Yet, beyond this, the immediate point was one on which he felt a profound repugnance to either deny or affirm. He remained silent. Presently, the Princess spoke again, "You love her as your father loved me, and she draws you after her as I drew him."

Those words struck deep into Deronda's filial imagination. A tenderness flickered in his glance, which his mother seized as an assent. Her passion rose. "But I was leading him the other way. And now your grandfather is getting his revenge."

"Mother," Deronda remonstrated gently, "let us not think of it in that way. I will admit that there may come some benefit from the education you chose for me. I prefer cherishing that benefit with gratitude to dwelling with resentment on the injury. I think it would have been right that I should have been brought up with the consciousness that I was a Jew, but it must always have been a good to me to have as wide an instruction and sympathy as possible. And now, you have restored me my inheritance—events have brought a fuller restitution than you could have made. You have been saved from robbing my people of my service and me of my duty. Can you not bring your whole soul to consent to this?"

Deronda paused in his plea. His mother listened intently, as if the cadence of his voice were captivating her ear, yet she shook her head slowly. He began again, with growing urgency.

"You have told me that you sought what you held the best for me. Open your heart to relenting and love toward my grandfather, who sought what he held the best for you."

"Not for me, no!" she cried, shaking her head with absolute denial and folding her arms tightly. "I tell you, he never thought of his daughter except as an instrument. Because I had wants outside his purpose, I was to be put in a frame and tortured. If that is the right law for the world, I will not say that I love it. If my acts were wrong—if it is God who is exacting from me that I should deliver up what I withheld—then who is punishing me because I deceived my father and did not warn him that I should contradict his trust? Well, I have told everything. I have done what I could. And *your* soul consents. That is enough. I have, after all, been the instrument my father wanted. 'I desire a grandson who shall have a true Jewish heart. Every Jew should rear his family as if he hoped that a Deliverer might spring from it.'"

Uttering these final sentences, the Princess narrowed her eyes, waved her head up and down, and spoke slowly with a strange, resonant chest-voice, as if quoting words she barely wished to utter.

"Were those my grandfather's words?" Deronda asked.

"Yes, yes; and you will find them written. I wanted to thwart him," the Princess admitted, with a sudden outburst of the passionate fury she had shown in their first interview. Then, more slowly, "You would have me love what I have hated since the time I was so high"—here she held her left hand a yard from the floor—"That can never be. But what does it matter? His yoke has been on me, whether I loved it or not. You are the grandson he wanted. You speak as men do—as if you felt yourself wise. What does it all mean?"

Her tone was abrupt and scornful. Deronda, wracked by painful feeling and the solemn urgency of the moment, had to cling desperately to a

"The unwilling brain feigns often what it would not..." Shelley's words echoed in Gwendolen Harleth's mind as the Mediterranean stretched out beneath her, a vast, indifferent blue separating her from the world she had left behind. Here, aboard Grandcourt's yacht, she was trapped in a gilded cage of cedar and silk, surrounded by the soft, oppressive scent of luxury that masked a rotting core. To the casual observer, perhaps even the foreign maids who feared seasickness, they appeared the epitome of a model couple in high life. They moved together with a well-bred silence, devoid of humor or chit-chat, a performance of royal despotism played out on a miniature stage.

Yet, beneath the surface of this polished exterior, Gwendolen's soul was a battlefield. She had sold her truthfulness and her sense of justice to secure this position, trading her integrity for the maintenance of her mother and the status of Mrs. Grandcourt. Now, however, the price she had paid was exacting a terrible toll. The beauty of the sea, the gentle breeze, the changing landscapes of Balearic islands and Sardinia offered no solace. Instead, they amplified a hunger in her heart that sicknessed the eye for beauty and made sweet-scented ease an oppression.

Her husband, Lord Grandcourt, seemed utterly oblivious to the tempest raging within her. He viewed their isolation not as a punishment, but as a triumph. He was a man who understood the mechanics of power intimately, believing himself immune to the moral repulsions that plagued ordinary men. He saw no contradiction in his own attractiveness and the "foolery" of sentimentality, nor did he comprehend the slow, corrosive decay of Gwendolen's affection. To him, her silence was compliance; her avoidance of his gaze was merely a lady's modesty. He believed that by fulfilling his side of the contract—providing rank, luxury, and security—he had secured her obedience. He did not realize that she had not married him out of love, nor that her conscience had awakened a purgatorial fire within her.

Their days drifted in a monotonous rhythm of tea, cigars, and silent strolls. Grandcourt would point out sails on the horizon or comment on the quality of the wine, expecting a suitable response, while Gwendolen played her part, offering polite, empty answers. Inside, however, her mind was a whirlwind of terrifying possibilities. She felt a profound dread, not just of her husband, but of herself. The vision of her past wrongs haunted her, casting a pale, ghastly light over every imagined attempt at freedom. She dreaded the thought of becoming a mother, fearing that a child would seal the injustice she had committed, binding her further to a life of distaste.

Hope, when it flickered, took the form of Daniel Deronda. He was the only anchor in this drifting ship, a terrible-browed angel whose restraint and moral strength offered a path to redemption. She clung to his memory, to the words he had spoken, turning her fear into a safeguard. "Keep your dread fixed on the idea of increasing your remorse," he had said. These words became her mantra, a shield against the demons of hatred and the impulses that threatened to consume her. She prayed silently, her lips sealed, for a sign, for a direction, for any intervention that might save her from the abyss of her own making.

The journey eventually led them to Genoa, where the ship was forced to stop for repairs following a squall. For Gwendonen, this delay brought a strange, fleeting sense of hope. She dreamed of escaping over the snowy peaks of Mont Cenis, only to wake with Deronda standing beside her on the palace stairs of the *Italia*. But the illusion shattered instantly when she realized Grandcourt was there. His presence transformed the encounter from a potential reunion into a confrontation. He saw in her altered demeanor, the new elasticity in her step, a sign of secret delight—a conspiracy against him. He interpreted her joy as anticipation of a tryst with Deronda, a betrayal he was convinced she was plotting.

The tension in the hotel room was palpable. Grandcourt, cool and calculating, sensed a shift in her resolve, a "fresh ease" that he mistook for defiance. When he suggested a boat ride, intending to assert his control once more, Gwendolen felt a chill run through her. The gleaming contentment vanished, replaced by a glacier-like coldness. She refused the boat, a spontaneous rebellion that terrified Grandcourt. He was used to total submission, and her resistance, however brief, unsettled him. He saw it as a challenge, a refusal to play the role of the docile wife.

Ultimately, his dominance prevailed. He ordered the boat, forcing her back onto the deck, where they sailed away in a tableau of pale, impassive elegance. As the boat cut through the water, Gwendolen's internal struggle intensified. Guiding the tiller under his watchful eyes, she felt the weight of his command pressing down on her. She was not afraid of the ocean's dangers, but of her own wishes, of the fierce, gathering hatred that burned within her. Images of evil deeds, of murder and despair, swirled through her mind like furies, threatening to seize her in the night.

As the sun dipped lower, casting a golden glow over the city and the mountains, Gwendolen's composure finally cracked. In a barely audible whisper, she cried out, "God help me!" The words slipped from her lips before she could stop them, a confession of her inner turmoil. Grandcourt, distracted by the navigation, did not hear her clearly. When she resumed her duties, feigning cheer, the tragedy of her situation deepened. She admitted that nothing seemed better than staying put, comparing their endless voyage to the *Flying Dutchman*, doomed to wander until Judgment Day. But inside, Gwendolen knew the truth: they were not wandering aimlessly. They were drifting toward a storm, and the only salvation lay in the moral compass of the man she loved, waiting for him to steer her back from the edge of ruin.

"The return to your knowledge,
Which seeks how much the thing is more perfect,
More feels the good, and thus the pain."
—Dante.

When Daniel Deronda encountered Gwendolen and Grandcourt upon the stairs, his heart was a storm-tossed sea, utterly preoccupied. Just minutes prior, he had been summoned for a second, harrowing interview with his mother. Now, standing amidst the bustle of the hotel, he felt a strange paralysis. Two hours after leaving her, the Princess Halm-Eberstein had departed, leaving the path clear for Deronda to journey to Mainz, to deliver Joseph Kalonymos's letter and claim the family chest. Yet, a heavy, indeterminate weight anchored him to Genoa. It was not logic, nor duty, but a retrospective grief that held him fast. In the solitude of his own mind, he relived the scene with a fresh, piercing acuity, weeping silently over a life so intimately connected to his own destiny, yet so tragically distant. He felt the world shift beneath his feet; the certitude of ties binding him to a lineage of wanderers altered the balance of his hopes and fears. Under the cover of night, he realized he had wandered in the wrong company, only to find, as dawn approached, that his kindred pitched their tents far away. A quivering imagination stirred within him, sensing a close bond with a grandfather whose strong impulses and deep loves were perhaps waking from a long slumber inside his own soul. Through this passionate meditation, Mordecai and Mirah stood ever present, clasping hands with him in a silent, sympathetic unity.

Such was the fiber of Deronda, quick and responsive, draped beneath a mantle of self-controlled reserve forged by early hardship.

A persistent bell signaled the hour, urging him to consult Bradshaw and prepare for the next train to Mainz. He thought of it, then thought of London, of the beings there who formed the strongest attachments of his life. But other wishes clung to Genoa, rooted in a force that makes us linger over interviews carrying the shadow of final farewells. He did not consciously decide to wait until Friday evening to attend the synagogue service, hoping to see the Grandcourts again. Instead, simply, he sat doing nothing, refusing to pack or ring for his bill. His mind drifted to the synagogue, imagining the faces there—perhaps unchanged from his grandfather's time—and hearing the Spanish-Hebrew liturgy. It sounded like a plant with wandering seeds, spreading across seasons and exiles, weaving a kinship between far-off lands and the homes of the displaced. Simultaneously, his thoughts turned to Gwendolen, haunted by anxious memories of what had passed. There lingered a half-admitted fear that it would be cruel, a form of hardness, to leave immediately without making some effort, despite Grandcourt's likely disapproval, to show his continued sympathy following their abrupt separation.

Thus, he deferred his departure. Dinner tasted of ash, and he rose quickly, asking the porter if Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt remained in the hotel and for their apartment number. The porter supplied the number but added, with a casual shrug, that they had gone out boating. This single piece of news possessed a power sufficient to split Deronda's focus, dividing it with the memories awakened among the sparse *talithim* and the keen, dark faces of worshippers. Their ease in handling awful prayers and invocations, with a familiarity that seemed like Hebrew dyed Italian, struck him deeply. He reflected that his grandfather, according to the Princess's hints, must have been nearly as exceptional a Jew as Mordecai himself. Were not men of ardent zeal and far-reaching hope everywhere exceptional? Those who hold visions, as Mordecai described, are the creators and feeders of the world, molding the passive lives that would otherwise wither into the narrow tenacity of insects, unshaken by thoughts beyond the reach of their antennae. A mournful impatience mingled with his growing solicitude for Gwendolen, urging him to hasten from the synagogue. He chose an evening walk toward the quay, a favorite haunt, drawn by the possibility that he might catch the Grandcourts returning from their boating trip. If so, he resolved to approach them deliberately, ignoring any grounds Grandcourt might have for wishing him elsewhere.

The sun had dipped behind a bank of clouds, casting only a faint, dying yellow light upon the waves, which churned under an active breeze. Deronda sauntered slowly within sight of the strand, observing the groups concentrating their attention on a sailing-boat approaching swiftly toward land, rowed by two men. Amidst the clamorous chatter in various tongues, Deronda suppressed questions, pushing his way to the foreground to become an unobstructed witness. Telescopes were raised, and loud speculations erupted. "It is the *milord* who has drowned!" one cried. Another insisted the prostrate figure seen below decks was *miladi*. A Frenchman, lacking a glass, ventured that it was *milord*, perhaps taking his wife out to drown her, a notion echoing a certain national practice. An English skipper retorted in his native idiom, dismissing the idea as nonsense that had "undergone a mining operation," finally deciding the reclining figure was indeed a woman. For Deronda, terribly excited by fluctuating terrors, the rhythm of the oars was shattered by swift visions of events, both possible and impossible, leading to this tragic conclusion—or a broken fragment thereof, with a worse half concealed. Could the woman snatched from the waters truly be Mrs. Grandcourt?

Soon, however, doubt vanished. The boat was pulled to shore. Deronda saw Gwendolen, half-raising herself on her hands by sheer effort, encased in a heavy covering of tarpaulins and pea-jackets. She was pale as one of the sheeted dead, shivering, with wet hair streaming down her face. Her eyes held a wild, amazed consciousness, as if she had woken in a world where judgment hung suspended, and the beings surrounding her were coming to seize her. The first rower who leaped onto the sand was soaked through and ran off. The sailors crowded around the boat, blocking Deronda's advance. He could only watch as Gwendolen cast scared glances, shrinking with terror as she was carefully, tenderly lifted out and led away by the strong, bronzed arms of the rough men. Her wet clothes clung to her limbs, adding to the impediment of her weakness. Suddenly, her wandering eyes fell upon Deronda, standing before her. Immediately, as if she had been expecting him, searching for him, she tried to stretch out her arms, though they were held back by her supporters. In a muffled voice, she whispered, "It is come, it is come! He is dead!"

"Hush, hush!" Deronda commanded, his tone authoritative yet gentle. "Quiet yourself." Turning to the men assisting her, he declared, "I am a connection of this lady's husband. If you will get her aboard the *Italia* as quickly as possible, I will undertake everything else."

He remained to learn from the remaining boatman that her husband had gone down irrecoverably, his boat left floating empty in the water. They, along with their companion, had heard a cry and arrived just in time to see the lady jump in after her husband, saving her from terrible damage.

Afterward, Deronda hastened to the hotel to ensure the best medical aid was provided. Once satisfied on this point, he telegraphed the event to Sir Hugo, begging him to arrive forthwith, and also to Mr. Gascoigne, whose residence at the rectory offered the nearest known route to inform Gwendolen's mother. Certain words Gwendolen had spoken in the past returned to him with the effectiveness of an inspiration: in moments of agitated confession, she had often mentioned her mother's presence as a possible help, if she could have secured it.

"The pang, the curse with which they died,
Had never passed away:
I could not draw my eyes from theirs,
Nor lift them up to pray."

Deronda did not undress that night. Gwendolen, after insisting on seeing him again before consenting to be stripped, had fallen into a profound, heavy silence. She had whispered to him with a repressed, burning eagerness, asking only that he promise to return when she summoned him in the morning. Yet a shadow lingered within him, a foreboding born of the fragility of her state. The possibility that a sudden fever might seize her, or that the lingering shock of the catastrophe could manifest in wild, broken words, weighed heavily on his mind. He instructed her attendant to call him instantly at any sign of alarm, making it clear to everyone that he was in constant communication with her friends in England and felt a binding duty to guard her safety. This role fell easily to him, for he was well known to Grandcourt's sole remaining servant, the old valet who had accompanied the late voyage.

When exhaustion from the wildly varied emotions of the day finally claimed Deronda, he slept, only to be torn from it by a tangled web of nightmares spun from yesterday's events. These visions woke him with an image drawn by his pressing anxiety. But morning had come, and there had been no summons—an augury that cheered him as he dressed, leading him to reflect that it was perhaps too early to inquire further. Later, he learned that she had endured a wakeful night but showed no violent signs of agitation, eventually succumbing to slumber. He marveled at the sheer force dwelling in this creature, so acutely alive to dread; he carried an irresistible impression that even under the effects of a severe physical shock, she was mastering herself with a determination of absolute concealment. As for his own sensibilities, he believed they had been dulled by the raw intensity of meeting his mother. To him, his current cares for Gwendolen seemed merely the fulfillment of claims, his more passionate sympathy lying in abeyance. Having lived so keenly in an experience quite apart from Gwendolen's lot, his present attentions felt like a revisiting of familiar scenes, lacking yet the complete revival of inner response.

In the meantime, Deronda set about obtaining a formal, legally recognized statement from the fishermen who had rescued Gwendolen. Few details emerged. The yacht in which Grandcourt had gone out was found drifting with its sail loose and was towed back. The fishermen surmised that Grandcourt had been knocked overboard by the flapping sail while tacking, noting that he likely could not swim. However, though they were nearby, their attention had first been arrested by a cry resembling that of a man in distress. While they hastened with their oars, they heard a shriek from the lady and saw her plunge into the water.

Upon returning to the hotel, Deronda was informed that Gwendolen had risen and desired to see him. He was shown into a room darkened by blinds and heavy curtains. Seated therein, she wore a white shawl wrapped around her, looking toward the open door like one waiting uneasily. Her long hair was gathered up and coiled carefully, and through it all, the blue stars in her ears retained their place. As she started impulsively to her full height, sheathed in white shawl, her face and neck not less pallid—save for a purple line beneath her eyes, her lips slightly apart with the peculiar expression of one accused and helpless—she appeared like the unhappy ghost of Gwendolen Harleth whom Deronda had last seen turning from her losses at the gaming table with firm lips and proud self-possession. The sight pierced him with pity, and the effects of all their past relations began to revive within him.

"I beseech you to rest—not to stand," said Deronda as he approached her. She obeyed, falling back into her chair.

"Will you sit down near me?" she asked. "I want to speak very low."

She sat in a large armchair, and he drew a small one near her side. The action touched her peculiarly. Turning her pale face fully upon his, which hovered very close, she spoke in the lowest audible tone, "You know I am a guilty woman?"

Deronda himself turned paler as he replied, "I know nothing." He dared not say more.

"He is dead." She uttered these words with the same undertoned decision.

"Yes," said Deronda, in a mournful suspense that made him reluctant to speak.

"His face will not be seen above the water again," Gwendolen said, her tone not louder but of suppressed, frantic eagerness, holding both hands clenched.

"No."

"Not by any one else—only by me. A dead face... I shall never get away from it."

With an inward voice of desperate self-repression, she spoke these final words, gazing away from Deronda toward something distant on the floor. She was seeing the whole event—her own acts included—through an exaggerating medium of excitement and horror. Was she in a state of delirium, entering a realm of concealment and necessity for self-repression? Such thoughts flashed through Deronda as a sort of hope. But imagine the conflict of feeling that kept him silent. She was bent on confession, and he dreaded hearing it. Against his better will, he shrank from the task laid upon him; he wished, and yet rebuked the wish as cowardly, that she could bury her secrets in her own bosom. He was not a priest. He dreaded the weight of this woman's soul flung upon his own with imploring dependence.

Yet she spoke again, hurriedly, looking at him.

"You will not say that I ought to tell the world? You will not say that I ought to be disgraced? I could not do it. I could not bear it. I cannot have my mother know. Not if I were dead. I could not have her know. I must tell you; but you will not say that any one else should know."

"I can say nothing in my ignorance," said Deronda, mournfully, "except that I desire to help you."

"I told you from the beginning—as soon as I could—that I was afraid of myself." There was a piteous pleading in the low murmur to which Deronda turned his ear only. Her face afflicted him too much. "I felt a hatred in me that was always working like an evil spirit—contriving things. Everything I could do to free myself came into my mind; and it got worse—all things got worse. That is why I asked you to come to me in town. I thought then I would tell you the worst about myself. I tried. But I could not tell everything. And *he* came in."

She paused, while a shudder passed through her, before continuing.

"I will tell you everything now. Do you think a woman who cried, and prayed, and struggled to be saved from herself, could be a murderess?"

"Great God!" said Deronda, in a deep, shaken voice. "Don't torture me needlessly. You have not murdered him. You threw yourself into the water with the impulse to save him. Tell me the rest afterward. This death was an accident that you could not have hindered."

"Don't be impatient with me." The tremor, the childlike beseeching in these words compelled Deronda to turn his head and look at her face. The poor quivering lips continued. "You said—you used to say—you felt more for those who had done something wicked and were miserable; you said they might get better—they might be scourged into something better. If you had not spoken in that way, everything would have been worse. I *did* remember all you said to me. It came to me always. It came to me at the very last—that was the reason why I—But now, if you cannot bear with me when I tell you everything—if you turn away from me and forsake me, what shall I do? Am I worse than I was when you found me and wanted to make me better? All the wrong I have done was in me then—and more—and more—if you had not come and been patient with me. And now—will you forsake me?"

Her hands, which had been so tightly clenched moments before, were now helplessly relaxed and trembling on the arm of her chair. Her quivering lips remained parted as she ceased speaking. Deronda could not answer; he was obliged to look away. He took one of her hands and clasped it as if they were going to walk together like two children; it was the only way in which he could answer, "I will not forsake you." And all the while he felt as if he were putting his name to a blank paper which might be filled up terribly. Their attitude, his averred face with its expression of a suffering which he was solemnly resolved to undergo, might have told half the truth of the situation to a beholder who had suddenly entered.

That grasp was an entirely new experience to Gwendolen: she had never before received from any man a sign of tenderness which her own being had needed, and she interpreted its powerful effect upon her into a promise of inexhaustible patience and constancy. The stream of renewed strength made it possible for her to proceed as she had begun—with that fitful, wandering confession where the sameness of experience seemed to nullify the sense of time or order in events. She began again in a fragmentary way,

"All sorts of contrivances in my mind—but all so difficult. And I fought against them—I was

"The unripe grape, the ripe, and the dried. All things change, not into nothing, but into that which is not yet." So wrote Marcus Aurelius, a man who understood the weight of time. For deeds are the pulse of existence; whether righteous or flawed, they beat onward long after the actor has ceased, echoing until the world falls silent.

It was late afternoon when she summoned him again. The light within the room was soft, muted by drawn blinds and tempered by the gentle breeze drifting through open windows. It was nearly the hour of her arrival the previous evening, brought in from the sea, yet here she sat, gazing fixedly at the water. Resting her cheek against her hand, she appeared less shattered than before, though a profound melancholy clouded her features. As Daniel Deronda approached, that sadness shifted into an anxious timidity. She did not offer her hand. "How long ago it is!" she whispered, then, "Will you sit near me again a little while?"

He moved to her side, just as he had before. Seeing her turn to him with an expression that hinted at a desire to speak, he waited patiently. Yet she looked back toward the window, silent, turning again with the same look, yet finding no words. Some fear held her tongue. To soothe her hesitation, Daniel averted his gaze. Soon, however, he heard her voice, imploring and thin.

"You will not say that anyone else should know?"

"Most decidedly not," Daniel replied firmly. "No action needs to be taken because of this. No injury exists that could be corrected by revelation. No retribution lies within the power of any mortal to dispense fairly."

For a long moment, she remained perfectly still, as if holding her breath. Then, softly, "But if I had not had that murderous impulse—that single moment—if I had cast the rope instantly... perhaps it would have prevented death?"

"No, I do not think so," Daniel answered slowly. "If he possessed the ability to swim, he must have succumbed to cramp. With your swiftest, most desperate effort, it seems impossible that you could have altered the outcome. That fleeting, dark will of yours likely could not have changed the course of events. Its impact remains confined to the motives within your own breast. Inside us, evil intent is mighty; sooner or later, it spills outward. Perhaps it breeds the vices that lead to wicked acts, but equally, it may forge the self-loathing that drives us toward better striving."

"I am saved from stealing from others," Gwendolen murmured, her voice trembling. "There are others who will take everything—their due share. I knew this sometime before I left town. Did you not suspect me of such dark desires regarding those matters?" She spoke with great hesitation.

"I had not considered that angle," Daniel admitted, "my mind was consumed by the immediate tragedy."

"Perhaps you do not quite grasp the beginning of it all," Gwendolen said slowly, as if battling a heavy reluctance. "There was another whom he ought to have married. I knew it, and I promised her I would stand aside. I left town—that was when you first saw me. But then we fell into poverty all at once, and misery overtook me. I was tempted. I told myself, 'I shall do as I please and set everything right.' I convinced myself. And everything changed. It became terrible. Then came hatred, and wicked thoughts. That was how it unraveled. I told you I feared myself. And I did what you advised—I tried to let that fear guard me. I imagined what would happen if I acted, feeling the dread of the morning approaching, wishing forever for night, yet even in the darkness, I saw something, saw death. If you truly knew how wretched I was, you might understand—but now, it has been useless. I care for nothing but shielding the rest from knowledge, especially poor Mamma, who has never known happiness."

Silence stretched between them before she spoke, her voice choked with a repressed sob. "You cannot bear to look at me anymore. You think I am too wicked. You do not believe I can become better—worth anything, worthy enough. I shall always be too wicked..." Her voice broke, leaving her helpless.

Daniel's heart pierced. He turned his eyes to her humble, beseeching face. "I believe you may become worthier than you have ever been," he said gently. "Worthy to lead a life that brings blessing. No evil condemns us to hopeless doom unless we love it, desire to continue it, and refuse to escape it. You *have* made efforts, Gwendolen. You will continue to make them."

"But you were the start of them," Gwendolen insisted, leaning forward with clasped hands, her gaze locked on his. Her face bore the piteous marks of a life experience compressed into twenty-four hours—a terrifying new reality lying just beyond the act that fulfilled a criminal desire. "I will endure any penance. I will live any life you command. But you must not abandon me. You must stay close. Had you been near me—if I could have poured my soul into your ears, I would have been different. Will you not abandon me?"

"It could never be my impulse to leave you," Daniel said promptly. His voice, like his eyes, carried an unintentional warmth that made his ready sympathy feel deeply personal, almost exclusive. In that moment, however, he was not entirely free from a premonition of a self-imprisonment. His fierce compassion for this stricken creature could not stop the rush of images regarding future difficulties. Though he met her pleading eyes, he did so with the painful awareness that her ear might hear a promise that time would eventually prove unfulfilled. He was offering an indefinite vow to an indefinite hope. Immediate and distant anxieties crowded his mind, shaping his next words.

"After a brief silence," he said, "I expect Sir Hugo Mallinger to arrive by tomorrow night at the latest. I harbor hope that Mrs. Davilow will follow him soon. Her presence will be your greatest comfort; it will give you a motive to spare her further pain."

"Yes, yes—I will try," Gwendolen agreed eagerly. "And you will not depart?"

"Not until Sir Hugo arrives."

"But shall we all travel to England?"

"As soon as possible," Daniel replied, avoiding specifics.

Gwendolen turned her gaze back to the window, an expression suggesting a gradual awakening to new thoughts. The twilight deepened perceptibly, yet Daniel observed a stirring in her eyes and hands, a return of perception in someone dazed by shock.

"You will always be with Sir Hugo now!" she said, looking at him. "You will always live at the Abbey—or perhaps at Diplow?"

"I am quite uncertain where I shall reside," Daniel said, blushing slightly.

Sensing his shift in color, Gwendolen realized she had spoken too hastily and fell silent. After a pause, she began again, eyes averted. "It is impossible to foresee how my life will unfold. I think now it would be better for me to be poor and compelled to labor."

"New prompts will emerge as the days pass," Daniel reassured her. "Amidst your friends again, you will discern fresh duties. Make it your current task to regain your strength and composure—to become as calm and composed as you were, before—" He hesitated.

"Before my mother returns," Gwendolen finished. "Ah! I must change. I have not examined myself. Would you recognize me," she asked, turning to face him, tears gathering, "if you met me now? Would you know me for the girl you saw at Leubronn?"

"Yes, I would recognize you," Daniel said, his tone mournful. "The external change is slight. I would have seen immediately that it was you, and that you had endured some great sorrow."

"Do not wish now that you had never seen me," Gwendolen implored, "do not wish that."

"I would despise myself for wishing it," Daniel confessed. "How could I possibly know what I wished? We must find our duties in what comes to us, not in imaginary alternatives. If I indulged in such foolish longing, I would not wish that I had never met you, but rather that I had been able to save you from this."

"You have saved me from worse," Gwendolen sobbed. "I would have been far worse had it not been for you. If you had not been good, I would have become more wicked than I am."

"It will be best for me to leave now," Daniel said, his spirit weary from the relentless strain of the scene. "Remember what we discussed regarding your task—to recover your health and peace before other friends arrive."

As he rose, she extended her hand to him in submission. But when he departed, she sank to her knees, overcome by hysterical weeping. The distance between them was vast. She was a soul exiled, beholding a possible life from which she had, through sin, severed herself.

She was discovered thus, crushed upon the floor. Such grief seemed natural for a gentle lady whose husband had perished in her sight.

"The trouble was immense, God knows;
He loved, and she refused."
—Nicholas Breton.

Time, it seems, measures itself by things far too imperfect to gauge the true depth of life. The vast distance the sun travels tells us nothing of how our hearts have aged, just as the acres of a field reveal nothing of the seeds quietly sprouting beneath the soil. A man might march south and stumble over a bone, meditating on it until he uncovers a new path for anatomy; or turn east and find a key to language that unlocks a forgotten saga of races. He might lead an expedition that carves new continents, suffer a maiming, and endure a whole epic of heroism and grit. Yet, when he returns after a few short months, he finds his neighbors still grumbling over the same parish grievances, sees the same elderly gentleman pacing the pavement, shaking his head at the same butcher's boy, and lingering at the same shop window to gaze at the same prints. If the sharpest thought moves with the speed of a greyhound, the slowest creeps along like a limpet, appearing stuck until, over a long while, it is revealed to be a steady, if invisible, advance. These disparities define the variable rhythm of human existence—from the thunderous surge of revolution that births a new soul, to the quiet, recurring hum of the familiar, marked only by hunger and the turning sky.

A stark reflection of this duality was visible in the year's passage. The brilliant, self-assured Gwendolen Harleth of the Archery Meeting had metamorphosed into a crushed penitent, driven to confess her unworthiness where, once, it would have been her greatest joy to be deemed worthy. Meanwhile, her family at Pennicote Rectory remained largely unchanged, save for outward habits and a recalibration of hopes to fit a reduced income, fewer visitors, and paler compliments. The rectory retained its former charm; the red and pink peonies on the lawn and the rows of hollyhocks by the hedges bloomed as vibrantly this season as last. The rector, Mr. Gascoigne, kept his cheerful faith in the goodwill of his patrons and his determination to earn it through diligent duty, regardless of whether anyone heard of it. He wrote two ecclesiastical articles annually, unsigned, attributing them to anonymous sages, though the patrons who received private copies surely knew his hand. Yet, he chewed no bitter cud of suspicion regarding their silence; instead, he added marginal notes to his own drafts to make them engaging loans, finding solace in the fact that the Archdeacon and other authorities took no issue with his general argument. There was a peaceful quality to his authorship—an existence in the air of the fields and downs, untouched by the "thrice-breathed breath" of criticism. It brought no Dante-like emaciation but rather assisted his nutrition with complacency, offering perhaps a more pervasive sense of accomplishment than the creation of a *Divina Commedia*.

Then there was the father's recovered delight in his favorite son, a happiness that outweighed the loss of eighteen hundred pounds a year. Whatever hidden shift Rex had undergone following the disappointment of his first love, it appeared secondary to the evidence of serious ambition sparked by the family's misfortune. Indeed, Mr. Gascoigne tended to view the previous year's distress—the "little affair"—as merely an evaporation of superfluous moisture, akin to the final stage of baking bread where the dough demands a specific finish. Rex had recently returned for a summer visit, bringing Anna home. While he displayed the old liveness with his siblings, he maintained his holiday habits of an eager student, rising early and closing himself away late to pursue a rigorous course of study.

"Do you regret choosing the law as your profession, Rex?" his father asked.

"There is no profession I would prefer," Rex replied. "I wish to spend my life as a first-rate judge and help draft a code. I reverse the famous saying. 'Give me something to do with making the laws, and let whoever wishes compose the songs.'"

"You'll have to stuff yourself with an immense amount of rubbish, I suppose—that's the downside," the rector noted.

"I don't see that legal rubbish is worse than any other kind. It isn't as bad as the trivial literature that clogs the mind. It doesn't dull one as much. Our wisest men have often been lawyers. Any methodical approach to viewing things as cases and evidence seems superior to a constant deluge of disjointed fragments leading nowhere. From a higher perspective, the foundations and evolution of law offer the most compelling insights into philosophy and history. Of course, there will be much drudgery, perhaps exasperation. But the greatest prizes in life cannot be won easily—I have learned that."

"Well, my boy, the finest omen of a man's success in his vocation is that he believes it to be the finest in the world. But I suspect most workers feel this when they enter with a will. Brewitt, the blacksmith, told me the other day that his apprentice lacked a mind for the trade. 'Yet, sir,' said Brewitt, 'what would a young fellow have if he didn't like blacksmithing?'

The rector treasured a paternal delight, allowing it to surface only in moderation. He had easily endured the departure of Warham, who had gone to India, but Rex represented the romance of later life—a son he recognized as superior to himself, envisioning a future eminence modeled on famous examples. To his wife, he declared with conviction, "Rex will be a distinguished man, Nancy, I am sure of it—as sure as Paley's father was about his son."

"Was Paley an old bachelor?" asked Mrs. Gascoigne.

"That is hardly relevant, my dear," the rector replied, forgetting the irrelevant detail. Mrs. Gascoigne felt her question had sounded somewhat weak.

This tranquil progression of time at the rectory was mirrored in the household across the road, the modest white house nestled among evergreens, known to villagers as "Jodson's." Mrs. Davilow's delicate face bore only a slight deepening of her mild melancholy, her hair sporting just a few additional silver strands due to the trials of the last year. Her four daughters had blossomed slightly, emerging from the shadows, while the good Jocosa maintained her neutral stance toward the world's pleasures and glories, reserving them for those not "in a situation."

In the low, narrow drawing room, expanded by two quaint projecting windows with lattices wide open to the scent of monthly roses on a July afternoon, the air was thick with the faint murmur of the garden and the occasional, clarifying ring of hooves and wheels. The scene was lively and crowded, Rex and Anna joining the usual group of six. Anna, always a favorite with her younger cousins, delighted them with tales of her London experiences and the acquaintances she'd made. On her first solo visit, countless inquiries had been directed at her regarding Gwendolen's residence in Grosvenor Square, what Gwendolen herself had said, and the gossip surrounding her. Had Anna visited Gwendolen after learning about the yacht? No. An answer that left speculation wild regarding the mysterious vessel, beyond the fact that Gwendolen had written just prior to departure stating she and Mr. Grandcourt were yachting in the Mediterranean, and again from Marseilles, expressing certainty that she would enjoy the trip, the cabins were elegant, and she wouldn't write again until she had filled pages with *ditto*s. Furthermore, this movement of Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt had been mentioned in "the newspapers," adding a striking dimension to the sisters' romantic narratives, with the book-devouring Isabel tossing in a corsair or two to ensure a happy ending.

However, when Rex was present, the girls, adhering to instructions, avoided this captivating topic. Today, conversation focused on the Meyricks and their extraordinary Jewish friends, eliciting astonished questions from minds for whom the idea of living Jews, outside of books, suggested a difference almost zoological—as if a strange race from Pliny's *Natural History* sleeping under the shade of its own ears. Bertha could not conceive what Jews believed now, harboring a vague notion that they rejected the Old Testament since it validated the New. Miss Merry thought Mirah and her brother "could never have been properly argued with," while the amiable Alice expressed indifference to Jewish beliefs, simply stating she "couldn't bear them." Mrs. Davilow gently corrected her, noting that the great Jewish families in society were perfectly respectable in both London and Paris, though admitting that lesser, unconverted Jews were objectionable. Isabel asked if Mirah spoke as they did, or if one could be with her without realizing she was a Jewess.

Rex, possessing no partisanship for the Israelites and having acquired a troubling familiarity with the minutiae of their ancient history through "cramming," amused himself by playfully exaggerating each speaker's notion, until Anna interceded, begging them to understand he was merely joking. Their laughter was cut short by the delivery of a letter for Mrs. Davilow. A messenger had brought it in great haste from the rectory. Inside lay a telegram. As Mrs. Davilow read and reread it in silence and agitation, all eyes turned to her with anxiety, yet no one dared speak. Finally looking up, seeing the young faces "painted with fear," she realized they might be imagining horrors worse than the truth, recalling her own first dread that rendered the words unintelligible. With a sob that was half-relief, she announced, "My dears, Mr. Grandcourt—" Pausing an instant, she began again

"I count myself in nothing else so happy / As in a soul remembering my good friends."

Sir Hugo Mallinger did not hasten his departure for Genoa with the same impatience that Mr. Gascoigne had displayed, nor did Daniel Deronda wish to leave until he had finally seen the baronet. Between Grandcourt's drowning and the tumultuous upheaval of Daniel's own life, reasons abounded for why his oldest friend would crave the unbridled communication of speech. In letters, Daniel had felt unable to divulge the particulars regarding the mother who had appeared and vanished like a spectral vision. It was not until the fifth evening, guided by a telegram, that Daniel waited for Sir Hugo at the station, expecting an arrival between eight and nine. As he gazed toward the platform, anticipating the sight of a kind, familiar face rooted in his earliest memories, a faint smile, despite his recent tragedies, curved his lips. This smile was fueled by the thought of Sir Hugo's joy in regaining mastery of his estates, the ability to leave them to his daughters, or—per a newly formed theory of inheritance pressing upon Daniel's mind—to adopt makeshift feminine offspring as intermediaries to secure a satisfactory male heir.

We might call ourselves churlish creatures if we were incapable of sharing in our fellows' joys, save when those joys align with our theories of righteous distribution and our highest ideals of human good. How sour our mouths would turn, how frozen our glances become! Yet, our own possessions and desires rarely adjust themselves to such lofty ideals. We must maintain some comradeship with imperfection; indeed, it is happily possible to feel gratitude even where we discern a mistake that may prove injurious, especially when the vehicle of that mistake is an affectionate intention pursued through a lifetime of kindly offices. Daniel's feelings and judgments stood strongly against Sir Hugo's action in becoming the agent of a falsehood—yes, a falsity—he could offer no gentler term for the concealment under which he had been reared. Still, the baronet likely possessed no clear knowledge of his mother's breach of trust. With his light, easy disposition toward life, Sir Hugo had deemed it a reasonable preference that his nephew be made an English gentleman, considering the mother's eccentric refusal to part from her child or remain merely a distant figure to him.

Daniel's affectionate gratitude toward Sir Hugo prompted him to seek grounds for excuse rather than blame. For it is as possible to be rigid in principle yet tender in censure as it is to suffer from the sight of things hung awry while remaining patient with the hand that hangs them. Had Sir Hugo, in his bachelorhood, been beguiled into viewing children primarily as products intended to make life agreeable for the fully grown, whose sole convenience mattered in their disposal, well, he had shared an assumption that, if not formally avowed, was massively acted upon in that era of world history. Daniel, with his keen memory of the painful inward struggles of his boyhood, could also recall the many signs that his harrowing experience had been entirely shut out from Sir Hugo's conception. Ignorant kindness might have the effect of cruelty; but to condemn it as if it were direct malice would be an ignorant *unkindness*, utterly remote from Daniel's large imaginative lenience toward others. Perhaps, after the searching scenes of the last ten days, wherein the curtain had lifted from the secrets of lives unlike his own, he was more than ever disposed to curb that rashness of indignation or resentment which bears an unpleasant likeness to the love of punishment. When he saw Sir Hugo's familiar figure descend from the railway carriage, the lifelong affection which had always been well accustomed to make excuses flowed in and submerged all newer knowledge that might have seemed fresh ground for blame.

"Well, Dan," said Sir Hugo, with a serious fervor, grasping Daniel's hand. He uttered no other words of greeting; there was too strong a rush of mutual consciousness. The next step was to give orders to the courier, followed by a proposal to walk slowly in the mild evening air, as there was no hurry to reach the hotel.

"I have taken my journey easily, and am in excellent condition," Sir Hugo remarked as they emerged under the starlight, which still held the faint lingering sheen of day. "I did not hurry in setting off, because I wished to inquire into things a little, and thus I caught sight of your letter to Lady Mallinger before I departed. But now, how fares the widow?"

"Growing calmer," Daniel replied. "She appears to be escaping the physical illness one might have feared following her plunge and terrible excitement. Her uncle and mother arrived two days ago, and she is being well attended to."

"Is there any prospect of an heir being born?"

"From what Mr. Gascoigne told me, I conclude there is not," Daniel said. "He spoke as if the question were whether the widow would retain the estates for her life."

"It will not be much of a wrench to her affections, I fancy, this loss of the husband?" Sir Hugo asked, glancing around at Daniel.

"The suddenness of the death has been a great blow to her," Daniel answered quietly, gently evading the deeper implications.

"I wonder whether Grandcourt gave her any notion what the provisions of his will contained?" Sir Hugo pressed.

"Do you know what they are, sir?" Daniel parried.

"Yes, I do," the baronet said quickly. "Good heavens! If there is no prospect of a legitimate heir, he has left everything to a boy he had by a Mrs. Glasher. You know nothing of the affair, I suppose, but she was a sort of wife to him for many years, and there are three older children—all girls. The boy is to take his father's name; he is already known as Henleigh, and henceforth he shall be Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt. The Mallinger fortune will be of no use to him, I am happy to say; but the young dog will have more than enough with his fourteen years' minority. No need to have had holes filled up with my fifty thousand pounds for Diplow that he had no right to touch. Meanwhile, my beauty, the young widow, is to endure a paltry two thousand a year and the house at Gadsmere—a nice sort of banishment for her if she chooses to shut herself up there, which I do not think she will. The boy's mother has lived there for recent years. I am perfectly disgusted with Grandcourt. I do not know that I am obliged to think the better of him because he is drowned, though, so far as my affairs are concerned, nothing in his life became him like leaving it."

"In my opinion, he did wrong when he married this wife—not merely in leaving his estates to the son," Daniel observed, somewhat dryly.

"I say nothing against his leaving the land to the lad," Sir Hugo countered. "But since he had married this girl, he ought to have given her a handsome provision, sufficient for her to live in a style fitting the rank he had raised her to. She ought to have had four or five thousand a year and the London house for her life; that is what I should have done for her. I suppose, as she was penniless, her friends could not stand out for a formal settlement; otherwise, it is ill-trusting to the will a man may make after marriage. Even a wise man generally lets some folly ooze out of him in his will—I know my father did, and if a fellow harbors any spite or tyranny within him, he is likely to bottle off a good deal for keeping in such a document. It is quite clear Grandcourt meant that his death should place an extinguisher on his wife, should she bear him no heir."

"And, in the other case, I suppose everything would have been reversed—an illegitimate child receiving the extinguisher?" Daniel asked, with some scorn.

"Precisely—Gadsmere and the two thousand. It's queer. One nuisance is that Grandcourt has made me an executor; but seeing he was the son of my only brother, I cannot refuse to act. And I shall mind it less if I can be of any use to the widow." Sir Hugo paused, his expression shifting. "Lush thinks she was not in ignorance about the family matters under the rose, nor the purport of the will. He hints that there was no very good understanding between the couple. But I fancy you are the man who knew most about what Mrs. Grandcourt felt or did not feel—eh, Dan?"

Sir Hugo did not pose this question with his usual jocularity, but rather with a lowered tone of interested inquiry. Daniel felt that any evasion would be misinterpreted. He answered gravely.

"She was certainly not happy. They were unsuited to each other. But as to the disposal of the property—from all I have seen of her, I should predict that she will be quite contented with it."

"Then she is not much like the rest of her sex; that's all I can say," Sir Hugo remarked, with a slight shrug. "However, she ought to be something extraordinary, for there must be an entanglement between your horoscope and hers—eh? When that tremendous telegram arrived, the first thing Lady Mallinger said was, 'How very strange that it should be Daniel who sends it!' But I have had something of the same sort in my own life. Once, at a foreign hotel, I found a lady abandoned by her husband without money. When I stepped forward to assist her, who should she be but an early flame of mine, who had been foolish enough to marry an Austrian baron with a long mustache and short affection? But it was an affair of my own that called me there

"But I shall say no more of this at this time; for this is to be felt and not to be talked of..." Jeremy Taylor once wrote words that echo through the quiet corridors of the human heart, suggesting that some truths are too fragile for the rough handling of conversation. There is a lineage of souls, ancient and unyielding, who mistake the sting of resentment for the warmth of justice. They punish the innocent because they believe they see the pattern of guilt, blind to the deeper, quieter logic of gratitude. Love, they argue, requires proof that exists only in the heart, not in the ledger. Yet, as the ancient masters of language knew, the root "love" must exist before the branch "lovable" can grow. One cannot demand affection where it has never taken root.

The journey to Mainz brought Elias Deronda to the Schuster Strasse, where the air held a sharp, clean chill. Inside the banking house, amidst the hum of commerce, he sought the man named Joseph Kalonymos. A servant guided him past the clutter of business to an inner chamber, where a figure sat at a table, surrounded by scattered letters. It was the same white-bearded man Deronda had glimpsed a year prior in Frankfurt, though now he appeared older, more weary, as if the weight of time pressed heavily upon his shoulders. Beside him lay a packed portmanteau, wrapped tightly, suggesting a departure imminent.

Upon Deronda's entrance, the old man did not rise immediately. Instead, he fixed his gaze upon the younger man with eyes that seemed to burn like black embers within a face of parchment and bone. When he finally spoke, his voice was low, tinged with a German accent that sounded both foreign and intimate.

"You seek me?" Kalonymos asked, a hint of irony softening his tone. "And now, it is you who stand before me."

"Yes," Deronda replied, stepping forward with a steady grace. "I seek you with gratitude, sir. You were a friend to my grandfather, and I owe you a debt for the trouble you have taken on my behalf." He spoke the language fluently, a dialect rich with cadences that bridged cultures.

Kalonymos smiled, then extended a hand. "Then you are no longer angry at being something more than merely an outsider? No longer bitter?"

"On the contrary," Deronda said, bowing slightly. "I thank you for saving me from a lifetime of ignorance regarding my parents, and for guarding the trust my grandfather placed in your hands."

"Sit," Kalonymos urged gently, pulling back his chair. "Sit." He removed his hat, revealing a crown of snow-white hair, and ran a hand through his beard, studying Deronda with an intensity that made the younger man feel exposed, yet strangely honored. In that moment, Deronda felt a strange, electric pull, as if he were standing on the threshold of a world his grandfather had dreamed of. To be seen by a man who had sworn loyalty to his ancestor, to hear a voice that echoed the hopes of a man who had died waiting for this reunion—it was as if the silence of the grave had been broken, and the spirit of Daniel Charisi whispered across the centuries.

"It pleases me," Kalonymos said, shifting into Hebrew, "that I was not called away to the ends of the earth before you arrived. I wished to see the likeness of my friend, alive and well, free from the shadow of shame." He paused, his eyes searching Deronda's. "You come not with pride, but with thanks. You acknowledge the blood that binds us. 'I am the grandson of Daniel Charisi.' Is this true?"

"Undoubtedly," Deronda affirmed. "Though I want you to know, sir, that I never intended to treat anyone with coldness simply because of their name. My hesitation was not malice, but confusion. How could I speak freely to a stranger about a mother I barely knew?"

"A sin!" Kalonymos exclaimed, clutching his chest. "A terrible theft from our people, akin to the ancient sorrows of Rome. But fortune has turned. I have saved what was lost. When Daniel Charisi and I were boys, we bound our fates together, swearing to be brothers in spirit. He said, 'Let us bind love with duty,' for duty is the law of the eternal heart. Though years separated us, the bond remained unbroken. When others tried to take his legacy, they failed. I protected the remnant he cherished. And now, I return it to its rightful heir."

Leaving the room briefly, Kalonymos returned with a clerk bearing a sturdy wooden box, adorned with intricate ironwork and etchings of Arabic script. The lid was lifted, revealing the key resting beneath a layer of leather.

"This is the key," Kalonymos said, handing it to Deronda with a mixture of trust and caution. "Keep it safe. I trust you to handle matters with wisdom."

"I will," Deronda promised, tucking the key into his breast pocket. "It holds more than gold; it holds the hope of a father and the promise of a future. May I ask further questions of my grandfather? Do you have time before you depart?"

"In an hour and eighteen minutes, I sail for Trieste," Kalonymos noted, glancing at his watch. "My sons await me. Would you allow me to introduce you to them? They live in comfort, unlike me, who choose the road."

"I would be honored," Deronda said warmly. "However, I fear I must return to England soon. Friends there wait for me, and I have been absent far too long."

"Then let them know you," Kalonymos suggested. "I am a wanderer, nearing the end of my days, carrying my own shroud. But my sons... they are united, wealthy, and strong. Since Karl the Great brought our ancestors from Italy, we have fought hard to secure our place. We have built a fortress of knowledge, feeding the minds of Germany, though many wear masks of gentleness."

"And what of your people, sir?" Deronda asked, leaning forward. "Have you seen little of their lives?"

"Not entirely," Kalonymos admitted. "But you, young man, have shown signs of preparation. You studied their history before the truth revealed itself to you. Did you not?"

"I did," Deronda confessed. "In seeking to understand my grandfather, I found myself drawn to the stories of our people. It seems fate was guiding me."

Kalonymos nodded slowly. "Perhaps you would have followed in his footsteps if the world had not forced another path. Look at you, Elias. You share his features, yet you bear his gentle strength rather than his iron will. Daniel Charisi had a line carved deep in his brow, a mark of determination. You have none of that hardness. He believed that a firm, if unjust, will was better than a wavering conscience. He hated indifference above all else."

"Was his knowledge limited, then?" Deronda ventured, thinking of the complexity of his grandfather's mind.

"Narrow? Never!" Kalonymos laughed softly, a sound like dry leaves rustling. "He drank in knowledge like a thirsty plant. He traveled the globe, spending his fortunes to see and learn. He understood that the strength of humanity lay in the balance between separation and connection. He feared that if our people lost themselves entirely among the nations, they would become indistinguishable grains in a vast field. He mingled sciences, arts, and laws, just as the great Arab scholars did in their golden age. Yet, despite our differences, we were soulmates. He looked always to the future of our people, pouring his soul into their destiny. I, however, found peace in the present, in the wandering, in the beauty of the world. We loved each other deeply, bound by a vow to defend one another until the end. I have kept my promise."

Kalonymos rose, and Deronda followed suit, holding the old man's gaze. "By honoring your oath, you have ensured justice for me. I would have lived a life of shadows without knowing the inheritance he prepared."

"Then prove yourself worthy," Kalonymos said, his voice dropping to a whisper. "What is your calling, young man?"

Elias hesitated. To admit he read law felt like a betrayal of the old man's expectation of a traditional path, yet to hide it felt like a lie.

"I cannot say," Deronda admitted quietly. "Not yet."

"You must choose," Kalonymos insisted, placing a hand on Deronda's shoulder. "A Jew must be diligent. Will you call yourself a Jew? Will you profess the faith of your fathers?"

"I will call myself a Jew," Deronda declared, his voice gaining strength. "But I do not intend to worship exactly as my fathers did. Their beliefs shifted with the times, and they learned from others. I wish to carry forward my grandfather's vision: to separate yet communicate. My first duty is to my people, and if there is work to be done to restore or elevate our common life, that shall be my vocation."

For a fleeting second, the air in the room seemed to shift. The necessity of speaking, the pressure to define oneself, crystallized Elias's resolve. He saw clearly, for the first time, what he truly wanted.

"Argue and look forward," Kalonymos murmured, a smile breaking through his stern features. "You are indeed the grandson of Daniel Charisi." He offered a blessing in Hebrew, and then, with a final nod, they parted ways.

Almost before Deronda had crossed the threshold of the bank, the old man was back on

A shadow fell across the white house at Pennicote, a chill that settled not just in the air, but in the hearts of those who lived within its walls. Beyond the estate, news traveled swiftly of Lord Grandcourt's demise, stirring a turmoil that demanded both expression and suppression. Within the household of Mrs. Meyrick, the atmosphere shifted with a peculiar, jagged tension.

Hans Meyrick had a ritual: delivering the *Times* to his mother, who devoured news from high politics to the latest matrimonials, finding comfort in the fates of fictional heroes and real-life strangers alike. Usually, Hans arrived precisely when Mirah finished teaching Mab her weekly lesson, claiming he wished to hear his sister sing. But today, the rhythm was broken. Hans entered the parlor with a jarring clatter, shaking the newspaper until it cracked like dry timber, shattering Mab's delicate rendition of "Lascia ch'io pianga."

The music died instantly. Mirah, her fingers hovering over the keys, spun around, startled by the harsh intrusion.

"Oh, Hans!" Mab cried, her voice trembling. "Why do you bring a noise more terrible than my singing?"

"What miracle is in the paper?" Mrs. Meyrick asked, leaning forward, her eyes wide. "Politics? A shift in the Austrians?"

"Nothing about politics," Hans replied, his voice dropping into a conspiratorial murmur that sent a shiver down his mother's spine. "Something from Italy."

Mirah's breath hitched. Her gaze darted toward Hans, then to her mother, searching for reassurance. Hans placed the paper before his mother, pointing a finger at a specific paragraph.

"Duchess Alfonso is alive," Hans announced, a grin twisting his features. "Her husband... well, he's drowned. But the best part? Deronda was there. At the same hotel. He saw her pulled from the sea by fisherman. They say she jumped in after him. A bit rash, don't you think? But Deronda was there to catch her."

Mirah sank back onto her stool, her hands clenching into fists, her eyelids lowering as if to block out the light. Mrs. Meyrick handed the paper to Mab, her face pale. "Poor soul," she whispered. "To leap into the water..."

"It was a slip of the mind," Hans dismissed, lounging in a chair near Mirah, his expression merry. "A jealous baritone, always glaring, always singing his own laments—it's hardly surprising the sea claimed him. Now the Duchess is free to marry a man with hair like silk and eyes that warm instead of freeze. And I'll bet I'm invited to the wedding!"

The air in the room grew heavy, suffocating. Mirah sprang up from her seat, her eyes blazing with a fierce, indignant fire.

"Hans!" she cried, her voice shaking with a depth of anguish she rarely showed. "Do not speak so lightly of life and death! You cannot call it 'luck.' If Deronda loved Mrs. Grandcourt, it would not be luck for anyone. It would be a tragedy for my brother! She would tear him away from me, Hans. You do not understand what it means to lose him!"

Silence crashed down upon them. The three siblings stood frozen. Mirah's face, usually flushed with health, was drained of color, her lips tight and white. Hans sat stunned, his playful mask shattered, his cheeks burning red beneath the weight of her accusation.

"I am a fool!" Hans stammered, rising abruptly. "A brute! I take back every word. I deserve whatever punishment comes." He looked at Mirah, his eyes wet with sudden remorse, and added with a faint, tragic smile, "I'd hang myself like Judas if I could."

Mirah did not soften. The pain within her was raw, a wound that bled openly. She sat back down at the piano, pressing the sheet music flat as if trying to pin down the chaos in her mind.

"Mirah speaks truth," Mab said softly, her voice thick with sympathy. "You toy with dangerous words, Hans. To joke of Deronda marrying her... it shows a darkness in your heart."

Hans bowed his head, shame etching lines into his forehead. "Forgive me, dears."

"We must finish the lesson," Mirah said, her voice regaining a measure of command, though it rang hollow. "Sing again, Mab."

Mab nodded eagerly, eager to escape the gravity of the moment. Mirah began to sing, pouring her sorrow into the melody, her voice swelling with a new, terrifying fullness. As she played, Hans lingered by the mantelpiece, watching her with a mixture of guilt and admiration, careful not to meet his mother's gaze.

When the final note faded, Mirah stood, clutching her shawl. "I must go home," she said. "Ezra waits."

She offered her hand to Mrs. Meyrick, hesitating before kissing her cheek, a gesture of apology mingled with fear. The older woman drew her close, murmuring, "God bless you, my dear." Mirah felt a sting of guilt for her harshness toward Hans, a pride that felt wrong, a shadow of ingratitude settling over her conscience.

Outside, the street air was crisp. Hans caught up with her, opening the door. "Shall I walk you home?" he asked, his voice tentative.

Mirah said nothing, allowing him to lead her. Inside the carriage, the space between them hummed with unspoken things. Hans watched her profile, his mind racing. He realized with a jolt that he had been a fool. He had suspected an attachment between Deronda and Mrs. Grandcourt, piecing together fragments of observation and whispers from Anna Gascoigne, but Mirah's anger had laid bare the truth: she feared losing him to her brother, to the world, to the inevitable distance of fate.

They parted at her door. Hans extended a hand, seeking forgiveness. "Come in," Mirah whispered, her voice melodic with a strange, softening resolve. "See my brother."

Inside, Mordecai sat by the window, his countenance radiant, holding a letter that held his entire world. He looked like a man who had conquered the grave.

"A letter from Daniel," he told them, his eyes shining. "He returns soon. Unexpected delays kept him away, but the promise remains."

Mirah slipped away to her room, closing the door gently. She did not weep. Instead, she sat, pressing her hands to her temples, as if trying to stem a flood. She splashed cold water on her face, watching the droplets fall, and for a moment, the old habits of her childhood returned—the need to endure, to hide, to perform. But beneath the surface, a new current ran.

She thought of Deronda, of the woman who had followed him, and the terrible, ugly knot of jealousy that twisted in her chest. It was not the noble, self-sacrificing love Mordecai spoke of in stories of maidens who died for kings. It was a selfish, burning desire to possess, to keep, to prevent the loss. It was a realization that shocked her: the love she had thought so pure, so reverent, had curdled into something sharp and painful.

"You have read too many plays," Mordecai's voice echoed from the other room, cutting through her thoughts. "You judge by fiction, not by your own heart."

Mirah stood still, listening to the silence, feeling the weight of a secret that threatened to swallow her whole. The future, with its uncertainties and the looming shadow of Deronda's return, stretched out before her, vast and unwieldy. She touched her brow, drying the dampness, and prepared to face the coming days, armed with a sorrow that was uniquely, painfully her own.

"Fortune is a fickle maid,
Who loves not to abide;
She brushes from thy brow the cloud,
And kisses thee aside.

But Misfortune holds thee fast,
And presses thee to heart;
She saith she hath no haste to pass,
And sits beside thy part."

A shadow that Mirah had watched approaching like the fulfillment of a warning now lingered, heavy with its accustomed baggage of sorrow. Turning from Knightsbridge, where she had just sung at a charity concert in a wealthy home, she felt footsteps matching her stride with unsettling precision. Her simple black concert dress, draped in a dust-cloak, offered no obvious pretext for alarm, nor did the crowd outside the venue suggest danger. Yet, a different fear gripped her: she imagined herself trailed by a ghost. To turn and confront the pursuer would be to invite a flood of emotion she deemed intolerable. If it were her father, he would demand recognition, forcing her to face him. She resolved to wait for that inevitable compulsion. Walking on, she did not hasten, but her mind raced ahead, rehearsing the encounter with grim certainty. A pang of regret struck her for having promised Mrs. Meyrick no concealment regarding her parentage. Driven by a desperate need to shield her brother from a sudden shock, she decided to turn before reaching her own door, choosing to summon the confrontation rather than submit to it passively.

As she approached the entrance of her small square, her intuition grew sharper, slipping from behind to her side until a hand grasped her wrist. "Mirah!" the voice called, soft and persuasive.

Mirah froze instantly. It was the voice she dreaded, yet it met her eyes with an intention to soothe. Her countenance remained grave, akin to facing an executioner, while his face wore a mask of feigned tenderness. Once handsome, with vibrant color, his features were now sallow and deeply lined, bearing a certain impudent smoothness born of seeking favor while accepting disdain. Light-built and active, he possessed a deceptive youthfulness that masked his nearing fifty-seventh year. His attire remained shabby, as she remembered it. This unworthy father, appearing now in a world where she lacked the circle of friends to buffer him from scorn, stirred within her a tumultuous mix of shame, grief, repulsion, and pity.

"It is you, father," she whispered, her voice tremulous.

"Why did you flee from me, child?" he began, his speech rapid, punctuated by jerky, illustrative gestures. "What terrified you? You knew I never compelled you against your will. I broke your engagement in the Vorstadt solely for your sake, seeing it ill-suited, yet you repaid me by abandoning me to the hardships that followed. I had secured you a position at the Dresden theater; I withheld the news to surprise you. And so you left me stranded, forced to vanish to avoid breach of contract. Think of the hardship, after I sacrificed everything to educate you, hoping it would become your fortune. What father devoted himself more fully? You know how I bore the sting of your silence, making the best of it. When I had no one but you, and was breaking under the strain of fighting with my wits alone, you chose to leave. To whom did you owe everything but me? Where was your gratitude? For what I cared, I might have perished in a ditch."

Lapidoth halted abruptly, not for lack of words, but having reached a pathos-laden climax. He produced a yellow silk handkerchief and gave a sudden, feminine sob. He genuinely felt betrayed by his daughter—a sentiment naturally potent in those unscrupulous souls who calculate debts without acknowledging counter-claims. Despite the tear, Mirah possessed enough resolve not to let him deceive her completely.

"You know why I left, father," she replied, her voice firmer than before, the first time she spoke accusations to him. "I had cause to distrust you, for I was certain you had deceived my mother. Had I trusted you, I would have stayed and labored for you."

"I never intended to deceive your mother, Mirah!" he protested, wiping his eyes, his voice struggling against further weeping. "I planned to take you back to her, but chances thwarted me at the critical moment. Then came news of her death. It was wiser for you that I remain where I was, allowing your brother to care for himself. Only you held any claim upon me. A particular friend managed affairs for me; I sent him funds to cover expenses. There remains one possibility—" a flicker of cunning crossed his mind—"he may have lied to extract money from me."

Mirah offered no reply. She could not voice the truth—that she believed not a single word of his fabrication—and merely signaled a desire to move forward, fearing that standing still might attract unwanted scrutiny. As they walked, the contrast between Mirah, radiant in the quiet dignity of an English lady, and this shabby, foreign, gesticulating man, whose jauntiness belied his plight, would surely arrest the gaze of passersby.

"You appear to have prospered, Mirah?" Lapidoth observed, examining her with emphatic scrutiny. "You know no want."

"Kind friends who found me in distress assisted me in finding work," Mirah answered, barely aware of her words as her thoughts turned to what she must soon reveal. "I teach lessons. I sing in private homes. I have just performed at a concert." She paused, adding with gravity, "I have very good friends who know all about me."

"And would you shame them by showing your father in this state? No wonder." Lapidoth's tone shifted to self-pity. "I came to England with no prospects save finding you. It was a mad quest, yet a father's heart is superstitious, drawn like a loadstone. I might have flourished abroad, free from the burden of you, rolling along like a ball. But loneliness breaks a spirit when one lacks purpose. I hoped my little Mirah would repent her departure when she looked back. I have suffered a sharp pinch working my way; I know not what depths await me next. Talents like mine are useless here. When a man wears rags, belief dies. I have been forced to sink lower for a shilling."

Mirah's anxiety surged at the thought of his further degradation, compelling her to intervene. Before she could respond to his fluent, rehearsed sentences, he asked promptly, "Where do you live, Mirah?"

"In this square. Not far from the house," she replied.

"In lodgings?"

"Yes."

"Anyone to care for you?"

"Yes," Mirah said, fixing her gaze on his keen, searching eyes. "My brother."

Lapidoth's lids fluttered as if lightning had struck them; his shoulders twitched. After a brief, perceptible pause, he asked, "Ezra? How did you know? How did you find him?"

"That requires telling later. Here we are at the door. My brother would not wish me to shut you out."

Mirah stepped onto the doorstep, her face turned downward toward her father standing on the pavement. Her heart hammered with the prospect of meeting Ezra. In granting leave to the father she had once obeyed, seeing him shrink from the direct admission he sought, she felt a unique, sympathetic humiliation—the stinging heart of reverence belonging to a nature intensely filial.

"Stay a moment, *Liebchen*," Lapidoth murmured, lowering his voice. "Tell me, what sort of man has Ezra become?"

"A good man—a wonderful man," Mirah answered slowly, striving to master the tremor in her throat. "Yet, twelve years ago, when friends found him, he was a poor worker. Once, strong and happy, he journeyed eastward, a land he loved. My mother called him back because... because she had lost me. He went to her, tending her through great tribulation, working until she died in grief. Ezra, too, lost his health and strength. The cold seized him returning, for she was forsaken. For years he has weakened, always poor, always laboring, yet filled with knowledge and greatness. All who approach honor him. To stand before him is like standing before a prophet of God." Her voice faltered, her heart throbbing. "Falsehoods serve no purpose."

She cast down her eyes, unable to bear the ignoble glimmer of frustration gathering in his face. Yet, he was swift in invention and decision.

"Mirah, *Liebchen*," he cooed in his old, caressing manner, "should I not seek to appear more respectable before my son? With a modest sum, I could dress properly and return home as a father ought. I could seek a decent post. With a clean shirt and coat, men would welcome me. I could serve as a courier, if I did not resemble a broken mountebank. I wish to be with my children, to forget and forgive. But you have never seen your father thus. Ten pounds, or perhaps you could bring it tomorrow..." He gestured vaguely. "I could be fitted out by tomorrow afternoon."

Mirah felt a temptation she struggled to resist. Obliging herself to look at him again, she replied, "I cannot deny you what you ask, father; but I have pledged not to act secretly toward you. It is hard to see you in need, yet we shall endure this briefly. Soon, you shall have new clothes, and we shall pay for them." Her practical sense recognized the wisdom of Mrs. Meyrick's condition.

Lapido

The contrast between Daniel Deronda's departure from England and his return was as stark as the shift from winter snow to spring bloom.
