He had sailed for Genoa with a heart shrouded in fog, unsure if the currents of his destiny would carry him away from the familiar shores of his upbringing or pull him deeper into the unknown tides of Jewish heritage. He returned, however, not merely free, but anchored. In his possession lay a charter of sorts, validating the ambitions that had stirred within him, a bond forged by experience that he welcomed with a humble grace despite the lack of promises regarding a secret, sleeping passion. Yet, something vital had shifted within him. For the first time, he dared to acknowledge the silent, steady presence of Mirah in his soul. Since his departure from Chelsea, where Mirah's farewell gaze had struck a chord of profound, protective tenderness, her influence had grown stronger, not as a rival to Gwendolen, but as a counterweight that softened the edges of his admiration for her. Where once his pity for Gwendolen had been fueled by a martyr-like self-sacrifice, it now flowed toward a gentler, more internalized compassion. Furthermore, his growing kinship with Mordecai had drawn Mirah closer to his thoughts, binding her fate to his moral struggles, particularly those surrounding her brother's honor. This connection had not slipped past his consciousness; indeed, it had become a source of inner turmoil, a "jealous fire" sparked by Hans's attentions. When his mother had accused him of loving a Jewess, evasion had felt like betrayal. Now, standing on the threshold of a new understanding, compelled by the urgency of Hans's actions and the weight of his mother's probing, Deronda felt a sudden, liberating clarity. The exhausting burden of self-suppression melted away, replaced by a fierce, impulsive drive. As he approached England from Mainz, the journey itself felt like an obstacle, a delay he could no longer afford. It was as if discovering his ancestry had granted him a second soul, transforming his impartial sympathy into a focused, practical loyalty. He sought no longer the detached wisdom of the observer, but the shared warmth of fellowship with those of his blood. He craved to be near Mordecai, to finally align his will with theirs, and to view Mirah through the lens of a new, open possibility, interpreting her every gesture with a fresh, hopeful intent. He trusted that Hans's advances would fail to sway Mirah, sensing that her feelings for him, whatever they were, resided in a channel that would not divert. To shock a woman who viewed him only as a Lord Chancellor into becoming his lover was a transition he feared, preferring a path of ease and mutual growth.

Consequently, why linger? Why hesitate at the London terminus? Deronda resolved to proceed directly from the station to the modest lodging in Brompton Square. Time was not to be lost. He had promised to visit Lady Mallinger at the Abbey the following day, and now twilight was descending. Above all, he needed to entrust the precious chest containing his grandfather's manuscripts to Mordecai. He desired that Mordecai study these documents both alone and in his company, and the promptness of this delivery would bring a profound satisfaction to Mordecai's heart. Thus, it filled Deronda's own heart with a deep contentment. The strongest currents of his nature rushed forward in unison: the fervent affection that delighted in fulfilling the desires of those near him, and the imaginative hunger for a relationship broad enough to illuminate the horizon of his daily existence. One must admit that in this classical, romantic, and historically charged position, as if emerging from hiding with his hereditary armor, he wore the somber, drab costume common to his contemporaries. He did not stop to consider if these muted tones suited him, for he rarely indulged in such introspection. Yet, his innate depth of character lent a radiant glow to his features, a flickering light in his skin and eyes as he stepped inside, wondering what secrets awaited. He entered the house with a silence that spoke of reverence.

That evening, mere hours after Mirah's painful interview with her father, the atmosphere within the lodgings remained heavy with unspoken sorrow. Mordecai, deeply moved by her grief and haunted by the tragic memories the incident had awakened, had abandoned his task of sorting through the scattered papers. Some lay strewn across the floor, untouched since the first paroxysms of anxiety. Neither Mordecai nor Mirah had moved to restore order. They sat frozen in a tableau of despair, unaware of the passing minutes, as the clock on the mantelpiece marked the slow fade of daylight. Mirah, forgetting the meals she had neglected, remained seated beside her brother, her hand clutched in his, while he leaned back with closed eyes and labored breath, appearing to Mirah as one whose soul could no longer inhabit its confined vessel. The thought of imminent death constantly visited her whenever she gazed upon his pale, motionless face. Now, to her overwhelming grief, was added the bitter regret that she could not have restrained the violent emotional storm that had shaken him. She watched him, her complexion pale, her eyes shimmering with the residue of recent tears, her hair in a disarray akin to a child's after waking, staring at that emaciated countenance as if it were a portrait of a dead joy that sustained her life through sorrow. Life, at that moment, stretched before Mirah not as a continuation, but as a terrifying repetition of sadness, shadowed by the presence of a father who lived as if already dead, creating a double bereavement.

Suddenly, the door opened. Though no one entered visibly, a familiar voice echoed softly, "Daniel Deronda—may he come in?"

"Come! Come!" cried Mordecai, rising instantly with a face irradiated by light and eyes wide open. He appeared scarcely surprised, as if he had anticipated the visitor since morning, while Mirah sprang up, her face flushing with a mixture of confusion and alarm.

Yet, when Deronda crossed the threshold, the air seemed to clear, like sunlight breaking through rain. No cloud could obstruct the benevolent beam of that moment. As he extended his right hand to Mirah, who stood near her brother's left, he placed his other hand gently upon Mordecai's shoulder. Standing thus, holding both of them, he uttered no word, reading their expressions until he asked anxiously of Mirah, "Has anything happened? Any trouble?"

"Do not speak of trouble now," Mordecai replied swiftly, sparing her the necessity of an answer. "There is joy in your face—let the joy be ours."

Mirah thought, *It is for something he cannot tell us.* They all sat down, with Deronda drawing a chair close before Mordecai.

"That is true," Deronda said, his voice emphatic. "I have a joy that will endure for us, even in the midst of the greatest troubles. I did not reveal the purpose of my journey abroad, Mordecai, because... never mind. I went to discover my parentage. And you were right. I am a Jew."

The two men clasped hands in a movement that seemed to flash through Mirah like an electric shock. But Deronda continued without pausing, speaking with the conviction of Mordecai's own mind as much as his own: "We share the same people. Our souls share the same vocation. Life nor death shall separate us."

Mordecai's answer burst forth in Hebrew, barely a whisper above a murmur, yet carrying the weight of liturgical bond: "Our God and the God of our fathers."

The weight of emotion pressed too heavily upon the speech, rendering it slower than usual, devoid of its customary rapid adaptation to shifting fervor.

Mirah sank to her knees beside her brother, gazing at his now illuminated face, which had moments prior appeared deathly. This action was an inevitable outlet for the violent reversal from despondency to a solemn gladness that washed over her, as if she were witnessing a sacred rite. For the moment, her thoughts of her own future were eclipsed entirely by the effect upon her brother.

"And it is not only that I am a Jew," Deronda continued, experiencing one of those rare moments where yearning and action became wholly unified, and reality aligned with ideal goodness. "But I descend from a strain that has ardently maintained the fellowship of our race—a lineage of Spanish Jews that produced many scholars and men of practical power. And I possess that which grants us a communion with them. My grandfather, Daniel Charisi, preserved manuscripts and family records stretching far back, hoping they would eventually reach the hands of his grandson. And now, his hope is fulfilled, despite the attempts to hide my parentage from me. I possess the chest containing them, along with his own papers, and it lies down below in this house. I intend to leave it with you, Mordecai, that you may assist me in studying the manuscripts. Some I can read with ease—those in Spanish and Italian. Others are in Hebrew, and I believe Arabic; there seem to be Latin translations as well. I was only able to examine them cursorily during my stay in Mainz. We shall study them together."

Deronda concluded with a bright smile, emanating from the habitual gravity of his countenance, seeming a revelation—a light that dispelled the shadows of doubt rather than the artificial smile that often discredits all expression. But when this joyful glance passed from Mordecai to rest upon Mirah, it acted like too much sunshine, causing her to alter her posture. She had knelt under an impulse where personal embarrassment was incongruous, particularly any thoughts regarding how Mrs. Grandcourt

—Dante: *Purgatorio*

It was scarcely a week after her mother's return from the sea that Gwendolen yielded to stay in Genoa. Yet, the desire to flee from that jewel of the ocean had served to gather fragments of her shattered courage. For what place, however idyllic, may not the inner eye transform into a circle of torment where blossoms burn like tongues of fire beneath the feet?

"I shall never look upon the Mediterranean again," Gwendolen whispered to her mother, who sensed the depth of this aversion without needing an explicit mention of her late husband.

Mrs. Davilow, though formally bound by the gravity of her loss, was secretly reveling in life more than she had since her daughter's wedding. It seemed that her beloved child had returned not simply with the old affections, but with a conscious, cherished need for her presence—a possession saved from the brink of eternal separation.

"Are you there, Mamma?" Gwendolen cried out in the dead of night, a bed prepared for her mother sharing the same chamber. She spoke with the unguarded vulnerability of a frightened girl, though the impetus behind her words was now different.

"Yes, dear. Can I do anything for you?"

"No, thank you. I only wished to know you were there. Do you mind my waking you?"

"Not at all, darling."

"It didn't feel real that you were with me. I wanted to make it real. I can endure things if you are beside me. But please, do not lie awake worrying about me. You must find happiness now. Let me make you happy at last—if I cannot, what will I do?"

"God bless you, dear. My greatest happiness is knowing you cherish me."

Yet the following night, hearing her mother sighing and restless, Gwendolen refused the offered sedative. "No, Mamma, I do not wish to sleep." "It would be so beneficial for you, my darling." "Do not tell me what is 'good' for me, Mamma!" Gwendolen replied with sudden impetuosity. "You do not know what is good for me. You and Uncle Hugo must not contradict me and dictate what benefits me when I feel it does not."

Silence fell over the room, for the mother did not wonder why the poor child was irritable. Then Gwendolen confessed, "I was always naughty to you, Mamma." "No, dear, no." "Yes, I was," Gwendolen insisted, her voice trembling. "It is because I was wicked that I am miserable now." She burst into sobs, the dam breaking. Her determination to keep silent regarding the grim realities of her marriage and its tragic conclusion erupted in these enigmatic outbursts of emotion.

Slowly, faint lights of understanding began to dawn upon Mrs. Davilow's mind. Information flowed from Sir Hugo to Mr. Gascoigne, and with some omissions, from the rector to himself. The kindly baronet, tending to the necessary arrangements regarding his nephew's death and the possibility of recovering the body, believed it his gentlest duty to use his friendship with the clergyman to reveal the purport of Grandcourt's will. He aimed to spare the rector the double shock of returning home only to discover the truth later. Though Sir Hugo might have spoken plainly, his benevolent motive drove him to break the news gradually. At first, he merely hinted that the widow was not as generously provided for as Mr. Gascoigne, or as the baronet himself had anticipated. Only finally, after vague references to large claims on Grandcourt, did he disclose the prior relations that, due to the lack of a legitimate heir, had diverted the estate's splendor elsewhere.

The rector was deeply wounded, recalling with renewed vividness how proudly and repulsively the deceased had behaved toward him. He remembered the hints he had received in those early days concerning the new occupant of Diplow—an undue addiction to pleasure, a life swept into private rubbish heaps that now presented themselves as a swarm of live caterpillars threatening respectable society. Yet, he did not let these thoughts surface as indignation. Instead, behaving like a worldly man who had become a conscientious clergyman, he responded with measured wisdom. "When a young man makes his will in health, he usually counts on living a long while. Probably Mr. Grandcourt did not believe this document would ever take effect." With a heavy heart, he added, "The effect is painful in more ways than one. Female morality suffers greatly from this marked advantage and prominence given to illegitimate offspring."

"Well, in point of fact," said Sir Hugo, with his usual comfort, "since the boy is there, this was truly the best alternative for disposing of the estates. Grandcourt had no nearer relative than his cousin. And it is a chilling thought that you leave this world solely for the benefit of a cousin. A man finds a certain pleasure in making a will if it benefits his own children, but it becomes a nuisance when you are bequeathing to a 'used-up' fellow like yourself—one whom you care nothing for. It is almost as bad as holding only a life interest in your estates. No, I forgive Grandcourt for that portion of his will. But between ourselves, what I cannot forgive him for is the shabby manner in which he has provided for your niece—_our_ niece, I shall say. She holds no position better than a doctor's widow. Nothing grates on me more than that posthumous stinginess toward a wife. A man ought to possess some pride and fondness for his widow. _I_ surely do. I consider it a true test of a man's character that he feels at ease regarding his death when he can envision his wife and daughters comfortable thereafter. I recall those fellows in the Crimean War who were willing to sink to the bottom of the sea if their widows were provided for."

"It has certainly surprised me," admitted Mr. Gascoigne, "especially since, standing as the father figure to my niece, I showed my trust in Mr. Grandcourt's apparent liberality by making no prior claims for her. That seemed to me proper under the circumstances. You may think me blameworthy."

"Not blameworthy exactly. I respect a man for trusting another. However, take my advice. Should you marry another niece, whether it be the Archbishop of Canterbury or anyone else, bind him down. Your niece cannot be married twice over. And if he is a good man, he will wish to be bound. As for Mrs. Grandcourt, I can only say that I feel my connection to her drawn closer because I believe she has not been well-treated. I hope you will urge her to rely on me as a friend."

So spoke the chivalrous Sir Hugo, disgusted that the young and beautiful widow of Mallinger Grandcourt was left with a mere two thousand a year and a house in a coal-mining district. To the rector, that income seemed less scandalous and devoid of mortifying privations; but in this conversation, the baronet felt a keener sense of the humiliation cast over his niece and her closest friends by the public revelation of her husband's affair with Mrs. Glasher. Like all men who are good husbands and fathers, he felt this humiliation acutely through the minds of the women primarily affected. Thus, the initial annoyance of hearing the facts paled in comparison to his dread of communicating them to Mrs. Davilow and anticipating Gwendolen's reaction when she learned the truth. The rector possessed an innocent conviction that his niece was unaware of Mrs. Glasher's existence, reasoning from sound masculine principles about what maidens and wives typically know, do, and suffer, and based on his imperfect observations of the specific maiden and wife in question. Not so Gwendolen's mother, who now perceived an explanation for much that had remained enigmatic in her child's conduct both before and after the engagement. Concluding that Gwendolen had somehow been informed of this clandestine marriage and the existence of the children, she hoped to uncover the extent of her daughter's knowledge through moments of affectionate confidence before and during their journey to England, preparing her gently for whatever disappointments lay ahead.

"I hope you do not expect me to become rich and grand, Mamma," Gwendolen said shortly after the rector's communication, sitting quietly in meditation after dressing. "Perhaps I shall have nothing at all."

Mrs. Davilow was startled, reflecting for a moment before answering, "Oh yes, dear, you will have something. Sir Hugo knows all about the will."

"That will not decide," Gwendolen interrupted abruptly.

"Surely, dear? Sir Hugo says you are to receive two thousand a year and the house at Gadsmere."

"What I have will depend entirely on what I accept," Gwendolen stated firmly. "You and my uncle must not attempt to cross me or persuade me regarding this. I will do everything I can to make you happy, but in anything concerning my husband, I must not be interfered with. Is eight hundred a year sufficient for you, Mamma?"

"Far more than enough, dear. You must not think of giving me so much." Mrs. Davilow paused, then asked, "Do you know who is to receive the estates and the remainder of the money?"

"Yes," Gwendolen replied, waving her hand dismissively. "I know everything. It is all perfectly right, and I wish never to have it mentioned."

Her mother fell silent, looking away and rising to fetch a fan-screen, a slight flush

"O, welcome, pure-eyed Faith, white-handed Hope, Thou hovering angel, girt with golden wings!"

Deronda did not answer Gwendolen's call without a flutter of unease within him. It was not pride that stirred him, but a sharp, piercing sympathy. He feared deeply that another's heart might demand more of him than he could possibly give. To deny the truth of her love, he realized with a sudden, heavy clarity, was no longer an intellectual exercise; it was a visceral awareness. Gwendolen's soul clung to his with a desperate, consuming need. One does not debate the anger or the disdain that vibrates through a stranger's voice; one simply feels it, undeniable and absolute. Deronda felt a young woman's fate hanging over him, poised on the edge of a chasm of despair. Anyone who knew him well would not marvel at his silent admission: if this tragedy had unfolded but a year prior, he would scarcely have paused to question whether he loved her. The driving force within him would have been immediate—to shield her from sorrow, to build a sanctuary for her life forever, to complete the rescue he had begun with the necklace.

But now, the chains of love and duty bound him differently. That primal impulse remained, yes, but it was tempered by a compassionate ache, a trembling at the thought of meeting her eyes and hearing her pleas again and again. Precisely because he held firm to the resolve that kept him apart, his pity for her lonely lot grew sharper, more stinging.

He waited for her in the back drawing-room—a space of white and crimson where they had once sat amidst music, where she had whispered that her future rested on his never leaving her, and her plea had seemed to dissolve into a song of *Per pietà non darmi addio*. Yet, that melody had belonged to Mirah's voice.

Deronda paced this familiar room, feeling a profound metamorphosis in his own life. The objects around him—from Lady Mallinger's gentle portrait to the lion heads on the chimney-piece—seemed to belong to a past existence, ghosts of a time he was merely visiting through memory rather than living in reality. The impressions he had recently endured were so deep, the conditions of his life so transformed, that the house, once a home, now felt alien. He stood with his hat in hand, awaiting the entrance of a young creature whose life was undergoing a tragic shift toward an uncertain end, a shift in which he felt his actions still dangerously entangled.

Gwendolen entered, altered. Not only by her mourning dress, but by a quiet, settled calm that he had not seen on her face since Genoa. Her contentment lay in his presence alone. There was no smile when they met, no warmth in their clasping hands; both were heavy with remembrance, burdened by anxious foreboding. "It was good of you to come," she said immediately, sitting down in the nearest chair. "Let us sit." He took the seat opposite her.

"I asked you to come because I need you to tell me what I ought to do," she began at once. "Do not hesitate to tell me what you think is right, even if it sounds harsh. I have decided to do it. Once, I was terrified of poverty; I could not bear the thought of serving others, and that is why I married. I have borne far worse things now. I believe I could endure poverty if you think it necessary. Do you know about my husband's will?"

"Yes, Sir Hugo informed me," Deronda replied, anticipating her question.

"Should I accept anything he left me?" Gwendolen asked, her voice tightening with nervous urgency. "I want to tell you what I've been thinking. Perhaps you don't realize how much I truly considered my mother when I married. I was selfish, yes, but I loved her, and I felt deeply about her poverty. The greatest comfort I found, when I was miserable, was knowing she was better off because of my marriage. Now, the hardest thing for me would be to see her in poverty again. I've been thinking... if I take enough to support her, and nothing for myself, it wouldn't be wrong. She was precious to me, and he took me from her, and he meant..."

Gwendolen broke off. She had prepared for this meeting by focusing solely on this question of right toward her mother, but the inquiry dragged up thoughts and reasons she could not articulate. Perilous memories swarmed between her words, agitating her speech until it trembled. She looked down helplessly at her hands, now bare of every ring save her wedding band.

"Do not harm yourself by speaking of that," Deronda said softly. "There is no need; the case is straightforward. I doubt I could judge wrongly. You seek my counsel because I am the only one to whom you have confessed the most painful parts of your experience, and I understand your scruples." He paused, allowing her to gather herself. The silence felt thick with the tenderness in his voice, giving her the courage to lift her eyes and look at him as he continued. "You are conscious of something you feel to be a sin against someone who is dead. You believe you have forfeited all rights as a wife. You shrink from accepting what belongs to his estate. You desire to punish yourself, to scourge the self that yielded to temptation rather than following your higher will. I have known this struggle myself. Do I understand you?"

"Yes—I want to be good. Not like what I have been," Gwendolen replied. "I will try to bear whatever you think I must. I have tried to show you the worst of myself. What ought I to do?"

"If no one else were involved," Deronda said, "I would not dare urge you against your remorseful impulses. However, I am guided now by your feelings regarding Mrs. Davilow, which seem to me entirely just. I cannot believe your husband's obligations to you are voided by your actions. He voluntarily stepped into your life and shaped its course in the most significant ways. Setting that aside, it was his duty, given his position, to provide for your mother, and he surely understood that if this will took effect, she would share the provision made for you."

"She has received eight hundred a year," Gwendolen noted. "What I hoped to do was take that amount and leave the rest." Having argued internally for so long that this was a permissible path, her mind could not instantly shift gears.

"I do not think it is your duty to set such limits," Deronda countered gently. "You would create a painful mystery for Mrs. Davilow; an income from which you exclude yourself would inevitably bitter her. Furthermore, your own path would become unnecessarily difficult. At Genoa, we agreed that the burden on your conscience is one no one should be forced to acknowledge. The future goodness of your life will be best served by sparing everyone else the pain of knowing this. In my judgment, you should simply follow the terms of your husband's will, letting your remorse influence only how you choose to live with your financial freedom."

As he spoke the final sentence, Deronda instinctively reached for the hat resting on the floor beside him. Gwendolen, acutely sensitive to his slightest movements, felt her heart leap as if it possessed a consciousness of its own, warning him not to depart. Simultaneously, she rose from her chair, forgetting to consider that his motion signaled his intent to leave. Deronda, of course, rose and stepped forward slightly.

"I will do what you tell me," Gwendonen said quickly. "But what else shall I do?" Those were the only simple words possible to her, and even they were too heavy for a spirit where her proud secrecy had crumbled. As the childish sentences fell from her lips, they struck her like a mirror of her own helplessness, and she could not stifle the sob that brought large tears to her eyes. Deronda, too, felt a crushing pain, though imminent consequences weighed heavily on his conscience. When she brushed away her tears, he asked in a gentle, questioning tone, "You will probably be traveling with Mrs. Davilow into the country soon."

"Yes, in a week or ten days." Gwendolen hesitated, turning her eyes vaguely toward the window as if gazing at some imaginary prospect. "I want to be kind to them all—they can be happier than I am. Is that the best I can do?"

"I think so. It is a duty beyond doubt," Deronda affirmed. He paused, feeling a weight of anxiety settle over his words. "Other duties will arise from this. Viewing your life as a debt may seem the dreariest perspective from afar, but it is not truly so. What makes life bleak is the lack of motive. Once you begin acting with this penitent, loving purpose you hold in your mind, you will find unexpected satisfactions. New needs will constantly emerge to carry you forward, day by day. Your life will grow like a plant."

Gwendolen turned her eyes to him with the look of one thirsting for unseen water. Deronda felt the gaze as if she were stretching her arms toward him from a deserted shore. His voice adopted an affectionate, imploring quality. "This sorrow, which has severed you to the root, has arrived while you are so young. Try to see it not as a ruin of your life, but as a preparation for it. A preparation..." Anyone listening to his tone would have thought he was begging for his own happiness. "See! You have been spared the gravest evils that could have stemmed from your marriage, which you feel was mistaken. You have witnessed a vision of injurious, selfish conduct—a glimpse of possible degradation. Imagine a stern angel, seeing

"One day still fierce 'mid many a day struck calm."
—BROWNING: *The King and the Book*.

While Gwendolen's thoughts remained fixed elsewhere, a quieter, more intimate current flowed between Ezra, Mirah, and the man who claimed them both. Their connection, previously obscured, was now drawn taut and illuminated.

Lapidoth had stepped onto the threshold, his mind already consumed by the thrill of a gamble larger than mere survival. Having surrendered the thirty shillings from his daughter's purse, his world narrowed to the mechanics of the game and the hope of doubling back. Hunger, a constant companion, paled before the insatiable need for the next roll, the next chance. To him, the act of wagering was a spiritual necessity, a way to suspend reality, to dance on the burning marl of fate rather than face the grim prospect of tomorrow's bread. Yet, beneath this fever dream, a primal need for sustenance lingered. He had slipped into a shabby existence where money was the only key to warmth, and now, holding Mirah's empty purse, he balanced two conflicting impulses: pawn it for immediate relief, or return it with a lie, claiming a debt paid, to preserve his fragile dignity. A lingering pride, the belief that his children owed him their loyalty, weighed heavily against the justice of Ezra's anger. After all, wasn't staying with his offspring the safest harbor? And why shrink from facing Ezra when luck might yet smile, offering supplies through Mirah's "good friends"? Lapidoth clung to the illusion of his past cleverness, convinced he could feign vitality despite the erosion of time.

His plan, however, unraveled as evening deepened. Walking the streets of Brompton, hoping to spot Mirah, he grew weary, then resigned. When he approached the house, the soft hum of a melody confirmed her presence. Inside, Mirah played "Herz, mein Herz" with a sorrowful grace, while Ezra listened, eyes closed, lost in a silent storm. Mrs. Adam's arrival shattered the moment. A knock followed by her hesitant announcement—"A gentleman below says he is your father, miss"—sent a jolt through the room.

"I will go down to him," Mirah offered, rising abruptly.

"No, Mirah," Ezra stopped her, his voice firm. "Let him come up, Mrs. Adam."

Mirah stood frozen, her fingers twisting together, sickness rising in her stomach. She watched Ezra, who had risen too, his face transformed. There was a severity in his knitted brow, hardened lips, and piercing gaze that she had never seen before. As Mrs. Adam ushered Lapidoth inside, casting a glance at the siblings, she murmured, "Father, surely enough." The resemblance was striking in outline, yet the spirit within was a stark contrast, forged by invisible fires into something formidable.

Lapidoth, masking a deep wince with a melancholy mask, spoke the words that tore the air apart. "Well, Ezra, my boy, you hardly know me after so many years."

"I know you—too well, father," Ezra replied, his tone slow, biting, rendering the title a reproach.

"You are not pleased?" Lapidoth rushed forward, his voice regaining a glib, defensive rhythm. "Appearances have been against me! When a man falls, he cannot choose his path... I've suffered enough!" He turned to Mirah, thrusting the purse toward her. "Here, my dear. I thought you'd worry about that note. I emptied it, you see—I had scores to pay. Here I stand, without a farthing, at your mercy. Turn me out, if you like, without a policeman. Just say the word, Mirah: 'Father, I'm done with you,' and I'll vanish like a spark." Tears welled instantly, a familiar weapon.

"You know I could never say it, father," Mirah whispered, her voice thick with anguish, knowing the truth behind his performance.

"Mirah, my sister, leave us!" Ezra commanded, his authority absolute.

Mirah hesitated, her eyes wide with fear and awe. She moved close to her brother, placing her hand in his, her voice barely audible above the silence. "Remember, Ezra—you said my mother would not have shut him out."

"Trust me, and go," Ezra said gently.

Mirah retreated, her heart pounding like a drum. As she ascended the stairs, she sat down, terrified of what might follow. Lapidoth, meanwhile, sensed the trap closing but adjusted his stance, preparing a shield of superior calm. He saw the tragedy in Ezra's pale face, interpreting it through the lens of his own self-preservation. To him, Ezra's grief was merely a dramatic performance, a religious howl in an open cathedral that offered little shelter from the hail of his own conscience.

"This home," Ezra began, his voice steady but heavy, "is sustained by the generosity of a friend and the labor of my sister. So long as we have a roof, we will not cast you out to the mercy of your vices. You are our father, and though you have broken your bond, we acknowledge ours. But I will never trust you. You fled with money, leaving debts unpaid; you abandoned my mother, stealing her child and breaking her heart; you became a gambler, where shame once lived, now sits an insatiable void. You were ready to sell my sister—you had the buyer, but the price was denied you. A man who has done these things cannot ever expect trust again. We will feed you, clothe you, give you a bed, because you are our father. But you will never be trusted. You are an evil man, and you made the misery of our mother. That fact brands our flesh, a wound that will never heal. But the Eternal has laid this burden on us. Even if human justice dragged you through the mud, if your body fell helpless before the scorn of the crowd, we would still say, 'This is our father.' Make way, that we may carry him out of our sight."

Lapidoth, expecting a softer rejection, was blindsided by the lightning of Ezra's truth. He could not predict the depth of the son's soul, nor the raw, hysterical spring it tapped. As Ezra finished, Lapidoth collapsed into a chair, crying like a woman, burying his face in his hands. It was a reaction inevitable to the shock, yet also a calculated resource, a tool he had honed since youth to deflect resentment.

Ezra sat in silence, exhausted by the eruption of feelings he had carried alone for years. His hands trembled on the chair's arms; he felt as though he had just stepped toward death. Suddenly, Mirah returned, her ear catching a sound that chilled her blood. Rushing to Ezra's side, she took his trembling hand, finding a sliver of strength in his grip. He did not look at her, his eyes fixed on the floor.

Lapidoth, sensing her approach, raised his head. With a plaintive hoarseness, he reached for her hand. "Good-bye, Mirah. Your father will not trouble you again. He deserves to die like a dog by the roadside, and he will. If your mother had lived, she would have forgiven me—thirty-four years ago I put the ring on her finger under the *Chuppa*, and we were made one. She would have forgiven me, and we should have spent our old age together. But I haven't deserved it. Good-bye."

As he rose, Mirah seized his arm, her voice shaking. "No, father, no!" Turning to Ezra, she pleaded, "Ezra, you have not forbidden him? Stay, father, and leave off wrong things. Ezra, I cannot bear it. How can I say to my father, 'Go and die!'?"

"I have not said it," Ezra answered, the effort tearing at him. "I have said, stay and be sheltered."

"And you will stay, father—and be taken care of—and come with me," Mirah insisted, guiding him toward the door.

It was exactly what Lapidoth desired. For a fleeting moment, the comfort of his daughter's dutiful attendance made the idea of changing his ways seem plausible. She led him downstairs to the parlor, indicating a bedroom behind it. "You will stay and be good, father. Think that you are come back to my mother, and that she has forgiven you—she speaks to you through me." Her tone was imploring, yet devoid of the caresses she once gave.

Lapidoth quickly composed himself, chattering easily about Mirah's improved voice and trivialities, trying to impress Mrs. Adam when she served supper, proving he was no ordinary beggar despite his ragged clothes. But as night fell, the illusions dissolved. Lying awake, his mind raced with calculations of Mirah's funds, replaying scenes from Continental casinos, visualizing the roulette wheels and the chances that had cheated him. These were the stronger visions haunting him, overshadowed by the worn frame of his irate son, whose terrible judgment echoed in the darkness. Ezra had passed the gaming tables like a ghost, his words hollowed out by the crushing weight of numbers and movements that seemed to weave the very fabric of Lapidoth's consciousness, eroding his resolve stone by stone.

For Daniel Deronda, returning from the Abbey, the discovery waiting at Brompton Lodgings was a bitter shock. His erstwhile sanctuary had been invaded by the "undesirable father" who now occupied the premises. Mirah, driven by necessity, had confessed to her father, Lapidoth, though she carefully curated the narrative. She omitted the depth of Deronda's aid, specifically the terrifying rescue from drowning, and minimized her shelter with the Meyricks. Her aim was to lead Lapidoth to believe that Deronda had merely met her through the Meyricks' circle. She could not endure the scrutiny of her father's gaze upon the true nature of her bond with Deronda. Consequently, Lapidoth, for his own reasons, refrained from pressing questions regarding her flight or arrival, yet his interest peaked at the revelation that his child possessed a benefactor seemingly elevated in the world.

It was Ezra who broke the news to Deronda of this new intruder. "I have grown calmer in witnessing him," Ezra remarked, his voice heavy with resignation. "I cling to the hope that Mirah's tenderness, coupled with the quiet rhythm of a peaceful life, may eventually draw him away from temptation. I have strictly forbidden her to entrust him with any funds, convincing her that his acceptance of money would seal his own ruin."

Deronda first encountered Lapidoth three days after his arrival. The tailor's measurements for new suits had not yet yielded garments, and Desperate to avoid presenting himself in his shabby attire, Deronda hoped to make a favorable impression. As he approached the door, he peered through the window. He was startled by the visitor's apparent youth, a detail Mirah had conveniently glossed over. To Deronda, this youthful visage seemed incongruous with the somber studies shared with the brooding Ezra. Yet, Lapidoth's imagination quickly filled the gap; he surmised that Deronda's primary motivation must be a romantic attachment to Mirah. To Lapidoth, this was a blessing. A suitor bound by love offered his daughter a shield against harsh judgment, whereas a tie solely to Ezra invited severity. Lapidoth, buoyed by this hope, adopted an air of amiability, striving to domesticate himself within their sphere. He engaged with Mirah's music, submitted to her rules regarding smoking (which his hostess, Mrs. Adam, detested), and strolled the square with his German pipe, fueled by tobacco Mirah graciously supplied. He wisely held his tongue regarding Mirah's steadfast refusal to give him money, accepting it as a solemn duty owed to her brother, content to wait.

On the following visit, Lapidoth, freshly dressed and preening before his mirror, sat in the room with Ezra. Ezra, engaging in a grim penance, had resolved to tolerate his father's presence whenever imposed. Deronda felt a chill distance toward the man who had blighted the lives of his wife and children; the sight of Lapidoth provoked a visceral repulsion. Undeterred, Lapidoth requested permission to remain and listen to Ezra read from the old chest. He proved unexpectedly useful, assisting in deciphering a complex German manuscript. Seizing the moment, Lapidoth suggested transcribing the document and offering his services to copy papers written in Roman characters. Although Ezra's vision was failing, Lapidoth's eyes remained sharp. Deronda, interpreting this eagerness as a sign of moral grace, accepted the offer. However, Ezra, wary of accidents involving fire or loss, insisted that all work be done within his immediate sight, effectively treating his father like a convict on furlough.

This arrangement cast a heavy shadow over Ezra's existence. Confining his attention to appease Deronda was a constant torment, akin to wearing an ill-fitting garment. He sought relief constantly—stepping out to smoke, pacing the floor, collapsing into silence marked by a frantic, dumb pantomime of facial expressions and gestures. When Mirah was present, he fell back into his old habits, gossipping about past adventures and theatrical plots, deluding himself into believing he could summon the vitality of his younger days. This behavior was a mortal affliction to Ezra. Even when Mirah intervened to remove her father to the parlor, the burden persisted. What kind of heroism is forged by a single resolve? It is the daily, unflinching endurance of consequences that stain the dawn with irritation and shame. These burdens were carried by Ezra and Mirah, along with countless other children of unworthy fathers, hoping that their sacrifices would stretch into a solid future.

Yet, Lapidoth's presence erected an invisible barrier between Deronda and Mirah. Both feared the intrusive inferences of the father's mind, leading to a mutual misunderstanding of each other's growing reserve. Soon, however, light began to pierce Deronda's confusion.

Following his return from the Abbey, Deronda visited Hans Meyrick, believing it a debt of friendship for Hans to know the reasons behind his recent journey. Hans was absent, supposedly in the country, but a week later, Deronda returned to find him in the studio. Hans appeared disheveled, his hair wet from a bath, his face worn and wizened. Before Deronda could speak, Hans stood before his easel, palette in hand, an act that seemed performed on short notice.

"You don't look as if you'd been in the country, old fellow," Deronda noted, observing Hans's gaunt appearance. "Is it Cambridge?"

"No," Hans replied curtly, dropping his palette with a sudden, clumsy motion. "I've been to 'I-don't-know-where.' No Man's Land. A mortally unpleasant country it is."

"You don't mean you've been drinking, Hans?" Deronda asked, sitting anxiously opposite.

"Not so good. I smoked opium. I intended to try it sometime to measure its bliss, and finding myself out of other joys, I thought it prudent to seize the chance. But I pledge you my word, I shall never touch that bliss again. It disagrees with my constitution."

"What troubled you? You wrote in high spirits."

"Oh, nothing specific. The world began to look seedy, like a cabbage garden with all the heads cut down. A malady of genius, I'm sure. And frankly, I was tired of being virtuous without reward, especially in this stifling London heat."

"Nothing else? No real vexation?"

Hans shook his head. "I came to discuss my affairs, but I cannot do so gracefully if you intend to hide yours."

"I have no affairs in the world," Hans declared lightly, "save a quarrel with a trinket dealer. Besides, since this is the first time you've spoken of your matters, you are only beginning to pay a long debt."

Deronda sensed artificiality in Hans's demeanor but trusted that frankness would return. "You laughed at the mystery of my journey to Italy," Deronda began. "It touched the roots of my happiness. I knew nothing of my parents, and I went to Genoa to meet my mother. My father died when I was an infant; my mother was the daughter of an eminent Jew, and my father was her cousin. Long before I left, probability pointed to this origin. I was prepared for the result, and glad to find myself a Jew."

"You need not expect me to feign surprise, Deronda," Hans said, shifting his posture to examine the heel of his slipper. "My mother told me. She went to the house the morning after you visited—the brother and sister informed her. You may imagine we cannot rejoice as they do. But whatever brings you joy, I shall eventually join in, *when* exactly that hour arrives, I cannot predict." Hans lowered his voice, a tone usually reserved for bad moods, yet devoid of fuss.

"I understand you cannot share my feelings," Deronda conceded, "yet I could not let silence grow between us on a matter casting new light on my future. I have adopted some of Mordecai's ideas and intend to strive for them, as far as one man's effort permits. I suspect I shall soon travel to the East and be gone for years."

Hans said nothing, rising to mix paint on his palette, his back to Deronda. The air grew heavy, charged with an irritating stillness. Presently, Hans spoke softly, without turning. "Excuse the question, but does Mrs. Grandcourt know of all this?"

"No!" Deronda snapped, anger flaring. "I beg you, Hans, cease jesting on that subject. Your notions are wide of the truth; they are the exact reverse."

"I am no more inclined to jest than I shall be at my own funeral," Hans retorted. "But I am not entirely sure you realize what those notions are."

"Perhaps not," Deronda admitted. "But let me say, once for all, that in relation to Mrs. Grandcourt, I have never held, and never shall hold, the position of a lover. If you have interpreted anything as proof of affection, you are supremely mistaken."

Silence lingered, thick and uncomfortable. Then Hans resumed. "Perhaps I have been mistaken in another interpretation, as well."

"What is that?"

"That you had no desire to occupy the position of a lover toward another woman, who is neither wife nor widow."

Deronda felt a sting. "I cannot pretend not to understand you, Meyrick. It is painful that our wishes should clash. I hope you will tell me if you have grounds to believe you might succeed."

"All thoughts, all passions, all delights,
Whatever stirs this mortal frame,
All are but ministers of Love,
And feed his sacred flame."

A fire burned within Daniel Deronda, hotter and more urgent than ever before. Hans Cohen's words regarding Mirah's need for relief from jealousy had acted as the final spark, igniting a resolve that left him trembling with anticipation. On his next visit to Ezra Levin, Deronda arrived with a singular, unyielding purpose: to secure a private audience with Mirah. If she returned his love, he believed he possessed the courage to face whatever consequences followed. More importantly, as her betrothed husband, he envisioned gaining a protective authority over her—a shield against the inevitable storms that threatened to batter her fragile existence, particularly in her fraught relationship with her father.

Though Deronda observed no outward signs of restlessness in Lapidoth, nor any waning effort from the old man to court his daughter, a deep foreboding gnawed at Daniel's heart. He sensed a future struggle looming, a potential mortification or an intolerable surge of domestic discord that could render Ezra and Mirah helpless victims. These anxieties would have grown sharper had Deronda understood the turmoil churning beneath Lapidoth's surface. What appeared to Ezra as a petty, desultory torment was, for Lapidoth, a grueling submission to restraint, endured only because he viewed it as a temporary price for eventual, unbridled freedom.

Lapidoth had originally intended to wait for a favorable opportunity, perhaps an occasion to extract a substantial sum from Deronda. Yet, driven by an imperious gambling instinct that wove a relentless web through his life, he found his curiosity sharpening into a desperate hunt. He was scouring the household, his mind fixated on locating Mirah's hidden money and keys. Mirah, however, possessed a practical clarity that thwarted him; having entrusted all her savings except for immediate necessities to Mrs. Meyrick's care, she created a fortress of safety around her resources. To Lapidoth, this felt like the stifling security of a lunatic asylum, where every outlet was blocked. In his mind, secretly appropriating a few notes from a desk would have been a harmless domestic right, a liberty men often stretch far beyond the legal boundaries. He felt grievously treated, convinced he deserved access to her earnings as readily as he had once devoured her apple tarts. Yet, despite his grievances, he remained submissive. The temptation to appeal directly to Deronda, offering his services in exchange for funds to buy his way out, grew strong. He reasoned that clever people with nothing to sell could command high prices for their absence, and surely Deronda would welcome the chance to be rid of him. But a lingering, almost superstitious awe of Ezra's formidable friend held him back, causing him to defer his plans indefinitely.

That afternoon, however, circumstances aligned with a cruel irony. Deronda entered the room buoyed by a gladdened consciousness, his spirits so high they illuminated his very demeanor. Simultaneously, Lapidoth reached a crisis point, his mind consumed by schemes of escape, and Deronda's newfound amiability only fed these delusions. So preoccupied was the old man that he failed to engage with the reading material placed before him, eventually rising to smoke and wander in the square. This sudden silence freed the two friends. Though Mirah was absent, Deronda knew she would return before his departure, and a secret, glowing hope filled him. He anticipated seeing a sweet, newfound recognition in her eyes, a warmth previously denied to him. His manner toward Ezra became playfully affectionate.

"This little room is too close for you, Ezra," Deronda remarked, pausing his reading. "The heat of this season here rivals Genoa's worst, yet you sit in shade while I bake. You deserve a better home. As the stronger half, I shall arrange things as I please." He smiled warmly at Ezra, who replied, "I am straitened for nothing save breath. You, who might roam a spacious palace, find yourself in a narrow cage. Yet, I cannot bid you 'go.'"

"Oh, the country would feel like exile while you remain," Deronda said, rising to pace the cramped space, fanning himself with a handkerchief. "To me, this is the happiest room in the world. Besides, I shall imagine myself in the East, preparing for my journey there someday." He spoke with emphasis, removing his heavy cravat and a large ring, placing them on a small table behind Ezra, whose desk was cluttered with books and papers.

"I have worn this ring since returning home," Deronda continued, resettling himself. "Yet, I am such a Sybarite that I constantly cast it off as a burden during tasks. I understand why Romans wore summer rings—if they did. Now, I shall focus better."

Soon, they were lost in their work. Deronda read a piece of Rabbinical Hebrew under Ezra's correction, ignoring the subtle shift in the atmosphere until Lapidoth re-entered, seating himself inconspicuously in the background.

Lapidoth's wandering eyes snapped to the table, where the ring sparkled on the dark wood. During his walk, his mind had been rehearsing a fantasy: approaching Deronda privately to request a sum for travel, expecting an immediate inquiry about the amount. This scenario haunted him, torn between the fear of asking too little and the regret of asking too much. His desire knew no limits, and he lacked a gauge for Deronda's generosity. But now, amidst these airy preparations, the ring lay there, detached and within easy reach. Its value was modest compared to his imagined fortunes, yet it presented itself as a solid, tangible fact. A lightning thought struck him—not yet an intention, but a flicker of madness: if he quietly pocketed the ring and slipped away, he would possess the means for comfortable escape, free from trouble or danger. Since it was Deronda's property, and Deronda would never prosecute his own "children" for theft, the act would pass unnoticed. The images followed each other rapidly, coalescing into a single vision. Lapidoth had never committed larceny, though legally, taking this item from a virtual relative who might otherwise give freely would technically constitute theft. Still, the prospect of a heavier gift, obtained swiftly, outweighed the risk. The mental image of grasping the ring repeated like an internal melody, only to sink back into rejection. Satisfied his urgent longing by deciding to descend and watch for Deronda's departure, he rose and stood by the window. He calculated the short path to the door beside the table, resolved to execute his plan the moment he joined Deronda outside.

However, a dominance of desire, akin to a drinker's thirst, overrode his conscious resolve. As he passed the table, his fingers fell silently upon the ring. Before he realized it, he stood in the hallway, the ring clutched in his hand. Without hesitation, he donned his hat and fled the house. The possibility of returning to his children receded into the distant fog, replaced by a frantic concentration on selling the ring and boarding a ship immediately.

Inside, Deronda and Ezra barely noticed his exit. Soon after, Mirah entered, causing a genuine disruption. Her hat remained on her head. Rising to greet her, Deronda found her confused, troubled by a sensation she could not name.

"I only came to ensure Ezra has his new draught," she stammered. "I must go straight to Mrs. Meyrick's to retrieve something."

"Pray, allow me to walk with you," Deronda urged earnestly. "I tire Ezra enough already, and my brain feels like melted wax. I wish to accompany you to Mrs. Meyrick's."

"Oh, yes," Mirah murmured, blushing deeper. A vague, electric feeling moved through her, marking something new in Deronda. Turning away to prepare the medicine, she poured Ezra's draught while he leaned back, eyes closed, unable to shake the troubling ideas that had filled his mind during the reading session. For a moment, Deronda stood lost in thought, envisioning the walk, until Mirah returned with the medicine. Then, suddenly remembering his discarded cravat, he excused his disheveled state, retrieved it from the table, and cried out in violent surprise, "Good heavens! Where is my ring?"

He began searching frantically on the floor. Ezra glanced around the corner of his chair. Mirah, swift as thought, moved to where Deronda searched, asking, "Did you lay it down?"

"Yes," Deronda replied, blind to any alternative explanation save that the ring had fallen into the shadows of the variegated carpet. He moved furniture pieces, digging with his hands and squinting.

But a different truth had seized Mirah, draining the color from her cheeks. She leaned close to Ezra's ear and whispered, "Was my father here?" Ezra nodded, his expression terrible with understanding. Mirah darted back to Deronda, who was still casting his eyes in hopeless exploration. "You have not found it?" she asked hurriedly.

Meeting her terrified gaze, Deronda's alarm surged. "Perhaps I put it in my pocket," he lied, pretending to feel for it.

Mirah watched him closely. "It is not there? You put it on the table!" Her voice was sharp, piercing through his deception, refusing to let him pretend to find it in his pocket. Immediately, she rushed from the room.

Deronda followed. She ran downstairs to the sitting room to find her father, checking the bedroom door, then the place where his hat usually hung. Returning, she stood with hands clas

—WORDSWORTH: *The Prelude*.

Sir Hugo carried out his plan of spending part of the autumn at Diplow, and by the beginning of October his presence was spreading some cheerfulness in the neighborhood, among all ranks and persons concerned, from the stately home of Brackenshaw and Quetcham to the respectable shop-parlors in Wanchester. For Sir Hugo was a man who liked to show himself and be affable, a Liberal of good lineage, who confided entirely in reform as not likely to make any serious difference in English habits of feeling, one of which undoubtedly is the liking to behold society well fenced and adorned with hereditary rank. Hence he made Diplow a most agreeable house, extending his invitations to old Wanchester solicitors and young village curates, but also taking some care in the combination of the guests, and not feeding all the common poultry together, so that they should think their meal no particular compliment. Easy-going Lord Brackenshaw, for example, would not mind meeting Robinson the attorney, but Robinson would have been naturally piqued if he had been asked to meet a set of people who passed for his equals. On all these points Sir Hugo was well informed enough at once to gain popularity for himself and give pleasure to others—two results which eminently suited his disposition. The rector of Pennicote now found a reception at Diplow very different from the haughty tolerance he had undergone during the reign of Grandcourt. It was not that the baronet liked Mr. Gascoigne; it was that he desired to keep up a marked relation of friendliness with him on account of Mrs. Grandcourt, for whom Sir Hugo's chivalry had become more and more engaged. Why? The chief reason was one that he could not fully communicate, even to Lady Mallinger—for he would not tell what he thought one woman's secret to another, even though the other was his wife—which shows that his chivalry included a rare reticence.

Deronda, after he had become engaged to Mirah, felt it right to make a full statement of his position and purposes to Sir Hugo, and he chose to make it by letter. He had more than a presentiment that his fatherly friend would feel some dissatisfaction, if not pain, at this turn of his destiny. In reading unwelcome news, instead of hearing it, there is the advantage that one avoids a hasty expression of impatience which may afterward be repented of. Deronda dreaded that verbal collision which makes otherwise pardonable feeling lastingly offensive.

And Sir Hugo, though not altogether surprised, was thoroughly vexed. His immediate resource was to take the letter to Lady Mallinger, who would be sure to express an astonishment which her husband could argue against as unreasonable, and in this way divide the stress of his discontent. And in fact when she showed herself astonished and distressed that all Daniel's wonderful talents, and the comfort of having him in the house, should have ended in his going mad in this way about the Jews, the baronet could say,

"Oh, nonsense, my dear! depend upon it, Dan will not make a fool of himself. He has large notions about Judaism—political views which you can't understand. No fear but Dan will keep himself head uppermost."

But with regard to the prospective marriage she afforded him no counter-irritant. The gentle lady observed, without rancor, that she had little dreamed of what was coming when she had Mirah to sing at her musical party and give lessons to Amabel. After some hesitation, indeed, she confessed it _had_ passed through her mind that after a proper time Daniel might marry Mrs. Grandcourt—because it seemed so remarkable that he should be at Genoa just at that time—and although she herself was not fond of widows she could not help thinking that such a marriage would have been better than his going altogether with the Jews. But Sir Hugo was so strongly of the same opinion that he could not correct it as a feminine mistake; and his ill-humor at the disproof of his disagreeable conclusions on behalf of Gwendolen was left without vent. He desired Lady Mallinger not to breathe a word about the affair till further notice, saying to himself, "If it is an unkind cut to the poor thing (meaning Gwendolen), the longer she is without knowing it the better, in her present nervous state. And she will best learn it from Dan himself." Sir Hugo's conjectures had worked so industriously with his knowledge, that he fancied himself well informed concerning the whole situation.

Meanwhile his residence with his family at Diplow enabled him to continue his fatherly attentions to Gwendolen; and in these Lady Mallinger, notwithstanding her small liking for widows, was quite willing to second him.

The plan of removal to Offendene had been carried out; and Gwendolen, in settling there, maintained a calm beyond her mother's hopes. She was experiencing some of that peaceful melancholy which comes from the renunciation of demands for self, and from taking the ordinary good of existence, and especially kindness, even from a dog, as a gift above expectation. Does one who has been all but lost in a pit of darkness complain of the sweet air and the daylight? There is a way of looking at our life daily as an escape, and taking the quiet return of morn and evening—still more the star-like out-glowing of some pure fellow-feeling, some generous impulse breaking our inward darkness—as a salvation that reconciles us to hardship. Those who have a self-knowledge prompting such self-accusation as Hamlet's, can understand this habitual feeling of rescue. And it was felt by Gwendolen as she lived through and through again the terrible history of her temptations, from their first form of illusory self-pleasing when she struggled away from the hold of conscience, to their latest form of an urgent hatred dragging her toward its satisfaction, while she prayed and cried for the help of that conscience which she had once forsaken. She was now dwelling on every word of Deronda's that pointed to her past deliverance from the worst evil in herself, and the worst infliction of it on others, and on every word that carried a force to resist self-despair.

But she was also upborne by the prospect of soon seeing him again: she did not imagine him otherwise than always within her reach, her supreme need of him blinding her to the separateness of his life, the whole scene of which she filled with his relation to her—no unique preoccupation of Gwendolen's, for we are all apt to fall into this passionate egoism of imagination, not only toward our fellow-men, but toward God. And the future which she turned her face to with a willing step was one where she would be continually assimilating herself to some type that he would hold before her. Had he not first risen on her vision as a corrective presence which she had recognized in the beginning with resentment, and at last with entire love and trust? She could not spontaneously think of an end to that reliance, which had become to her imagination like the firmness of the earth, the only condition of her walking.

And Deronda was not long before he came to Diplow, which was a more convenient distance from town than the Abbey. He had wished to carry out a plan for taking Ezra and Mirah to a mild spot on the coast, while he prepared another home which Mirah might enter as his bride, and where they might unitedly watch over her brother. But Ezra begged not to be removed, unless it were to go with them to the East. All outward solicitations were becoming more and more of a burden to him; but his mind dwelt on the possibility of this voyage with a visionary joy. Deronda, in his preparations for the marriage, which he hoped might not be deferred beyond a couple of months, wished to have fuller consultation as to his resources and affairs generally with Sir Hugo, and here was a reason for not delaying his visit to Diplow. But he thought quite as much of another reason—his promise to Gwendolen. The sense of blessedness in his own lot had yet an aching anxiety at his heart: this may be held paradoxical, for the beloved lover is always called happy, and happiness is considered as a well-fleshed indifference to sorrow outside it. But human experience is usually paradoxical, if that means incongruous with the phrases of current talk or even current philosophy. It was no treason to Mirah, but a part of that full nature which made his love for her the more worthy, that his joy in her could hold by its side the care for another. For what is love itself, for the one we love best?—an enfolding of immeasurable cares which yet are better than any joys outside our love.

Deronda came twice to Diplow, and saw Gwendolen twice—and yet he went back to town without having told her anything about the change in his lot and prospects. He blamed himself; but in all momentous communication likely to give pain we feel dependent on some preparatory turn of words or associations, some agreement of the other's mood with the probable effect of what we have to impart. In the first interview Gwendolen was so absorbed in what she had to say to him, so full of questions which he must answer, about the arrangement of her life, what she could do to make herself less ignorant, how she could be kindest to everybody, and make amends for

In the intricate tapestry of human life, where every season blends seamlessly into another like the eternal days of myth, joys and sorrows coexist in a single breath. While one hand gathers the ripe fruit, another scatters seeds into the earth; where one cultivates the vine, another crushes the grapes underfoot. Thus, within the span of a single existence, harvest and bloom remain eternally intertwined, until the Great Gardener calls us home and plants us afresh in His unseen fields.

Yet, among all the gifts of affection, none surpasses the profound solace of binding one's beloved life to our own. It grants us the sacred duty to guard their happiness, to transform hardship into comfort, and to turn the bitter wells of past suffering into springs of pure joy. This protective love burned brightly within Deronda for Mirah. Even as a child, her steps had stumbled upon sharp stones, and when he first laid eyes upon her young countenance, it appeared to him as a portrait of utter despair.

But now, she radiated like a flower of deep violet petals edged with pale ivory, blooming in the warm sun of contentment. To her, every shadow of grief belonged to a life shared with Deronda—a union she could name only "good." He marveled at the steady, radiant cheer that illuminated her very movements, at the peaceful stillness of her resting postures, and felt a swelling pride within himself that saving her from pain was a treasure sufficient unto itself. She knew nothing of Hans's internal battle nor of Gwendolen's heartache. Since Deronda's secret devotion to her had been revealed, she interpreted Gwendolen's anxious concern merely as the gratitude of a dependent friend, a perspective reinforced by Deronda's gentle, distant allusions to Mrs. Grandcourt. Mirah was ready to accept his word that he had been a rescuer to many souls; the only mystery to her lay in why destiny had chosen her above all others to walk constantly beside him.

Thus, when the bridal veil descended upon Mirah, it concealed no tremor of doubt, but only a holy awe at receiving a magnificent gift that demanded mighty stewardship. Under the velvet canopy stood perhaps the fairest pair ever united, worthy of prayers for offspring whose hearts beat in truth. Their lips spoke the marriage vows with an unblemished sincerity, and the blessing they invoked promised a fulfillment forged in the iron strength of their mutual pledge. By the ancient rites of their faith, they were wed. And just as no feast is truly grand unless it welcomes the kin of the heart, few would take offense to learn that the guest list included the entire Cohen household, minus the tiny infant who happily chattered through her teething troubles at home. How could Mordecai bear the thought of excluding those who had shared his hardships from sharing his triumph?

Mrs. Meyrick, having long ago conquered her biases, arrived alongside her three daughters, all buoyed by the knowledge that Mirah's marriage to Deronda had turned their family's history into a cherished romance. Each woman, mother and daughter alike, felt a generous swell of pride for having given so freely of themselves to the girl who needed them. They mused that if Hans could have been present, it would have been ideal, but Mab wisely noted that men often pay the price for being inconvenient. Perhaps Kate and Amy had loved Mr. Deronda too? Yet, being women, they spared themselves the ridicule of such folly.

Their sacrifices were honored by a speech from Mr. Cohen, possessing a rare quality of authenticity that defied convention. Jacob, eating with youthful vigor, contributed a string of soft, whinnying laughter to accompany his father's words—not irreverently, but with a lively sense that his family was shining. Adelaide Rebekah, clad in a fresh Sabbath gown, maintained a stately air of solemn responsibility throughout.

Mordecai's brilliant eyes, sunken deeply within their large sockets, swept the room with the benevolent warmth of a spirit already lifted to an aloof plane where selfish desires held no sway, leaving only pure sympathy alive. Yet, inevitably, his gaze drifted back to Deronda, alighting there with a renewed gleam of trusting affection.

The wedding feast was humble, yet Mirah departed with splendid gifts. Friends who had heard of the betrothal had devised gracious tokens. Sir Hugo and Lady Mallinger presented a complete outfit for Eastern travels, along with a precious locket inscribed, *"To the bride of our dear Daniel Deronda all blessings. H. and L. M."* The Klesmers offered a flawless watch, similarly adorned with a tender message.

But far more valuable than gold or jewels arrived for Deronda from the neighborhood of Diplow on the morning of his nuptials. It was a letter bearing these words:

"Do not think of me sorrowfully on your wedding-day. I have remembered your words—that I may live to be one of the best of women, who make others glad that they were born. I do not yet see how that can be, but you know better than I. If it ever comes true, it will be because you helped me. I only thought of myself, and I made you grieve. It hurts me now to think of your grief. You must not grieve any more for me. It is better—it shall be better with me because I have known you.

Preparations for the journey to the East commenced immediately, for Deronda could not refuse Ezra's wish that they depart at once, allowing the old man to travel with them rather than keeping them to watch over him. Deronda harbored little hope that Ezra's life would endure the voyage, for symptoms indicated that the final stage of his illness had arrived. Yet Ezra himself had declared, "Never mind where I die, so that I am with you."

However, Deronda did not set sail with them. One bright morning, he told Deronda, "Do not quit me today. I shall die before it is ended."

He requested to be dressed and seated in his easy chair as was his custom, with Deronda and Mirah flanking him on either side. For several hours, he remained strangely silent, making no effort to speak, though occasionally he looked at them with eyes filled with a restful, meaningful tranquility, as if to assure them that while this remaining fragment of time was difficult, an ocean of peace lay beneath him.

It was not until late in the afternoon, as the light faded, that he took hold of each hand in his own. Looking steadily at Deronda, he whispered, "Death is coming to me as the divine kiss which is both parting and reunion—which takes me from your bodily eyes and gives me full presence in your soul. Where thou goest, Daniel, I shall go. Is it not begun? Have I not breathed my soul into you? We shall live together."

He paused, and Deronda waited, sensing that further words might follow. But slowly, with evident effort, Ezra pressed their hands, raised himself, and uttered the Hebrew confession of the Divine Unity, a prayer that for generations has lingered on the lips of the dying Israelite. Then he sank gently back into his chair, saying no more. It was many hours before he ceased to breathe, cradled between the arms of Mirah and Deronda.

"Nothing is here for tears, nothing to wail
Or knock the breast; no weakness, no contempt,
Dispraise or blame; nothing but well and fair,
And what may quiet us in a death so noble."
